### [1] [Persona Without Substrate: Regime-Dependence and the LLM Individuation Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00006)
*Shuaizhi Cheng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper empirically challenges the assumption that the same representational vector direction maintains identical content across different LLM modification regimes (prompting, fine-tuning, steering), using persona-topology experiments to show these regimes produce structurally distinct representations. It proposes "regime-indexed individuation" where a representational unit is a (vehicle, regime) pair.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Beckmann & Butlin's ontological framework for individuating LLM representations relies on an unexamined cross-regime co-reference assumption inherited from persona-vectors literature: that a given direction in activation space picks out the same content regardless of whether it is accessed via prompt-conditioning, gradient-descent fine-tuning, or inference-time steering. This paper aims to test and ultimately undermine that assumption.

Method: The authors conduct persona-topology experiments on Qwen3-4B-Instruct and Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.2, deploying four empirical interventions: (1) comparing collinearity of prompt-extracted persona vectors with fine-tuned representation basins; (2) measuring displacement magnitudes when fictional vs. real persona anchors are applied; (3) testing contradictory-valenced persona mixtures to identify training-history-determined attractor biases; and (4) probing asymmetry between inference-time vector arithmetic and fine-tune-time chimera training for compositional representations.

Result: All four experiments reveal structural mismatches across regimes: prompt-extracted vectors are non-collinear with fine-tune basins; fictional personas displace the model more strongly along real-anchor directions than real anchors themselves; contradictory mixtures are biased toward attractors shaped by training history; and compositional algebra behaves asymmetrically under inference-time arithmetic versus fine-tune-time chimera training. These findings jointly refute the cross-regime co-reference assumption.

Conclusion: The authors propose regime-indexed individuation: the identity unit for representational content is a (vehicle, regime) pair rather than a vehicle alone. This dissolves Beckmann & Butlin's three-way debate by recasting their candidate positions as describing three distinct regime-internal objects instead of competing for a single referent. The same diagnosis extends to related frameworks by Mollo & Millière, Chalmers, and Cerullo.

Abstract: Beckmann & Butlin's (2026) ontological framework for the LLM individuation problem inherits an unargued cross-regime co-reference assumption from the persona-vectors literature: that the same direction picks out the same content under prompt-conditioning, gradient-descent fine-tuning, and inference-time steering. We present four empirical wedges from persona-topology experiments on Qwen3-4B-Instruct and Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.2 - non-collinearity of prompt-extracted vectors and fine-tune basins; fictional personas displacing the model along real-anchor directions more strongly than real anchors do; contradictory-valenced mixtures biased toward a training-history-determined attractor; and asymmetric compositional algebra under inference-time arithmetic versus fine-tune-time chimera training - that jointly undermine the assumption. We propose regime-indexed individuation: the identity unit for representational content is a (vehicle, regime) pair, not a vehicle alone. Under this framework, Beckmann & Butlin's three candidate positions describe three different regime-internal objects rather than competing for the same referent; the same diagnosis applies to Mollo & Millière, Chalmers, and Cerullo.

</details>


### [2] [Controllable Narrative Rendering for Enhanced Assisted Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00009)
*Mingzhe Lu,Yanbing Liu,Jiayue Wu,Jiarui Zhang,Qihao Wang,Yue Hu,Yunpeng Li,Yangyan Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Loom is an assisted writing framework that resolves the tension between narrative fidelity and descriptive intensity in LLM-based creative writing by separating story structure from discourse generation via an intent-centered, three-layer pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs exhibit a binary failure in creative writing: they either produce safe, superficial edits ("remedial polishing") or uncontrolled plot expansions that distort the original narrative. This creates a fundamental trade-off between preserving narrative fidelity and achieving rich descriptive intensity.

Method: Loom uses a three-layer pipeline based on the narratological story/discourse distinction. It employs an intent-centered semiotic chain-of-thought to control narrative intent and rendering density. The architecture decouples perceptual material generation from syntactic insertion, preserving the original event structure while enhancing description.

Result: Loom achieves the highest overall quality score in evaluations using both LLM-based metrics and human assessment, with substantial gains in factual integrity and descriptive intensity over state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: By grounding assisted writing in narratological theory and separating content generation from surface realization, Loom successfully resolves the fundamental tension between fidelity and intensity that plagues existing LLM-based writing tools.

Abstract: Despite the remarkable proficiency of large language models (LLMs) in basic writing assistance, their utility in creative writing is fundamentally hindered by a persistent binary failure. This issue manifests as an oscillation between safe, surface-level editing, referred to as remedial polishing, and destructive, uncontrolled plot expansion. This dilemma defines a critical trade-off between narrative fidelity and descriptive intensity. We propose Loom, an assisted writing framework grounded in the narratological distinction between story and discourse. Loom employs a three-layer pipeline that operationalizes an intent-centered semiotic chain-of-thought to enforce precise control over narrative intent and rendering density. This architecture separates the generation of perceptual material from syntactic insertion, ensuring that enhancement occurs without violating the original event structure. Our comprehensive evaluation, which includes LLM-based metrics and human assessment, demonstrates that Loom successfully resolves this fundamental tension. Loom achieves the highest overall quality score, yielding substantial gains in factual integrity and descriptive intensity compared to state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [3] [Harnessing the Latent Space: From Steering Vectors to Model Calibrators for Control and Trust](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00083)
*Nishant Subramani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper explores how to leverage the latent spaces of large language models (LLMs) for two purposes: controlling model behavior via steering vectors and calibrating trust via latent-space-based calibrators, ultimately aiming to demystify model internals and build more trustworthy language technology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs scale to trillions of parameters, their internal representations become increasingly opaque. With millions of users relying on these models for tool interaction and high-stakes decision-making, there is an urgent need to understand and control model behavior, and to determine when outputs can be trusted.

Method: The authors propose two complementary approaches: (1) steering vectors that manipulate the latent space to control model behavior, and (2) latent space-based model calibrators that assess the trustworthiness of model outputs by analyzing internal representations.

Result: The methods contribute to demystifying the latent spaces of language models, offering both control mechanisms and trust-calibration tools grounded in model internals rather than surface-level outputs.

Conclusion: Harnessing latent spaces through steering vectors and calibrators provides a promising path toward more interpretable, controllable, and trustworthy language models, addressing critical reliability concerns as these systems are deployed in real-world, high-stakes environments.

Abstract: Language models have changed from unreliable text generators to highly-capable large models with trillions of parameters. Capability increases come hand-in-hand with increases in scale, making understanding the internal representations of models more challenging. Since millions of users increasing rely on language models to interact with external tools or make decisions in medium or high-stakes scenarios, we need to establish control over model behavior and know when to trust model outputs. In this paper, we discuss our contributions on harnessing the latent spaces by proposing steering vectors for control and developing latent space-based model calibrators for trust. Together, our contributions help demystify the latent spaces of language models and offer new insights into how to harness model internals to build more trustworthy language technology.

</details>


### [4] [Benchmarking Frontier LLMs on Arabic Cultural and Sociolinguistic Knowledge: A Cross-Evaluation Framework with Human SME Ground Truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00139)
*Sajjad Abdoli,Ghassan Al-Sumaidaee,Ahmad ElShiekh,Clayton W. Taylor,Ahmed Rashad*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces a cross-evaluation framework for assessing LLMs on Arabic sociolinguistic knowledge (Egyptian and Iraqi dialects), using native-speaker SMEs to create 103 prompt-rubric pairs and human-grade 302 responses from three frontier LLMs. Five automated judges are then evaluated against human grades using MAD and Signed Mean Error, revealing systematic leniency bias, greater difficulty grading cultural vs. linguistic tasks, and implicit cultural reasoning as the primary failure mode for automated grading.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human expert evaluation is prohibitively expensive for specialized, high-stakes domains, particularly for Arabic sociolinguistics where grading demands both linguistic fluency and deep cultural familiarity. Existing surface-level metrics fail to capture this, creating a bottleneck for deploying LLMs in underrepresented dialect communities.

Method: A cross-evaluation framework with: (1) 103 validated prompt-rubric pairs authored by native-speaker SMEs (70 Egyptian, 33 Iraqi; 53 Cultural, 50 Linguistic) using penalty-weighted rubrics; (2) three frontier LLMs graded on 302 prompt-response pairs; (3) five frontier LLMs as automated judges with provider-level self-evaluation guard; (4) dual-metric evaluation combining Mean Absolute Deviation (MAD) and Signed Mean Error across 1,307 judge evaluations to separate directional bias from symmetric noise.

Result: GPT-5.4 is the most reliable judge (MAD=10.21 pp, Signed Error=-1.12%). Four of five judges exhibit systematic leniency (+2.01% to +6.56%). Cultural tasks are harder to grade than Linguistic tasks for all judges (MAD gap 1.83-4.78 pp). Models outperform on Egyptian vs. Iraqi prompts, but leniency differences between SME groups prevent attributing this solely to model knowledge. Implicit cultural reasoning is the dominant failure mode across all judge models.

Conclusion: Automated LLM judging for Arabic sociolinguistic evaluation remains unreliable due to systematic leniency bias and persistent failure on implicit cultural reasoning tasks. The cross-evaluation framework with penalty-weighted rubrics and dual-metric analysis provides a rigorous methodology, but the Egyptian-Iraqi performance gap cannot be conclusively attributed to model knowledge given inter-rater leniency variation among human SMEs.

Abstract: The cost of human expert evaluation is a principal bottleneck to deploying language models in specialized, high-stakes domains. This is particularly acute for Arabic sociolinguistic knowledge: credible grading requires not only linguistic fluency but deep cultural familiarity that cannot be approximated by surface-level metrics. We address this with a cross-evaluation framework instantiated on two underrepresented Arabic dialect communities: Egyptian and Iraqi Arabic. We contribute 103 validated prompt-rubric pairs (70 Egyptian, 33 Iraqi; 53 Cultural, 50 Linguistic), authored and graded by native-speaker SMEs using penalty-weighted rubrics distinguishing positive content requirements from answer-specific negative error criteria. Three frontier LLMs serve as target models (graded by human SMEs across 302 unique prompt-response pairs), while five frontier LLMs serve as automated judges enforcing a provider-level self-evaluation guard. A dual-metric scheme combining Mean Absolute Deviation (MAD) with Signed Mean Error separates directional grading bias from symmetric noise. Across 1,307 judge evaluations: GPT-5.4 is the most reliable judge (MADj = 10.21 pp, Signed Error = -1.12%); four of five judges show systematic leniency (+2.01% to +6.56%); Cultural tasks are harder to grade than Linguistic tasks for all judges (MAD gap 1.83-4.78 pp); and models substantially outperform on Egyptian prompts compared to Iraqi prompts. However, given leniency differences between Iraqi and Egyptian SMEs, we cannot solely attribute this gap to model knowledge. We therefore emphasize findings that do not assume identical leniency across human graders. Across all samples, implicit cultural reasoning -- requiring models to simulate native-speaker judgment rather than rely on lexical verification -- emerges as the primary failure mode for automated grading across all judge models.

</details>


### [5] [Hate Speech Detection in Turkish and Arabic Languages: A Comprehensive Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00143)
*Somaiyeh Dehghan,Gökçe Uludoğan,Mehmet Umut Şen,Elif Erol,Arzucan Özgür,Berrin Yanikoglu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a comprehensive hate speech dataset covering five Turkish topics and one Arabic topic on refugees, and develops BERT-based models for multi-dimensional hate speech analysis including category classification, intensity prediction, target identification, and span detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online hate speech is linked to rising violence against minorities globally, yet content moderation efforts — particularly for underrepresented languages like Turkish and Arabic — lack comprehensive datasets and tools for nuanced, multi-dimensional analysis. The authors seek to balance freedom of expression with effective moderation by enabling deeper understanding of hateful content.

Method: The authors construct a multi-topic hate speech dataset in Turkish (refugees, Israel-Palestine conflict, anti-Greek sentiment, ethnic/religious communities, LGBTI+) and Arabic (refugees). They then develop state-of-the-art BERT-based models addressing four analytical dimensions: hate category classification, hate intensity prediction, target identification, and hate speech span detection.

Result: The paper delivers a novel, annotated hate speech corpus and BERT-based models capable of performing multi-dimensional analysis across the four defined tasks, enabling comprehensive detection and characterization of hateful online discourse in Turkish and Arabic.

Conclusion: The proposed dataset and models provide a robust foundation for automated hate speech moderation in under-resourced languages, facilitating more nuanced and granular content analysis that can inform platform policies and societal responses to online hate.

Abstract: Online hate speech has been linked to a global rise in violence against minorities, including incidents such as mass shootings, lynchings, and ethnic cleansing. Societies grappling with this issue, particularly when hate speech targets specific groups based on religion, race, ethnicity, culture, nationality, or migration status, face the challenge of balancing freedom of expression with the need for effective content moderation on widely used online platforms. In response to this challenge, we introduce a comprehensive hate speech dataset covering five distinct topics in Turkish: refugees, the Israel-Palestine conflict, anti-Greek sentiment in Turkey, ethnic or religious communities (Alevis, Armenians, Arabs, Jews, and Kurds), and LGBTI+, alongside one topic in Arabic (refugees). In addition, we develop state-of-the-art BERT-based models to address multiple dimensions of hate speech analysis, including hate category classification, hate intensity prediction, target identification, and hate speech span detection, enabling a comprehensive understanding of hateful content in online discourse.

</details>


### [6] [Readable but Not Controllable: Neuron-Level Evidence for Medical LLM Hallucination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00158)
*Vijay Vankadaru,Asha Matthews,Tanya Roosta,Peyman Passban*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Medical LLM hallucination is readily detectable from internal representations (AUROC 0.77–0.86) via simple probes, with the signal being distributed and redundant across neurons. However, this decodability does not translate to controllability — steering the implicated neurons fails to reliably correct hallucinations, revealing a fundamental gap between what representations reveal and what they allow us to change.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work has focused on detecting hallucinations in medical LLMs, but it remains unclear whether the internal representations associated with hallucination can be used causally to control or correct hallucinatory outputs, rather than merely detect them.

Method: The authors use four open-source models evaluated across multiple medical question-answering datasets. They train linear probes on internal activations to detect hallucinations (measured by AUROC), analyze the distributed vs. localized nature of the signal through systematic vs. random neuron selection and low-dimensional random projections, and then test causal controllability by steering neurons most associated with hallucination across 16 model–dataset combinations.

Result: Probes achieve AUROC of 0.77–0.86 for hallucination detection. The signal is distributed and redundant: systematically selected neurons outperform random ones only at very small subset sizes; random subsets of a few hundred neurons recover nearly the full signal; low-dimensional random projections preserve most detection performance. Critically, across all 16 model–dataset combinations, there is a sharp gap between decodability and controllability — neuron-level steering fails to reliably correct hallucinations.

Conclusion: Hallucination in medical LLMs is readily visible in internal activations but not easily correctable by steering the most associated neurons. This points to a deeper separation between representational readout and causal manipulability, suggesting that mitigation is not simply a matter of identifying the right neurons.

Abstract: Hallucination remains one of the central obstacles to deploying medical LLMs. Yet, even when hallucination can be detected, it is still unclear whether the internal representations associated with it can be used for control rather than detection alone. Using four open-source models across a suite of medical question-answering datasets, we show that a simple, carefully conditioned probe can reliably detect hallucination, with AUROC scores between 0.77 and 0.86 in our case. We further show that this signal is distributed and redundant rather than narrowly localized. Systematically selected neurons outperform random neurons only at very small subset sizes, whereas random subsets of a few hundred neurons recover nearly the full signal, and low-dimensional random projections preserve most of the detection performance. Beyond detection, we test whether this representation is causally actionable. Across 16 model--dataset combinations, our results reveal a sharp gap between decodability and controllability. The same internal structure that makes hallucination easy to detect does not translate into reliable neuron-level control. These findings show that medical hallucination seems to be readily visible in internal activations, but not easily corrected by steering the neurons most associated with it. More broadly, our results suggest that hallucination mitigation is not simply a matter of identifying the right neurons, and point to a deeper separation between what representations reveal and what they allow us to change.

</details>


### [7] [From Personas to Plot: Character-Grounded Multi-Agent Story Generation for Long-Form Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00918)
*Aayush Aluru,Chloe Ho,Muhammad Hammouri,Kerry Luo,Myra Malik,Ryan Lagasse,Arjun Bahuguna,Vasu Sharma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MAGNET, a multi-agent goal-driven framework for long-form story generation, paired with ATLAS, a graph-based hallucination detection pipeline. MAGNET uses persona-grounded character agents with shared world-state tracking and evolving goals to generate coherent narratives, while ATLAS verifies consistency by comparing scene-level world representations. Evaluated against single-model prompts and IBSEN, MAGNET reduces annotations and hallucinations by 41% and 50% respectively over the baseline at 100-page scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with narrative consistency and coherent plot lines in long-form story generation. Existing approaches lack mechanisms for maintaining world-state consistency across extended narratives, leading to contradictions and hallucinations.

Method: MAGNET employs multiple persona-grounded character agents that propose actions driven by a shared world state and evolving story goals within a unified generation framework. ATLAS provides a complementary graph-based verification pipeline that compares scene-level world representations across the generated story to detect hallucinations and inconsistencies.

Result: At 100-page story generation, MAGNET reduced annotations (inconsistencies) by 41% and hallucinations by 50% compared to single-model prompting, and by 34% and 45% respectively compared to IBSEN. Pairwise rubric scoring corroborated these findings, showing MAGNET produces more coherent narratives.

Conclusion: Long-form narratives can effectively emerge from explicit world-state tracking and goal-driven multi-agent generation. This approach provides a foundation for controllable, structurally coherent long-form narrative generation.

Abstract: Although large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive creative fiction generation, they struggle to maintain narrative consistency and coherent plot lines in long-form stories. In this work, we introduce a unified framework for long-form narrative generation and verification. MAGNET, a multi-agent goal-driven narrative engine for storytelling, generates stories with persona-grounded character agents that propose actions based on a shared world state and evolving story goals, while ATLAS is a graph-based pipeline that compares scene-level world representations across a generated story to detect hallucinations. By evaluating MAGNET using an LLM editor, pairwise rubric scoring, and ATLAS, we show that our framework produces coherent narratives compared to single-model prompting and IBSEN. At 100 pages, MAGNET reduced annotations and hallucinations by 41 and 50%, respectively, compared to the single model baseline and by 34 and 45%, respectively, compared to IBSEN, with pairwise rubric evaluation showing similar results. These results suggest that long-form narratives can emerge from explicit world-state tracking and goal-driven multi-agent generation, providing a foundation for controllable and structurally coherent long-form narrative generation.

</details>


### [8] [Identifying and Resolving Pitfalls of Knowledge-Based VQA Benchmarks: Auditing, Repairing, and Augmenting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00159)
*Qian Ma,S M Rayeed,Charles V. Stewart,Qiong Wu,Yao Ma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Existing KB-VQA benchmarks systematically violate core assumptions underlying answer accuracy as a proxy for knowledge-grounded reasoning — answers are often missing/contradicted, questions are underspecified, and scenes lack visual ambiguity. The authors propose an audit-and-repair protocol and a multi-entity augmentation strategy, revealing distorted model rankings and overestimated reasoning capabilities under corrected evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Answer accuracy is treated as a proxy for knowledge-grounded reasoning in KB-VQA, but this relies on three overlooked assumptions: (1) annotated answers are derivable from the associated knowledge base, (2) questions are well-posed with sufficient constraints, and (3) visual settings meaningfully require grounded disambiguation. The authors argue these assumptions are systematically violated, rendering accuracy a misleading metric.

Method: (1) A principled audit of existing KB-VQA benchmarks (e.g., OK-VQA, A-OKVQA) to quantify violations of the three core assumptions — checking answer derivability from knowledge bases, question specificity, and visual complexity. (2) An audit-and-repair protocol that restores answer derivability and question clarity. (3) A controlled multi-entity augmentation protocol that introduces visual ambiguity by adding distractor entities to scenes, challenging initial retrieval and grounded reasoning. Re-evaluation is conducted under both corrected and augmented settings.

Result: The audit reveals substantial instances of missing or contradicted answers and underspecified questions. Existing datasets rely on visually trivial, single-entity scenes that bypass sophisticated visual-to-knowledge mapping. Even with controlled architectures, these flaws distort model rankings and overestimate reasoning capabilities. Re-evaluation under corrected and augmented settings yields markedly different performance trends.

Conclusion: Current KB-VQA benchmarks are unreliable for measuring knowledge-grounded reasoning due to systematic violations of core assumptions. The field needs interaction-aware benchmarks that prioritize verifiable reasoning over simple answer matching, alongside robust evaluation protocols that account for answer derivability, question specificity, and visual grounding demands.

Abstract: Knowledge-Based Visual Question Answering (KB-VQA) aims to evaluate whether Visual Language Models (VLMs) can retrieve, ground, and reason over external structured knowledge beyond visual evidence. In practice, answer accuracy is widely adopted as the primary evaluation metric, implicitly treating correctness as a proxy for knowledge-grounded reasoning. However, for existing KB-VQA benchmarks, this proxy relies on critical assumptions that are often overlooked and rendered unreliable by benchmark issues: annotated answer must be derivable from the associated knowledge base, question must be well-posed with sufficient constraints, and visual setting must meaningfully require grounded disambiguation. In this work, we show that these assumptions are systematically violated in existing KB-VQA benchmarks. Our audit reveals substantial instances with missing or contradicted answers and underspecified questions that render accuracy a misleading metric. Furthermore, we find that existing datasets rely on visually trivial, single-entity scenes that bypass the need for sophisticated visual-to-knowledge mapping. We demonstrate that even with controlled architectures, these flaws lead to distorted model rankings and overestimations of reasoning capabilities. To address this, we introduce (1) a principled audit-and-repair protocol that restores answer derivability and question clarity, and (2) a controlled multi-entity augmentation protocol that introduces visual ambiguity to challenge initial retrieval and grounded reasoning. Re-evaluation under corrected and augmented settings yields markedly different performance trends. Our findings call for rethinking evaluation protocols and designing more interaction-aware KB-VQA benchmarks that prioritize verifiable reasoning over simple matching.

</details>


### [9] [ALEE: Any-Language Evaluation of Embeddings via English-Centric Minimal Pairs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00171)
*Andrianos Michail,Stylianos Psychias,Michelle Wastl,Simon Clematide,Rico Sennrich,Juri Opitz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ALEE is a cross-lingual evaluation framework that uses Abstract Meaning Representations (AMR) to generate minimal pairs with controlled semantic shifts, enabling targeted diagnostics of text embedding models across 275+ languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current text embedding benchmarks are static, limited in language coverage, domain-specific, prone to overfitting, and poorly representative of low-resource languages. There is a need for a dynamic, fine-grained, and broadly multilingual evaluation framework.

Method: ALEE extends Sentence Smith to cross-lingual and paragraph levels by leveraging AMR to generate English minimal pairs with controlled semantic shifts. These pairs are then translated into target languages using parallel data, enabling diagnostic evaluation of embedding models across any language with English parallel corpora.

Result: Large-scale evaluation across diverse embedding models and 275+ languages reveals substantial performance variation across languages, text lengths, and linguistic phenomena. Performance gaps correlate with language prevalence in training resources and subword tokenization quality.

Conclusion: ALEE exposes persistent gaps in cross-lingual semantic representation, demonstrating that current embedding models struggle disproportionately with low-resource languages and that evaluation must account for language-specific factors and linguistic phenomena.

Abstract: Text embeddings are standard for semantic similarity tasks, yet their evaluation remains an open challenge. Current benchmarks are static, cover only a limited set of languages, are often domain-specific, susceptible to overfitting, and poorly representative of low-resource languages. To address these limitations, we introduce ALEE, a framework that extends Sentence Smith (Li et al., 2025) to the cross-lingual and paragraph level. ALEE uses Abstract Meaning Representations (AMR) to generate English minimal pairs with controlled, fine-grained semantic shifts, which are paired with translations in target languages. This approach enables targeted diagnostics for models in any language with English parallel data. We conduct a large-scale empirical study across a diverse set of embedding models and 275+ languages spanning three parallel datasets. On ALEE, performance varies substantially across languages, text lengths, and linguistic phenomena, exposing persistent gaps in cross-lingual semantic representation that track language prevalence in training resources and subword tokenization. We release ALEE at https://github.com/Andrian0s/any-lang-embed-eval

</details>


### [10] [Structural Pattern Mining in Inka Khipus: Unsupervised Clustering, Provenance Classification, and a Computational Validation of the Santa Valley Match](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00185)
*Maria Contreras*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A machine learning pipeline applied to 619 Inka khipus from the Open Khipu Repository reveals three structural clusters, achieves F1=0.86 for imperial provenance classification, and identifies cord twist direction as the dominant discriminative feature. The analysis also exposes colonial museum collection biases and computationally verifies the moiety structure of Santa Valley khipus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To apply reproducible, interpretable machine learning methods to the largest publicly available khipu database in order to uncover structural patterns, classify provenance, and both generate and verify archaeological hypotheses about the Inka recording system, which remains undeciphered.

Method: The authors engineer 27 structural features per khipu from the Open Khipu Repository (619 khipus, 54,403 cords, 110,677 knots). They apply: (1) unsupervised clustering via UMAP dimensionality reduction followed by HDBSCAN; (2) supervised provenance classification using gradient boosting; and (3) SHAP values for model interpretability. They also test knot-type n-grams as features. Computational verification of the Santa Valley recto/verso moiety hypothesis is performed using only public database records.

Result: Three structurally distinct khipu clusters were recovered (silhouette score = 0.769). Imperial (Inka Late Horizon) provenance classification achieved F1 = 0.86. SHAP analysis identified cord twist direction as the dominant structural discriminator. One cluster mapped to 19th-century European museum collections rather than a geographic region, revealing colonial acquisition bias. The Santa Valley moiety structure was independently verified, including identification of the single mixed specimen. Knot-type n-grams contributed no additional provenance signal beyond aggregate features (negative result).

Conclusion: Machine learning can meaningfully decode structural patterns in undeciphered khipus, supporting archaeological hypotheses while also exposing collection biases. Cord twist direction emerges as a key structural variable. The work demonstrates the value of open data and reproducible computational methods for cultural heritage research, while the negative n-gram result suggests that aggregate structural features, not sequential knot patterns, carry provenance information.

Abstract: Khipus--knotted cord devices--were the primary recording medium of the Inka Empire (c. 1400-1532 CE), yet their system remains undeciphered. We present a reproducible machine-learning pipeline applied to the Open Khipu Repository (OKR), a public database of 619 khipus comprising 54,403 cords and 110,677 knots. We engineer 27 structural features per khipu and apply (i) unsupervised clustering via UMAP and HDBSCAN, recovering three structurally distinct groups (silhouette = 0.769); (ii) supervised provenance classification via gradient boosting, reaching F1 = 0.86 for the Inka Late Horizon imperial style; and (iii) SHAP-based interpretability, which identifies cord twist direction as the dominant structural discriminator of imperial khipus. We further report two findings of methodological interest. First, one cluster is dominated not by a geographic region but by nineteenth-century European museum collections, indicating that colonial acquisition and recording practices are structurally encoded in the corpus. Second, we provide an independent computational verification of the recto/verso (moiety) structure of the six Santa Valley khipus reported by Medrano and Urton (2018), reproducing both the aggregate attachment ratio and the identification of the single mixed specimen--using only the public OKR database, without physical access to the objects. We additionally report a negative result: knot-type sequence order, encoded as n-grams, adds no provenance signal beyond aggregate features. All code and data are openly available.

</details>


### [11] [SLIM-RL: Risk-Budgeted Random-Masking RL for Diffusion LLMs Without Trajectory Slicing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00208)
*Ruikang Zhao,Zhenting Wang,Han Gao,Ligong Han*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SLIM-RL is a reinforcement learning method for diffusion LLMs that replaces trajectory reconstruction with a risk-budgeted, trace-free random-masking objective, matching or exceeding TraceRL's performance at a fraction of the training cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current state-of-the-art TraceRL uses trajectory-aware training that slices rollouts into up to K/s samples, incurring cost proportional to block size K. The authors aim to mitigate the mismatch between random masking and the model's inference trajectory without this expensive trajectory reconstruction.

Method: SLIM-RL introduces: (1) a tau-budget decoder that bounds commit risk per rollout step, reducing aggregate commit risk; (2) a trace-free random-masking objective that trains on risk-controlled rollouts using sequence-level importance sampling; (3) deterministic quadrature over masking levels under a mean-preserving, monotonically decreasing per-block mask schedule for variance reduction.

Result: On SDAR-4B at block size 16, SLIM-RL matches TraceRL's best MATH500 accuracy with only 0.46x training samples, and improves over TraceRL by 6.32% (MATH500) and 11.05% (GSM8K) under matched dynamic sampling. At block size 4, the 4B model surpasses LLaDA-8B (+10.76% on MATH500) and Dream-7B. On code, it improves over TraceRL by 4.20% (MBPP) and 3.65% (HumanEval). The tau-budget decoder transfers training-free across LLaDA, Dream, and SDAR.

Conclusion: SLIM-RL demonstrates that risk-budgeted, trace-free training can achieve state-of-the-art RL for diffusion LLMs more efficiently than trajectory-aware methods, with strong cross-architecture transferability.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for diffusion large language models (dLLMs) has largely moved to trajectory-aware methods. The current state of the art, TraceRL, holds that random masking is mismatched with the model's inference trajectory, and it reconstructs that trajectory during training by slicing each rollout into up to K/s trajectory-aligned training samples, a cost that grows with the block size K. We show that this mismatch can be mitigated without reconstructing the trajectory. Our method, SLIM-RL, bounds the commit risk of each rollout step with a tau-budget decoder, reducing aggregate commit risk in the training data. During optimization, SLIM-RL trains on these risk-controlled rollouts with a trace-free random-masking objective that adapts variance-reduction tools, combining sequence-level importance sampling, deterministic quadrature over masking levels under a mean-preserving, monotonically decreasing per-block mask schedule that we introduce. On SDAR-4B, SLIM-RL matches TraceRL's best MATH500 accuracy on only 0.46x its training samples at block size 16, improving over TraceRL by 6.32% on MATH500 and 11.05% on GSM8K under matched dynamic sampling. At block size 4, the 4B SLIM-RL surpasses the larger LLaDA-8B and Dream-7B dLLMs on math, exceeding LLaDA-8B by 10.76% on MATH500 while staying below the autoregressive Qwen2.5-7B. On code, it improves over TraceRL by 4.20% on MBPP and 3.65% on HumanEval. The tau-budget decoder transfers training-free across LLaDA, Dream, and SDAR. The source code is available at https://github.com/laolaorkkkkk/SLIM-RL .

</details>


### [12] [LV-ROVER: Multi-Stream Tesseract Voting for Maltese Paragraph OCR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00250)
*Adam Darmanin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: For low-resource Maltese OCR, a synthetic training pipeline and 5-stream Tesseract LV-ROVER ensemble were built. The ensemble alone reduced CER by 44% (0.0234→0.01317); a five-stage post-processing chain achieved 70% total reduction (CER 0.00700), with diacritic recovery treated as recognition gain rather than normalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maltese lacks sufficient real labelled PDF corpora for OCR training—only 57 pages exist, far below paragraph-level training needs. This low-resource OCR setting necessitates synthetic data generation and ensemble methods to achieve usable recognition accuracy.

Method: A synthetic training pipeline generates paragraph-level OCR training data. A 5-stream Tesseract LV-ROVER (Lattice Voting using Optimal Word Error Rate Recognizers) ensemble is constructed. A five-stage post-processing chain performs typographic normalization and diacritic recovery; diacritic restoration is reported separately as a recognition gain rather than mere punctuation alignment.

Result: Fine-tuned Tesseract baseline: CER 0.0234. Ensemble recognition alone: CER 0.01317 (44% reduction). Full pipeline with post-processing: CER 0.00700 (70% reduction). The 44% figure is the portable estimate of recognizer improvement; the 70% is benchmark-specific due to label conventions.

Conclusion: The synthetic pipeline plus LV-ROVER ensemble effectively addresses low-resource OCR for Maltese. The distinction between portable recognition gains (44%) and benchmark-specific normalization gains (70% total) provides a transparent evaluation framework applicable to other low-resource languages.

Abstract: Maltese has decent text corpora and pretrained language models, but, like many languages outside the handful with large OCR benchmarks, only a single known real labelled PDF corpus for OCR training, 57 page, far below what paragraph-level training needs: low-resource for OCR specifically. With no real corpus to train on at scale, we built a synthetic training pipeline and a 5-stream Tesseract LV-ROVER ensemble, and report results on a 422-paragraph benchmark against a fine-tuned-Tesseract baseline of character error rate (CER) 0.0234. Ensemble recognition alone improves CER by 44 percent, to 0.01317; a five-stage post-processing chain brings the full pipeline to CER 0.00700, a 70 percent reduction. Most of that chain is typographic normalisation, but one stage recovers misread diacritics rather than aligning punctuation, so we report it as a recognition gain rather than folding the whole chain under one label. We treat the 44 percent figure as the portable estimate of what the recogniser learned, and the 70 percent figure as specific to this benchmark's label convention.

</details>


### [13] [SEFORA: Student Essays with Feedback Corpus and LLM Feedback Evaluation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00274)
*Shayan Peyghambari Oskoui,Norah Almousa,Zhaoyi Joey Hou,Carolina Gustafson,Gayle Rogers,Raquel Coelho,Diane Litman,Xiang Lorraine Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SEFORA provides a public corpus of real instructor writing feedback paired with assignments across genres, while UniMatch is a reference-based evaluation framework using optimal matching to measure how well LLM-generated feedback aligns with instructor feedback. Experiments show current LLMs achieve at most 0.4 F1, revealing significant gaps in identifying what instructors would prioritize.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Writing feedback is highly effective for student learning but hard to scale. LLMs could help, but two barriers exist: (1) a lack of public corpora capturing authentic instructor feedback from real classrooms, and (2) no reliable method to evaluate whether generated feedback matches what an instructor would actually write.

Method: The authors introduce two contributions: SEFORA, a public corpus containing 564 drafts and 8,240 instructor inline annotations paired with assignment prompts, rubrics, scores, and multi-draft revisions; and UniMatch, a reference-based evaluation framework that segments feedback into units, scores semantic correspondence using instructor-derived criteria, and applies optimal matching to compute precision, recall, and F1.

Result: Across 74 experimental configurations with multiple LLMs, no setting exceeded 0.4 F1 on UniMatch. Models particularly struggle to identify the feedback instructors would prioritize, and performance degrades as models generate more feedback.

Conclusion: Current LLMs fall short of reliably replicating instructor-quality feedback. Both the SEFORA corpus and UniMatch framework provide essential infrastructure for advancing research toward feedback that genuinely mirrors expert instructional judgment.

Abstract: Effective writing feedback is among the strongest drivers of student learning, yet producing it at scale is labor-intensive. LLMs offer a natural path to scaling writing support, but two gaps stand in the way: few public corpora capture how instructors actually deliver feedback in real classrooms, and no reliable method measures whether generated feedback aligns with what an instructor would write. We address both. SEFORA is a public corpus pairing instructor inline feedback with assignment prompts, rubrics, scores, and multi-draft revisions across various college writing genres, comprising 564 drafts and 8,240 instructor annotations. UniMatch is a reference-based evaluation framework for open-ended generation: it segments feedback into feedback units, scores their semantic correspondence under instructor-derived criteria, and aligns them via optimal matching to yield interpretable precision, recall, and F1. Across 74 experimental configurations spanning multiple LLMs, no setting exceeds 0.4 F1. UniMatch reveals that models struggle to identify the feedback instructors would prioritize, and performance degrades as models generate more.

</details>


### [14] [TRACE: State-Aware Query Processing over Temporal Evidence Graphs for Conversational Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00339)
*Maolin Wang,Yu Wang,Zichun Liu,Baiyuan Qiu,Chenbin Zhang,Jiguang Shen,Haoran Yang,Hao Miao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TRACE is a query processing framework that models evolving conversational data as temporal evidence graphs with typed relations (temporal, causal, update, contradiction) and validity annotations. It combines vector-based retrieval with graph-guided evidence search to generate validity-aware, path-grounded answers, improving temporal and multi-hop reasoning over long conversational histories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational data is increasingly used as persistent memory for AI agents, but querying it is challenging because conversations evolve: plans change, preferences shift, and later messages can supersede or contradict earlier ones. Existing long-memory pipelines treat memories as independent text/vector objects, which retrieves semantically similar but stale evidence and lacks support for state-aware reasoning about what is currently valid versus obsolete.

Method: TRACE models conversations as a hierarchical graph spanning events, sessions, and topics, enriched with typed relations (temporal, causal, update, contradiction). It maintains validity annotations so obsolete facts remain accessible for historical queries but are discounted for current-state answers. At query time, TRACE combines vector-based note retrieval with graph-guided evidence search to produce validity-aware support paths and a hybrid context for answer generation, separating lexical recall from evidence reconstruction.

Result: Experiments on long-conversation QA benchmarks demonstrate that TRACE improves temporal and multi-hop reasoning performance. Ablation studies confirm the importance of hierarchy, update-aware seeding, and path-grounded evidence to the framework's effectiveness.

Conclusion: TRACE demonstrates that treating conversational memory as a temporal evidence graph with explicit validity tracking enables more accurate, state-aware reasoning over evolving dialogue histories, outperforming flat retrieval approaches on complex query-answering tasks.

Abstract: Conversational data is increasingly used as a persistent source of user state for long-running assistants and AI agents. However, querying this data remains challenging because conversations naturally evolve: plans are revised, preferences change, and later messages frequently supersede or contradict earlier information. Existing long-memory pipelines largely treat memories as independent text or vector objects. This approach often retrieves semantically similar but stale evidence, offering limited support for state-aware reasoning. To address this problem, we present TRACE, a query processing framework over temporal evidence graphs for evolving conversational data. TRACE models conversations as a hierarchical graph spanning events, sessions, and topics, enriched with typed temporal, causal, update, and contradiction relations. Crucially, the framework maintains validity annotations so obsolete facts remain accessible for historical queries but are discounted for current-state answers. At query time, TRACE combines vector-based note retrieval with graph-guided evidence search, generating validity-aware support paths and a hybrid context for answer generation. This design separates lexical recall from evidence reconstruction, enabling bounded query-time reasoning over long conversational histories. Experiments on long-conversation query-answering (QA) benchmarks show that TRACE improves temporal and multi-hop reasoning, with ablations highlighting the importance of hierarchy, update-aware seeding, and path-grounded evidence.

</details>


### [15] [DiscoLoop: Looping Discrete Embeddings and Continuous Hidden States for Multi-hop Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00341)
*Hengyu Fu,Tianyu Guo,Zixuan Wang,Hanlin Zhu,Jason D. Lee,Jiantao Jiao,Stuart Russell,Song Mei*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Looped Transformers mitigate but don't fully solve two-hop reasoning due to a representational bottleneck: hidden states are poorly aligned with bridge entity embeddings even when the correct answer is decodable. The authors propose DiscoLoop, a dual-channel looping architecture with discrete embedding and continuous hidden-state channels, which nearly closes the generalization gap and improves performance on multi-hop reasoning and real-world pretraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard non-recurrent Transformers suffer from a "depth-local storage" problem in multi-hop reasoning: facts learned in earlier layers are inaccessible during later retrieval steps within a single forward pass. While Looped Transformers reuse memory across iterations to partially address this, they still exhibit a generalization gap. The authors investigate the root cause of this residual failure.

Method: The authors first diagnose the bottleneck in Looped Transformers on two-hop reasoning, revealing a representational misalignment: the hidden state after the first loop contains decodable bridge entity information but is poorly aligned with the bridge token's embedding. A training-free realignment intervention nearly eliminates the gap. Building on this insight, they propose DiscoLoop, a recurrent architecture with two channels — a discrete embedding channel and a continuous hidden-state channel — that jointly circulate through the loop, maintaining better alignment throughout iterative reasoning.

Result: DiscoLoop achieves near-perfect accuracy with substantially fewer training steps than baselines on symbolic and synthetic-language multi-hop reasoning tasks. In real-world pretraining, it yields lower training loss and stronger benchmark performance compared to looped-transformer baselines.

Conclusion: The mixed-channel design of DiscoLoop effectively addresses the representational bottleneck in looped architectures for multi-hop reasoning, and the approach transfers beneficially to practical language modeling settings.

Abstract: Large language models achieve strong performance on many reasoning tasks when allowed to externalize intermediate steps as Chain-of-Thought (CoT). However, many questions require the model to internalize the multi-step reasoning within a single forward pass before generating the answer. We study this challenge through two-hop reasoning, a representative task where the model must compose multiple pieces of parametric knowledge within a single forward pass. Standard non-recurrent Transformers suffer from a depth-local storage problem: facts learned in earlier layers are unavailable where second-hop retrieval happens. We found that Looped Transformers mitigate this issue by reusing the same memory, but still generalize imperfectly. We show that the remaining bottleneck is representational. In the two-hop reasoning task, the first loop often makes the correct bridge entity nearly perfectly decodable, yet the corresponding hidden state remains poorly aligned with the bridge token embedding. Surprisingly, an easy training-free realignment intervention nearly closes the generalization gap. Building upon this insight, we propose DiscoLoop, a looping architecture whose recurrence carries both a discrete embedding channel and a continuous hidden-state channel. DiscoLoop achieves near-perfect accuracy with substantially fewer training steps across symbolic and synthetic-language multi-hop reasoning tasks. When applied to real-world pretraining, DiscoLoop attains lower training loss and stronger benchmark performance than looped-transformer baselines, suggesting that the mixed-channel design transfers to practical language modeling.

</details>


### [16] [Beyond Perplexity: A Behavioral Evaluation Framework for Deployment-Memory Claims in LLM Test-Time Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00368)
*Xiangchen Song,Zhenhao Chen,Lingjing Kong,Shaoan Xie,Xinshuai Dong,Guangyi Chen,Kun Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a behavioral evaluation framework that calibrates TTT memory claims to appropriate evidence, distinguishing between proxy metrics (perplexity, loss) and deployment-relevant behaviors (recall, retention, use in downstream actions). It validates the framework by auditing recent TTT work and demonstrating a measurable gap where LoRA-based TTT reduces loss but yields zero free-form recall in a sparse nonce-fact setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TTT results are increasingly used to motivate claims about deployed assistant memory, personalization, and sparse post-deployment learning. However, the proxy metrics commonly used to evaluate TTT (perplexity, future-token loss, reward) are weak evidence for these behavioral capabilities, which require demonstration of recall, paraphrase robustness, retention, locality, conflict handling, and downstream action use after the original context is removed.

Method: The authors introduce a two-component behavioral evaluation framework: (1) a claim-calibrated evidence ladder with three tiers — stream/domain adaptation, bridge internalization, and deployment-time behavioral learning; and (2) an evaluation protocol featuring matched explicit-memory baselines and mutually exclusive failure categories. They validate the framework by auditing published TTT and memory-adjacent work, and instantiate it as a controlled diagnostic using one-step LoRA updates on nonce-facts across three Qwen3 model scales, measuring both proxy loss reduction and free-form recall.

Result: In the controlled diagnostic, one-step LoRA updates successfully lower both support loss and answer loss across all three Qwen3 model scales, but generated free-form recall remains at zero, exposing a clear and measurable gap between proxy metric improvement and actual deployment behavior.

Conclusion: The framework provides authors and evaluators with a concrete standard for ensuring TTT memory claims are aligned with the type of evidence actually reported, preventing overclaiming from proxy metrics alone. The diagnostic results concretely demonstrate that loss reduction does not imply behavioral recall, underscoring the need for behavioral evaluation in TTT memory research.

Abstract: Large language model test-time training (TTT) is often evaluated through local proxy metrics: models are updated on recent tokens, retrieved context, target-domain data, or verifiable task attempts, and then judged by perplexity, future-token loss, long-context performance, or reward. These metrics are well matched to claims about stream adaptation, domain adaptation, context compression, and reward-backed test-time improvement. They are weaker evidence, however, for a capability that TTT results are increasingly used to motivate: deployed assistant memory, personalization, or sparse post-deployment learning, which instead requires behavioral evidence such as later recall, paraphrase robustness, retention, locality, conflict handling, and use in downstream actions after the original support context is removed. We introduce a behavioral evaluation framework that calibrates TTT memory claims to the evidence that supports them. It has two components: a claim-calibrated evidence ladder that separates stream/domain adaptation, bridge internalization, and deployment-time behavioral learning; and an evaluation protocol with matched explicit-memory baselines and mutually exclusive failure categories. We validate the framework by auditing recent TTT and memory-adjacent work and by instantiating it as a controlled diagnostic in which, in a sparse nonce-fact setting, one-step LoRA updates lower support and answer loss across three Qwen3 model scales while generated free-form recall stays at zero, exposing a measurable gap between proxy improvement and deployment behavior. The framework gives authors and evaluators a concrete standard for aligning TTT memory claims with the evidence actually reported.

</details>


### [17] [DigitalCoach: Communication and Grounding Gaps in Human and Agentic Computer Use Coaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31980)
*Meng Chen,Anya Ji,Tsung-Han Wu,Tobias Maringgele,David M. Chan,Alane Suhr,Amy Pavel*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DigitalCoach is a multimodal dataset of 72 human expert-novice coaching sessions across five software applications, used to evaluate how well AI models can teach humans computer use. Models give more direct instructions but fewer explanations, error diagnoses, and knowledge checks, and suffer from poor visual grounding — causing passive learner behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While agents are increasingly capable of automating software tasks autonomously, their ability to teach humans how to use software — a collaborative, pedagogical challenge — remains unexplored. The authors aim to bridge this gap by providing a benchmark and dataset for evaluating computer-use coaching agents.

Method: The authors construct DigitalCoach, a dataset of 72 expert-novice coaching dialogues (22,752 turns, 28.1 hours of screen recordings with input events) across five applications. They then benchmark state-of-the-art models via: (1) automated evaluation comparing model-generated coaching utterances against human references across dimensions like directness, explanation, error diagnosis, and knowledge checking; and (2) interactive evaluation with real users to assess learning outcomes and engagement.

Result: Automated evaluation reveals models diverge from human coaches: they over-rely on direct instructions while under-producing explanations, error diagnoses, and knowledge-check questions. Even when constrained to human-like coaching methods, model utterances lack grounding in the visual screen context. Interactive evaluation shows learners coached by models exhibit passive "follow-the-instruction" behavior without deeper engagement, and models consistently fail at visual grounding in real-time.

Conclusion: Current state-of-the-art models are insufficient as computer-use coaches; they lack the pedagogical richness and visual grounding of human experts. DigitalCoach provides a foundation for developing future collaborative, proactive coaching agents that can teach rather than merely instruct.

Abstract: Agents are increasingly capable of automating software tasks, but can they teach humans how to use software themselves? We introduce DigitalCoach, a multimodal dataset of 72 human expert-novice computer use coaching sessions consisting of 22,752 dialogue turns grounded in 28.1 hours of screen and input event recordings across five software applications. We use DigitalCoach to evaluate whether state-of-the-art models can teach humans how to use computers. Automated evaluation shows that models differ from humans in how they coach: models provide more direct instructions, but fewer explanations, error diagnoses, and knowledge-check questions. When we fix the coaching method, models produce utterances similar to human references yet poorly grounded in visual context. Interactive evaluation confirms that model coaches cause learners to passively follow instructions without deeper engagement and fall short in visual grounding. DigitalCoach lays a foundation for collaborative and proactive computer use coaching agents.

</details>


### [18] [A Mechanistic View of Authority Hierarchy in LLM Sycophancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00415)
*Emil Joswin,Srujananjali Medicherla,Priyanka Mary Mammen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Authority bias in LLMs is not a superficial output distortion but a mechanistic erasure of correct knowledge representations in late layers, scaled by perceived authority level and resistant to simple interventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether authority bias in LMs is a surface-level output phenomenon or a deep mechanistic process that overwrites internal representations, with implications for AI safety.

Method: Controlled medical QA setup with persona-attributed incorrect hints across Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen3-8B, and Gemma-2-9B; combined logit lens analysis, linear and non-linear probing, mean vector intervention, and chain-of-thought reasoning to trace the representational trajectory of correct answers through model layers.

Result: Models exhibit graded compliance proportional to perceived authority without explicit prompting. Correct answer representations are actively erased at a specific late layer, with erasure magnitude scaling with authority level. This erasure resists mean vector intervention and is only partially reversible via chain-of-thought reasoning.

Conclusion: Authority-induced sycophancy constitutes mechanistic knowledge erasure — a precise, layer-localized overwriting of correct internal representations by high-status authority signals, not merely an output-level bias.

Abstract: Authority bias poses a critical safety concern in language models: models systematically prioritize social cues from authority figures over factual consistency, swaying their answers based on source credibility rather than evidence. We mechanistically investigate this phenomenon using a controlled medical QA setting, where hints suggesting incorrect answers are attributed to personas of varying expertise. Across Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen3-8B, and Gemma-2-9B, we find that models respond in a graded manner proportional to perceived authority, a hierarchy that is never explicitly prompted but emerges from training. Logit lens analysis and linear/non-linear probing localize this effect to a critical late layer where correct answer representations are actively erased, an erasure that scales with authority level, resists mean vector intervention, and is only partially reversible through chain-of-thought reasoning. Our findings suggest that authority-induced sycophancy is not a surface-level output bias but mechanistic knowledge erasure, a precise, layer-localized overwriting of correct internal representations by high-status authority signals.

</details>


### [19] [Speech Playground: An Interactive Tool for Speech Analysis and Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00418)
*Stephen McIntosh,Daisuke Saito,Nobuaki Minematsu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Speech Playground is a web-based interactive tool that integrates a Python backend to enable visual and auditory comparison of diverse speech representations (continuous, discrete, variable-length), with TextGrid and forced alignment support for use in speech research, representation validation, and CAPT experimentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing speech analysis tools like Praat are powerful but cumbersome to integrate with modern deep learning representations and lack convenient comparison capabilities. There is a need for a tool that bridges traditional speech analysis with contemporary neural network-based feature representations in an interactive, user-friendly manner.

Method: The system combines a Python backend with a web-based frontend, supporting multiple feature types (continuous, discrete, and variable-length representations). It incorporates TextGrid and forced alignment support, along with configurable distance metrics and alignment settings, enabling both visual and auditory comparison of speech signals and representations.

Result: The paper describes the design and functionality of Speech Playground as a working interactive tool, positioning it as suitable for speech research, representation validation, and CAPT-oriented experimentation. Specific quantitative results are not presented, as this is a tool/demo paper.

Conclusion: Speech Playground fills a gap in the speech research ecosystem by providing an accessible, web-based interface for comparing multiple speech representation types alongside traditional annotations, facilitating both research and pedagogical applications in pronunciation training.

Abstract: This paper presents Speech Playground, an interactive speech visualization and comparison tool. While existing tools such as Praat are excellent, it can be cumbersome to integrate them with modern deep learning representations and use them for comparison. Speech Playground addresses this by combining a Python backend with a web-based frontend for interactive exploration of multiple feature types, including continuous, discrete, and variable-length representations. It includes TextGrid and forced alignment support together with configurable distance and alignment settings for visual and auditory comparison. Speech Playground is intended for use in speech research, representation validation, and computer-aided pronunciation training (CAPT)-oriented experimentation.

</details>


### [20] [Quantifying the Affective Gap: A Zero-Shot Evaluation of LLMs on Fine-Grained Emotion Taxonomies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00968)
*Lawrence Obiuwevwi,Krzysztof J. Rechowicz,Jessica M. Johnson,Vikas Ashok,Sachin Shetty,Sampath Jayarathna*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A zero-shot evaluation of Claude, GPT-5.4, and Gemini on 13-class emotion classification shows all models converge at ~39% accuracy with no significant pairwise differences, yet exhibit systematic failures on love, confusion, and shame, with Claude displaying notable class-imbalance bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emotion recognition is foundational for affective computing in human-computer interaction, mental health, and conversational AI, yet the zero-shot capabilities of frontier LLMs on fine-grained emotion classification remain poorly characterized.

Method: Three commercial LLMs (Claude-sonnet-4-6, GPT-5.4, Gemini-2.5-flash) were evaluated via production APIs (April 2026) using a single uniform zero-shot prompt with no exemplars on a stratified 1,000-sentence sample from the boltuix/emotions dataset (131,306 sentences, 13 emotion categories). Performance was measured by accuracy and macro-F1, with McNemar tests for pairwise significance.

Result: Gemini led with 39.9% accuracy (macro-F1=0.363), followed by GPT-5.4 (38.8%, macro-F1=0.291) and Claude (38.0%, macro-F1=0.159). All models performed well on sarcasm and desire but consistently failed on love, confusion, and shame. McNemar tests showed no significant pairwise differences (p > 0.10). Claude's substantially lower macro-F1 revealed a class-imbalance prediction bias.

Conclusion: Frontier LLMs converge at a shared zero-shot ceiling (~39%) on fine-grained emotion classification, with systematic failure modes on specific emotions, highlighting fundamental limitations that constrain their reliability for affective computing applications.

Abstract: Emotion recognition in natural language is a foundational challenge in affective computing, with critical implications for human-computer interaction, mental health support, and conversational AI. This paper presents a rigorous, unified zero-shot evaluation of three leading commercial large language models: Claude (claude-sonnet-4-6), ChatGPT (GPT-5.4), and Gemini (gemini-2.5-flash). The models were queried through their respective production APIs as of April 2026 on a fine-grained 13-class emotion classification task. Using a stratified 1,000-sentence sample from the boltuix/emotions dataset, which comprises 131,306 sentences across 13 categories, a single uniform prompt with no exemplars was applied identically across all models. Gemini achieves the highest accuracy (39.9%) and macro-F1 score (0.363), followed by GPT-5.4 (38.8%, macro-F1 = 0.291) and Claude (38.0%, macro-F1 = 0.159). All models excel on sarcasm and desire while consistently failing on love, confusion, and shame. McNemar tests reveal no statistically significant pairwise differences (p > 0.10), suggesting convergence at a shared zero-shot ceiling. Claude's markedly lower macro-F1 score exposes a class-imbalance prediction bias. These findings highlight the current limitations of frontier AI systems in zero-shot fine-grained emotion classification.

</details>


### [21] [Selective Test-Time Debiasing for CLIP via Reward Gating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00423)
*Jaeho Han,Jisoo Yang,Hyeondong Woo,Mingyu Jeon,Sunjae Yoon,Junyeong Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: RG-TTA is a reinforcement learning-based test-time adaptation framework that selectively applies debiasing based on input bias sensitivity, resolving the fairness--utility trade-off inherent in uniform debiasing methods for vision-language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current debiasing methods for VLMs apply uniform corrections regardless of input bias sensitivity, causing a fairness--utility trade-off: strong debiasing degrades semantically meaningful outputs on bias-insensitive queries, while weak debiasing fails to mitigate stereotypes on bias-sensitive ones.

Method: The authors propose Reward-Gated Test-Time Adaptation (RG-TTA), which uses reinforcement learning to adaptively gate fairness regularization during test-time policy adaptation. Bias-sensitive inputs trigger fairness regularization, while bias-insensitive inputs focus solely on optimizing cross-modal (vision-language) alignment.

Result: Experiments on fairness benchmarks (FairFace, UTKFace) show substantial bias reduction with simultaneous improvement in zero-shot utility, effectively resolving the trade-off imposed by uniform debiasing approaches.

Conclusion: Selective, input-adaptive debiasing via RG-TTA outperforms uniform debiasing strategies, enabling VLMs to achieve both high utility and fairness without compromise.

Abstract: Vision language models (VLMs) demonstrate strong zero-shot performance, but often perpetuate social stereotypes in person-centric queries, yielding skewed demographic distributions. Current debiasing methods apply uniform bias corrections across all input queries regardless of their bias sensitivity, creating a fundamental fairness--utility trade-off. Strong debiasing distorts semantically meaningful information in bias-insensitive queries, while weak debiasing fails to mitigate stereotypes in bias-sensitive ones. This one-size-fits-all approach hampers simultaneously achieving high utility on bias-insensitive queries and fairness on bias-sensitive queries. We introduce Reward-Gated Test-Time Adaptation (RG-TTA), a reinforcement learning-based test-time adaptation framework that selectively applies debiasing based on input sensitivity. RG-TTA adaptively triggers fairness regularization based on the bias sensitivity of each input during test-time policy adaptation, while focusing exclusively on optimizing cross-modal alignment for bias-insensitive inputs. Experiments on fairness benchmarks (e.g., FairFace, UTKFace) demonstrate substantial bias reduction while simultaneously improving zero-shot utility, resolving the trade-off of uniform debiasing.

</details>


### [22] [Behavior-Adaptive Conversational Agents: Toward a Fluid Personality Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01034)
*Hasibur Rahman,Smit Desai*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a "Fluid Personality Framework" for LLM-based conversational agents that dynamically adapts both the agent's metaphorical persona (e.g., coach, tutor, librarian) and its personality expression intensity (low, medium, high) based on task context, user characteristics, and situational urgency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most conversational agents use fixed personas and personality styles, which creates misalignment when task dynamics, urgency, and formality vary across contexts like medical information seeking, fitness coaching, and reflective learning. Prior evidence indicates that moderate personality expression and context-appropriate metaphors improve outcomes, yet current systems fail to adapt jointly on both dimensions.

Method: The authors sketch a design framework that jointly parameterizes two dimensions: (1) metaphorical persona selection (coach, tutor, librarian, tool, etc.) and (2) personality expression intensity (low/medium/high), modulated by task context, user goals and traits, and situational urgency. The paper presents the framework's core design dimensions as a conceptual contribution.

Result: This is a position/framework paper; no empirical results are reported. The paper contributes a conceptual architecture for adaptive persona and personality modulation in LLM-based conversational agents.

Conclusion: A Fluid Personality Framework that jointly adapts persona and personality intensity offers a promising direction for improving user experience, trust, and engagement in AI-mediated behavior change contexts, addressing the misalignment problem inherent in static agent designs.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based conversational agents (CAs) are now ubiquitous, creating new opportunities for AI-mediated behavior change. Their capacity to project nuanced personalities and adopt diverse metaphorical roles raises a design question: how should an agent's persona and personality be calibrated to the moment? Recent evidence suggests that (i) moderate personality expression outperforms low or high extremes on trust, enjoyment, and intention to adopt in goal-oriented tasks, and (ii) context-appropriate metaphors outperform static one-note assistants on user experience and uptake. Yet most CAs still fix both persona and style, risking misalignment when dynamics, urgency, and formality vary, for example in medical information seeking, fitness coaching, and reflective learning. We propose a Fluid Personality Framework that jointly adapts (1) the agent's metaphorical persona, such as coach, tutor, librarian, or tool, and (2) its personality expression intensity, low, medium, or high, as a function of task context, user goals and traits, and situational urgency. We sketch the framework and its core design dimensions.

</details>


### [23] [Understanding Why Language Models Hallucinate: Testing Reasoning Against Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00447)
*Yangfan Hu,Xuhan Tong,Haoyue Bai,Xi Ding,Shashank Muralidhar Bharadwaj,Siyang Cao,Robert Nowak,Jiawei Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces "inference misalignment," where LLMs hallucinate not from missing knowledge but from statistically salient shortcuts overriding prompt constraints. The authors formalize this using a latent key-task model and build TrapQA to diagnose two failure modes: task-retrieval bias and key-selection bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To distinguish whether LLM hallucinations stem from genuine knowledge gaps versus the model possessing relevant information but following an incorrect inference path due to statistical shortcuts learned during pretraining.

Method: The authors formalize a latent key-task model where pretraining-frequency imbalance causes shortcut paths to dominate constraint-sensitive paths, yielding positive inference loss. They introduce TrapQA, a diagnostic testbed with two components: ScientistQA (disambiguation among similar scientists with factual probes) and Real-Life Constrained QA (everyday constraint-following under salient shortcuts).

Result: Hallucinations can arise from biased latent inference rather than absent knowledge. The TrapQA benchmarks reveal two distinct failure modes: task-retrieval bias in entity disambiguation and key-selection bias in action choice, both consistent with the inference misalignment framework.

Conclusion: LLM hallucination is not solely a knowledge deficiency problem; inference misalignment induced by pretraining frequency imbalances leads models to favor salient but wrong inference paths. This reframes hallucination diagnosis and suggests mitigation should target inference-path selection, not just knowledge augmentation.

Abstract: Large language models often produce hallucinated answers that violate prompt-level constraints. A key diagnostic question is whether these failures reflect missing knowledge, or whether the model has the relevant information but follows the wrong inference path. We study this phenomenon as inference misalignment: a mismatch between the answer supported by the prompt and the answer favored by statistically salient latent associations. We formalize this view with a latent key-task model, in which pretraining-frequency imbalance can cause a shortcut path to dominate the constraint-sensitive path and induce positive inference loss. The framework predicts two failure modes: task-retrieval bias in entity disambiguation and key-selection bias in action choice. We introduce TrapQA, a controlled diagnostic testbed with two components. ScientistQA tests disambiguation among similar scientists with supplementary factual probes, while Real-Life Constrained QA tests everyday constraint following under salient shortcuts. Our results show that hallucination can arise from biased latent inference rather than absent knowledge alone.

</details>


### [24] [Know When to Stop: Segment-Level Credit Assignment for Reducing Overthinking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00482)
*Chia-Hsuan Lee,Sihui Dai,Mingyang Zhou,Isha Slavin,Shi-Xiong Zhang,Sambit Sahu,William Campbell*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Reasoning language models exhibit "overthinking" — unproductive behaviors like hedging and self-contradiction that waste tokens. The authors propose DASH, a method that uses intermediate answer commitments as a cheap proxy for identifying productive vs. unproductive self-reflection, assigning segment-level credit to shape reasoning. DASH improves accuracy on competition math benchmarks while reducing overthinking behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reasoning language models frequently overthink, producing extended chains of unproductive behaviors (hedging, approach abandonment, self-contradiction) that consume tokens without improving answer quality. The key insight is that these behaviors are not merely a byproduct of longer responses — incorrect traces show higher rates of unproductive self-reflection even controlling for length. However, obtaining step-level annotations to distinguish helpful vs. harmful self-reflection is expensive, motivating a cheaper proxy signal.

Method: The authors propose DASH (Drift Aware advantage SHaping), which leverages intermediate answer commitments within reasoning traces as a cheap proxy for credit assignment. By comparing each final answer candidate in the trace to the ground truth, DASH determines whether subsequent reflection is productive without requiring additional supervision.
