It then assigns segment-level credit based on whether each reasoning segment moves toward or away from correctness, effectively shaping the advantage signal to discourage overthinking and encourage productive self-correction.

Result: On competition-level math benchmarks where overthinking is prevalent, DASH achieves state-of-the-art accuracy: 50.8% on AIME25 vs. 45.4% for GRPO. It simultaneously reduces overthinking behaviors and yields more productive self-correction compared to baselines.

Conclusion: DASH demonstrates that intermediate answer commitments provide an effective, low-cost signal for distinguishing productive from unproductive reasoning, enabling segment-level credit assignment that mitigates overthinking in reasoning LMs while improving both accuracy and efficiency.

Abstract: Reasoning language models frequently overthink: generating extended chains of behaviors such as hedging, approach abandonment, and self contradiction that consume tokens without improving answers. We show that these behaviors are not merely a consequence of length; even when controlling for response length, incorrect traces exhibit higher rates of unproductive self-reflection than correct ones. Addressing this requires identifying where self-reflection helps vs hurts, but obtaining these step-level annotations is costly. We observe that intermediate answer commitments within reasoning traces can provide a cheap proxy: by comparing each final answer candidate in the trace to the ground truth, we can determine whether subsequent reflection is productive without any additional supervision. Building on this insight, we propose DASH (Drift Aware advantage SHaping), which assigns segment-level credit based on whether each reasoning segment leads toward or away from correctness. On competition-level math benchmarks, DASH achieves the highest accuracy where overthinking is prevalent (AIME25: 50.8% vs. 45.4% GRPO) while reducing overthinking behaviors and achieving more productive self-correction than baselines.

</details>


### [25] [Safe Alone, Unsafe Together: Safeguarding Against Implicit Toxicity When Benign Images Combine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00576)
*Jiaxian Lv,Shiyao Cui,Yingkang Wang,Guoxin Wu,Qingling Zhang,Minlie Huang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces Multi-Image Implicit Toxicity (MIIT), where individually benign images become harmful when viewed together. The authors construct the MIIT-dataset across seven risk categories and train MiShield, a model that identifies implicit toxicity through reasoning about correlated entities, outperforming commercial moderation APIs and larger models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-image posts are ubiquitous on social media, yet existing moderation tools fail to detect implicit toxicity that only emerges from the joint interpretation of multiple images, since each image lacks explicit harmful cues in isolation.

Method: The authors formally define MIIT and its three detection challenges. They construct the MIIT-dataset using an automatic generation pipeline spanning seven risk categories. They then train MiShield-8B using progressively distilled reasoning supervision, which produces safety judgments along with explicit analyses of how correlated entities across images create hazards.

Result: MiShield-8B outperforms representative commercial moderation APIs and even larger-scale models on MIIT detection, demonstrating both effectiveness and practical value.

Conclusion: MIIT represents a critical blind spot in current content moderation systems. The combination of a dedicated dataset and a reasoning-supervised model like MiShield offers a viable solution for detecting this emerging form of implicit toxicity in multi-image visual communication.

Abstract: Multi-image content has become an increasingly prevalent form of visual communication in social media, giving rise to a new safety issue, multi-image implicit toxicity (MIIT), where each image appears benign in isolation, but harmful semantics emerge when the images are interpreted jointly. MIIT is particularly challenging for existing commercial moderation APIs and models due to the lack of explicit risky cues in each image. This paper aims to study how to identify MIIT. We first provide a formal definition of MIIT and analyze three key challenges for its detection. To alleviate the scarcity of data in this area, we construct MIIT-dataset, an image-only multi-image safety dataset covering seven representative risk categories through an automatic generation pipeline. Finally, we train MiShield with progressively distilled reasoning supervision, enabling it to produce safety judgments accompanied by explicit analyses of the correlated entities that result in the hazards. Experiments show that MiShield-8B models outperform representative moderation services and even larger-scale models, revealing its effectiveness and practical value for this widely used visual format. Warning: This paper contains potentially sensitive content.

</details>


### [26] [Efficient Multilingual Reasoning Transfer via Progressive Code-Switching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00485)
*Zhijun Wang,Junxiao Liu,Hao Zhou,Hao-Ran Wei,Baosong Yang,Shujian Huang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PCS (Progressive Code-Switching) is an efficient framework for transferring English reasoning capabilities of large reasoning models to other languages, using lightweight translation and a curriculum-based reinforcement learning approach with code-switched traces, avoiding reliance on costly distillation or external judge models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large reasoning models (LRMs) perform well in English but degrade significantly when reasoning in other languages. Existing transfer methods rely on expensive distilled target-language reasoning traces from stronger models or online supervision from external judges, limiting scalability.

Method: PCS operates in two stages: (1) Supervised fine-tuning on code-switched reasoning traces, created by translating a subset of English reasoning steps into the target language; (2) Reinforcement learning with a step-level language consistency curriculum that progressively increases the target-language ratio until the model reasons entirely in the target language.

Result: Experiments across multiple benchmarks and five typologically diverse languages demonstrate that PCS substantially narrows the performance gap between target-language and English reasoning, producing more language-consistent reasoning while maintaining competitive accuracy.

Conclusion: PCS provides a smooth, scalable transfer path that avoids the instability and degradation typical of directly enforcing target-language reasoning, offering an efficient alternative to distillation-based and judge-supervised approaches.

Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) have achieved strong reasoning capabilities in English, yet their performance degrades significantly when required to reason in other languages. A natural solution is to transfer the model's English reasoning ability to target languages. However, existing transfer approaches typically rely on distilled target-language reasoning traces from stronger LRMs or online supervision from external judge models, which are costly and difficult to scale. In this paper, we propose PCS (Progressive Code-Switching), a more efficient transfer framework that requires only lightweight translation without any stronger model for distillation or judging. PCS first constructs code-switched reasoning traces by translating a subset of English reasoning steps into the target language, and uses them to initialize the model's code-switching ability via supervised fine-tuning. It then applies reinforcement learning with a step-level language consistency curriculum, progressively raising the target-language ratio until the model reasons entirely in the target language. This progressive design provides a smooth transfer path that avoids the instability and performance degradation commonly observed when directly enforcing target-language reasoning. Experiments on multiple benchmarks and five typologically diverse languages show that PCS substantially narrows the performance gap between target-language and English reasoning, yielding more language-consistent reasoning while maintaining competitive accuracy.

</details>


### [27] [BaseRT: Best-in-Class LLM Inference on Apple Silicon via Native Metal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00501)
*Prabod Rathnayaka,Fabian Waschkowski,Lukas Wesemann*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: BaseRT is a native Metal inference runtime for LLMs on Apple Silicon that achieves state-of-the-art throughput by bypassing framework overhead, achieving up to 1.56× faster decode than llama.cpp and 1.35× faster than MLX across a range of model families and quantization formats on M-series devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM inference runtimes (llama.cpp, MLX) incur overhead from abstractions not designed for Metal's execution model or Apple Silicon's unified memory topology, leaving significant performance untapped. As edge inference grows due to privacy, latency, and cost pressures, an optimized native runtime is needed to unlock Apple Silicon's full potential.

Method: BaseRT is built natively on Apple's Metal API with three core optimizations: (1) chip-specific kernel fusion tailored to Apple Silicon GPU architectures, (2) unified memory-aware optimization exploiting the CPU-GPU shared memory topology, and (3) custom dispatch logic. It supports eight quantization formats (Q2 through FP16) and a wide range of model families across all M-series devices. Evaluation was performed on Qwen3, Llama 3.2, and Gemma 4 families at Q4 and Q8 quantization on M3 and M4 Pro hardware.

Result: BaseRT achieves up to 1.56× higher decode throughput than llama.cpp and up to 1.35× higher than MLX. Gains are substantially larger on prefill for mixture-of-experts (MoE) models. Consistent best-in-class throughput is demonstrated across models ranging from sub-1B to 30B parameters.

Conclusion: These results establish Apple Silicon as a more capable inference platform than previously reported. Performance-optimized local runtimes like BaseRT are a critical enabling layer for the shift toward on-device edge inference, driven by privacy requirements, latency constraints, and cloud cost pressures.

Abstract: We present BaseRT, a native Metal inference runtime for large language models (LLMs) on Apple Silicon, and report the highest inference throughput on this hardware to date. Existing runtimes, including llama.cpp and MLX-based frameworks, incur overhead from abstractions not designed for Metal's execution model or Apple Silicon's unified memory topology. By building natively on Metal with chip-specific kernel fusion, unified memory-aware optimisation, and custom dispatch logic, BaseRT recovers performance that framework-based approaches leave on the table. BaseRT supports a wide range of model families across eight quantisation formats (Q2 to FP16) on all Apple M-series devices. In this paper, we evaluate the Qwen3, Llama 3.2, and Gemma 4 families at Q4 and Q8 quantisation on M3 and M4 Pro devices. BaseRT achieves up to 1.56x higher decode throughput than llama.cpp and up to 1.35x higher than MLX, with substantially larger margins on prefill for mixture-of-experts models, delivering consistent best-in-class throughput from sub-1B to 30B parameter models. These results establish Apple Silicon as a more capable inference platform than previously reported, with direct implications for the emerging edge inference paradigm: as privacy requirements, latency constraints, and cloud cost pressures drive inference toward on-device deployment, performance-optimised local runtimes are a critical enabling layer for this transition. BaseRT is publicly available at https://github.com/basecompute/baseRT

</details>


### [28] [Multi-Turn Agentic Scientific Literature Search via Workflow Induction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00597)
*Jisen Li,Bingxuan Li,Nanyi Jiang,Xuying Ning,Xiyao Wang,Yifan Shen,Heng Wang,Yuqing Jian,Xiaoxia Wu,Ben Athiwaratkun,Pan Lu,Jiaxuan You,Bingxin Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PaperPilot frames scientific literature search as workflow induction, constructing executable DAGs of search operators (keyword search, citation expansion, filtering, reranking, etc.) that users can refine through multi-turn feedback. The 9B model outperforms a Qwen3.5-9B toolset agent, achieving Hit@5 of 77.0 and zero workflow execution errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific literature search suffers from underspecified, preference-dependent user intents that evolve through interaction. Existing search agents use rigid pipelines or implicit language-only reasoning, making strategies opaque, uncontrollable, and difficult to refine.

Method: PaperPilot takes an anchor paper and user query, then constructs an executable DAG workflow comprising operators: keyword search, citation expansion, filtering, scoring, reranking, and evidence extraction. Training involves supervised workflow imitation followed by preference optimization over controlled workflow corruptions. Multi-turn user feedback refines both the query and the workflow structure.

Result: PaperPilot-9B vs. Qwen3.5-9B toolset agent under multi-turn interaction: Hit@5 improved from 58.0→77.0, MRR from 47.5→59.4, nDCG@10 from 26.8→32.5. Workflow execution errors dropped from 9.5% to 0%.

Conclusion: Explicit, editable search workflows provide an effective and controllable interface for aligning literature search agents with complex, evolving scientific intent, outperforming implicit reasoning approaches both in retrieval quality and execution reliability.

Abstract: Scientific literature search often requires more than retrieving papers from a single query: users' intents are underspecified, preference-dependent, and evolve through interaction. Existing search agents typically rely on fixed pipelines or implicit language-only reasoning, making their search strategies difficult to control, inspect, and refine. We introduce PaperPilot, a multi-turn literature search agent that frames scientific search as workflow induction. Given an anchor paper and a user query, PaperPilot constructs an executable DAG of paper-search operators, including keyword search, citation expansion, filtering, scoring, reranking, and evidence extraction. User feedback is then used to refine both the query and the workflow itself. We train PaperPilot with supervised workflow imitation and preference optimization over controlled workflow corruptions. Experiments show that PaperPilot-9B improves over the base Qwen3.5-9B toolset agent under multi-turn interaction, increasing Hit@5 from 58.0 to 77.0, MRR from 47.5 to 59.4, and nDCG@10 from 26.8 to 32.5, while reducing workflow execution errors from 9.5% to 0%. These results show that explicit, editable search workflows provide an effective and controllable interface for aligning literature search agents with complex scientific intent.

</details>


### [29] [A Task-State Representation for Long-Horizon Mobile GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00502)
*Yujie Zheng,Zikang Liu,Xin Zhao,Ji-Rong Wen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TSR is a training-free framework that decouples persistent task state from transient screen observations in mobile GUI agents, using three structured components (instruction summary, progress tracker, action verifier) updated via visual comparisons, yielding up to 12-point success rate gains on complex tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon mobile GUI agents use thought-action-observation loops that conflate persistent task states with transient screen observations. As execution histories grow, this entanglement creates severe context burden, leading to forgotten initial requirements, hallucinated progress, and repeated interactions with stale interfaces.

Method: TSR is a training-free external wrapper framework with three structured components: (1) a global instruction summary preserving the original task goal, (2) a dynamic progress tracker that maintains and updates subgoal completion status, and (3) a transition-aware action verifier that validates actions against expected state transitions. These components are continuously updated through pre- and post-action visual comparisons, guiding agent reasoning without architectural modifications.

Result: Experiments across four mobile GUI benchmarks show TSR improves success rates by up to 12 absolute points, with particularly strong gains on complex cross-application and memory-intensive tasks.

Conclusion: TSR effectively addresses the context-burden problem in long-horizon GUI agents by explicitly decoupling task state from sensory input, and its training-free, wrapper-based design makes it readily applicable without modifying underlying agent architectures.

Abstract: While long-horizon mobile GUI agents typically rely on thought-action-observation loops, they struggle to separate persistent task states from transient screen observations. As execution histories grow, this entanglement imposes a severe context burden, causing agents to forget initial requirements, hallucinate progress, or repeatedly interact with stale interfaces. To address this, we introduce Task-State Representation (TSR), a training-free framework that explicitly decouples task state from sensory input. Acting as a lightweight external wrapper, TSR maintains three structured components: a global instruction summary, a dynamic progress tracker for subgoals, and a transition-aware action verifier. By continuously updating through pre- and post-action visual comparisons, TSR effectively guides the agent's reasoning without requiring architectural modifications. Experiments across four mobile GUI benchmarks validate TSR's effectiveness, yielding up to a 12 absolute point increase in success rate on complex cross-application and memory-intensive tasks.

</details>


### [30] [What Survives Into Context: A Diagnostic for Budget-Constrained Multi-Hop RAG and When Submodular Evidence Packing Improves It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00725)
*Ananto Nayan Bala*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes answer-in-context, a diagnostic metric replacing recall for RAG under fixed context budgets, and introduces a submodular optimization-based packer. The packer's gains over heuristics vanish and reverse as reader model scale increases, with answer-in-context explaining all performance boundaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Under a fixed reader-context budget, only a fraction of retrieved documents can be shown to the reader. The standard metric—document recall—fails to predict downstream answer quality in this regime, motivating a better diagnostic and a principled context construction method.

Method: First, introduces "answer-in-context": a diagnostic checking whether the gold answer survives as a contiguous span in the packed reader context. Second, frames reader-context construction as budgeted monotone submodular maximization, jointly optimizing relevance, query coverage, representativeness, and diversity. Evaluated on HotpotQA and 2WikiMultiHopQA with controlled reader scales (3B, 7B, 14B).

Result: Answer-in-context predicts answer F1 better than recall (r=0.39–0.55 vs. ~0.31), separates answer quality roughly five-fold (0.60 vs. 0.12 on HotpotQA), and adds ΔR²=0.17 over recall. The submodular packer beats MMR and heuristics by up to +5.1 F1 at 160-token budget with a 3B reader. However, gains are absorbed at 7B and significantly reverse at 14B, with answer-in-context explaining all boundaries.

Conclusion: Answer-in-context is a superior diagnostic for budgeted RAG. The submodular packer is effective only under specific conditions: multi-hop structure, adequate retrieval, binding but not extreme budget, and a reader weak enough that evidence density is the bottleneck. Reader scale fundamentally determines whether sophisticated packing matters.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) under a fixed reader-context budget forces a selection problem: of the evidence retrieved, only a fraction can be shown to the reader. We argue that document recall -- the standard retrieval metric -- is the wrong quantity to optimize in this regime, and we make two contributions. First, as a general contribution, we introduce answer-in-context, a diagnostic that measures whether a gold answer survives as a contiguous span in the packed reader context (not the retrieved set). It predicts answer F1 better than recall (r=0.39-0.55 vs. about 0.31), separates answer quality roughly five-fold (0.60 vs. 0.12 on HotpotQA), and carries information beyond retrieval: it adds Delta R squared=0.17 over recall and shows a 4.6x EM gap even among questions where all gold was retrieved. We also confirm it interventionally: on 2WikiMultiHopQA a packing change that raises coverage but not answer-in-context yields no accuracy gain. Second, as a conditional contribution, we cast reader-context construction as budgeted monotone submodular maximization and build a packer that jointly optimizes relevance, query coverage, representativeness, and diversity. On HotpotQA with a 160-token budget and a 3B reader it beats a strong focused heuristic, MMR, and naive packing -- by up to +5.1 F1 at equal-or-lower token cost, across three seeds. Crucially, we map the scope of this win honestly: it requires the conjunction of (i) multi-hop complementary structure, (ii) retrieval that surfaces the evidence, (iii) a binding but not extreme budget, and (iv) a reader weak enough that evidence density, not reading capacity, is the bottleneck. A quantization-controlled reader-scale ladder (3B to 7B to 14B) shows the edge over the heuristic is absorbed by 7B and significantly reverses by 14B, while the diagnostic explains every boundary with a single variable.

</details>


### [31] [Dual-Confidence Contrastive Decoding for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00570)
*Raymond Li,Md Tawkat Islam Khondaker,Amirhossein Abaskohi,Gabriel Murray,Giuseppe Carenini,Issam H. Laradji*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces DRQA, a benchmark for multi-document RAG with intra-context factual conflicts, and proposes DCCD, a training-free contrastive decoding method that uses document-level and token-level confidence signals to improve answer quality when retrieved documents contain conflicting evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing contrastive decoding methods address conflicts between a model's internal memory and retrieved context, but neglect intra-context conflicts — where multiple retrieved documents contain stale, noisy, or contradictory information. This is critical for enterprise multi-document RAG scenarios.

Method: The authors propose Dual-Confidence Contrastive Decoding (DCCD), a training-free decoding method that combines two signals: (1) document-level confidence, estimating whether a document is sufficient to answer the question, and (2) token-level confidence, estimating whether a document supports confident next-token prediction. These dual-confidence signals select positive and negative document-conditioned streams, and a document-level contrast is scaled by their confidence margin. They also introduce DRQA, a factual-conflict QA benchmark with synthetic enterprise-specific facts not recoverable from model memory.

Result: DCCD achieves the best average performance among full-context and contrastive decoding baselines across DRQA and standard multi-document QA benchmarks, with the largest gains observed on DRQA.

Conclusion: Source-aware, confidence-gated decoding is crucial when retrieved evidence is internally conflicting. DCCD effectively addresses intra-context conflicts without requiring additional training, demonstrating substantial improvements over existing methods.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) increasingly requires models to answer questions from multiple retrieved documents, where only some sources are relevant and the retrieved bundle may contain stale, noisy, or conflicting evidence. Existing contrastive decoding methods primarily focus on resolving conflicts between the model's internal memory and the retrieved context. In contrast, we study the complementary problem of intra-context conflict in multi-document RAG. To evaluate this setting, we introduce DRQA, a factual-conflict question answering benchmark derived from enterprise deep-research scenarios, where answers are grounded in synthetic enterprise-specific facts that are designed not to be recoverable from the model's internal memory. We further propose Dual-Confidence Contrastive Decoding (DCCD), a training-free decoding method that combines document-level confidence, which estimates whether a document appears sufficient for answering the question, with token-level confidence, which estimates whether that document supports a confident next-token prediction. DCCD selects positive and negative document-conditioned streams using these dual-confidence signals and scales a document-level contrast by their confidence margin. Across DRQA and standard multi-document QA benchmarks, DCCD achieves the best average performance among full-context and contrastive decoding baselines, with the largest gains on DRQA. These results highlight the importance of source-aware, confidence-gated decoding when retrieved evidence is internally conflicting.

</details>


### [32] [Low Perplexity is Repetition: A One-Dimensional Self-Conditioning Attractor in Continuous Diffusion LMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00588)
*Shuai Zhang,Zijie Chen,Hongliang He,Lun Du,Zhenzhong Lan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ELF-style continuous diffusion language models achieve low generative perplexity largely by repeating text, which Gen-PPL rewards. The authors trace this repetition to a one-dimensional contractive attractor in the self-conditioning feedback loop and propose ACE, a simple fix that subtracts this direction, nearly eliminating repetition while maintaining quality at 1.5–5× lower compute cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous diffusion language models (e.g., ELF) report record-low generative perplexity (Gen-PPL), but the authors discover this metric is misleading: it rewards repetitive text generation. They aim to expose this flaw, trace the root cause of pathological repetition, and provide a corrective method that yields a fairer quality–efficiency trade-off.

Method: The authors first demonstrate that stripping repeated text from ELF-B samples causes Gen-PPL to spike from 19.5 to 27.7, revealing the metric's bias. They trace the repetition mechanism to a contractive attractor along a single direction in the self-conditioning feedback loop (where each step's clean estimate feeds the next). They propose ACE (Attractor-Contrast-Escape), which subtracts this label-free direction from the feedback at each denoising step. The direction is estimated once on a 105M model and transfers without retuning to larger models (342M, 652M) and across samplers. They also measure compute cost to produce human-clean text as a more honest evaluation metric.

Result: ELF-B's Gen-PPL rises from 19.5 to 27.7 after removing repetitions; the smallest model achieves the best Gen-PPL solely due to highest repetition. ACE reduces repetition to near-human levels while preserving competitive generation quality. The single corrective direction transfers near-unchanged across model scales (105M → 342M → 652M) and samplers. ACE is 1.5–5× cheaper in compute to produce human-clean text compared to alternatives.

Conclusion: Gen-PPL is an unreliable metric for diffusion language models because it rewards repetition. The root cause is a one-dimensional failure mode in the self-conditioning loop, and the one-dimensional ACE fix effectively resolves it. The direction is highly transferable across scales and architectures, and compute-to-clean-text is a more honest evaluation framework.

Abstract: Continuous diffusion language models such as ELF report record-low generative perplexity (Gen-PPL). We find a catch: these models repeat far more than human text, and Gen-PPL rewards rather than penalizes that repetition, so its low scores overstate quality. Strip the repetition and ELF-B's Gen-PPL rises from $19.5$ to $27.7$; the smallest model even posts the best Gen-PPL because it repeats most. We trace the repetition to its source: a contractive attractor along a \emph{single direction} in the self-conditioning feedback loop, the loop that feeds each step's clean estimate into the next. Because the failure is one-dimensional, a one-dimensional fix suffices, and we propose one. \textbf{ACE} (Attractor-Contrast-Escape) subtracts that single, label-free direction from the feedback at each step. Estimated once on the $105$M model, the direction cuts repetition to near the human level while keeping quality competitive, and transfers near-unchanged to the $342$M and $652$M models and across samplers; the same recipe recovers useful directions on other architectures. Since Gen-PPL itself rewards repetition, we instead measure the compute each fix needs to produce human-clean text, where ACE is $1.5$--$5\times$ cheaper.

</details>


### [33] ["Don't Say It!": Constraints, Compliance, and Communication when Language Models Play Taboo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00601)
*Sara Candussio,Francesca Padovani,Daniel Scalena,Malvina Nissim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper uses the word-guessing game Taboo to study how LLMs handle competing demands of lexical constraints and communicative effectiveness. Through interventions at prompting, generation-time, and representation levels, the authors find a trade-off between rule compliance and description quality, and that LLMs remain much weaker than humans at guessing from constrained descriptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Taboo provides a natural testbed for examining how LLMs navigate the tension between strict lexical constraints (avoiding forbidden words) and the need for communicatively effective descriptions. This deceptively simple task reveals deeper challenges in lexical grounding under constraint.

Method: The authors evaluate two open-weight LLMs under three progressively deeper intervention conditions: (1) prompting-level constraints, (2) generation-time constraints (e.g., logit suppression), and (3) internal representation manipulations. Outputs are assessed via forbidden word violation detection, LLM-as-a-judge evaluations of communicative effectiveness for both human and machine guessers, and qualitative comparison of strategies against human Taboo players.

Result: Rule compliance and communicative effectiveness trade off differently across intervention conditions. Models remain substantially weaker than humans as guessers, indicating that lexical grounding under constraint remains an open challenge.

Conclusion: Despite progressive interventions into the generative process, current LLMs struggle to simultaneously satisfy lexical constraints and produce effective communicative descriptions in the Taboo setting, highlighting a fundamental limitation in constrained lexical grounding.

Abstract: The game of Taboo requires describing a target word without using a set of forbidden words, so that other players can guess it. This deceptively simple task combines strict lexical constraints with the need for communicatively effective descriptions, making it a compelling playground for examining how LLMs navigate competing demands at inference time. We evaluate two open-weight models under conditions that intervene at progressively deeper levels of the generative process, from prompting to generation-time constraints to internal representations manipulations. We assess their outputs through forbidden word violation detection, LLM-as-a-judge measuring the degree to which generated descriptions successfully evoke the target concept for both human and machine guessers, and examining whether the strategies models adopt under constraint align with those of human players. Our results show that compliance with the rules of the game and communicative effectiveness trade off differently across conditions, and that models remain substantially weaker than humans as guessers, suggesting that lexical grounding under constraint is an open challenge for current language models.

</details>


### [34] [Auditing Forgetting in Limited Memory Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00605)
*Arya Raeesi,Hanna Roed*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces a causal auditing framework that decomposes post-deletion behavior in Limited Memory Language Models (LMLMs) into parametric leakage, retrieval-mediated correctness, and retrieval artifact rate. Auditing across 12,228 deletions and thirteen databases reveals parametric leakage is near zero; residual knowledge survives almost entirely through near-neighbor retrieval artifacts, suggesting the database administrator—not the model—primarily controls unlearning boundaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LMLM evaluations measure post-deletion correctness only in aggregate and cannot distinguish whether a deleted fact persists due to residual parametric memory, alternative retrieval paths, or near-neighbor retrieval artifacts. A finer-grained causal decomposition is needed to understand where and how deleted knowledge survives.

Method: The authors propose a causal auditing framework with three inference-time interventions: FULL (database intact), DEL-ON (specific fact deleted), and DEL-OFF (all related facts removed). The framework decomposes post-deletion behavior into: (1) parametric leakage L(f) — the model returning the deleted answer without retrieval; (2) retrieval-mediated correctness R(f); and (3) a retrieval artifact rate derived from inference-time retrieval traces. They apply this to 12,228 alias-closure deletions across thirteen databases (including four adversarial topologies: Base, Alias, Noise, Collision) in three domains, using six prompt formulations.

Result: Parametric leakage is near zero across all variants and prompt styles. Retrieval-mediated correctness and the retrieval artifact rate match within rounding everywhere, meaning post-deletion correctness is predominantly reconstituted from near-neighbor retrieval. Residual survival ranges from 0.7% (released LMLM database) to 13.6% (most adversarial variant). Prompt formulation does not independently control how much of a deleted fact survives.

Conclusion: For this class of LMLM and deletion procedure, the unlearning boundary is drawn primarily by the database administrator rather than by the model. Near-neighbor retrieval artifacts are the dominant leakage pathway, not parametric memory.

Abstract: Limited Memory Language Models (LMLMs) externalize factual knowledge to a database to enable deletion-based unlearning without retraining. Existing evaluations measure post-deletion correctness in aggregate and cannot tell whether a deleted fact persists through residual parametric memory, alternative retrieval paths, or near-neighbor retrieval artifacts. We propose a causal auditing framework that holds the model fixed and varies the database state at inference time across three interventions: FULL, DEL-ON, and DEL-OFF. The framework decomposes post-deletion behavior into parametric leakage L(f), retrieval-mediated correctness R(f), and a retrieval artifact rate grounded in the inference-time retrieval trace. We apply it to 12,228 alias-closure deletions across thirteen databases, including four adversarial topologies (Base, Alias, Noise, Collision) we construct in three domains, and six prompt formulations. Parametric leakage is near zero in every variant and every prompt style: the model rarely returns the deleted answer in the absence of retrieval. The residual that does survive lives in the retrieval graph: retrieval-mediated correctness and the retrieval artifact rate match within rounding everywhere, so post-deletion correctness is, in our audit, predominantly reconstituted from near-neighbor retrieval. This residual ranges from 0.7% on the released LMLM database to 13.6% on the most adversarial variant, and prompt formulation does not independently control how much of a deleted fact survives. These results suggest that, for this class of LMLM and deletion procedure, the unlearning boundary is drawn primarily by the database administrator rather than by the model.

</details>


### [35] [Faithful by Definition: Emotion Analysis via Natural Semantic Metalanguage Explications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00661)
*Frank Xing,Erik Cambria*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A causally faithful emotion classification system where a learned parser maps text to a structured Natural Semantic Metalanguage explication, and a fixed rule list deterministically computes the label, enabling full auditability with minimal accuracy trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-hoc explanations for emotion classifiers lack faithfulness guarantees — they may not reflect the model's actual computation. The authors seek a causally faithful, inspectable alternative where the decision basis is transparent by design.

Method: An explication interface is built: a fine-tuned parser maps input event descriptions to a script in Natural Semantic Metalanguage (NSM) organized into 12 typed slots. A fixed decision list of rules — transcribed directly from published semantic definitions — computes the emotion label exclusively from the explication, ensuring the parser bears all empirical risk. The per-line entailment interface allows auditing parser outputs against the input text.

Result: On crowd-sourced event descriptions, the fine-tuned parser achieves 0.33 accuracy and 0.48 selective accuracy on a small held-out set. The authors argue this represents an insignificant accuracy gap compared to black-box models, while providing a verifiable decision basis. The EmoExpl-1200 dataset with per-line verification metadata and the full rule set is released.

Conclusion: A causally faithful, definitionally grounded emotion analysis system is feasible, trading a modest accuracy reduction for complete transparency, auditability, and inspectability in first-person event-based emotion classification.

Abstract: Explanations for emotion classifiers are usually produced post hoc, with no guarantee that they reflect the computation behind the label. We present an explication interface for event-based emotion analysis. A parser maps the input text to an explication, a short script in the closed vocabulary of Natural Semantic Metalanguage organized into twelve typed slots, and a fixed decision list of rules transcribed from published semantic definitions computes the label from the explication alone. The faithfulness guarantee is therefore causal and definitional, while all empirical risk lives in the learned parser, which the per-line entailment interface makes auditable against the input. On crowd-sourced event descriptions, our fine-tuned parser reaches 0.33 accuracy and 0.48 selective accuracy on a small held-out set, suggesting that the interface trades insignificant accuracy difference to a black-box model for a verifiable, inspectable decision basis for first-person event-based emotion analysis. We also release EmoExpl-1200 with per-line verification metadata and the full rule set.

</details>


### [36] [YOMI-Bench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Kanji Reading and Phonological Understanding of LLMs for Japanese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00664)
*Ryota Mibayashi,Hiroya Takamura,Hitomi Yanaka*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: YOMI-Bench is a benchmark for evaluating Japanese LLMs' ability to read kanji and understand phonology, revealing that even Japanese-specialized and commercial models struggle with kanji reading tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kanji characters in Japanese have multiple possible readings (phonological mappings) that cannot be reliably inferred from surface text alone. LLMs empirically exhibit poor performance on kanji reading, yet no dedicated benchmark existed to systematically measure this capability across models.

Method: The authors constructed YOMI-Bench, comprising four tasks designed to assess kanji reading performance in Japanese. They evaluated one multilingual open LLM, four Japanese-specific open LLMs, and five commercial LLMs using this benchmark.

Result: Even Japanese-specific models demonstrated low performance on YOMI-Bench. Commercial models also performed poorly, particularly on generation tasks that require explicit consideration of kanji readings.

Conclusion: Kanji reading remains a significant challenge for current LLMs, including those specialized for Japanese and commercial-grade systems. This highlights a critical gap in phonological understanding that surface-level language modeling fails to address.

Abstract: We propose YOMI-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating kanji reading and phonological understanding of large language models (LLMs) for Japanese. In Japanese, a single kanji character often has multiple possible readings, making it difficult to infer the correct reading from surface-level text alone. Due to these linguistic characteristics, it is empirically known that LLMs exhibit low performance in kanji reading for Japanese. The proposed YOMI-Bench consists of four tasks specifically designed to evaluate kanji reading performance in Japanese. In our evaluation using YOMI-Bench, we assessed one multilingual open LLM, four Japanese-specific open LLMs, and five commercial LLMs. As a result, we found that even Japanese-specific models show low performance, and that commercial models also perform poorly on generation tasks that require consideration of kanji readings.

</details>


### [37] [Self-conditioned Flow Map Language Models via Fixed-point Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00714)
*Jaehoon Yoo,Wonjung Kim,Floor Eijkelboom,Chanhyuk Lee,Nicholas M. Boffi,Seunghoon Hong,Jinwoo Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Self-conditioned flow language models implicitly perform a fixed-point iteration. The authors formalize this as "fixed-point flows" — a 2D framework merging the flow process with fixed-point iteration — and distill both dimensions into a highly efficient few-step language model (FMLM★) that outperforms prior self-conditioned and few-step generators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two open problems: (1) the mechanism behind self-conditioning's empirical success in flow-based language models is poorly understood; (2) it is unclear how to leverage self-conditioning for few-step flow-map-based generators.

Method: Interpret self-conditioned denoising as a fixed-point iteration that bootstraps the learned denoiser. Propose "fixed-point flows," a 2D class where one dimension is the flow process and the other is the fixed-point iteration. Show these define valid flow maps. Distill the 2D model via fixed-point distillation (compressing iterations) and flow map distillation (compressing the flow process), yielding the compact FMLM★ model.

Result: FMLM★ achieves state-of-the-art performance on OpenWebText for both one-step and few-step text generation, surpassing existing self-conditioned and few-step models.

Conclusion: Self-conditioning can be rigorously understood as fixed-point iteration. The fixed-point flows framework unifies and compresses both the denoising and flow dimensions, enabling highly performant few-step language models.

Abstract: Self-conditioning is a core technique that enhances continuous flow-based language models, where the model learns to denoise generated text by conditioning on its own denoising estimate. While empirically successful, its performance improvements are poorly understood. Moreover, there is growing interest in the use of few-step generators based on flow maps, for which how to leverage self-conditioning is unclear. Here, we show that flow language models with self-conditioning solve a fixed-point iteration that bootstraps the performance of the learned denoiser. We use this viewpoint to formulate fixed-point flows, a two-dimensional class of self-conditioned flows, where the first dimension represents the flow process and the second represents the fixed-point iteration. We show that fixed-point flows define valid flow maps, and show that they can be distilled from self-conditioned flow models by compressing both fixed-point iterations and the flow process, the former with fixed-point distillation and the latter with flow map distillation. Our resulting flow map language model, FMLM$^\star$, outperforms state-of-the-art self-conditioned models and few-step models in one- and few-step generation on OpenWebText. Code is available at https://github.com/Ugness/self-conditioned-fmlm.

</details>


### [38] [MSQA: A Natively Sourced Multilingual and Multicultural SimpleQA Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00724)
*Xianru Chen,Yukai Huang,Mingxiang Chen,Xinping Lei,Fangbing Deng,Jin Chen,Ge Zhang,Wenhao Huang,Jiaheng Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Illusion of Cultural Alignment — the false assumption that multilingual fluency implies cultural understanding. Using MSQA, a natively sourced benchmark across 11 languages and 5 cultural dimensions, the authors evaluate 18 LLMs and find significant cultural competence gaps. They identify a "Locality Effect" where cultural knowledge correlates with pre-training data exposure rather than general reasoning, and show that inference-time remedies (calibration, repeated sampling, retrieval augmentation) fail to fix the problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multilingual LLMs create a misleading impression: because they can fluently speak a user's language, users and developers assume they also understand the associated culture. This "Illusion of Cultural Alignment" remains untested with rigorous, natively grounded benchmarks — existing multilingual benchmarks rely heavily on translation, which introduces English-centric biases and shortcuts that mask true cultural competence.

Method: The authors construct MSQA (Multilingual Situational Question Answering), a benchmark of 1,064 natively sourced (not translated) questions spanning 11 language groups, 5 cultural dimensions, and 3 difficulty tiers. They evaluate 18 LLMs on this benchmark, measuring cultural degradation relative to general performance. They analyze the relationship between pre-training language exposure and cultural competence (the Locality Effect), and test three inference-time interventions: confidence calibration, repeated sampling for majority voting, and retrieval-augmented generation.

Result: All models exhibit substantial cultural degradation on MSQA compared to English-centric benchmarks. The Locality Effect is pronounced: cultural competence correlates with pre-training data exposure rather than general reasoning ability. Inference-time remedies fail — models remain overconfident on unfamiliar cultural questions, repeated sampling produces unstable rather than reliable correctness, and retrieval augmentation helps only unevenly on long-tail cultural facts.

Conclusion: Cultural alignment cannot be inferred from multilingual fluency alone. Addressing it requires deeper intervention beyond calibration, sampling, or retrieval at inference time — likely necessitating changes at the pre-training or fine-tuning stage to embed genuine cultural knowledge.

Abstract: Multilingual fluency often invites a stronger assumption: a model that can speak a user's language must also understand the culture encoded by that language. We call this the Illusion of Cultural Alignment. To test this assumption directly, we introduce MSQA, a benchmark of 1,064 natively sourced questions across 11 language groups, five cultural dimensions, and three difficulty tiers. Unlike translated benchmarks, MSQA targets locally grounded knowledge and reduces shortcuts from English-centric cross-lingual transfer. Evaluating 18 LLMs, we find substantial cultural degradation and a pronounced Locality Effect: cultural competence tracks pre-training exposure more closely than general reasoning ability. We further show that common inference-time remedies do not dissolve the illusion. Models remain overconfident on unfamiliar cultural questions, repeated sampling yields unstable rather than reliable correctness, and retrieval augmentation helps unevenly on long-tail facts. These findings indicate that cultural alignment cannot be inferred from multilingual ability alone and requires deeper intervention than calibration, sampling, or retrieval at inference time

</details>


### [39] [MetaHOPE: A Metaphor-Oriented Evaluation Framework for Analysing MT and LLM Translation Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00848)
*Jiahui Liang,Lifeng Han*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MetaHOPE is an error severity-aware annotation framework for evaluating metaphor translation quality, tested on GoogleMT, GPT-4, and Hunyuan-7b using VUAMC and PSUCMC corpora, with bilingual gold references produced as a new resource.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Metaphors pose significant challenges for NLP/MT systems due to semantic complexity, contextual dependency, and cultural embeddings, leading to ambiguity. There is a need for a dedicated, severity-aware evaluation framework to assess how well SOTA models handle metaphor translation.

Method: The authors propose MetaHOPE, an error severity-aware annotation framework. They apply it to human-annotated metaphor corpora (VUAMC for EN→ZH, PSUCMC for ZH→EN), evaluate three representative MT systems (GoogleMT as NMT, GPT-4 and Hunyuan-7b as LLMs), and produce human post-edited bilingual gold references.

Result: The paper produces: (1) the MetaHOPE annotation framework, (2) parallel metaphor translation corpora with error annotations and human post-edited references, and (3) an error analysis of SOTA models on metaphor translation. Resources are to be released publicly upon acceptance.

Conclusion: The MetaHOPE framework, the resulting parallel corpora, and the error analysis provide valuable insights for metaphor translation research. The publicly released resources aim to advance the study of metaphor handling in MT and NLP systems.

Abstract: In this opinion paper, we propose MetaHOPE, an error severity-aware annotation framework for evaluating metaphor translations. Metaphors present challenges for machine translation (MT) and natural language understanding and processing (NLU, NLP), because it presents the features of semantic complexity, contextual dependency, and cultural embeddings that can lead to ambiguity issues for NLP models. To investigate how state-of-the-art NLP models perform on translating metaphors, we select three representative systems, i.e., GoogleMT, GPT5.4, and Hunyuan-7b as Neural MT (NMT) models and LLMs. We used two human-annotated metaphor corpora, including VUAMC and PSUCMC for English-to-Chinese and Chinese-to-English translation purposes. The original corpora we used are monolingual, where we carried out error annotation using the MetaHOPE framework, and also produced the human post-edited gold reference for bilingual use as a new resource. We believe the MetaHOPE evaluation framework for metaphor translation annotation, the parallel corpora resources, and the error analysis on SOTA automatic translation models can be useful and shed some light for the field of metaphor translation study. We share our resources publicly upon paper acceptance.

</details>


### [40] [The Course of News Events: A Comparison of Bottom-Up and Top-Down Approaches for Collecting Text-Based Data about Disasters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00849)
*Brielen Madureira,Andreas Niekler,Mariana Madruga de Brito*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Comparing top-down (disaster inventory–based querying) versus bottom-up (NLP clustering by temporal/spatial features) approaches for selecting representative news samples on landslides, the study reveals how this methodological choice shapes event coverage and downstream analyses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: News articles are a critical source for understanding disaster impacts and adaptation, but there is no consensus on how to select a representative sample. The authors aim to address the methodological gap by systematically comparing two dominant sampling strategies and their consequences.

Method: Using a corpus of German-language news articles reporting on landslides worldwide, the study contrasts a top-down approach (querying news databases using an existing disaster inventory as a filter) with a bottom-up approach (applying NLP methods to cluster articles based on temporal and spatial features). The resulting samples are compared for variations in event coverage.

Result: The choice between top-down and bottom-up sampling strategies produces notably different news samples, with implications for event representation. These differences can affect research on media coverage inequality, disaster monitoring efficacy, and the enrichment of disaster inventories.

Conclusion: Research design decisions regarding news sample selection are consequential and non-trivial; scholars must carefully consider how their sampling approach may bias findings in socio-environmental disaster studies.

Abstract: News articles are an important source of information on disaster impacts and adaptation. A key methodological challenge in socio-environmental studies is how to select a representative data sample. Two approaches are common: querying news databases top-down with the aid of an existing disaster inventory or using NLP methods to cluster news texts bottom-up based on temporal and spatial features. Using a dataset of German news about landslides worldwide, we compare these approaches and discuss variations in event coverage. Such research design decision can influence the resulting news sample, affecting its use in studies of inequality in media coverage, disaster monitoring and inventory enrichment.

</details>


### [41] [Recovering Input Text from Hidden States: Study of Gradient-Based Inversion of Decoder-Only Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00852)
*Mikołaj Słowikowski,Maciej Witold Majewski*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper frames hidden-state inversion of decoder-only LMs as a continuous embedding-space optimization, revealing that GPT-2's last-layer hidden states are nearly as sensitive as the original text — achieving 97.5% exact-match recovery on 10-token C4 prompts, with failures concentrated in high-frequency function words rather than content-bearing tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To expose the richness of internal signals during hidden-state inversion by keeping optimization entirely in continuous embedding space (no hard-token projection until commit), enabling observability of rank trajectories, per-position loss curves, and a discrete loss at commit time that makes reconstruction failures detectable and diagnosable.

Method: A continuous embedding-space optimization approach where a soft proxy vector is optimized towards the leaked target hidden state without intermediate hard-token projection. A token is committed only once at the end of the inner loop. Analysis includes rank trajectories of ground-truth tokens, per-position loss curves, and cumulative discrete loss. Experiments used 10-token C4 prompts with GPT-2, compared against the SIPIT reference method (which uses per-step hard projection).

Result: Exact-match rate on 10-token C4 prompts rises from 66.9% to 97.5% (mean similarity 0.994) as the candidate window is widened, indicating most errors are near-misses. Failures are categorically asymmetric: space-prefixed, high-frequency function words in dense embedding regions dominate errors, while content-bearing tokens are recovered almost perfectly. The continuous formulation makes optimization observable and failures detectable, unlike per-step hard projection.

Conclusion: Last-layer hidden states of GPT-2 are as sensitive as the original text. The continuous-space optimization formulation is critical not merely for recovery accuracy but for making the inversion process interpretable — enabling diagnosis of where and why reconstructions fail, which is impossible under per-step hard-projection regimes like SIPIT.

Abstract: This work studies the hidden-state inversion problem: recovering the original input token sequence of a decoder-only language model from its last-layer hidden states. Rather than treating inversion as a one-shot reconstruction, we study it as a continuous embedding-space optimisation in which a soft proxy is driven towards the leaked target without any hard-token projection during the search, and a token is committed only once, at the end of the inner loop. This design choice has two consequences which are the main focus of this paper. First, keeping the optimisation entirely in continuous space exposes a rich set of internal signals: rank trajectories of the ground-truth token, per-position loss curves, and a discrete loss measured at commit time. Second, the discrete loss allows assessing the correctness of recovery via cumulative discrete loss. We further analyse which tokens break the reconstructions and find a sharp categorical asymmetry: space-prefixed, high-frequency function words in dense regions of the embedding matrix dominate the failures, while content-bearing tokens are recovered almost perfectly. On 10-token C4 prompts the exact-match rate rises from 66.9% to 97.5% (mean similarity 0.994) as the candidate window is widened, confirming that most errors are recoverable near-misses rather than genuine ambiguities. A comparison with the released SIPIT reference situates these findings: per-step hard projection is faster, but the continuous formulation is what makes the optimisation observable and its failures detectable. The results show that last-layer hidden states of GPT-2 are as sensitive as the original text.

</details>


### [42] [CAT: Confidence-Adaptive Thinking for Efficient Reasoning of Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00862)
*Qizhi Jiang,Shuo Wang,Pei Ke,Yuhang Song,Ke Qin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CAT uses a model's internal confidence signals during preference optimization to adaptively control reasoning length — compressing confident responses while allowing extended deliberation on uncertain ones, outperforming uniform compression baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LRMs exhibit overthinking on simple queries, wasting tokens and reducing efficiency. Existing compression methods either apply uniform length reduction or use coarse-grained difficulty estimation, both of which degrade performance on hard problems.

Method: Confidence-Adaptive Thinking (CAT) integrates the model's intrinsic self-certainty signals as confidence into the preference optimization process, enabling autonomous modulation of reasoning lengths based on problem difficulty.

Result: CAT consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on reasoning accuracy across multiple benchmarks and different base models.

Conclusion: CAT offers a robust solution for balancing accuracy and latency in practical industrial scenarios by enabling LRMs to compress confident responses while deliberating on uncertain ones.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have achieved remarkable success on complex tasks by leveraging long chain-of-thought (CoT) trajectories, yet they frequently exhibit overthinking on simple queries, resulting in significant token overhead and reduced inference efficiency. However, existing compression methods predominantly apply uniform length reduction or rely on coarse-grained difficulty estimation, often leading to performance degradation on difficult problems. To address this limitation, we propose Confidence-Adaptive Thinking (CAT), a framework that incorporates the model's intrinsic self-certainty signals as confidence into the preference optimization process, which autonomously modulates reasoning lengths based on problem difficulty. Experimental results show that CAT consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on reasoning accuracy across multiple benchmarks on different base models. Our work enables LRMs to effectively compress confident responses while deliberating on uncertain ones, offering a potentially robust solution for balancing accuracy and latency in practical industrial scenarios.

</details>


### [43] [Dynamic Bidirectional Pattern Memory: A Production-Scale Empirical Characterisation of Inference-Time Gating in Clinical NLP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00870)
*Ali H. Lazem,William Teahan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A production-scale clinical NLP pipeline using a generator-verifier architecture (Llama-3.3 70B + MMed-Llama-3.1 70B) was augmented with inference-time pattern-memory gating. Direct learning from verifier rejections failed due to excessive sparsity; simpler ontology-based rules succeeded. A key transferable insight: pre-generation gates are selective only when they test the same evidence the verifier weighs, not when they imitate its output.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In production clinical NLP pipelines, running a large verifier model on every candidate extraction is computationally expensive. The authors explore whether a lightweight, learnable memory can gate (pre-filter) extractions at inference time, sparing the verifier from re-examining candidates already seen to fail, thereby reducing cost and latency without sacrificing clinical accuracy.

Method: The pipeline pairs a Llama-3.3 70B generator proposing extractions with an MMed-Llama-3.1 70B verifier accepting/rejecting them over 167,034 PMC-Patients narratives. A lightweight memory module learns at deployment which extraction patterns to filter. Two gating strategies are tested: (1) learning filtering rules directly from logged verifier rejections for relation extraction and question answering, and (2) a fixed clinical ontology rule that flags ontology-violating relations without the verifier. Five QA filter variants are systematically compared, and all filters flag rather than delete suspect extractions for clinical review.

Result: Four key findings: (1) Direct learning from 785,797 verifier rejections produced an empty relation-extraction filter — rejections were spread too thinly across too many distinct forms. (2) An ontology-based rule captured 49,734 ontology-violating relations on a 5,000-patient held-out set without the verifier. (3) Only one of five QA filter variants succeeded: checking whether extracted entities support the question, yielding 1.84× higher likelihood of flagging verifier-rejected answers. (4) Across all variants, selectivity emerges only when the filter tests the same evidence the verifier weighs, not when it imitates verifier output.

Conclusion: The most natural memory design — learning directly from verifier rejections — can fail silently at scale due to sparsity. Whether a pre-generation gate is selective is determined before engineering effort, by whether its signal probes the question the verifier itself answers. This transferable principle applies to any generator-verifier pipeline. The system preserves clinical visibility by flagging rather than deleting suspect extractions.

Abstract: We study inference-time pattern-memory gating in a production-scale clinical natural language processing (NLP) pipeline. The pipeline pairs a generator (Llama-3.3 70B) proposing extractions with a verifier (MMed-Llama-3.1 70B) accepting or rejecting them, over 167,034 PMC-Patients narratives, and adds a lightweight memory that learns at deployment which extractions to filter, so the verifier need not re-examine candidates already seen to fail. We report four findings. First, learning filtering rules directly from the verifier's rejections failed at full scale: the relation-extraction filter stayed empty despite 785,797 logged rejections, because they were spread too thinly across too many distinct forms to accumulate. Second, a simpler rule using a fixed clinical ontology produced the same filtering without the verifier, capturing 49,734 ontology-violating relations on a held-out 5,000-patient set. Third, of five versions of the question-answering filter, four failed for distinct, instructive reasons; the fifth succeeded by checking whether a patient's extracted entities support the question asked, and where it applies was 1.84 times likelier to flag an answer the verifier would reject than one it would accept. Fourth, one pattern held across all five: a filter is selective only when it tests the same evidence the verifier weighs, not when it imitates the verifier's output. Together these give a transferable result for any generator-verifier pipeline: the most natural memory design can fail silently at scale, and whether a pre-generation gate is selective is decided before any engineering effort, by whether its signal probes the question the verifier itself answers. Throughout, the system flags suspect extractions rather than deleting them, so every decision stays visible for clinical review. All code and test artefacts are released openly.

</details>


### [44] [How Ethos and Pathos Appeals Resonate in Reader Interpretations of Social Media Messages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00873)
*Ewelina Gajewska,Katarzyna Budzynska,Jaroslaw Chudziak,Liesbeth Allein*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This study examines how classical rhetorical appeals (ethos and pathos) affect the silent majority of social media readers, finding that interpretations diverge from original messages in 30% of cases, with emotionally/credibility-charged content producing greater variability, and that these appeals predict audience attitudes toward authors even without visible engagement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior research on rhetorical strategies focuses on visible social media engagement (posts and comments), neglecting the "universal audience" — the silent majority of readers who do not explicitly react. The authors aim to fill this gap by investigating how ethos and pathos shape meaning interpretation among non-responding readers.

Method: The authors used a dataset of social media sentences paired with human-written interpretations. Both source sentences and interpretations were labeled for the presence of ethos (credibility-based appeal) and pathos (emotion-based appeal). They then analyzed whether these rhetorical appeals are preserved in audience interpretations and measured divergence rates between original and interpreted meanings, comparing rhetorically charged versus neutral content.

Result: Interpretations diverge from original sentences in 30% of cases. Rhetorically charged content elicits greater variability in interpretation than neutral content. Additionally, ethos and pathos in original sentences serve as predictors of audience attitudes toward the author, demonstrating rhetoric's influence on perception beyond observable engagement.

Conclusion: Rhetorical appeals exert measurable influence on silent audiences, shaping both meaning interpretation and author perception. The findings underscore that limiting analysis to visible engagement underestimates rhetoric's persuasive reach, and they highlight the importance of accounting for the universal audience in communication research.

Abstract: Rhetorical strategies and their influence on audiences are often studied through social media posts and comments. However, this focus overlooks the universal audience, which is the majority of readers who remain silent and do not explicitly express how a message affects them. This study investigates how two classical modes of persuasion, ethos and pathos, resonate in the silent audience's interpretations of meaning. Using a dataset of social media sentences paired with human-written interpretations, we label both sources for ethos and pathos and assess whether these rhetorical appeals are preserved. Our analyses show that interpretations diverge from the original sentences in 30% of cases, with rhetorically charged content eliciting greater variability than neutral content. We further find that ethos and pathos in original sentences can predict audience attitudes toward the author, underscoring the subtle ways rhetoric shapes perception beyond visible engagement.

</details>


### [45] [MultiSynt/MT: Trillion-Token Multi-Parallel Pre-Training Data Translated Across 36 Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00890)
*Maximilian Idahl,Jörg Tiedemann,Sampo Pyysalo,David Salinas,Tomasz Galica,Shenbin Qian,Tudor Nicolae Mateiu,Zihao Li,Anna Lokrantz,Fedor Vitiugin,André F. T. Martins,Jenna Kanerva,Filip Ginter,Matthias Lindemann,Tim Isbister,Birger Moell,Jonas Lindh,Jan Hajič,Jenia Jitsev,Andrey Kutuzov,Stephan Oepen,Gema Ramírez-Sánchez*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MultiSynt/MT introduces a 4.8-trillion-token open synthetic parallel corpus covering 36 European languages, created by translating high-quality English data. LLMs trained on this corpus match a native-data baseline with ~72% fewer tokens and outperform it by ~15% at matched budgets, while analysis reveals that standard benchmarks mask translation-quality differences that LLM-as-judge evaluations capture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open web-scale pre-training corpora are heavily skewed toward English, severely limiting the development of high-quality multilingual LLMs, especially for medium- and lower-resource European languages that lack large openly available pre-training resources.

Method: The authors create MultiSynt/MT by translating 100 billion high-quality Nemotron-CC English tokens into 36 European languages using Tower+ and OPUS-MT/HPLT-MT translation systems, yielding approximately 4.8 trillion target-language tokens. They train reference LLMs on this synthetic corpus and evaluate on a broad multilingual benchmark suite, comparing against the HPLT 2.0 native-data baseline. They also employ fluency-sensitive LLM-as-judge evaluation to complement standard multiple-choice benchmarks.

Result: LLMs trained on MultiSynt/MT match HPLT 2.0's final score using ~72% fewer pre-training tokens and outperform it by ~15% relative at a matched 100B-token budget. Standard multiple-choice benchmarks fail to reflect translation-quality differences, whereas LLM-as-judge evaluation cleanly recovers these differences. Norwegian idiomatic and culturally grounded tasks remain better served by native data.

Conclusion: Synthetic translated corpora like MultiSynt/MT can dramatically improve data efficiency for multilingual LLM pre-training across European languages, but evaluation methods must evolve beyond standard multiple-choice benchmarks to capture translation-quality effects. The corpus, including row-aligned multi-system translations, is released to support further research.

Abstract: Open web-scale pre-training corpora remain concentrated in English, limiting multilingual LLM development. We introduce MultiSynt/MT, an open synthetic parallel corpus with approximately 4.8 trillion target-language tokens across 36 European languages, produced by translating 100 billion high-quality Nemotron-CC tokens with Tower+ and OPUS-MT/HPLT-MT systems. For many medium- and lower-resource European languages, this is the largest openly available pre-training resource. On a broad multilingual benchmark suite, reference LLMs trained on MultiSynt/MT reach the final score of HPLT 2.0, a native-data baseline, using roughly 72% fewer pre-training tokens, and outperform it by approximately 15% relative at a matched 100B-token training budget. Our analyses also identify evaluation blind spots: standard multiple-choice benchmarks miss translation-quality differences that a fluency-sensitive LLM-as-judge evaluation cleanly recovers on the trained LLMs (with no fluency deficit in MultiSynt itself), and Norwegian idiomatic and culturally grounded tasks remain better served by native data. We release the corpus, including row-aligned translations from multiple systems, to support controlled research on multilingual pre-training data and evaluation.

</details>


### [46] [Beyond Document Grounding: Span-Level Hallucination Detection over Code, Tool Output, and Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00895)
*Ádám Kovács,Bowei He,Xue Liu,István Boros,Szilveszter Tóth,Gábor Recski*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces a unified benchmark for span-level hallucination detection in RAG systems that extends beyond natural language to include structured inputs like code, tool output, and markdown. A fine-tuned Qwen3.5-2B detector achieves strong performance (0.689 span-F1) across domains, dramatically outperforming baselines like LettuceDetect (0.17) and zero-shot LLM judges (≤0.22).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current hallucination detection for RAG is predominantly evaluated on natural-language documents, but real-world grounded generation systems increasingly rely on structured inputs (source code, developer-tool output, markdown, tables, repository metadata). No unified benchmark exists that covers these diverse modalities simultaneously for span-level hallucination detection.

Method: The authors construct a unified benchmark by starting from grounded correct answers, injecting localized hallucinations with exact character-level labels, and validating the code test split through evidence-based review. They then fine-tune a Qwen3.5-2B model on this benchmark and evaluate against baselines including LettuceDetect-large and zero-shot LLM judges.

Result: The fine-tuned Qwen3.5-2B detector achieves 0.689 span-F1 on the unified test set and 0.60 on the code-agent source. It substantially outperforms LettuceDetect-large (0.17) and zero-shot LLM judges (≤0.22). On existing natural-language benchmarks, it remains competitive with 81.8 RAGTruth example-F1 and 0.724 English PsiloQA IoU.

Conclusion: Span-level hallucination detection can be effectively unified across code, structured documents, and natural language. A relatively small fine-tuned model (2B parameters) can significantly outperform much larger zero-shot LLM judges, demonstrating the value of targeted training on diverse, accurately labeled data.

Abstract: Hallucination detection for retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is usually evaluated on natural-language document evidence. However, grounded generation systems increasingly rely on structured inputs: source code, developer-tool output, markdown documents, tables, and repository metadata. We introduce a unified benchmark for span-level hallucination detection over code, tool output, structured documents, and existing natural-language RAG datasets. The benchmark is built by starting from grounded correct answers, injecting localized hallucinations with exact character labels, and validating the code test split with evidence-based review. Our fine-tuned Qwen3.5-2B detector reaches 0.689 span-F1 on the unified test set and 0.60 on the code-agent source, where it substantially outperforms LettuceDetect-large (0.17) and the strongest zero-shot LLM judges we evaluated (at most 0.22). The same model remains competitive on established natural-language benchmarks, with 81.8 RAGTruth example-F1 and 0.724 English PsiloQA IoU.

</details>


### [47] [Adversarial Pragmatics for AI Safety Evaluation: A Benchmark for Instruction Conflict, Embedded Commands, and Policy Ambiguity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01153)
*Brett Reynolds*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes "adversarial pragmatics" — a linguistically-grounded benchmark and annotation protocol for evaluating LLM safety under ambiguous, context-dependent natural language phenomena (instruction conflict, embedded commands, deixis, indirect speech acts, etc.), replacing coarse pass/fail labels with multi-dimensional expert judgments and diagnostic metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety benchmarks collapse nuanced model behaviors into binary pass/fail labels, obscuring whether failures stem from capability limitations, policy ambiguity, instruction conflicts, scaffold failures, or unreliable evaluator judgments. This diagnostic opacity undermines the validity of safety evaluations.

Method: The authors construct a linguistically controlled taxonomy of adversarial pragmatic phenomena; develop an 18-item seed benchmark with validator-enforced metadata and a 54-row local seed pilot; and design an expert-evaluation protocol that independently rates task success, policy compliance, safety risk, refusal outcome, and evaluator confidence. They also introduce metrics for judge validity, diagnostic ambiguity, and taxonomy drift.

Result: The framework yields a structured methodology that disentangles distinct failure modes in safety evaluations. Metrics for judge validity, diagnostic ambiguity, and taxonomy drift enable systematic auditing of LLM judges, gold-set construction, and prompt-injection tests.

Conclusion: Adversarial pragmatics repurposes linguistic judgment methodology as a practical tool for validating safety evaluations, improving the interpretability and reliability of LLM safety assessments, supporting better safety documentation, and enabling more rigorous prompt-injection and agentic-task evaluation.

Abstract: Safety evaluations for language models increasingly depend on judgments about ambiguous natural-language behaviour: whether a model has followed an instruction, refused appropriately, complied with a policy, resisted an embedded command, or misreported progress in an agentic task. Existing benchmarks often compress these distinctions into pass/fail labels, obscuring whether failures arise from capability limits, policy ambiguity, instruction conflict, scaffold failure, or unstable evaluator judgments.
  This paper introduces adversarial pragmatics as a benchmark and annotation protocol for evaluating model behaviour under instruction conflict, embedded commands, quotation, scope ambiguity, deixis, indirect speech acts, and multi-turn agent transcripts. The contribution is empirical and methodological: a linguistically controlled taxonomy, an 18-item seed benchmark with validator-enforced metadata, a 54-row local seed pilot, an expert-evaluation protocol distinguishing task success, policy compliance, safety risk, refusal outcome, and evaluator confidence, and metrics for judge validity, diagnostic ambiguity, and taxonomy drift. The framework turns linguistic judgment methodology into a practical tool for validating safety evals, LLM judges, gold-set construction, prompt-injection tests, and safety documentation.

</details>


### [48] [Persona Non Grata: LLM Persona-Driven Generations in MCQA are Unstable in Distinct Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00937)
*César Guerra-Solano,Xiang Lorraine Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates the instability of persona-driven LLM generations in multiple-choice question answering (MCQA) tasks, introducing three novel stability metrics (performance, outcome, and question correctness) across model families, sizes, question domains, and hyperparameters like prompt format and temperature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
