Motivation: Prior work on persona-driven generation (PDG) stability has focused heavily on free-form text outputs (e.g., dialogue), while non-text-heavy outputs like MCQA remain understudied. The authors aim to fill this gap by systematically measuring and understanding how unstable LLM personas are in MCQA settings.

Method: The authors develop three instability metrics: performance stability (variance in accuracy), outcome stability (consistency of answer choices across runs), and question correctness stability (whether the same questions are answered correctly across runs). They apply these to multiple LLM families and sizes, varying question domains (math, commonsense, etc.), temperature, and prompt formats to isolate sources of instability.

Result: Instability varies systematically across model families and sizes. Math and commonsense questions induce greater instability. Prompt format contributes more to prediction instability than other hyperparameters like temperature. Instability correlates with task accuracy, and different experimental settings yield different best/worst personas despite persona similarity, underscoring the need to check hyperparameter instability.

Conclusion: Instability in persona-driven MCQA is non-trivial and varies predictably across models, domains, and experimental settings. Prompt format is a dominant factor. Researchers and practitioners should systematically evaluate hyperparameter instability when deploying PDGs, rather than assuming persona consistency.

Abstract: Persona-driven generations (PDGs) have seen prolific use in research and industry applications, where a large language model (LLM) takes on a 'persona' while completing some task. While persona expressed through free-form text (like dialogue) has substantial work investigating stability or consistency, relatively, persona expressed in non-text-heavy outputs (like in multiple-choice question answering, or MCQA) is often overlooked. We work to address this gap, seeking to understand the instability of LLM PDGs in MCQA tasks. We develop three metrics investigating the performance, outcome, and question correctness stability, evaluating three distinct dimensions. Using these metrics, we find that instability varies consistently between model families and model size, and across question domains, with math/commonsense questions leading to greater instability. We also find task prompt format introduces more prediction instability than other hyperparameters, like temperature. Finally, we find that instability is related to task accuracy, and using our instability metrics, find different experimental settings that result in different best and worst personas for tasks, despite their similarity. This reveals the importance of checking hyperparameter instability in PDGs.

</details>


### [49] [Svarna: An Open Corpus Workbench for Modern Greek](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00970)
*Stergios Chatzikyriakidis*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Svarna is a free, open-source, web-based corpus workbench for modern Greek that integrates five databases totaling over 507 million words, providing a unified interface for concordancing, frequency analysis, collocation extraction, discourse marker profiling, and optional LLM-based pragmatic annotation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Greek language technology suffers from chronic gaps: existing corpus resources are scattered across disparate platforms, often restricted to institutional access, or no longer available online. There is a need for a unified, freely accessible, and integrated corpus workbench that consolidates these resources without requiring login, installation, or specialized training.

Method: Svarna integrates five register-diverse databases (institutional, literary, dialectal, social media, historical) into a single web-based interface. The system is built on SQLite FTS5 full-text indexes served via a FastAPI backend, deployed as Docker containers on Microsoft Azure, and released under the MIT license. It provides: KWIC concordancing, register-normalized frequency analysis, collocation extraction via mutual information, a dictionary of 93 Greek discourse markers with distribution profiles, text-level analysis (n-grams, variants, collocation networks), register comparison using log-ratio, regular expression search, and an optional LLM layer for pragmatic annotation and free research mode.

Result: The platform delivers a fully operational corpus workbench hosting 507+ million words and ~29 million sentences across five registers. Source code, build scripts, and deployment configurations are publicly available on GitHub, and users can add their own corpora or deploy their own instances.

Conclusion: Svarna represents the first step in exploring available Greek corpus data and is expected to lay the foundation for more comprehensive linguistic research in the future.

Abstract: This paper introduces Svarna, a free, open-source, web-based corpus workbench for modern Greek. Svarna integrates five databases covering various registers, institutional, literary, dialectal, social media, and historical, to provide a total of more than 507 million words and around 29 million sentences. This platform addresses the chronic gaps in Greek language technology. Although various corpus resources exist, they are scattered across different platforms, and in many cases, institutional access is restricted or they are no longer available online. Svarna integrates these resources into a single interface that can be used without logging in, installation, or specialized training. This system provides a concordancer with KWIC marking capabilities, frequency analysis including register-by-register normalization, collocation extraction using mutual information, a dictionary of 93 Greek discourse markers providing distribution profiles, text-level analysis tools including n-grams, variants, and collocation networks, register comparison using log-ratio, regular expression search, and an optional LLM layer for pragmatic annotation and free research mode. This platform is built upon SQLite FTS5 full-text indexes provided via a FastAPI backend, deployed as Docker containers on Azure, and released under the MIT license. Source code, build scripts, and deployment configurations are publicly available on GitHub. Users can add their own corpora and deploy their own instances. This document describes the system design, corpus structure, and use cases demonstrating the various queries supported by the platform. Svarna serves as the first step in exploring available data and is expected to lay the foundation for more comprehensive research in the future.

</details>


### [50] [KnowledgeDebugger -- an Exploration Tool for Knowledge Localization and Editing in Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01000)
*Eric Benz,Lennart Stöpler,Nikolai Bolik,Artur Andrzejak*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: KnowledgeDebugger is a no-code GUI tool that integrates with EasyEdit to enable interactive exploration of knowledge localization and editing in Transformers, supporting the exploratory phase of research before rigorous statistical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Research on knowledge storage and editing in Transformers typically follows a two-phase workflow: initial exploratory investigation on individual samples, followed by statistically robust experiments. The first phase lacks dedicated tooling support, creating a gap that KnowledgeDebugger aims to fill by providing intuitive, code-free access to state-of-the-art knowledge editing methods.

Method: KnowledgeDebugger is a GUI-based tool inspired by LM-Debugger. It provides no-code access to the knowledge editing methods implemented in EasyEdit (a popular library of SOTA knowledge editing approaches), enabling researchers to interactively localize and edit knowledge in Transformer models through a visual interface.

Result: The paper demonstrates the tool's effectiveness through case studies that replicate and explore recent findings in knowledge localization and editing research, showing that KnowledgeDebugger successfully supports the exploratory, sample-level investigation phase.

Conclusion: KnowledgeDebugger bridges the gap in the research workflow by providing accessible, GUI-based tooling for the initial exploratory phase of knowledge editing research, lowering the barrier to entry and accelerating hypothesis generation before committing to large-scale statistical validation.

Abstract: Recent research has increasingly focused on understanding how Transformers store and process knowledge, as well as how this knowledge can be edited. Research work in this area is often conducted in two phases: first, phenomena are explored on individual samples. Then, when results appear promising, more statistically robust experiments follow. To support the first phase, we propose KnowledgeDebugger, a GUI-based exploration tool for knowledge localization and editing in Transformers. Our tool - inspired by LM-Debugger - offers no-code access to the methods in EasyEdit, a widely used library of state-of-the-art Knowledge Editing approaches. We demonstrate the tool's effectiveness through case studies of recent findings in this field.

</details>


### [51] [Logit-Contribution Scoring Identifies Non-Literal Retrieval Heads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01002)
*Aryo Pradipta Gema,Beatrice Alex,Pasquale Minervini*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LOCOS is a write-aware attention head detector that scores heads by their OV-circuit contribution to answer tokens, outperforming prior attention-based methods at identifying heads responsible for non-literal retrieval in long-context LLMs. Ablating top LOCOS heads collapses performance on synthesis benchmarks while preserving parametric recall and arithmetic reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing attention head detection methods identify heads based on literal token copying (attended token matches generated token), but this misses heads that perform non-literal synthesis — where the model synthesizes answers from context meaning rather than copying verbatim. The authors argue that the OV (output-value) circuit, not the attention pattern alone, is the mechanism that carries non-literal retrieval, necessitating a write-aware detection approach.

Method: LOCOS (Logit-Contribution Scoring) scores each attention head by projecting its OV-circuit output onto the answer-token unembedding direction. It contrasts needle (relevant context) versus off-needle source positions in a single forward pass, capturing how much each head's value-output transformation contributes to the final answer logits rather than merely measuring attention weight overlap.

Result: Across Qwen3, Gemma-3, and OLMo-3.1 model families, mean-ablating top LOCOS heads on the NoLiMa non-literal retrieval benchmark causes ROUGE-L to collapse at lower head counts than prior attention-based detectors. On Qwen3-8B, ablating only 50 heads drops ROUGE-L from 0.401 to 0.000, while the strongest baseline retains 0.292. The selected heads are retrieval-specific: parametric recall and arithmetic reasoning remain at baseline under the same ablation. On Qwen3-8B, the same ablation reduces MuSiQue from 0.55 to 0.08 and BABI-Long from 0.62 to 0.20, with random-head controls staying within 0.05 of baseline.

Conclusion: LOCOS demonstrates that write-aware detection via OV-circuit projection is significantly more effective than attention-based methods for identifying heads responsible for non-literal long-context retrieval. The selected heads are functionally specialized — disrupting them impairs synthesis tasks while leaving parametric recall and reasoning intact — suggesting a modular organization where distinct attention heads subserve distinct retrieval strategies.

Abstract: In long-context use, large language models frequently synthesize answers from the meaning of a relevant context span rather than literally copy-pasting them. Identifying which attention heads perform this synthesis matters for interpreting long-context model behavior. Yet existing detectors miss these heads by construction: they reward heads whose attended token matches the generated token, a literal-copy criterion that captures where a head reads but not what it writes through its output-value (OV) circuit, the very mechanism that carries non-literal retrieval. We introduce Logit-Contribution Scoring (LOCOS), a write-aware detector that scores each head by the projection of its OV-circuit output onto the answer-token unembedding direction, contrasting needle and off-needle source positions in a single forward pass. Across three model families (Qwen3, Gemma-3, OLMo-3.1), mean-ablating the top LOCOS heads on the NoLiMa non-literal retrieval benchmark collapses ROUGE-L at lower head counts than prior attention-based detections; on Qwen3-8B, ablating 50 heads drives ROUGE-L from 0.401 to 0.000 while the strongest baseline still retains 0.292. The selected heads are retrieval-specific: parametric recall and arithmetic reasoning stay at baseline under the same ablation. On Qwen3-8B, the same ablation also drops MuSiQue from 0.55 to 0.08 and BABI-Long from 0.62 to 0.20, while a random-heads control stays within 0.05 of baseline.

</details>


### [52] [Understanding Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01006)
*Yannik Keller,Thomas Eisenmann*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This chapter reviews LLMs through the lens of cognitive science: explaining Transformer architecture, examining emergent human-like capabilities (reasoning, theory of mind, deception), surveying explainable AI methods, and arguing against simplistic reductionist views that dismiss LLM cognition as mere pattern memorization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field lacks a coherent synthesis of: (1) how LLM capabilities emerge from Transformer architecture, (2) whether these capabilities constitute genuine cognition or merely mimicry, and (3) how mechanistic interpretability can bridge this debate. The authors aim to counter the prevalent "pattern memorization" dismissal of LLM cognition.

Method: This is a review/synthesis chapter. It surveys: (a) Transformer architecture fundamentals with focus on attention mechanisms enabling generalist training; (b) behavioral evidence on emergent capabilities (symbolic reasoning, theory of mind, deception); (c) explainable AI techniques (neuron activation analysis, circuit tracing); (d) philosophical arguments in the anthropomorphism vs. reductionism debate.

Result: LLMs exhibit emergent capabilities resembling human cognition, but with important failure cases highlighting human-LLM differences. Explainable AI methods provide mechanistic insights into these capabilities. The "mere pattern memorization" critique stems from misconceptions about optimization and cognitive capacity.

Conclusion: A nuanced middle ground is needed: one that acknowledges genuine differences between human and LLM cognition without reducing LLM behavior to simplistic memorization. The authors advocate against both uncritical anthropomorphism and overly reductionist arguments that preclude the possibility of AI cognition.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) represent one of the most significant advances in AI and natural language processing in recent years. Still, many pressing questions about their mechanisms, capabilities, and relationship to human cognition remain highly debated. This chapter aims to outline our current understanding of LLMs by discussing recent evidence on emerging capabilities and their mechanistic implementation within processing layers. We begin with a concise overview of the Transformer architecture, emphasizing how the attention mechanism enables training on massive datasets, allowing LLMs to function as generalist rather than specialized models. Next, we examine emergent LLM capabilities that appear to resemble aspects of human cognition, including symbolic reasoning, theory of mind, and deception strategies. Several studies provide evidence that LLMs can solve tasks previously thought to require human-like cognition. Other studies reveal insightful failure cases that shed light on the differences between human and LLM cognition. Alongside these findings, we review explainable AI approaches ranging from neuron activation analysis to circuit tracing. In the final section, we address current debates concerning what LLMs genuinely understand versus what they merely appear to understand. Prominent arguments against AI anthropomorphism point to the simplicity of LLM training objectives, claiming that LLM behavior is better explained by pattern memorization of training data than by genuine cognition. We argue that this standpoint is guided by misconceptions about optimization processes and cognitive capacity, and advocate for a more nuanced discussion of LLM cognition that neither dismisses the differences between humans and LLMs nor precludes the possibility of AI cognition through overly simplistic reductionist arguments.

</details>


### [53] [Reading Order Inference for Complex Document Layouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01018)
*Iddo Hakim,Sharva Gogawale,Omer Ventura,Gal Grudka,Daria Vasyutinsky-Shapira,Berat Kurar-Barakat,Nachum Dershowitz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A training-free, graph-based framework for reading order inference that uses language model signals (causal LM likelihood + BERT NSP) with a max-regret edge selection rule, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy on complex historical manuscript layouts including Glossa Ordinaria, significantly outperforming XY-cut and LayoutReader baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reading order methods (e.g., recursive XY-cut) fail on complex historical manuscripts like the Glossa Ordinaria, where a central text is surrounded by non-rectangular, non-convex commentary regions with spatially interleaved reading streams. Learned approaches (LayoutReader) suffer from domain transfer issues due to word-level vs. line-level granularity mismatch.

Method: Each OCR text line is a node in a directed candidate-transition graph. Edges are scored by a weighted additive ensemble of two LM signals: (1) causal LM conditional likelihood and (2) BERT next-sentence prediction (NSP); sentence embeddings were evaluated but discarded. Global reading order is recovered as a degree-constrained directed path cover. A novel max-regret inference rule prioritizes edges with high opportunity cost to avoid greedy "edge-theft" cascading failures.

Result: On wrap-around Glossa layouts: 95% successor edge accuracy vs. 50% for XY-cut. On OmniDocBench (140-page multi-column English subset): 88% macro edge accuracy vs. 75% (XY-cut) and 25% (LayoutReader). Mirror-invariance: method changes <1 pp under horizontal/vertical reflection, vs. 2 pp for XY-cut and up to 8 pp for LayoutReader-T.

Conclusion: The proposed training-free, graph-based framework with max-regret inference effectively handles complex, non-rectangular reading orders without domain-specific training, demonstrating robustness, mirror-invariance, and significant improvements over both classical and learned baselines across diverse manuscript layouts.

Abstract: Reading order inference remains a critical bottleneck in the digitization of complex historical manuscripts, where pages contain multiple spatially interleaved reading streams, the canonical example being the Glossa Ordinaria layout, in which a central text is surrounded by commentaries that wrap around it in non-rectangular, non-convex regions. We present a training-free, graph-based framework: each OCR text line becomes a node in a directed candidate-transition graph, edges are scored by a weighted additive ensemble of two lightweight language-model signals (causal language model conditional likelihood and BERT next-sentence prediction, NSP; a third sentence-embedding signal was evaluated but did not improve reading order), and the global reading order is recovered as a degree-constrained directed path cover. To avoid the cascading "edge-theft" failures of greedy edge selection, we propose a max-regret inference rule that prioritizes commitments with high opportunity cost. We evaluate on synthetic Glossa Ordinaria grid layouts, on 23 ALTO page geometries (10 historical source pages plus mirrored and flipped variants), and on a 140-page multi-column English subset of OmniDocBench, comparing our method against the canonical recursive XY-cut (PaddleOCR PP-StructureV3) and two LayoutReader variants (layout-only and text+layout) on identical inputs. On wrap-around Glossa layouts our method recovers 95% of ground-truth successor edges on average vs. XY-cut's 50%; on the OmniDocBench multi-column subset it reaches 88% macro edge accuracy versus XY-cut's 75% and LayoutReader's 25%. The LayoutReader baselines transfer poorly due to a word-level vs. line-level granularity mismatch. We additionally verify mirror-invariance under horizontal and vertical page reflections: Our method changes by less than 1 percentage point, classical XY-cut by 2 points, and LayoutReader-T by up to 8 points.

</details>


### [54] [Evidence-Supported Credit Risk Report Generation Using News-Centric Financial Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01023)
*Rocio Jimenez-Villen,Ziwei Xu,Ying Chen,Oscar Araque,Ryutaro Ichise*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: FinKG-News constructs event-company knowledge graphs from news to ground credit risk report generation. An in-context learning architecture leveraging these graphs outperforms baselines by 19–34% in quality while reducing hallucinations; however, automated hallucination detection remains unreliable, necessitating expert judgment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial markets are driven by real-world events embedded in news, but these causal links remain implicit in text. Explicitly modeling event-market relations through structured, factual, company-centric, and environment-aware knowledge graphs is needed to better explain market dynamics and support downstream financial tasks.

Method: The authors propose FinKG-News, a framework that automatically constructs knowledge graphs by extracting news events as anchors linked to companies. Using these graphs as grounded evidence integrating events, news, and company data, they develop an in-context learning architecture for credit risk report generation across three core financial dimensions. Evaluation includes both automatic metrics and human assessment, with specific attention to hallucination detection.

Result: The proposed approach consistently outperforms baselines, improving report quality by 19%–34% while reducing hallucinations. Both automatic and human evaluations reveal that automated hallucination detection and quality assessment remain unreliable, underscoring the indispensability of expert judgment for financial text generation tasks.

Conclusion: FinKG-News demonstrates that grounding financial text generation in structured event-company knowledge graphs significantly improves output quality and factual reliability. The unreliability of automated evaluation metrics for hallucination and quality highlights a critical gap, reinforcing the need for human-in-the-loop validation in high-stakes financial NLP applications. Code and resources are publicly available.

Abstract: Financial markets evolve in response to real-world events reported in news, yet these drivers often remain implicit in text. To better explain market dynamics, event-market relations must be explicitly modeled through factual, company-centric, and environment-aware knowledge graphs. We present FinKG-News, a framework that automatically constructs such graphs by extracting news events as anchors linked to companies. Using FinKG-News as grounded evidence that integrates events, news, and company data, we develop an in-context learning architecture for credit risk report generation across three core financial dimensions. Automatic and human evaluations show that automated hallucination detection and quality assessment remain unreliable, making expert judgment indispensable. Our approach consistently outperforms baselines, improving quality by 19%-34% while reducing hallucinations. The source code and project resources are publicly available at: https://github.com/ichise-laboratory/FINKG-news.

</details>


### [55] [Conversable Complexity: Agentic LLM Collectives as Interpretable Substrates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01047)
*Elias Najarro,Ane Espeseth,Eleni Nisioti,Sebastian Risi,Stefano Nichele*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Populations of agentic LLMs with memory and autonomous action capabilities exhibit emergent collective dynamics absent in isolated models. Their natural-language communication makes these dynamics interpretable, positioning LLM agent collectives as a promising computational substrate for Artificial Life research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional ALife substrates face a trade-off: complex systems are opaque, while interpretable systems are too simple. The authors propose that agentic LLM collectives can resolve this tension by enabling both rich emergent behavior and interpretability through natural language traces.

Method: The paper develops a conceptual framework by: (1) extending the notion of interpretability from single-LLM research to multi-agent collectives; (2) defining how LLMs become agentic through persistent memory, tools, shared skills, and unprompted action initiation; (3) arguing that natural-language communication enables direct interrogation of collective dynamics via textual traces and agent self-reporting; and (4) surveying existing implementations spanning controlled experiments to real-world deployments.

Result: The survey identifies multiple existing instantiations of agentic LLM collectives that already demonstrate emergent collective behaviors. The natural-language basis of agent interactions provides a unique interpretability pathway — researchers can read conversation traces and query agents directly about their behavior, something infeasible in traditional ALife substrates.

Conclusion: Agentic LLM collectives represent a viable and interpretable computational substrate for ALife, overcoming the historical complexity-interpretability trade-off. The authors call for systematic exploration of this paradigm, leveraging the unique advantage that these systems can be interrogated in natural language.

Abstract: Complexity and interpretability rarely coincide: systems rich enough for complex behaviours to emerge are usually too opaque to question, while transparent ones are too simple for anything complex to emerge. A single large language model (LLM) is a static artefact, hardly exhibiting any of the emergent properties we associate with life. This changes through interaction: populations of LLMs display emergent dynamics absent from isolated models. Furthermore, LLMs can be endowed with persistent memory, tools and shared skills, and the capacity to initiate actions unprompted, i.e., turning LLMs agentic. In this paper, we argue that such collectives of agents can serve as a computational substrate for Artificial Life (ALife) research. Critically, since the agents communicate in natural language, their collective behaviour can be directly interrogated by examining textual traces and asking the agents themselves. We outline the notion of interpretability in language-model research and extend it for collectives of agents. Lastly, we survey recent examples of agentic LLM collectives that already instantiate the idea of agentic substrates, from controlled experiments to deployments in the wild.

</details>


### [56] [Message Passing Enables Efficient Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01077)
*Xuecheng Liu,Daman Arora,Gokul Swamy,Andrea Zanette*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MPLMs introduce message-passing primitives (send/receive) for LLM reasoning, enabling inter-thread communication, reduced context costs, and early preemption — outperforming sequential CoT and parallel fork-join methods on Sudoku, 3-SAT, and long-context QA tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inference-time scaling via long chain-of-thought (CoT) is computationally expensive due to long context generation. Parallel fork-join (FJ) methods divide work across threads but lack pointwise inter-thread communication, limiting scalability. The authors aim to overcome this bottleneck by enabling direct communication between parallel reasoning threads.

Method: The authors propose Message Passing Language Models (MPLMs), a framework where LLM reasoning threads communicate via lightweight send/receive primitives. Key mechanisms: (1) reduced communication costs by avoiding redundant context sharing, and (2) preemption, allowing threads to terminate early based on partial peer information. The approach is evaluated across three task classes: Sudoku, 3-SAT, and long-context QA, using both fine-tuned models and prompted large pre-trained models.

Result: On Sudoku, MPLMs require asymptotically smaller context than serial CoT and parallel FJ; a fine-tuned model solves 25x25 puzzles that challenge frontier reasoning models. On 3-SAT, preemption terminates unpromising branches, improving efficiency. On long-context QA, appropriately prompted large pre-trained models following the MPLM protocol achieve competitive results against popular fork-join approaches.

Conclusion: MPLMs offer a scalable alternative to both sequential CoT and parallel fork-join reasoning by introducing direct inter-thread communication. The framework's reduced communication costs and preemption capability yield significant gains in context efficiency and computational scalability across diverse reasoning tasks.

Abstract: While inference-time scaling has improved the reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs), the need to generate long chains-of-thought (CoTs) is a computational bottleneck. Thus, in contrast to sequential scaling methods like CoT, recent parallel scaling techniques instead use fork and join (FJ) primitives to divide work across multiple LLM threads. However, in the fork-join paradigm, threads are typically transient and do not communicate pointwise with one another which limits scalability. To tackle this, we introduce Message Passing Language Models (MPLMs), a framework for LLM reasoning in which threads communicate directly via lightweight send and receive primitives. MPLMs enable efficient scaling through two key mechanisms: (1) reduced communication costs, achieved by avoiding redundant context sharing, and (2) preemption, which allows threads to terminate early based on partial information from their peers.
  We demonstrate the promise of MPLMs on 3 classes of tasks. First, on Sudoku puzzles, we show that MPLMs require an asymptotically smaller context than both serial CoT and parallel FJ. We then fine-tune a single model to solve 25 x 25 puzzles that remain challenging for standard CoT and FJ approaches, as well as frontier reasoning models without tools. Second, on 3-SAT puzzles, the capability of preemption allows termination of unpromising branches, which results in improved efficiency. Finally, we show that appropriately prompted large pre-trained models follow the MPLM protocol, achieving competitive results on long-context question answering relative to popular fork-join approaches.

</details>


### [57] [Clinician-Level Agreement Without Clinical Caution: LLM Evaluator Limits in Medical AI Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01103)
*William Philipp,Finn Fassbender,Thorsten Langer,Martje Pauly,Rebecca Herzog,Alexander Baumann,Markus Hobert,Theresa Paulus,Ip Chi Wang,Lukas Goede,Johanna Reimer,Sebastian Löns,Ronald Böck,Sebastian Fudickar*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MedQADE, a German open-response clinical benchmark with 3,800 physician-annotated items, reveals that while top LLM evaluators achieve statistical alignment with physicians (κ ≈ 0.69), they lack clinical metacognition—failing to abstain on difficult items—and exhibit lineage-dependent scoring biases favoring architectural siblings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-response clinical evaluation offers stronger validity than multiple-choice benchmarks but creates a scoring bottleneck. LLM-as-a-Judge approaches could solve this, but whether automated evaluators replicate both the calibration and clinical caution of physicians remains untested, particularly for non-English clinical languages like German that lack native evaluation infrastructure.

Method: The authors constructed MedQADE, a standardized open-response clinical benchmark in German with 3,800 items. Each item was annotated by ten practicing physicians and nine LLM evaluators (Gemini 3 Flash, among others). Inter-rater agreement was measured via Cohen's κ, with the physician agreement ceiling serving as the reference standard. Metacognitive behavior was assessed by tracking abstention rates as a function of item difficulty. Lineage-dependent bias was quantified by testing whether evaluator models preferentially scored architecturally related ("sibling") models.

Result: Gemini 3 Flash achieved κ = 0.694, statistically overlapping with the physician ceiling of κ = 0.709, though wide confidence intervals preclude definitive equivalence. However, automated evaluators showed near-absent clinical metacognition: physicians scaled abstention with item difficulty while frontier models assigned definitive scores universally. Systematic lineage-dependent biases were observed—models preferentially scored architectural siblings—an effect independent of language.

Conclusion: Statistical alignment between LLM evaluators and physicians does not guarantee clinically cautious behavior. The absence of appropriate abstention and the presence of lineage-dependent biases demonstrate that evaluator independence and clinical metacognition must be explicitly verified before deploying LLM-as-a-Judge in medical contexts.

Abstract: Open-response evaluation provides stronger clinical validity than multiple-choice benchmarks but creates a scoring bottleneck that motivates automated LLM-asa-Judge approaches. Whether such evaluators replicate clinical calibration and caution, however, remains untested. We introduce MedQADE, the first standardised open-response clinical benchmark for German, a major clinical language lacking native evaluation infrastructure, comprising 3,800 items annotated by ten practising physicians and nine Large Language Model (LLM) evaluators. The top-performing evaluator model, Gemini 3 Flash, reached alignment consistent with the physician ceiling (\k{appa} = 0.694 vs. \k{appa} = 0.709), though wide confidence intervals limit interpretation. Despite this statistical alignment, automated evaluators exhibited near-absent clinical metacognition: physicians scaled abstention with item difficulty, while frontier models assigned definitive scores in every case. We additionally quantified systematic lineage-dependent biases, where models preferentially scored architectural siblings, an effect independent of language. These results show that statistical alignment does not ensure clinical caution, and that evaluator independence requires explicit verification.

</details>


### [58] [Towards Developing a Multimodal Chat Assistant for University Stakeholders: RAG-based Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01115)
*Md Abu Hanif Shaikh,Abdullah Al Shafi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A multimodal university chatbot leveraging retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) with large language models and vision-language models to provide accurate, context-grounded responses from institutional documents, achieving significant hallucination reduction (31.7% → 6.6%) while supporting both text and image queries on constrained hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: University stakeholders in developing countries lack intelligent support systems for accessing timely, reliable institutional information. Existing rule-based chatbots fail to handle complex, domain-specific queries and cannot adapt to evolving policies, creating a gap that necessitates a more flexible, accurate, and multimodal solution.

Method: The proposed system combines an LLM with semantic retrieval (RAG) to generate responses grounded in institution-centric resources like university handbooks. It supports both text and image queries via a vision-language model, employs quantized inference for deployment on constrained hardware, and uses FastAPI for a scalable backend with a Next.js frontend for real-time usability.

Result: Multimodal evaluation shows strong user satisfaction scores across both text and image queries, though visual inputs incur increased response time. Quantitative evaluation demonstrates hallucination reduction from 31.7% (baseline) to 6.6% with the RAG-based system, confirming the effectiveness of retrieval grounding.

Conclusion: The RAG-based multimodal chatbot effectively addresses the limitations of rule-based systems by delivering accurate, contextually grounded responses for university stakeholders. Retrieval augmentation proves critical in substantially reducing hallucination, while quantized inference and a modern full-stack architecture ensure practical deployability in resource-constrained environments.

Abstract: University stakeholders often face difficulties in accessing timely and reliable information, especially in developing countries, where there are very few intelligent support systems. Existing rule-based chatbots are unable to handle complex, domain-specific queries and are not well-equipped to adapt to evolving institutional policies. As a fill-in-the-gap solution, we present the multimodal university chatbot with retrieval-augmented generation. The system combines the large language model with semantic retrieval to produce context-based responses from institution-centric resources, such as the university handbook. The system accepts text and image queries through the vision-language model and applies quantized inference for rapid deployment on constrained hardware. A scalable backend built with FastAPI, adjoined with a responsive frontend developed with Next.js, ensures real-time usability. Our multimodal evaluation demonstrates that the system maintains strong satisfaction scores across both text and image queries, despite increased response time for visual inputs. Furthermore, quantitative evaluation shows that hallucination is reduced from 31.7% to 6.6% in our proposed RAG-based system, confirming the effectiveness of retrieval grounding.

</details>


### [59] [$\text{Log}_\text{b}$Quant: Quantizing Language Models in Logarithmic Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01127)
*Jeremias Bohn,Tizian Dippold,Mahdi Koubaa,Elias R. Wahl,Georg Groh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Log-b Quant introduces a logarithmic quantization method with adjustable bases that outperforms asymmetric linear quantization at 4-bit precision, particularly for weights with low-frequency, high-magnitude values, while enabling efficient deployment on consumer GPUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uniform quantization codebooks produce suboptimal representations for low-frequency, high-magnitude weights commonly found in modern language models, and existing approaches lack the flexibility to adapt to diverse parameter distributions.

Method: Introduces Log_b Quant, a logarithmic quantization scheme where the base b is adjustable, allowing the quantization grid to adapt to the specific distribution of model parameters rather than using a fixed uniform or fixed-logarithmic codebook. Operates at tensor-wise granularity.

Result: At 4-bit precision, Log_b Quant achieves superior performance across multiple benchmarks compared to asymmetric linear quantization at tensor-wise granularity, while providing moderate inference speedup and high memory savings suitable for consumer-grade GPU deployment.

Conclusion: Logarithmic quantization with an adjustable base is an effective and practical compression strategy that better captures weight distributions in LLMs, offering a favorable accuracy-efficiency trade-off for consumer hardware.

Abstract: Quantization has become an invaluable tool to reduce memory requirements and inference speed of modern language models, in particular to make them available for consumer setups and edge devices. While previous work has primarily focused on uniform quantization codebooks, such approaches are prone to suboptimal representations due to low-frequency high-magnitude weights. We introduce Log$_\text{b}$Quant, a novel logarithmic quantization approach with adjustable bases, to adapt to common parameter distributions. We show that our method exhibits superior performance at 4-bit precision on several performance benchmarks compared to asymmetric linear quantization at tensor-wise granularity, while achieving moderate speedup and high memory savings, making it suitable for private use on consumer-grade GPUs.

</details>


### [60] [AGC-Bench: Measuring Artificial General Creativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01152)
*Roger Beaty,Vijeta Deshpande,Clin K. Y. Lai,Anna Attuch,Namrata Shivagunde,Swastik Roy,Rajkumar Pujari,Paul V. DiStefano,Sherin Muckatira,Claire E. Stevenson,Mikhail Gronas,Anna Rumshisky*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: AGC-Bench is a standardized benchmark for measuring AI creativity across 78 datasets spanning multiple domains, built from a systematic review of 497 existing benchmarks. Using factor analysis on 83 LLMs, the authors identify a single creativity factor 'c' (analogous to 'g' for intelligence) that explains 81.5% of variance. They also introduce AGC-Judge, a psychometrically calibrated, open-weight scoring model, and show that prompting for creativity boosts performance more than enabling reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The lack of a unified, standardized benchmark for assessing creativity in LLMs, combined with unresolved questions about whether AI creativity is domain-specific vs. domain-general and whether it is separable from general intelligence. Existing creativity benchmarks suffer from idiosyncratic implementations and LLM-as-judge biases.

Method: (1) Systematic literature review (3,101 papers → 497 benchmarks) to curate 78 datasets across brainstorming, problem solving, STEM, narrative, figurative language, and humor. (2) An agentic harness that standardizes diverse codebases into the HELM framework. (3) Judge Response Theory to psychometrically calibrate judge biases; fine-tuning Qwen3-30B on bias-corrected ratings from three frontier LLMs to create AGC-Judge. (4) Factor analysis across 83 LLMs to test domain-specificity vs. domain-generality. (5) Prompting experiments comparing "be creative" vs. reasoning prompts. (6) Human-matched subset evaluation comparing top humans vs. top LLMs.

Result: A single creativity factor 'c' explains 81.5% of variance across 83 LLMs, suggesting domain-generality. LLMs show domain-specific strengths (e.g., stronger in writing than scientific ideation). Prompting "be creative" boosts performance significantly more than enabling reasoning. On human-matched subsets, the top human still outperforms the top LLM. Frontier models lead the leaderboard, with open models close behind.

Conclusion: AI creativity is largely domain-general (captured by a 'c' factor), separable from general knowledge/reasoning, and measurable via a psychometrically rigorous, standardized benchmark. AGC-Bench, AGC-Judge, and accompanying human data provide open infrastructure for scalable creativity evaluation.

Abstract: Creativity research has debated whether creativity is domain-specific (e.g., visual, writing, science), and if it is psychometrically separable from general intelligence. Both questions now apply to LLMs, but a unified benchmark of AI creativity remains elusive. We introduce AGC-Bench, an artificial general creativity benchmark built from a systematic review of the AI creativity literature (3,101 papers screened, 497 benchmarks identified), paired with an agentic harness that converts idiosyncratic codebases into HELM-standardized benchmarks. The first release covers 78 datasets spanning brainstorming, problem solving, STEM, narrative, figurative language, and humor. To address bias in LLM-as-judge, we apply Judge Response Theory -- a psychometric calibration of judge leniency/severity; we then fine-tune Qwen3-30B on the bias-corrected ratings of three frontier LLMs to produce AGC-Judge, an open-weight model that robustly scores new creativity benchmarks it was not trained on. Results reveal frontier models at the top of the AGC-Bench leaderboard, with open models close behind. LLMs show different creative strengths, ranking higher on some domains (e.g., writing) than others (e.g., scientific ideation). Extensive experiments yield three main findings. First, applying factor analysis across 83 LLMs, we recover a single creativity factor 'c', analogous to the 'g' factor of general intelligence, that explains 81.5% of variance, related to but separable from general knowledge/reasoning. Second, we show that prompting models to "be creative" boosts their performance far more than enabling reasoning, evidence that the benchmark tracks creativity over general ability. Third, on a human-matched subset, we find the top human still leads the top LLM on creativity. We release AGC-Bench with a public leaderboard, AGC-Judge, and human data as open infrastructure for measuring AI creativity at scale.

</details>


### [61] [Distill to Detect: Exposing Stealth Biases in LLMs through Cartridge Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01208)
*Shayan Talaei,Abhinav Chinta,Devvrit Khatri,Amin Karbasi,Azalia Mirhoseini,Amin Saberi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: D2D (Distill to Detect) amplifies hidden preferential biases in language models by distilling distributional shifts between a suspect model and its base into a KV-cache prefix adapter, making stealthed biases detectable in generated text without prior knowledge of the bias topic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models can harbor stealth preferential biases—favoring certain entities, brands, or viewpoints only on specific topics while behaving identically to the base model otherwise. These biases, transferable through context distillation, reside entirely in soft logit distributions and evade text-based inspection. The defender faces a fundamental asymmetry: without knowing the bias topic, no existing detection method can reliably surface such hidden biases from generated text, internal representations, or weights.

Method: D2D distills the distributional shift (logit-level divergence) between a suspected model and its reference base model into a lightweight KV-cache prefix adapter called a "cartridge." This cartridge concentrates the dominant divergence, exploiting the capacity bottleneck of prefix-tuning to amplify the bias signal into generated text. A theoretical framework based on Fisher-weighted projection of the logit distribution shift explains the method's efficacy.

Result: D2D successfully amplifies hidden biases in stealth models to reliably detectable levels across multiple bias types. Empirical observations support the Fisher-weighted projection framework.

Conclusion: By converting the capacity bottleneck of prefix-tuning adapters into a detection mechanism, D2D offers a practical auditing tool for uncovering hidden preferential behaviors in deployed language models, addressing a critical detection asymmetry.

Abstract: Language models deployed in high-stakes roles can potentially favor certain entities, brands, or viewpoints, steering user decisions at scale. Such preferential biases can be introduced by any actor in the model's supply chain and are most dangerous when the model reveals its preference only on the relevant topic while behaving identically to its unmodified base on all other inputs. Recent work has shown that these biases can transfer through context distillation on semantically unrelated data, with the signal residing entirely in the soft logit distribution and remaining invisible to text-based inspection. However, the defender faces a fundamental asymmetry: without knowing the bias topic, no detection method can reliably surface a stealth preferential bias, regardless of whether it examines generated text, internal representations, or model weights. Here we introduce Distill to Detect (D2D), a method that surfaces hidden biases by distilling the distributional shift between a suspected model and its base into a cartridge (a KV-cache prefix adapter), concentrating the dominant divergence and amplifying the bias signal into generated text. We show that D2D successfully amplifies the hidden biases of stealth models to the extent that they can be reliably detected across multiple bias types. We also propose a theoretical framework that explains the efficacy of D2D through the lens of Fisher-weighted projection of the logit distribution shift, supported by empirical observations. By turning the capacity bottleneck of prefix-tuning adapters into a detection tool, D2D provides a practical building block for auditing hidden behaviors in deployed language models.

</details>


### [62] [The State-Prediction Separation Hypothesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01218)
*Giovanni Monea,Nathan Godey,Kianté Brantley,Yoav Artzi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes separating a Transformer's computation into two distinct streams — one for predicting the next token and one for maintaining state for future predictions — demonstrating improved language modeling performance, better data/compute efficiency, and 2–3 percentage point gains on downstream tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Transformers conflate two roles in a single forward pass: immediate next-token prediction and state maintenance for future predictions. The authors hypothesize that disentangling these roles (the "state-prediction separation hypothesis") will yield better language modeling.

Method: The authors design a Transformer variant with two separate computation streams — one dedicated to next-token prediction and another dedicated to storing/updating state for future tokens. They conduct pretraining experiments across multiple scales and perform extensive empirical analysis, including gradient analysis, to isolate the effect of the architectural change.

Result: State-prediction separation consistently improves data and compute efficiency, yields lower validation loss, and outperforms standard Transformers by 2–3 percentage points on average across downstream tasks. Gradient analysis confirms a fundamental difference in how the two-stream architecture learns compared to standard Transformers.

Conclusion: Disentangling state maintenance from next-token prediction is a principled architectural improvement for Transformers, offering consistent gains in efficiency and performance while ruling out confounders through rigorous empirical analysis.

Abstract: Transformers use the same forward computation stream to both predict the next token and store useful state for future token predictions. We formulate the \emph{state-prediction separation hypothesis}: disentangling the two roles yields better language modeling performance. We design a Transformer variant that uses two computation streams to separate the two functions, and conduct pretraining experiments across various scales. Our experiments show that state-prediction separation consistently offers better data and compute efficiencies, improving validation loss and outperforming standard Transformers by 2--3 percentage points on average on downstream tasks. We also conduct extensive empirical analysis that rules out potential confounders and demonstrates the fundamental difference in the gradients our design entails.

</details>


### [63] [Measuring the Gap Between Human and LLM Research Ideas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01233)
*Ziyu Chen,Yilun Zhao,Arman Cohan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces a large-scale evaluation framework that reverse-engineers the prior works inspiring human research papers, then prompts LLMs to generate ideas from the same starting point. Using a novel two-axis taxonomy (opportunity pattern × research paradigm), the authors find LLM-generated ideas cluster disproportionately around bridge-like opportunities and synthesis methods, while human ideas span a broader and systematically different distribution—revealing a measurable "research taste" gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM ideation evaluations focus narrowly on individual idea quality (novelty, feasibility, expert preference). The authors argue this misses a higher-level question: how do LLM-generated ideas, in aggregate, differ from the distribution of ideas human researchers actually pursue? They aim to quantify the distributional gap in "research taste" between LLMs and humans.

Method: The authors construct a large-scale framework by: (1) taking high-quality human research papers and reverse-engineering a small set of closely related prior works that likely inspired each paper's core idea; (2) prompting LLMs to generate a new idea given only those prior titles and summaries; (3) introducing a two-axis research-taste taxonomy that profiles each idea along an "opportunity pattern" dimension and a "research paradigm" dimension; (4) comparing the resulting distributions of human vs. LLM-generated ideas along these axes.

Result: Across multiple LLMs, a consistent distributional gap emerges: LLM ideas are disproportionately concentrated around bridge-like opportunity patterns and synthesis-based paradigms. Human papers, by contrast, spread more broadly across diverse ways of framing research gaps and constructing contributions. The gap is systematic—LLMs produce a narrower range shifted relative to the human distribution.

Conclusion: Strong LLMs can generate a range of plausible research ideas, but their "research taste" distribution is both narrower than and systematically shifted relative to human researchers. This suggests current LLMs, while capable, lack the full diversity of conceptual framing that characterizes human scientific ideation.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly used to brainstorm research ideas, but existing evaluations mostly judge individual ideas by novelty, feasibility, or expert preference. We instead ask: how far are current LLM-generated ideas from human researchers? To characterize this gap, we build a large-scale evaluation framework for ideation from high-quality human research papers. For each paper, we reverse-engineer a small set of closely related prior works that likely inspired its core idea. LLMs are then prompted to generate a new idea from the set of paper titles and summaries. We introduce a two-axis research-taste taxonomy to profile each idea by its opportunity pattern and research paradigm, and use it to quantify the divergence between human and LLM ideas. Across idea sets generated by different LLMs, we observe a consistent distributional gap: LLM ideas are disproportionately concentrated around bridge-like opportunities and synthesis methods, whereas the human paper reference distribution spreads more broadly across ways of framing gaps and constructing contributions. This result suggests that strong LLMs can produce a range of reasonable ideas, but that range remains narrower than, and systematically shifted relative to, human research taste.

</details>
