# cs.HC [[Back]](#toc)

### [64] [Comparing the Emotional Impact of Thematic Versus Episodic Framing in Visualization Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00103)
*Poorna Talkad Sukumar,Maurizio Porfiri,Oded Nov*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Textual framing in data visualizations affects emotional responses: episodic framing (specific events) elicits more negative emotions than thematic framing (broader trends), and this heightened negative emotion indirectly increases support for gun control policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While textual framing's effects on comprehension, recall, and perceived bias are well-documented, its influence on viewers' emotional responses remains underexplored. The study aims to fill this gap by examining how episodic vs. thematic framing shapes emotional and attitudinal reactions to data visualizations.

Method: A preregistered, between-subjects online experiment (N=800) where participants viewed identical U.S. mass shooting data visualizations with three framing conditions: (1) thematic title only, (2) thematic title with annotation, and (3) episodic title with annotation. Emotional valence and policy attitudes were measured, followed by mediation analysis.

Result: Episodic framing produced significantly more negative emotional valence than both thematic conditions. Adding an annotation to a thematic title did not alter emotional impact. Framing had no significant direct effect on policy attitudes, but mediation analysis revealed a significant indirect pathway: episodic framing → increased negative emotion → greater gun control support.

Conclusion: Emotion is a critical, underexamined mechanism through which textual framing influences responses to data visualizations, serving as a mediator between framing and policy attitudes.

Abstract: Although textual framing in data visualizations is known to influence comprehension, recall, and perceptions of bias, its effects on viewers' emotional responses remain underexplored. Drawing on two widely studied framing strategies in political communication, we examine how episodic framing (foregrounding a specific event) versus thematic framing (foregrounding broader trends) affects emotional and attitudinal responses to visualizations. We conducted a preregistered, between-subjects online experiment (N = 800) in which participants viewed identical visualizations of U.S. mass shooting data that varied only in textual framing: a thematic title, a thematic title with annotation, or an episodic title paired with the same annotation. Results show that episodic framing elicited significantly more negative emotional valence than both thematic conditions. In contrast, adding an annotation to a thematic title did not alter emotional impact. While framing did not significantly affect policy attitudes, mediation analysis revealed a significant indirect effect: increased negative emotion under episodic framing predicted greater support for gun control. These findings position emotion as a critical, yet underexamined, dimension of how textual framing shapes responses to data visualizations.

</details>


### [65] [May (A)I Beautify Your Visualization? Expert Judgments of Acceptable Aesthetic Alterations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00239)
*Kalina Borkiewicz,Jixian Li,Joshua A. Levine,Katherine E. Isaacs*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A survey of 95 visualization experts reveals that the acceptability of alterations to 3D scientific visualizations depends on the semantic meaning of the transformation, not whether it operates at the presentation or data level. AI-authored changes are consistently rated less acceptable than identical human-made ones, suggesting a need for guardrails in visualization tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI-based and other tools make visual design increasingly accessible, there is a growing need to understand the trade-offs between improving aesthetics and preserving data integrity in 3D scientific visualizations. The authors investigate what transformations experts consider acceptable and how authorship (human vs. AI) influences these judgments.

Method: An expert survey (N=95) was conducted with visualization researchers, practitioners, and domain scientists. Participants evaluated fifteen alterations spanning presentation-level adjustments (lighting, camera position) and data-level modifications (error removal, gap filling), applied by both human and AI systems, and rated their perceived acceptability.

Result: Perceived acceptability is driven primarily by the transformation's semantic meaning rather than whether it operates at the presentation or data level. Certain modifications are consistently judged more permissible than others. Crucially, AI-generated transformations are consistently rated as less acceptable than identical human-produced changes, even though the relative ordering of alteration types remains largely stable across authorship conditions.

Conclusion: The findings reveal a meaningful distinction between more permissible and more sensitive alterations, suggesting that both designers and AI-assisted visualization tools should incorporate constraints and guardrails that reflect these differing acceptability thresholds.

Abstract: In 3D visualizations of natural phenomena, improving aesthetics can provide measurable benefits, but often involves transformations that affect how the data is perceived. As a growing range of tools - including AI-based methods - make visual design and modification more accessible, it is increasingly important to understand trade offs and concerns when making these changes. We conducted an expert survey (N=95) with visualization researchers, practitioners, and domain scientists, investigating reactions to fifteen alterations spanning presentation-level adjustments (e.g., lighting, camera position) and data-level modifications (e.g., removing errors, filling gaps), applied by both humans and AI systems. Results show differences in perceived acceptability are driven by the transformation's meaning, regardless of whether it operates at the presentation or data level. Additionally, certain modifications were consistently judged as more permissible than others regardless of human or AI authorship. While this relative ordering remains largely stable, AI-generated transformations are consistently rated as less acceptable than identical human-produced changes. These results reveal a distinction between more permissible and more sensitive alterations, and suggest the need for both designers and AI-assisted visualization tools to incorporate constraints and guardrails that reflect these differences.

</details>


### [66] [Child Safety in Generative AI: An Expert-Guided and Incident-Grounded Evaluation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00395)
*Haein Kong*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Proposes a child-safety-focused evaluation framework for generative AI that combines expert risk guidelines with real-world AI incident data, applies it to the education domain, and finds that Llama Guard models perform poorly at detecting education-related unsafe prompts from children and adolescents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI safety evaluation frameworks target general user populations and overlook risks specific to children and adolescents. As generative AI adoption among younger users grows, there is a critical need for evaluation frameworks that account for child-specific harms.

Method: The framework identifies hazard categories by integrating expert-guided risk factors with real-world AI incident data. These categories are used to construct a synthetic test set for model evaluation. The framework is applied specifically to the education domain, where three Llama Guard models are evaluated on their ability to detect unsafe user prompts.

Result: Current Llama Guard models struggle to identify education-related unsafe user prompts, demonstrating a significant gap in child-specific safety detection capabilities.

Conclusion: Future work should extend the evaluation framework to additional risk categories and incorporate domain experts more deeply throughout the evaluation pipeline to improve child safety in generative AI systems.

Abstract: As generative AI is increasingly used by children and adolescents, there is a growing need for risk evaluation frameworks that account for child-specific harms. However, most existing safety evaluation frameworks focus on general user populations, often overlooking risks unique to younger users. To address this gap, we propose an evaluation framework that integrates expert-guided risk factors with real-world AI incident data for child safety. The framework identifies hazard categories from expert guidelines and AI incident databases and uses this information to construct a synthetic test set for model evaluation. Particularly, we apply the framework to the education domain and evaluate three Llama Guard models on their ability to detect unsafe user prompts. Our results show that current Llama Guard models struggle to identify education-related unsafe user prompts. We conclude by discussing how future work can extend the evaluation to additional risk categories and incorporate domain experts throughout the evaluation pipeline.

</details>


### [67] [A Penny for Your Prompts: Experiments Detecting and Mitigating LLM Usage by Survey Respondents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00403)
*Zane Xu,Nathan Malkin*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This study quantifies the prevalence of LLM-assisted survey responding across crowdsourcing platforms (Prolific vs. Mechanical Turk), tests mitigation strategies (anti-AI instructions, disabling copy-paste), and recommends detection methods including keystroke analysis and AI-targeted questions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used by crowdsourcing participants to complete surveys, threatening the validity of collected research data. The authors sought to quantify how widespread this behavior is and evaluate effective countermeasures.

Method: The authors conducted a series of surveys (N=250 total) varying four conditions: (1) crowdsourcing platform (Prolific vs. Mechanical Turk), (2) survey length, (3) explicit requests not to use AI, and (4) disabling copy-paste functionality. They analyzed keystroke data, response patterns, and conducted their own browser-use agent detection experiments to identify LLM-assisted responses.

Result: LLM-assisted response frequency varied dramatically by platform — under 10% on Prolific versus over 80% on Mechanical Turk. Mitigation measures (anti-AI instructions, disabling copy-paste) reduced LLM usage but did not necessarily improve overall data quality. No participants were found using browser-use agents at the time of the survey. Distinct keystroke signatures and response characteristics reliably distinguished LLM-assisted from genuine human responses.

Conclusion: Researchers should actively screen for LLM-generated survey responses by recording and analyzing keystroke data and by designing instructions and questions specifically crafted to detect AI assistance. Platform choice is a critical factor in data integrity.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used by participants on crowdsourcing platforms when responding to surveys, potentially undermining the validity of collected data. Our study aims to quantify the prevalence of this behavior and investigate methods to detect and prevent it. In a series of surveys (N = 250), we examined conditions such as platform choice, survey length, requests not to use AI, and disabling copy-paste functionality. We were able to identify distinct characteristics of LLM-assisted responses and found that their frequency varied widely, from under 10% on Prolific to over 80% on Mechanical Turk. Mitigation measures reduced LLM usage but did not necessarily improve data quality. No participants employed browser-use agents at the time of our survey, but we report on our own detection experiments. We recommend that researchers actively screen survey responses for LLM usage by recording and analyzing keystroke data and crafting instructions and questions aimed at AI.

</details>


### [68] [A Simple Solution to Improving Human Supervision of Algorithms: Evidence from Smart Vending](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00420)
*Minda Zhao,Brian Rongqing Han,Xin Chen,Tao Zhu*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A constrained override policy—limiting workers to two downward overrides per machine—reduces excess inventory by 1.28% without harming sales, outperforming both no-override and free-override regimes by enabling selective filtering of high-value human interventions in AI-driven inventory replenishment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Organizations face a tension in human-AI collaboration: granting full override rights to human supervisors can degrade AI performance due to bias and noise, while prohibiting overrides entirely risks discarding valuable private human information. The paper seeks an optimal middle-ground design for structuring override rights in autonomous AI systems.

Method: The authors propose a constrained override policy that caps overrides per decision episode and test it via a randomized field experiment with 553 workers at a large Chinese smart vending machine retailer (59,000+ machines, 4,000+ SKUs). Workers were randomly assigned to three conditions: no overrides, free (unlimited) overrides, or a two-per-machine limit on downward overrides. Causal effects are estimated using local average treatment effects (LATE), with further analysis by worker experience, SKU incentive level, and SKU growth stage. A simulated personalized policy is also explored.

Result: Free overrides reduce inventory by 1.95% but also cut sales by 1.19%. Constrained overrides reduce inventory by 1.28% without any sales loss, as workers selectively target better SKUs. Gains are strongest among experienced workers, high-incentive SKUs, and growth-stage SKUs. A simulated personalized override policy further increases sales probability by 9.1%.

Conclusion: Limiting override rights through a constrained policy enables selective filtering, preserving the value of private human information while mitigating human bias and noise. The approach is scalable, low-cost, and requires no algorithmic redesign, additional training, or information customization—offering practical benefits for retail, logistics, and resource planning operations.

Abstract: Organizations increasingly deploy autonomous artificial intelligence (AI) systems for operational decisions, such as inventory replenishment. Yet fully granting override rights can degrade performance due to human bias and noise, while prohibiting them may overlook valuable private information. This raises a key question: How should override rights be structured to improve human supervision of autonomous AI? Methodology/results: We propose a constrained override policy that limits overrides per decision episode to enable selective filtering that prioritizes high-value overrides. We tested it through a randomized field experiment with 553 workers at a major Chinese smart vending machine retailer that manages more than 59,000 machines and 4,000 SKUs. Workers were assigned to no overrides, free overrides, or a two-per-machine limit on downward overrides. Free overrides reduce inventory by 1.95% but also cut sales by 1.19%. Constrained overrides reduce inventory by 1.28% without harming sales, as workers select better SKUs to override, confirmed via local average treatment effects. Gains are largest for experienced workers, high-incentive SKUs, and growth-stage SKUs. A simulated personalized policy further increases sales probability by 9.1%. Managerial implications: Academics gain novel insights from the causal effects of discretion design in human-supervised AI, emphasizing selective filtering to enhance decision quality. Managers can benefit from a scalable, low-cost policy for operations such as retail, logistics, and resource planning, reducing excess inventory without sales loss while harnessing private human information, with no need for algorithmic redesign, information customization, or additional training.

</details>


### [69] [Gaze-Informed Proactive AI Assistance for Children's Picture Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00445)
*Zekun Wu,Man Su,Huiyong Li,Tomohiro Nagashima,Anna Maria Feit*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Ollie is a gaze-informed proactive AI assistant that uses children's eye gaze to determine when and what narrative descriptions to provide during picture exploration, outperforming random assistance in maintaining children's attention and guiding visual focus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on proactive LLM assistance has focused on adults and explicit task-oriented requests, neglecting children who often express interests and needs through nonverbal behaviors like gaze. There is a gap in understanding how proactive AI can support children by leveraging implicit nonverbal cues.

Method: The authors developed Ollie, a system that uses children's gaze to estimate attention, identify visual focus, and select relevant picture regions for an LLM to generate narrative descriptions. A within-subject experiment compared gaze-informed assistance against random assistance, with evaluations from children, parents, and a kindergarten teacher.

Result: Gaze-informed assistance sustained children's attention on their current focus longer and guided them more effectively to related picture regions. Children, parents, and the teacher rated Ollie positively, judging it as better matching children's interests compared to random assistance.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates the feasibility of using gaze as an implicit input modality for proactive child-centered AI assistance and offers design implications for future child-centered AI systems.

Abstract: Proactive assistance with large language models (LLMs) has received growing attention in the human computer interaction (HCI) community. However, most past work on proactive LLMs' assistance has focused on adult users and task-oriented settings, leaving open how such systems could support children, whose interests and needs are often expressed through gaze and other nonverbal behaviors rather than explicit requests. In this study, we focus on two key challenges of proactive assistance in children's picture exploration: when to provide assistance and what assistance to provide based on children's nonverbal behaviors. To address these challenges, we introduce Ollie, a gaze-informed proactive artificial intelligence (AI) assistant that offers short narrative descriptions based on where a child is looking. Ollie uses children's gaze to estimate their attention, identify their current visual focus, and select a related picture region for the LLM to verbally describe. In a within-subject experiment, we compared gaze-informed assistance with random assistance. Results show that gaze-informed assistance kept children's attention on their current focus for a longer period of time, and guided them more effectively to related picture regions. Children, parents, and a participating kindergarten teacher viewed Ollie positively and consider that it better matched children's interests when compared with the random assistance. This work shows the feasibility of using gaze as an implicit input for proactive AI assistance for children and provides design implications for future child-centered AI systems.

</details>


### [70] [Draped Surfaces: A Contour-Adaptive Interface Overlaid on the Physical Environment for Mixed Reality Workspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00518)
*SoonUk Kwon,Barrett Ens,Pourang Irani*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: CAMEO is a contour-adaptive MR interface that drapes virtual windows onto physical surfaces instead of floating them in mid-air. This reduces occlusion, improves access to real-world objects, and maintains text legibility, offering a new design paradigm that balances immersion, readability, and environmental awareness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional MR workspaces use cockpit-like layouts with floating windows that surround the user. While these provide easy access to digital content, they cause occlusion of the physical environment and restrict interaction with real-world objects. The authors sought to overcome this trade-off.

Method: The authors developed CAMEO, a contour-adaptive MR interface that drapes virtual windows onto physical surfaces, integrating digital content with nearby items. They evaluated CAMEO through two controlled user studies: (1) measuring hand-movement detours compared to flat mid-air surfaces, and (2) assessing text legibility under controlled window deformation versus flat surfaces.

Result: Study 1 showed that draping virtual windows onto surfaces reduces hand-movement detours, enabling more direct interaction with nearby physical items. Study 2 demonstrated that controlled window deformation does not significantly impair text legibility compared to flat surfaces.

Conclusion: CAMEO offers a novel design paradigm for MR workspaces that successfully balances immersion, readability, and environmental understanding, addressing the occlusion and accessibility limitations of conventional floating-window layouts.

Abstract: Conventional Mixed Reality (MR) workspaces are frequently organized in cockpit-like layouts, where multiple floating windows surround the user. While this configuration facilitates access to digital content, it often induces occlusion, reducing understanding of the physical environment and limiting access to real-world objects. To overcome this challenge, we present the Contour-Adaptive Mixed Environment Overlays (CAMEO), a contour-adaptive MR interface that drapes virtual windows onto physical surfaces. This design integrates digital content with nearby items, thereby improving users' visual access to background objects and supporting interaction with them. We evaluate CAMEO in two controlled studies. The first demonstrates that draping reduces hand-movement detours relative to flat mid-air surfaces, enabling more direct interaction with nearby items. The second shows that controlled window deformation does not significantly impair text legibility when compared to flat surfaces. Together, these findings contribute a novel design paradigm for MR workspaces that balances immersion, readability, and environmental understanding.

</details>


### [71] [AI, Trust, and Teaming: The Humans-as-Handlers Approach for Autonomous and Opaque AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00523)
*Nathan G. Wood*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper argues that opaque, autonomous AI systems in high-stakes domains should initially be treated analogously to dogs, with humans cast as "handlers" rather than "users." This reframing clarifies lines of responsibility and traces a developmental trajectory toward viewing AI systems as genuine collaborators rather than mere artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems become increasingly autonomous and opaque, especially in high-impact domains like medicine and warfare, traditional notions of human users and deployers fail to adequately capture the ethical and legal responsibilities involved. Trust-based human-machine teaming requires a new conceptual framework for understanding the human-AI relationship.

Method: The paper employs conceptual analysis and analogical reasoning, drawing a structured analogy between autonomous AI systems and domesticated animals (particularly dogs). It identifies points of similarity and disanalogy, then refines the analogy to separate fitting from unfitting aspects, ultimately using it to ground a normative argument about appropriate human-machine relationships.

Result: The machine-animal analogy yields three key findings: (1) humans should be recast as "handlers" rather than "users" of opaque autonomous systems; (2) this recasting establishes clearer, more traceable lines of human responsibility for system outcomes; and (3) the analogy, while imperfect, serves as a viable starting point that can be refined by divesting it of unfitting animal-relationship elements.

Conclusion: The author concludes that the ideal trajectory for human-machine teaming should move beyond treating autonomous AI systems as mere artifacts and toward viewing them as authentic collaborators in pursuing complex goals, thereby establishing a more robust foundation for trust and responsibility.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) is becoming ubiquitous, and across domains, increasingly autonomous systems are carrying out tasks which raise significant ethical and legal challenges which demonstrate a need for strong human-machine teams rooted in trust. In this article, I argue that within highly impactful areas (such as medicine or warfighting) there are grounds for us initially treating autonomous and opaque systems as relevantly analogous to dogs (or other animals with which we have close relationships). Under this analogy, humans making use of these systems are not to be viewed as "users" or "deployers" of these systems, but instead take the role of "handlers". This recasting of roles shifts the way we view humans, AI-enabled and autonomous systems, and the relations between them, and moreover clarifies the clear and traceable lines of responsibility humans have for the outcomes brought about when using these systems. In developing this point, I clarify that the machine-animal analogy does admit disanalogous elements, but that its touch-points ground it as a starting point. I then explore how we can divest the humans-as-handlers approach of those aspects of our relationships with animals which are unfitting for how we engage with and make use of autonomous and AI-enabled systems. I conclude by arguing that the trajectory of human-machine teamings for autonomous and AI-enabled systems should be a state where we authentically view these not as artifacts which we simply make use of, but as collaborators with which we pursue complex goals and carry out complex tasks.

</details>


### [72] [You Shall Not Pass! Where and Why Developers Draw The Line on AI Autonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00533)
*Rudrajit Choudhuri,Christian Bird,Carmen Badea,Marco Gerosa,Anita Sarma*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A mixed-methods study of 448 Microsoft developers found that accepted AI autonomy varies by task type and individual traits. Developers prefer less autonomy for identity-defining, human-facing, and design work, and more autonomy when they have greater AI experience and risk tolerance. Task accountability and identity reduce acceptance of AI decision-making, while task demands increase it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI increasingly performs software engineering work, the boundary between human and AI effort is shifting. Understanding where developers draw the line on AI autonomy is critical for designing tools and roles that preserve meaningful human work.

Method: Mixed-methods study with 448 professional developers at Microsoft, drawing on cognitive appraisal theory, work design theory, and automation research. The study investigated accepted levels of AI autonomy across various software engineering tasks.

Result: Most developers accepted AI producing work under their oversight, but accepted autonomy varied significantly across tasks and individuals. Acceptance was lowest for identity-defining, human-facing, and design-oriented work. Higher AI experience and risk tolerance correlated with greater acceptance. Task accountability lowered the odds of allowing AI to act on developers' behalf; task identity lowered the odds of granting AI decision-making autonomy. Task demands increased willingness to delegate decision-making to AI.

Conclusion: Preferences for AI autonomy reflect how developers cognitively experience their work, carrying important implications for designing meaningful work in AI-augmented software engineering environments.

Abstract: As AI takes on more software work, the line between human and AI effort is shifting. Where developers draw that line around AI autonomy bears on how we design tools and roles that preserve meaningful work. Drawing on cognitive appraisal theory, work design, and automation research, we conducted a mixed-methods study of 448 professional developers at Microsoft to investigate their accepted levels of AI autonomy across software engineering work. Most developers accepted AI producing work under their oversight, although accepted autonomy varied substantively across tasks and individuals. Acceptance was lowest for identity-defining, human-facing, and design-oriented work, and higher among developers with more AI experience and risk tolerance. Task accountability was associated with lower odds of allowing AI to act on developers' behalf, whereas task identity was associated with lower odds of granting AI decision-making autonomy. Task demands had the opposite effect, increasing willingness to delegate decision-making to AI. Our findings suggest that preferences for AI autonomy reflect how developers cognitively experience their work, highlighting important considerations for designing meaningful work.

</details>


### [73] [AI-Centered Grand Challenges in Visual Analytics for Healthcare: Synthesizing the VAHC 2025 Community Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00542)
*Jürgen Bernard,David Gotz,Robert S Laramee,Silvia Miksch,Gabriela Morgenshtern,Renata G. Raidou,Alessio Arleo*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper reports on the VAHC 2025 workshop's consolidation of collective expertise to identify five Grand Challenges at the intersection of AI, healthcare, and visualization: trust and bias, data and infrastructure, explainability and communication, human-AI interaction, and model reliability and validation. Each challenge cluster includes recommendations for cross-disciplinary research directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid evolution at the intersection of AI, healthcare, and visualization poses cross-disciplinary challenges that resist easy resolution. After 16 editions of the VAHC workshop, the community sought to consolidate collective experience and expertise to prioritize future research directions, with AI emerging as the most consistently recurring concern across all discussions.

Method: The authors combined thematic coding of 15 accepted VAHC 2025 workshop papers with structured group discussions among 40+ participants, organized around three themes: "Technical innovation vs. clinical reality," "Human-centered and scalable VAHC," and "From foundations to actionable insights." This was followed by post-workshop reflexive analysis, contextualized against broader literature.

Result: Five Grand Challenge clusters were distilled, each with recommendations: (1) trust and bias, (2) data and infrastructure, (3) explainability and communication, (4) human-AI interaction, and (5) model reliability and validation. AI emerged as the most consistently recurring concern across all three discussion groups.

Conclusion: The identified challenges and research directions serve as a starting point for discussion and cross-disciplinary collaboration among the healthcare, AI, and visualization communities. Supplemental materials are publicly available on OSF.

Abstract: The intersection of AI, healthcare, and visualization is evolving rapidly, posing challenges that cut across disciplinary boundaries and resist easy resolution. The Visual Analytics in Healthcare workshop (VAHC), co-located every other year at the IEEE VIS conference and the AMIA (American Medical Informatics Association) annual conference, has served as a forum to connect the visualization and medical informatics community since 2010. In 2025, to celebrate the 16th edition, we used the workshop as an opportunity to consolidate the community's collective experience (and expertise) and identify Grand Challenges where the field should prioritize going forward. We combined thematic coding of the 15 accepted VAHC workshop papers with structured group discussions among more than 40 participants, organized around three major themes: "Technical innovation vs. clinical reality", "Human-centered and scalable VAHC", and "From foundations to actionable insights", followed by post-workshop reflexive analysis. Across all three groups, AI emerged as the most consistently recurring concern. In this paper, we report our AI-centered insights from the VAHC 2025 group activity, contextualize them against the broader literature along five Grand Challenges themes, and distill them into five challenge clusters, each concluded with recommendations for future research directions that cross disciplinary boundaries: (1) trust and bias, (2) data and infrastructure, (3) explainability and communication, (4) human-AI interaction, and (5) model reliability and validation. We share these challenges and their associated research directions as a starting point for discussion and collaboration across the healthcare, AI, and visualization communities. All supplemental materials are available at https://osf.io/p79uj.

</details>


### [74] [Understanding How Humans Inject Knowledge into Machine Learning Workflows through Visual Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00969)
*Yiwen Xing,Philip Beaucamp,Joyraj Chakraborty,Afrah Farea,Yuanzhe Jin,Saiful Khan,Gennady Andrienko,Natalia Andrienko,Min Chen*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A survey of over 200 VIS4ML papers from the past decade analyzing how humans inject knowledge into ML workflows through interactive visualization, identifying pathways of knowledge transfer and explaining VIS4ML phenomena via model-building and cost-benefit frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ML workflows involve extensive human input (labeling, feature engineering, architecture design, hyper-parameter tuning), yet no systematic understanding exists of how interactive visualization mediates human knowledge injection into these workflows. The authors aim to fill this gap by surveying and analyzing the VIS4ML literature.

Method: The authors collected a corpus of over 200 VIS4ML papers from IEEE VIS conferences (past decade) and developed a coding scheme to analyze them from four perspectives: ML characteristics, visualization design, interaction mechanisms, and user actions. The coded dataset was then analyzed to identify patterns and pathways of human knowledge injection.

Result: The analysis reveals distinct pathways through which human knowledge is transferred to ML workflows via interactive visualization. The phenomena are explained using (1) a conceptual model that frames VA as model building, and (2) an information-theoretic cost-benefit analysis that positions VA as an optimization mechanism for ML workflows.

Conclusion: The work provides unequivocal evidence for the merits of using visual analytics in ML workflows. A comprehensive repository of surveyed papers, analysis results, and figures is made publicly available online.

Abstract: Visual analytics (VA) plays an increasingly important role in supporting machine learning (ML) workflows. In the field of visualization, such approaches and techniques are referred to as VIS4ML. While ML models are mostly learned automatically, the corresponding ML workflows receive a variety of human inputs, such as data labelling, feature engineering, model architecture designing, hyper-parameter tuning, and so on. In this work, we surveyed over 200 VIS4ML papers to gain an understanding of how humans inject their knowledge into ML workflows through interactive visualization. We collected a corpus of VIS4ML papers from the IEEE VIS conferences in the past decade. We developed a coding scheme to facilitate the literature research from four perspectives: characteristics of ML, visualization, interaction, and actions. The analysis of the coded dataset allows us to observe different pathways that transfer human knowledge to ML workflows via interactive visualization. Building on the analysis, we explain the phenomena of VIS4ML using the conceptual model that views VA as model building and the information-theoretic cost-benefit analysis that reasons VA as for optimizing ML workflows. This work provides unequivocal evidence showing the merits of using VA in ML workflows. The full list of surveyed papers, along with all analysis results and figures, is available at https://vis4ml4hd.github.io/ml-knowledge-inject-va/.

</details>


### [75] [Visualizing Engineering Fundamentals: Design of Mixed Reality and Physical Toolkits for Effective Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00979)
*Mohammad Abu Nasir Rakib,Sharmin Akter,Eshwara Prasad Sridhar,Somik Biswas,Md Rassel Raihan,Mahmudur Rahman*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A user study with 24 participants compared three instructional modalities for Engineering Mechanics — classroom instruction alone, with mixed-reality, and with physical toolkits — revealing that multimodal, interactive experiences combining visualization and hands-on interaction are valued, while complex visualizations pose challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how mixed-reality applications and physical toolkits affect student workflows and engagement in Engineering Mechanics, and to derive human-centered design guidelines for educational tools.

Method: A user study with 24 participants comparing three conditions: (1) classroom instruction alone, (2) classroom instruction with a mixed-reality application, and (3) classroom instruction with physical toolkits. Thematic analysis was conducted on participant feedback.

Result: Learner workflows and engagement with fundamental mechanics problems varied across instructional modalities. Participants valued multimodal, interactive experiences blending visualization with hands-on interaction but reported difficulties with complex or unclear visualizations.

Conclusion: The findings inform human-centered design guidelines for developing mixed-reality and physical educational tools in engineering, emphasizing the importance of intuitive, clear visualizations paired with interactive, hands-on experiences.

Abstract: This study examined students' experiences with mixed-reality applications and physical toolkits in Engineering Mechanics to inform design guidelines for educational tools. In a user study with 24 participants, we compared classroom instruction alone, classroom instruction with a mixed-reality application, and classroom instruction with physical toolkits. Thematic analysis of participant feedback revealed that learners' workflows and engagement with fundamental mechanics problems varied across instructional modalities. Participants valued multimodal and interactive experiences that combined visualization with hands-on interaction, while reporting challenges with complex or unclear visualizations. These insights support the human-centered design of mixed-reality and physical tools for engineering education.

</details>


### [76] [SenseWalk: Agent-Based Semantic Trajectory Simulation Powered by Large Language Models in Zoned Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00989)
*Ziyue Lin,Xinhang Xie,Kangyi Wang,Siming Chen*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: SenseWalk is an interactive system that uses LLM-powered agents combined with a social force model to simulate semantically coherent and physically plausible human movement trajectories, addressing the difficulty of collecting real-world semantic trajectory data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world semantic trajectory data collection is costly and lacks rich semantic information, while existing simulation tools require substantial technical expertise, creating barriers for practitioners who need to model human movement with implicit behavioral patterns.

Method: SenseWalk combines Large Language Models (LLMs) with the social force model in a simulation workflow that balances physical plausibility with semantic coherence. A user-friendly interface allows customization of simulation configurations and output analysis. Evaluation includes a quantitative experiment and a user study with 12 participants.

Result: The paper reports a quantitative experiment evaluating simulation effectiveness and a user study (n=12) assessing usefulness and efficiency, though specific numerical results are not detailed in the abstract.

Conclusion: SenseWalk demonstrates that integrating LLM-powered agents with traditional physical models offers a practical, accessible solution for semantic trajectory simulation, bridging the gap between domain expertise requirements and usable simulation tools.

Abstract: Semantic trajectory analysis has recently emerged as an approach for modeling human movement by capturing implicit patterns and behaviors through semantic information (e.g., visitors' profiles and goals) beyond raw spatial paths to better understand why people move in certain ways. However, analyzing semantic trajectories in real-world scenarios remains challenging, as collecting high-quality data is costly and often lacks rich semantic information. Meanwhile, existing simulation tools require substantial technical expertise, which makes them difficult for practitioners to adopt. To address these limitations, the paper proposes ${SenseWalk}$, an interactive system that supports simulating semantic trajectories by LLM-powered agents. We develop a simulation workflow that combines LLMs and the social force model to balance physical plausibility and semantic coherence. A user-friendly interface is designed to facilitate users in customizing the simulation configuration and analyzing simulation outputs. We also conduct a quantitative experiment to evaluate the effectiveness of our simulation workflow, and a user study (n=12) to assess the usefulness and efficiency of our system.

</details>


### [77] [Touching and Feeling the Data: A Reusable Software Pipeline for Tactile Statistical Graphs in Accessible Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01214)
*Lawrence Obiuwevwi,Krzysztof J. Rechowicz,Jessica M. Johnson,Erika Frydenlund,Vikas Ashok,Sachin Shetty,Sampath Jayarathna*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A JavaScript pipeline (~1500 lines) automatically generates 3D-printable tactile graphs (STL files) for blind/low-vision students in under 250ms, using perception-based design parameters, five modular chart builders, and an optional LLM layer for image-to-chart extraction with mandatory teacher review.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tactile graphs (3D-printed) enable blind and low-vision students to access statistical visualizations through touch, but classroom adoption is bottlenecked by the need for specialized CAD skills and hours of manual labor per graph. The authors frame this as a software automation problem.

Method: A three-layer pipeline in JavaScript: (1) automatic derivation of tactile design parameters from plate dimensions using tactile perception research; (2) shared chart scaffolding with five modular builders (scatter, bar, histogram, line, box plots); (3) an optional multi-modal LLM layer that extracts structured chart specifications from uploaded images, with mandatory teacher review before printing. Output is print-ready binary STL files.

Result: The pipeline generates print-ready binary STL files in under 250 milliseconds, covering five chart types. The system is compact at ~1500 lines of JavaScript.

Conclusion: The pipeline successfully addresses the production bottleneck for tactile statistical graphics, enabling rapid, automated generation suitable for classroom use. Limitations are acknowledged but not detailed in the abstract.

Abstract: Statistical visualization is usually treated as a visual medium, but data can also be touched. Three dimensional printed tactile graphs let blind and low vision students feel distributions, trace trends, and explore relationships through direct haptic interaction. Yet classroom scale use remains limited because producing each graph in CAD software requires specialized skill and hours of manual work. We address this bottleneck as a software problem through a three layer reusable pipeline in about 1500 lines of JavaScript. The first layer derives tactile design parameters automatically from plate dimensions using tactile perception research. The second provides shared chart scaffolding and five modular builders for scatter, bar, histogram, line, and box plots. The optional third layer uses a multi-modal large language model to extract structured chart specifications from uploaded images, with mandatory teacher review before print generation. The pipeline produces print ready binary Standard Tessellation Language files in under 250 milliseconds. We present the design, performance, and limitations.

</details>


### [78] [Surfacing Variations to Calibrate Perceived Reliability of MLLM-generated Image Descriptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15692)
*Meng Chen,Akhil Iyer,Amy Pavel*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper presents a system that surfaces variations across multiple MLLM responses to help blind and low-vision (BLV) users detect unreliable AI-generated image descriptions without visual inspection, achieving a 4.9x improvement in identifying unreliable claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs offer BLV people access to visual information but frequently produce errors that are undetectable without sight. Existing workarounds (cross-checking tools, consulting sighted people) are time-consuming and impractical. There is a need for a systematic method to help BLV users identify unreliable MLLM outputs without requiring visual verification.

Method: The authors (1) developed a design space for eliciting and presenting variations in MLLM descriptions, (2) built a prototype system with three variation presentation styles, and (3) conducted a user study with 15 BLV participants to evaluate the approach against single-description baselines.

Result: Presenting variations increased users' ability to identify unreliable claims by 4.9x compared to single descriptions and significantly decreased perceived reliability of MLLM responses. 14 of 15 participants preferred seeing variations, and all expressed interest in using the system for real-world tasks.

Conclusion: Surfacing response variations is an effective, practical strategy for helping BLV users detect MLLM errors without sighted assistance, with strong user preference and broad applicability to daily visual tasks.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) provide new opportunities for blind and low vision (BLV) people to access visual information in their daily lives. However, these models often produce errors that are difficult to detect without sight, posing safety and social risks in scenarios from medication identification to outfit selection. While BLV MLLM users use creative workarounds such as cross-checking between tools and consulting sighted individuals, these approaches are often time-consuming and impractical. We explore how systematically surfacing variations across multiple MLLM responses can support BLV users to detect unreliable information without visually inspecting the image. We contribute a design space for eliciting and presenting variations in MLLM descriptions, a prototype system implementing three variation presentation styles, and findings from a user study with 15 BLV participants. Our results demonstrate that presenting variations significantly increases users' ability to identify unreliable claims (by 4.9x using our approach compared to single descriptions) and significantly decreases perceived reliability of MLLM responses. 14 of 15 participants preferred seeing variations of MLLM responses over a single description, and all expressed interest in using our system for tasks from understanding a tornado's path to posting an image on social media.

</details>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [79] [Representation as a Bottleneck for Mechanistic Interpretability: The Manifestation Unit Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00089)
*Hussein Chouman,Wataru Sasaki,Tomokazu Matsui,Hirohiko Suwa,Keiichi Yasumoto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Manifestation Units, a typed tuple protocol (E, S, R, D, G, plus T for transformers) that structures neural-network component-level interpretability findings into a standardized, queryable, and composable format. The protocol improves retrieval performance, satisfies causal criteria, and reveals a core S+R field pair, providing schema infrastructure for mechanistic interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mechanistic interpretability research produces rich per-component analyses (selectivity tables, circuit diagrams, feature lists), but these outputs are locked in non-composable, non-queryable per-study notebooks. They cannot be easily reused, audited, or acted upon downstream. The authors identify the representation layer between raw analyses and downstream use as an independent bottleneck worthy of systematic study.

Method: The authors propose Manifestation Units — a typed tuple protocol with fields (E, S, R, D, G) extended with attention-head primitives (T) for transformers. These units organize per-component statistics into structured fields that are populated automatically and queried through hybrid retrieval. The protocol is instantiated across three architectures: beta-VAE (generative vision), CNN (discriminative vision), and GPT-2 (language). Evaluation includes retrieval performance against unstructured baselines, causal sufficiency and necessity testing for CNN filters under matched-budget controls, attention-head set-recovery of known IOI circuit members, and field ablation to identify core vs. redundant/interfering fields.

Result: Two main findings: (1) Typed structure substantially outperforms unstructured baselines on retrieval across all architectures tested. (2) CNN filters retrieved via the schema satisfy causal sufficiency and necessity criteria under matched-budget controls. The schema absorbs attention-head primitives without modification and set-recovers known IOI circuit members under retrieval-budget-matched controls. Field ablation reveals an irreducible two-field core (S+R), with the remaining fields being either redundant or actively interfering with retrieval/causal performance.

Conclusion: The work positions Manifestation Units not as frontier-scale validation but as schema infrastructure for mechanistic interpretability — a standardized representation layer that makes component-level analyses reusable, composable, queryable, and actionable for downstream audit and intervention tasks.

Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability has produced a rich inventory of component-level analyses that characterise what neural-network components encode and how they interact. Their outputs, however, are not easily reusable: selectivity tables, circuit diagrams, and feature lists remain locked in per-study notebooks - non-composable, not queryable in natural language, and not directly actionable for downstream audit or intervention. We study the representation layer that sits between these analyses and downstream use as a bottleneck that can be evaluated independently, and introduce Manifestation Units, a typed tuple protocol (E, S, R, D, G) extended with attention-head primitives (T) for transformer architectures, organising per-component statistics into structured fields populated automatically and queried through hybrid retrieval. Instantiated across generative vision (beta-VAE), discriminative vision (CNN), and language (GPT-2), the protocol supports two findings: typed structure substantially outperforms unstructured baselines on retrieval, and CNN filters retrieved by the schema satisfy causal sufficiency and necessity criteria under matched-budget controls. The schema absorbs attention-head primitives without modification, set-recovers known IOI circuit members under retrieval-budget-matched controls, and reveals an irreducible two-field core (S+R) with remaining fields either redundant or actively interfering. We present this as schema infrastructure for mechanistic interpretability rather than frontier-scale validation.

</details>


### [80] [SNAP-FM: Sparse Nonlinear Accelerated Projection for Physics-Constrained Generative Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00095)
*Alaina Kolli,Theodoros Xenakis,Utkarsh Utkarsh,Pengfei Cai,Rafael Gomez-Bombarelli,Alan Edelman,Christopher Vincent Rackauckas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Generative models for physical simulation lack guarantees on conservation laws. The authors propose using block-sparse nonlinear optimization on GPUs (via ExaModels.jl + MadNLP.jl) to efficiently enforce nonlinear constraints during sampling, accelerating Physics-Constrained Flow Matching while maintaining constraint satisfaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constrained sampling can enforce physical conservation laws at inference time, but nonlinear constraint projection is computationally expensive due to repeated optimization steps. Standard ML frameworks obscure the block-sparse structure inherent in batched PDE constraints, making efficient batched nonlinear optimization impractical.

Method: The authors exploit the block-sparse structure (Jacobian and KKT systems) induced by sample-wise batching and local PDE couplings. They expose this structure using ExaModels.jl and solve the resulting sparse nonlinear programs with MadNLP.jl's GPU sparse factorization. This is applied to Physics-Constrained Flow Matching (PCFM) across linear, nonlinear, 1D, and 2D PDE constraint benchmarks.

Result: The sparse GPU nonlinear optimization approach accelerates nonlinear constraint projection while maintaining constraint satisfaction across diverse PDE benchmarks with linear, nonlinear, one-dimensional, and two-dimensional constraints.

Conclusion: Sparse GPU nonlinear optimization is a practical foundation for constrained generative sampling in scientific machine learning, enabling efficient, exact enforcement of physical constraints at inference time.

Abstract: Generative models have emerged as scalable surrogates for physical simulation, yet they offer no guarantee that their outputs respect the conservation laws, boundary conditions, and nonlinear invariants that govern the underlying physics. Constrained sampling closes this gap, enforcing such constraints exactly at inference time without retraining, but at a computational cost: projection, correction, and trajectory-optimization steps are repeated during sampling, with these steps becoming expensive for nonlinear constraints. Standard ML frameworks exacerbate this: their dense tensor algebra and limited sparse solver composability obscure the structure that physical constraints naturally induce, making efficient batched nonlinear optimization difficult to realize in practice. We address this bottleneck by exploiting the structure that sample-wise batching and local PDE couplings induce in the projection subproblems -- namely, block-sparse Jacobian and KKT systems -- exposing this structure using ExaModels.jl and solving the resulting sparse nonlinear programs with MadNLP.jl and GPU sparse factorization. Applied to Physics-Constrained Flow Matching (PCFM), on PDE benchmarks with linear, nonlinear, one-dimensional, and two-dimensional constraints, this approach accelerates nonlinear constraint projection while maintaining constraint satisfaction. These results show that sparse GPU nonlinear optimization is a practical foundation for constrained generative sampling in scientific machine learning.

</details>


### [81] [SemiScope: Disentangling Classifier Tuning and Joint Optimization in Semi-Supervised Security Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00113)
*Rui Shu,Tianpei Xia,Jingzhu He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether gains from optimizing semi-supervised learning (SSL) pipelines on tabular security data come from SSL-classifier interactions or merely from tuning the downstream classifier. Using a controlled experiment with Bayesian Optimization, the authors find that classifier hyperparameter optimization alone recovers ~86% of the gain, making full SSL pipeline tuning largely unnecessary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SSL is widely used in security applications with scarce labeled data, but as a black box. Recent work claims large improvements from jointly optimizing SSL pipelines, yet it is unclear whether these gains stem from meaningful SSL-classifier synergy or simply from better classifier tuning. The authors aim to disentangle these effects for binary tabular security data.

Method: The authors build SemiScope, an analysis instrument using Bayesian Optimization to jointly tune SSL settings, confidence filtering, oversampling, and classifier hyperparameters. The critical control, Tuned-Clf, fixes SSL to defaults while receiving the same 100-trial classifier HPO budget and validation-set threshold tuning. Paired TOST equivalence testing with a ±1.0 g-measure smallest effect of interest is used to compare SemiScope and Tuned-Clf at 10% labels across five security datasets.

Result: SemiScope outperforms all default SSL baselines by 0.7–12.7 g-measure points. However, Tuned-Clf is statistically equivalent to SemiScope on 4 of 5 datasets (Phishing is inconclusive). Classifier HPO alone recovers a median 86% of SemiScope's gain over Default Self-Training + Random Forest.

Conclusion: The main contribution is the decomposition protocol itself. A simpler recipe suffices: use Self-Training, tune the classifier with Bayesian Optimization, and tune the decision threshold on validation data. This reaches within 1 g-measure of Supervised RF at 20–30% labels on four datasets (40% on Drebin), matching or surpassing the label efficiency of default SSL pipelines.

Abstract: Background. Labeled data for security classification is scarce. Semi-supervised learning (SSL) propagates labels from a small labeled pool to larger unlabeled pools. Yet security applications often use SSL as a black box: default parameters, a fixed classifier, and no handling of pseudo-label-induced class imbalance. Aims. Recent work reports sizeable gains from optimizing SSL pipelines via joint search, AutoML, or per-component tuning. These gains are hard to attribute: they may reflect useful SSL-classifier interactions, or mostly from simply tuning the downstream classifier. We disentangle these effects for binary tabular security data with classical SSL and tree-based classifiers. Method. We build SemiScope as an analysis instrument, not a deployment recommendation. It uses Bayesian Optimization to jointly tune SSL settings, confidence filtering, oversampling, and the classifier. The key control, Tuned-Clf, fixes SSL to defaults but gets the same 100-trial classifier budget and validation-set threshold tuning as SemiScope. At 10% labels, we compare them with paired TOST using a +/-1.0 g-measure smallest effect of interest. Results. SemiScope beats every default SSL baseline on all five datasets, improving over the strongest by 0.7-12.7 points. Under the equal-budget control, Tuned-Clf is statistically equivalent to the full pipeline on 4 of 5 datasets; Phishing is inconclusive. Classifier HPO alone recovers a median 86% of SemiScope's gain over Default Self-Training (ST) + Random Forest (RF). Conclusions. The reusable contribution is the decomposition protocol. A simpler recipe suffices: use Self-Training, tune the classifier with Bayesian Optimization, and tune the decision threshold on validation data. It reaches within 1 g-measure of Supervised RF at 20-30% labels on four datasets and 40% on Drebin, at the same or lower label rate than Default ST + RF on every dataset.

</details>


### [82] [A Filtered Mixture-of-Generators for Fully Synthetic Survival Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00127)
*Niccolò Maria Rizzi,Eugenio Lomurno,Alberto Archetti,Matteo Matteucci*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FoGS improves survival analysis on small clinical cohorts by treating synthetic data construction as sample selection from a diverse pool of tabular generators, filtered via an ensemble of survival models to achieve real-data-competitive downstream performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical survival analysis suffers from small cohorts, long follow-up times, and privacy constraints that prevent data sharing. Existing tabular generative models require large training sets and perform poorly on small survival datasets — a single generator cannot adequately characterize the population, and downstream models trained on synthetic data underperform relative to real-data training.

Method: FoGS proposes a two-level pipeline. Four architecturally distinct tabular generators produce a candidate pool of synthetic samples. Each sample is scored by an ensemble of seven survival models (trained on real data) using proper scoring rules as a per-sample plausibility proxy. An outer loop optimizes a selection policy — comprising generator quotas, scorer weights, a random complement, and stratified balancing on event time and censoring — against held-out downstream performance. An inner loop tunes the downstream model (XGBoost-Cox). The framework reframes generation as filtered sample selection rather than pure generation.

Result: On 16 public datasets under train-on-synthetic, test-on-real evaluation, FoGS achieves mean improvements of +2.17 in C-index and +0.67 in IBS (0–100 scale). Both metrics improve on 9 of 16 datasets, and at least one metric improves on 13 datasets (one-sided Wilcoxon p=0.039 and p=0.035). It matches or exceeds real-data training on most cohorts, with no significant change in nearest-neighbour privacy margin relative to unfiltered sampling.

Conclusion: Sample filtering over a heterogeneous generator pool is a viable substitute for real-data training in privacy-restricted clinical survival analysis settings, effectively bridging the gap between synthetic data generation and real-data downstream performance.

Abstract: Survival analysis models time-to-event data, but in clinical settings training data are costly and scarce: events accrue over years of follow-up, cohorts are small, and privacy regulations restrict sharing across institutions. Tabular generative models promise augmentation and privacy-preserving cohort sharing, yet are themselves data-hungry -- on the small cohorts typical of survival analysis, a single generator rarely characterizes the population well enough for downstream models trained on its output to match real-data performance. FoGS (Filtered Mixture-of-Generators for Survival analysis) reframes synthetic-data construction as sample selection rather than generation. A candidate pool is drawn from four architecturally distinct tabular generators, and each sample is scored by an ensemble of seven survival models trained on real data, using proper scoring rules as a per-sample plausibility proxy. A two-level pipeline optimizes, in its outer loop, a selection policy -- generator quotas, scorer weights, a random complement, and stratified balancing on event time and censoring -- against held-out downstream performance, while an inner loop tunes the downstream model (XGBoost-Cox). On 16 public datasets under train-on-synthetic, test-on-real (C-index and IBS, $0$--$100$ scale), FoGS yields mean improvements of $+2.17$ in C-index and $+0.67$ in IBS, improving both metrics on 9 of 16 datasets and at least one on 13 (one-sided Wilcoxon $p=0.039$ and $p=0.035$). It matches or exceeds real-data training on most cohorts, with no significant change in nearest-neighbour privacy margin relative to unfiltered sampling. Sample filtering over a heterogeneous generator pool is thus a viable substitute for real-data training in privacy-restricted clinical settings.

</details>


### [83] [GRPO, Dr. GRPO, and DAPO Are Three Operations on One Number: The Group-Standard-Deviation Identity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00152)
*Yong Yi Bay,Kathleen A. Yearick*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GRPO, Dr. GRPO, and DAPO — three seemingly distinct RL methods for language model reasoning — are shown to be equivalent to adjusting a single parameter: the standard deviation of binary correctness scores across sampled answers. This "group-standard-deviation identity" reveals that the disagreement among sampled answers fully determines the magnitude of the training update, with split groups driving all learning and unanimous groups contributing nothing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Three popular methods for training language models to reason (GRPO, Dr. GRPO, DAPO) are widely treated as independent algorithmic tricks. The authors challenge this fragmentation by demonstrating that these methods are fundamentally variants of the same underlying mechanism, arguing that the field lacks a unified understanding of what actually drives learning in these frameworks.

Method: The authors derive the "group-standard-deviation identity," proving analytically that for binary (right/wrong) rewards, the training update magnitude equals the standard deviation of correctness scores within a group of sampled answers. This identity unifies GRPO (normalizes by standard deviation), Dr. GRPO (drops normalization), and DAPO (discards zero-deviation groups) as three settings of the same underlying quantity. The theoretical result is validated empirically on the Big-Math dataset and in a controlled training run.

Result: The group-standard-deviation identity shows that: (1) split groups (answers evenly right and wrong) produce the largest updates and carry all learning signal; (2) unanimous groups (all right or all wrong) produce zero update; (3) the standard deviation also determines which problems deserve the most sampling weight and how many attempts each problem needs. Empirical results on Big-Math and controlled training runs confirm these theoretical predictions.

Conclusion: What appears as a benign normalization choice is in fact the central dial controlling where and how strongly learning occurs. The three methods — GRPO, Dr. GRPO, DAPO — are not competing innovations but three positions on a single control, unifying what was previously seen as disparate algorithmic design choices under one theoretical framework.

Abstract: Three of the most popular methods for training language models to reason look like three different tricks. They are not. All three adjust a single number: standard deviation, reflecting how much a prompt's sampled answers disagree. When such a model is trained, it answers each problem many times, and an automatic checker marks every answer right or wrong. The standard deviation of those marks measures the disagreement: largest when the answers split evenly between right and wrong, and zero when they all agree. Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) divides by this number, GRPO Done Right (Dr. GRPO) drops the division, and Decoupled Clip and Dynamic Sampling Policy Optimization (DAPO) discards the groups where it is zero. Each is presented as its own fix, yet this paper proves they are three settings of one dial. That dial is not cosmetic: for right-or-wrong rewards, the disagreement is exactly the size of the training update, the group-standard-deviation identity. A split group teaches the most, while a unanimous group teaches nothing and falls silent. The same result says which problems deserve the most weight and how many tries each one needs. This paper confirms the intuition on a large real difficulty dataset (Big-Math) and in a controlled training run. What looks like a harmless normalization step is the dial that decides where learning happens and how strongly.

</details>


### [84] [EVOTS: Evolutionary Transformer Search for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00154)
*AbdElRahman ElSaid,Damir Pulatov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EVOTS introduces an evolutionary neural architecture search framework that discovers task-adaptive Transformer-like models for multivariate time-series forecasting, using a modular genome representation with a repair mechanism. Evaluated on four ETT benchmarks across multiple forecasting settings and horizons, the evolved architectures achieve competitive or improved MSE compared to a strong Transformer baseline, demonstrating that evolutionary search can effectively discover flexible, high-performing architectures within practical runtime constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing approaches for multivariate time-series forecasting rely on fixed Transformer architectures despite significant variation across tasks and forecasting settings. Evolutionary neural architecture design remains underexplored in this domain, creating an opportunity to discover more task-adaptive architectures automatically rather than relying on hand-crafted design rules.

Method: EVOTS uses an evolutionary neural architecture search framework with a modular genome representation that flexibly encodes attention, feed-forward, and projection components. A repair mechanism enforces structural validity throughout evolution. The framework is evaluated on four ETT datasets (ETTh1, ETTh2, ETTm1, ETTm2) under three forecasting settings (univariate-to-univariate, multivariate-to-univariate, multivariate-to-multivariate) with prediction horizons of 96, 192, 336, and 720 steps.

Result: In the multivariate-to-multivariate setting, evolved architectures achieved competitive and often improved MSE relative to a strong Transformer baseline. The paper also reports performance differences across forecasting settings and provides wall-clock training time as a coarse computational cost metric.

Conclusion: Evolutionary search can effectively discover flexible, high-performing Transformer-like architectures for multivariate time-series forecasting within practical runtime constraints, offering a viable alternative to fixed-architecture approaches.

Abstract: Evolutionary neural architecture design for multivariate time-series forecasting remains underexplored, with most approaches relying on fixed Transformer architectures despite substantial variation across tasks and forecasting settings. This paper introduces an evolutionary neural architecture search framework for discovering task-adaptive Transformer-like models for time-series forecasting (EVOTS). Architectures are encoded using a modular genome representation that enables flexible composition of attention, feed-forward, and projection components, while a repair mechanism enforces structural validity throughout the evolutionary process. This formulation allows effective exploration of a diverse architecture space without relying on hand-crafted design rules. The proposed approach is evaluated on four benchmark datasets from the ETT family (ETTh1, ETTh2, ETTm1, and ETTm2) under multiple forecasting settings, including univariate-to-univariate, multivariate-to-univariate, and multivariate-to-multivariate prediction, with horizons of 96, 192, 336, and 720. In the multivariate-to-multivariate setting, the evolved architectures achieve competitive and, in several cases, improved mean squared error relative to a strong Transformer-based baseline. Additional analyses examine performance differences across forecasting settings and report wall-clock training time to provide a coarse indication of computational cost. Overall, the results demonstrate that evolutionary search can effectively discover flexible and high-performing Transformer-like architectures for multivariate time-series forecasting within practical runtime constraints.

</details>


### [85] [FRAME: Learning the Adaptation Domain with a Mixture of Fractional-Fourier Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00162)
*Tom Saliencro,Maya Lindqvist,Rohan Desai,Priya Nair,Daniel Whitmore*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fractional-Fourier Mixture of Experts (F3MoE) introduces learnable fractional-Fourier orders per expert in a MoE adapter, interpolating between spatial (LoRA) and Fourier domains. This lets each low-rank update operate in its most compact domain, yields naturally decorrelated experts, and improves multi-task performance on LLaMA and Qwen with negligible overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PEFT methods fix the domain of weight updates: LoRA operates spatially, while spectral methods use a fixed Fourier basis. The authors argue that no single basis is optimal across all tasks, layers, or tokens — the choice of domain should itself be learned.

Method: F3MoE is a mixture-of-experts adapter where each expert carries a learnable scalar fractional-Fourier order α ∈ [0,1]. This continuously interpolates between spatial (α=0, recovering LoRA) and Fourier (α=1, recovering spectral adapters) domains. The fractional Fourier transform is computed via an O(d log d) chirp-FFT surrogate. Experts at different orders are mutually incoherent, making them naturally decorrelated and reducing interference. The order α is trained with a separate optimizer alongside standard MoE routing.

Result: On LLaMA-3.1-8B and Qwen2.5-7B, F3MoE outperforms strong MoE-LoRA and spectral baselines (FlyLoRA, FourierMoE, HMoRA) across commonsense, mathematical, code, and knowledge benchmarks, while maintaining a small active-parameter budget. Learned orders specialize by task and layer in interpretable ways.

Conclusion: The domain of PEFT updates is a learnable degree of freedom. Fractional-Fourier MoE enables the model to place each low-rank update in its most compact domain, yielding decorrelated experts, reduced interference, and improved performance with negligible computational overhead.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) reparameterizes weight updates in a fixed basis: low-rank adapters operate in the spatial domain, while a recent line of spectral methods operates in a fixed Fourier domain. We argue that the choice of domain is itself a design degree of freedom that should be learned, and that no single basis is optimal across tasks, layers, or tokens. We introduce Fractional-Fourier Mixture of Experts, a mixture-of-experts adapter in which every expert carries a learnable fractional-Fourier order that continuously interpolates between the spatial domain (recovering vanilla LoRA) and the Fourier domain (recovering a spectral adapter). Routing tokens through experts that occupy different points on this spatial-spectral continuum lets the model place each low-rank update in the domain where it is most compact, and -- because fractional-Fourier operators of different orders are mutually incoherent -- makes the experts naturally decorrelated, which reduces interference and improves multi-task composition. The order is a single scalar per expert, trained with a separate optimizer, and the transform is computed with an $\mathcal{O}(d\log d)$ chirp--FFT surrogate, so Fractional-Fourier Mixture of Experts adds negligible cost over standard MoE-LoRA. Across commonsense, mathematical, code, and knowledge benchmarks on LLaMA-3.1-8B and Qwen2.5-7B, Fractional-Fourier Mixture of Experts improves over strong MoE-LoRA and spectral baselines -- including FlyLoRA, FourierMoE, and HMoRA -- while keeping the active-parameter budget small, and analysis shows that the learned orders specialize by task and layer in interpretable ways.

</details>


### [86] [Verifiable Rewards for Calibrated Probabilistic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00164)
*Sadanand Singh,Allam Reddy,Manan Chopra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Training calibrated aleatoric forecasters via reinforcement learning is hindered by noisy single-outcome rewards and policy gradient corruption of reasoning chains. The authors propose a verifiable, label-free reward (state-conditioned empirical win rate from past outcomes) combined with gradient masking or direct prediction, achieving betting-market-level calibration on NFL win probability with a 7B model—without human labels or supervised fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning with proper scoring rules (e.g., Brier score) should theoretically produce calibrated probabilistic forecasters, but in practice degrades calibration. Existing remedies target epistemic uncertainty, not aleatoric forecasting where the forecast itself is the output and the label is a single stochastic draw. The authors aim to solve calibration degradation in aleatoric settings, using NFL in-game win probability as a testbed.

Method: The authors introduce a verifiable, label-free reward: a state-conditioned empirical win rate computed from past outcomes, eliminating single-outcome label noise. They prevent the policy gradient from corrupting the chain of thought via two techniques: (1) direct prediction (bypassing CoT for the final forecast) and (2) a gradient mask that blocks gradients from flowing through the reasoning chain. Training uses this reward alone—no human labels or SFT.

Result: A 7B model trained with this reward alone reaches betting-market calibration via direct prediction and is better calibrated than a zero-shot frontier model. That frontier model and a tabular estimator match this model's Brier score, indicating the market's remaining edge stems from live in-game information beyond their shared inputs. Masking the gradient (rather than dropping CoT) preserves reasoning that standard CoT training corrupts.

Conclusion: Label-free, verifiable rewards based on historical empirical rates combined with gradient isolation from the reasoning chain enable RL-trained forecasters to achieve strong calibration in aleatoric settings without human supervision, preserving interpretable reasoning that naive RL would otherwise degrade.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards can in principle train calibrated probabilistic forecasters, since a proper scoring rule such as the Brier score is computed from outcomes alone and is minimized in expectation by the true probability. In practice it degrades calibration, and existing remedies address epistemic uncertainty, where a model's confidence accompanies a verifiably correct or incorrect answer. We study aleatoric forecasting, where the forecast itself is the output and the label is one stochastic outcome, taking NFL in-game win probability as a testbed with the betting market as a reference. Rewarding the realized per-play outcome fails, because the single outcome is a noisy target and the policy gradient corrupts the chain of thought. We introduce a verifiable, label-free reward, a state-conditioned empirical win rate estimated from past outcomes, that removes the label noise, and we keep the gradient off the reasoning, by direct prediction or a gradient mask, so it cannot be corrupted. Trained with this reward alone, without human labels or supervised fine-tuning, a 7B model reaches the calibration of the betting market by direct prediction and is better calibrated than a zero-shot frontier model. That frontier model and a tabular estimator reach the same Brier score as this model, identifying the market's small remaining edge as live in-game information beyond their shared inputs. Masking the gradient, rather than dropping the chain of thought, preserves reasoning from which the forecast follows, which ordinary chain-of-thought training corrupts.

</details>
