### [87] [Scaling Up Thermodynamic AI Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00170)
*Andrew G. Moore*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A scalable backpropagation-based training method for Ising machine hardware enables thermodynamic inference, achieving 94.9% on CIFAR-10 and 76.0% on CIFAR-100, with a mathematical theory relating inference cost to accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Thermodynamic computing using Ising models is promising for low-power AI inference, but scalable training methods for large models on such hardware are lacking.

Method: A purely backpropagation-based algorithm for training deep convolutional networks that leverages the theoretical correspondence between high-temperature Gibbs-sampled Ising systems and feed-forward neural inference. The method includes mathematical theory relating inference cost to accuracy, controlling autocorrelation times, and algorithms for computing optimal inference schedules.

Result: Image classification models achieved 94.9% accuracy on CIFAR-10 and 76.0% on CIFAR-100 under binary Gibbs sampling. Asymptotic analysis shows inference cost is bounded by a controllable tradeoff with performance.

Conclusion: Thermodynamic AI models trained via backpropagation for Ising hardware are viable and scalable, with well-characterized cost-accuracy tradeoffs, motivating further hardware development for high-temperature thermodynamic computing.

Abstract: Thermodynamic computing devices based on the Ising model show great promise for low-power AI inference and edge computing, but scalable methods for training large models for such hardware remain limited. Prior theory shows that the time-averaged behavior of high-temperature Gibbs-sampled Ising systems can implement feed-forward neural inference. We turn this theoretical correspondence into a scalable and purely backpropagation-based algorithm for training deep convolutional networks for thermodynamic inference on Ising machine hardware. Our image classification models achieve accuracies of 94.9% on CIFAR-10 and 76.0% on CIFAR-100 under binary Gibbs sampling. We then develop and experimentally validate a mathematical theory relating inference cost to accuracy and controlling autocorrelation times. Subsequently, we calculate asymptotic results showing that inference cost is bounded by a well-controlled tradeoff with performance and exhibit algorithms for computing optimal inference schedules. Finally, we discuss implications for hardware development and the future of high-temperature thermodynamic AI models.

</details>


### [88] [TallyTrain: Communication-Efficient Federated Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00173)
*Radhakrishna Achanta,Will Reed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TallyTrain compresses federated learning communication by transmitting only argmax class indices (log₂C bits per probe) instead of full soft labels, and combines this with sparse parameter merges. It matches or outperforms soft-label distillation with up to 1000× less communication, and its bandwidth-bridge variant Pareto-dominates FedAvg, FedProx, and FedDF baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning faces two bandwidth bottlenecks that worsen with scale: (1) large model sizes limit the frequency of parameter-averaging merges, and (2) large output vocabularies (class counts) make per-probe soft-label distillation prohibitively expensive. These constraints motivate a radically compressed communication protocol that collapses the class-count axis while preserving or improving model quality under non-IID data distributions.

Method: TallyTrain transmits only each peer's argmax class index per probe, requiring ⌈log₂C⌉ bits regardless of vocabulary size. This hard-label consensus is combined with sparse parameter merges to create a "bandwidth-bridge" variant. The approach leverages the insight that under non-IID training, confidently wrong peers are filtered out by majority voting, whereas soft-label averaging amplifies their noise.

Result: TallyTrain matches or beats soft-label distillation across standard benchmarks while using up to three orders of magnitude less communication. The bandwidth-bridge variant Pareto-dominates every tested operating point of FedAvg, FedProx, and FedDF baselines.

Conclusion: Hard-label consensus via argmax transmission is not merely a compression technique but can be preferable to soft-label distillation under non-IID conditions. Composing this with sparse parameter merges yields a protocol that dominates existing federated learning methods across the full bandwidth–accuracy trade-off spectrum.

Abstract: Federated learning is bandwidth-bound on two orthogonal axes: model size, which limits how often parameter-averaging methods can afford to merge, and class count, which makes per-probe soft-label distillation prohibitive at large vocabularies. Both ceilings tighten as modern systems scale. We collapse the class-count axis to $\lceil \log_2 C \rceil$ bits per probe by transmitting only each peer's $\arg\max$ class index, where $C$ is the number of output classes. The resulting protocol, TallyTrain, is not merely compressed: under non-IID training it can be preferable to soft-label distillation, because under-trained peers are confidently wrong and majority voting filters this noise where soft-label averaging amplifies it. Across standard benchmarks, TallyTrain matches or beats soft-label distillation at up to three orders of magnitude less communication. We also relax the model-size axis: we compose the cheap hard-label consensus with sparse parameter merges to obtain a bandwidth-bridge variant, which Pareto-dominates every tested operating point of the standard FedAvg, FedProx and FedDF baselines.

</details>


### [89] [Play Like Champions: Counterfactual Feedback Generation in Latent Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00190)
*Andrzej Białecki,Adam Mastalerz,Han Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Latent Maps of Performance, a framework that models player improvement in StarCraft II as counterfactual path generation through a learned representation space, using a Guided VAE trained on 23,305 pro replays to generate actionable improvement trajectories via four traversal strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While RL agents have achieved superhuman performance in many games, research has focused on defeating humans rather than helping them improve. Unlike chess and Go where AI aids training, RTS games like StarCraft II lack principled frameworks for translating expert knowledge into actionable feedback for human players.

Method: The authors trained a Guided Variational Autoencoder on 23,305 professional StarCraft II tournament replays to learn a latent representation of gameplay. They formulated player improvement as algorithmic recourse in this latent space and devised four counterfactual traversal strategies: linear interpolation, iterative optimal transport, density-regularized gradient ascent, and neural flow matching. These generate multi-step improvement trajectories from losing to winning profiles while staying grounded in observed expert behavior. Feedback is extracted at multiple granularities.

Result: The four traversal strategies were verified on out-of-distribution amateur replay data. Each strategy successfully generated improvement trajectories toward winning configurations while maintaining fidelity to expert behavior. A trade-off exists between the different path-finding methods.

Conclusion: There is an inherent trade-off among the proposed traversal strategies for counterfactual path generation. The authors call for future research to focus on developing model solutions specifically aimed at human player improvement rather than merely surpassing human performance.

Abstract: Recent advances in reinforcement learning have produced superhuman agents across a wide range of competitive games. As a byproduct, researchers have begun studying how these agents play, extracting behavioral representations, analyzing decision structure, and modeling the latent geometry of expert performance. However, this growing body of work has overwhelmingly focused on defeating human players rather than providing feedback, leaving a critical gap in creating model solutions to improve human players. Unlike chess and Go, where AI has become integral to player training, real-time strategy (RTS) games lack principled frameworks for translating expert knowledge into actionable feedback. We introduce Latent Maps of Performance, a framework for counterfactual path generation. We focus on StarCraft~II data to model player improvement as an algorithmic recourse within a learned representation space. As inspiration for our work, we have looked at the championship model used in sports science. We trained a Guided Variational Autoencoder model on 23,305 professional tournament replays, enabling counterfactual traversal between losing and winning gameplay profiles. To fulfill our goal, we have devised and verified four traversal strategies on out-of-distribution (OOD) data randomly sampled from a dataset of amateur replays, namely linear interpolation, iterative optimal transport, density-regularized gradient ascent, and neural flow matching, each designed to generate multi-step improvement trajectories that remain grounded in observed expert behavior while moving a player's profile toward winning configurations. Feedback is extracted at multiple granularities to support players at different stages of improvement. Finally, we conclude that there is a trade-off between the path-finding methods we employ and hope that future research will focus on developing model solutions for human improvement.

</details>


### [90] [TRIE: An Evaluation Framework for Stochastic PDE Surrogates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00196)
*Bharat Srikishan,Javier E. Santos,Nikhil Muralidhar,Charles D. Young*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRIE is an evaluation framework for stochastic PDE surrogates that tests invariant measure preservation, predictive uncertainty quality, and generative efficiency. Across stochastic Kuramoto–Sivashinsky and Kolmogorov flow equations, generative models consistently outperform pointwise-trained and approximate-uncertainty baselines, with latent generative models providing a ~12× speedup while retaining statistical fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many scientific systems exhibit intrinsic stochasticity from forcing, unresolved degrees of freedom, or noisy observations, requiring distributional rather than pointwise forecasting. Deterministic neural surrogates fail to capture statistical measures and forecast uncertainty, and existing uncertainty methods lack rigorous, multi-criteria evaluation for stochastic PDE settings.

Method: TRIE evaluates models along three axes: (1) reproducing invariant measures via Wasserstein distances on marginal/spectral/temporal statistics, (2) trustworthy predictive uncertainty via CRPS, calibration diagnostics, and rank histograms, and (3) efficient probabilistic generation. The framework is demonstrated on stochastic Kuramoto–Sivashinsky and stochastic Kolmogorov flow across 11 parameter values, comparing pointwise-trained surrogates, Monte Carlo dropout, heteroscedastic Gaussian likelihoods, and generative models (including latent generative models with automatic dimension discovery).

Result: Standard pointwise-trained surrogates produce plausible short rollouts but fail to match long-time statistical structure. Monte Carlo dropout and heteroscedastic Gaussian methods yield stochastic forecasts that are often miscalibrated and overconfident. Generative models provide the most consistent performance, accurately capturing invariant measure statistics and achieving the lowest CRPS in all probabilistic settings. Latent generative models with automatic dimension discovery retain statistical fidelity while reducing inference time by roughly 12×.

Conclusion: Generative models are the most reliable approach for stochastic PDE surrogate forecasting under TRIE criteria. Latent generative variants offer a favorable accuracy–efficiency trade-off. The TRIE framework and released benchmark aim to standardize reproducible evaluation of stochastic PDE surrogates.

Abstract: Many scientific systems exhibit uncertainty from stochastic forcing, unresolved degrees of freedom, or imperfect observations, making reliable surrogate forecasting fundamentally distributional rather than pointwise. For such systems, deterministic neural surrogates fail to capture statistical measures and forecast uncertainty. We introduce TRIE, an evaluation framework for stochastic PDE surrogates that asks whether models reproduce invariant measures, provide trustworthy predictive uncertainty, and scale to efficient probabilistic generation. We demonstrate TRIE on two stationary chaotic spatially extended SPDEs, stochastic Kuramoto--Sivashinsky and stochastic Kolmogorov flow, across 11 parameter values. Our evaluation shows that standard pointwise-trained neural surrogates can produce plausible short rollouts while failing to match long-time statistical structure. Approximate uncertainty methods such as Monte Carlo dropout and heteroscedastic Gaussian likelihoods produce stochastic forecasts, but are often miscalibrated and overconfident under temporal and spatial uncertainty diagnostics. Across these criteria, generative models provide the most consistent performance, accurately capturing invariant measure statistics and achieving the lowest CRPS in all reported probabilistic settings. Finally, we show that latent generative models with automatic dimension discovery retain much of this statistical fidelity while reducing Kolmogorov inference time by roughly $12\times$. We release our code and data at https://github.com/scailab/TRIE-SPDE-Bench to support reproducible evaluation of stochastic PDE forecasting models.

</details>


### [91] [StateFlow: Dual-State Recurrent Modeling for Long-Horizon Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00197)
*Haroon Gharwi,Yue Dai,Kai Shu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: StateFlow extends VARNN to long-horizon forecasting by using a dual-state recurrent architecture that captures both primary temporal dynamics (hidden-state trajectory) and structured prediction deviations (residual-memory trajectory). A two-stage training strategy and chunk-based decoder enable direct multi-step forecasting with competitive performance against strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon multivariate time series forecasting suffers from non-stationarity, regime shifts, and error accumulation. The original VARNN addresses variability tracking via residual-memory states but is limited to one-step regression, lacking direct multi-step forecasting capability.

Method: StateFlow uses VARNN as a dual-state recurrent backbone producing two trajectories: (1) a hidden-state trajectory for primary dynamics (trend, seasonality, level changes), and (2) a residual-memory trajectory capturing local prediction deviations via nonlinear recurrent transformation of one-step errors. A chunk-based decoder summarizes both trajectories and maps them to the future horizon. Training follows a two-stage strategy: first optimizing the VARNN encoder with a one-step base prediction objective, then training a horizon-specific decoder for direct multi-step forecasting.

Result: StateFlow achieves competitive performance on standard LTSF benchmarks against linear, recurrent, convolutional, and Transformer-based baselines, while maintaining linear recurrent encoding and a compact model design.

Conclusion: StateFlow successfully extends VARNN to long-horizon forecasting by decoupling primary dynamics from residual error signals, combining a two-stage optimization with a chunk-based decoder. The approach proves effective while remaining computationally efficient, suggesting dual-state recurrent architectures are a viable alternative to more complex models for LTSF tasks.

Abstract: Long-horizon multivariate time series forecasting (LTSF) remains challenging due to non-stationarity, regime shifts, and error accumulation. The Variability-Aware Recursive Neural Network (VARNN) is designed to track such variability by maintaining a residual-memory state driven by one-step prediction errors. However, its original formulation is limited to one-step sequence regression and does not directly support multi-step forecasting. In this work, we extend VARNN to long-horizon forecasting and introduce StateFlow, a recurrent forecasting framework that uses VARNN as a dual-state recurrent backbone to capture two complementary signals from the lookback sequence: a hidden-state trajectory representing primary temporal dynamics, including trend, seasonality, level changes, and recurring patterns, and a residual-memory trajectory representing structured local prediction deviations, driven from a nonlinear recurrent transformation of errors between one-step base predictions and observed values. A chunk-based decoder separately summarizes these trajectories and maps them to the future horizon for direct multi-step forecasting. We further employ a two-stage optimization strategy that first trains the VARNN encoder through a one-step base prediction objective to optimize the internal representations over the lookback sequence, and then trains a horizon-specific decoder for direct multi-step forecasting. Experiments on standard LTSF benchmarks show that StateFlow achieves competitive performance against strong linear, recurrent, convolutional, and Transformer-based baselines while preserving linear recurrent encoding and a compact model design.

</details>


### [92] [Device Passport: Enabling Spatio-Temporal Pretrained Models to Generalize Across Input Layouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00249)
*Geeling Chau,Ran Liu,Juri Minxha,Wenhui Cui,Erdrin Azemi,Ellen L. Zippi,Behrooz Mahasseni,Christopher M. Sandino*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Device Passport introduces a channel embedding technique using experts and mixture models that combine functional activity with metadata, improving cross-layout transfer for biosignal foundation models, particularly when pretraining and downstream layouts differ substantially.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: New device layouts suffer from scarce training data, making it impractical to train dedicated models per layout. Biosignal foundation models pretrained on large datasets could generalize, but existing channel embedding methods—relying solely on functional information or fixed positional embeddings—struggle when pretraining and downstream layouts diverge significantly.

Method: Device Passport learns a mixture-of-experts channel embedding that conditions on both each channel's functional activity and its metadata (e.g., spatial location, device type). This contrasts with prior approaches that use only one modality. The method is evaluated via controlled subset-transfer experiments and realistic transfer to ear-EEG decoding.

Result: Device Passport is competitive across all settings and outperforms the strongest learned baseline specifically in the layout-transfer regimes that motivate the work—namely, when pretraining and downstream layouts differ substantially.

Conclusion: Channel embedding design is a critical factor for successfully reusing large-scale pretrained biosignal models on novel devices, and jointly modeling functional activity with metadata via mixture models offers a promising direction.

Abstract: New device layouts pose a challenging modeling problem due to the lack of large datasets for each specific layout. Biosignal foundation models offer a plausible solution if they are able to generalize to new layouts effectively. To improve cross-layout transfer, we study how different channel embedding techniques behave when pretraining layouts differ substantially from the downstream decoding layout. We propose Device Passport, a new channel embedding technique that learns experts and mixture models that take each channel's functional activity and metadata as input. This contrasts with prior embedding methods, which typically use only functional information or only metadata to look up learned or fixed positional embeddings. Across controlled subset-transfer experiments and realistic transfer to ear-EEG, Device Passport is competitive overall and improves over the strongest learned baseline in the layout-transfer regimes that motivate this work. These results suggest that channel embedding design is a key consideration when reusing large-scale pretrained biosignal models on new devices.

</details>


### [93] [Distributionally Robust Linear Regression With Block Lewis Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00252)
*Naren Sarayu Manoj,Kumar Kshitij Patel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A fast algorithm for group distributionally robust least squares achieving (1+ε)-approximation using Õ(rank^(1/3) ε^(-2/3)) linear system solves, improving over interior point methods and matching state-of-the-art for ℓ∞ regression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for group distributionally robust (GDR) least squares (e.g., interior point methods) are computationally expensive. The goal is to develop a faster algorithm that achieves near-optimal solutions with fewer linear system solves, particularly improving performance in moderate accuracy regimes.

Method: The algorithm leverages a geometric construction based on block Lewis weights to relate the empirical GDR problem to a carefully chosen least squares problem, then applies accelerated proximal methods. The approach reduces the problem to solving Õ(min{rank(A), m}^(1/3) ε^(-2/3)) linear systems of the form AᵀBA, where B is block-diagonal.

Result: The algorithm obtains a (1+ε)-multiplicative optimal solution for GDR least squares. It improves over known interior point methods in moderate accuracy regimes and matches state-of-the-art guarantees for the ℓ∞ regression special case. It also supports smooth interpolation between average least squares loss and distributionally robust loss.

Conclusion: Block Lewis weights combined with accelerated proximal methods yield a novel, fast algorithm for GDR least squares that advances the computational state-of-the-art, with theoretical guarantees matching or improving upon prior work across different accuracy regimes.

Abstract: We present an algorithm for the group distributionally robust (GDR) least squares problem. Given $m$ groups, a parameter vector in $\mathbb{R}^d$, and stacked design matrices and responses $\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{b}$, our algorithm obtains a $(1+\varepsilon)$-multiplicative optimal solution using $\widetilde{O}(\min\{\mathsf{rank}(\mathbf{A}),m\}^{1/3}\varepsilon^{-2/3})$ linear-system-solves of matrices of the form $\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}$ for block-diagonal $\mathbf{B}$. Our technical methods follow from a recent geometric construction, block Lewis weights, that relates the empirical GDR problem to a carefully chosen least squares problem and an application of accelerated proximal methods. Our algorithm improves over known interior point methods for moderate accuracy regimes and matches the state-of-the-art guarantees for the special case of $\ell_{\infty}$ regression. We also give algorithms that smoothly interpolate between minimizing the average least squares loss and the distributionally robust loss.

</details>


### [94] [Learning dynamical systems from noisy data with Weak-form Kernel Ridge Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00257)
*Max Kreider,John Harlim,Daning Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Weak-form Kernel Ridge Regression (WKRR), a method that combines the weak formulation with kernel ridge regression to learn dynamical systems from noisy data. The weak formulation acts as a filter, improving noise robustness, and the method outperforms baselines on chaotic benchmarks (up to 64D) and real-world 15,000D fluid data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kernel ridge regression works well on clean data but struggles with noisy measurements in dynamical systems. The weak formulation has been observed to filter noise, but a principled understanding and integration with kernel methods was lacking.

Method: The authors first provide a bias-variance error decomposition to explain the filtering mechanism of the weak formulation. They then propose WKRR, which integrates the weak formulation (test-function-based integral form of differential equations) with kernel ridge regression, yielding a simple, noise-robust learning framework.

Result: WKRR is effective on both clean and noisy data, outperforming several baseline methods. It is demonstrated on chaotic benchmark systems in up to 64 dimensions and on 15,000-dimensional real-world fluid flow data.

Conclusion: The weak formulation serves as an effective noise filter when combined with kernel learning. WKRR is simple to implement, robust to noise, and scales to high-dimensional real-world dynamical systems.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of complex dynamical systems from noisy measurements remains a significant challenge in scientific computing. Kernel ridge regression learning strategies are often effective when applied to clean data, but have limited success with noisy data. Recent work has observed that a weak formulation can act to filter noisy data, and different learning strategies have achieved increased noise robustness with a weak-form framework. In this manuscript, we give an overview of the filtering mechanism behind the weak formulation and provide a bias-variance error decomposition. Using these insights, we combine a weak formulation with a kernel learning strategy to propose Weak-form Kernel Ridge Regression (WKRR) for learning dynamical systems. The proposed framework is simple to implement, effective for both clean and noisy data, and outperforms several baseline methods. We demonstrate the performance of WKRR on chaotic benchmark systems in up to 64 dimensions, as well as 15,000-dimensional real-world fluid data.

</details>


### [95] [Validating Causal Abstraction Metrics on Simulated Complex Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00267)
*Maxime Méloux,Tiago Pimentel,François Portet,Maxime Peyrard*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a benchmark of ten complex systems with ground-truth causal explanations and evaluates over thirty candidate metrics for measuring the validity of high-level abstractions. The authors find that only causal metrics incorporating faithfulness testing reliably discriminate valid from invalid abstractions, and they propose the Causal Abstraction Error (CAE) — a continuous validity metric that passes all discrimination tests and converges with as few as 30 sampled interventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Science aims to produce high-level causal explanations of complex systems, but there is no consensus on how to quantitatively measure whether such abstractions are valid. The authors seek to fill this gap by systematically comparing candidate metrics across diverse systems.

Method: The authors construct a benchmark of ten complex systems (discrete/continuous state spaces, static/dynamical regimes) with consensual ground-truth causal explanations and invalid contrastive conditions. Within a unified causal abstraction framework, they systematically evaluate over thirty metrics spanning observational, functional, information-theoretic, and causal families. They then introduce CAE, a continuous validity metric with an explicit faithfulness test over unmapped variables.

Result: Only causal metrics reliably discriminate valid from invalid abstractions, and only when they incorporate faithfulness testing over unmapped variables. CAE passes all discrimination tests across every system and converges with as few as 30 sampled interventions.

Conclusion: CAE is proposed as a general-purpose metric for discovering and validating high-level causal explanations, offering a principled, empirically grounded solution to the long-standing problem of abstraction validation.

Abstract: A central goal of science is to produce valid explanations of complex systems: high-level causal accounts that faithfully reflect the behavior of lower-level mechanisms. Yet no consensus exists on how to measure whether a proposed high-level explanation is actually valid. We introduce a benchmark of ten complex systems spanning both discrete and continuous state spaces, as well as static and dynamical regimes, each equipped with consensual ground-truth causal explanations and invalid contrastive conditions. Within a unified causal abstraction framework, we systematically evaluate over thirty candidate metrics drawn from observational, functional, information-theoretic, and causal families. Our results show that only the latter reliably discriminates valid from invalid abstractions, and only when incorporating faithfulness testing over unmapped variables. Building on these findings, we introduce the Causal Abstraction Error (CAE), a continuous validity metric with an explicit faithfulness test, which passes all discrimination tests across every system and can converge with as few as 30 sampled interventions. We offer it as a general-purpose metric for the discovery and validation of high-level explanations.

</details>


### [96] [Entropy-Regularized Probabilistic Gates for Sparse Model Discovery in Scarce-Data Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00275)
*Krishna Harsha Kovelakuntla Huthasana,Alireza Olama,Andreas Lundell*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes entropy regularization of probabilistic gates with an L0 constraint to maintain uncertainty during sparse federated optimization, preventing premature commitment to sparse support. Evaluated under data heterogeneity and partial client participation, the method consistently outperforms Fed-IHT and post-training pruning of FedAvg in both test performance and sparsity recovery on high-dimensional small-sample regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning under data heterogeneity and partial client participation is challenging, especially in the high-dimensional small-sample regime (d >> N) where sparse models are needed for communication/computational efficiency but standard magnitude-based pruning fails to explore parameter uncertainty. There is a need for a mechanism that prevents early, suboptimal commitment to a sparse support during federated training.

Method: The method uses probabilistic gates with an L0 constraint on sparsity, enabling sampling from competing sparse configurations. Entropy regularization is applied to the gate distributions to maintain uncertainty and prevent premature convergence to a single sparse support. This is studied under data heterogeneity, client participation heterogeneity, and varying sparsity levels in the federated setting.

Result: Experiments on synthetic and real-world benchmarks show consistent improvements over Federated Iterative Hard Thresholding (Fed-IHT) and pruning after dense FedAvg training, both in statistical test performance and sparsity recovery accuracy.

Conclusion: Entropy regularization of gate distributions effectively maintains uncertainty in sparse federated optimization, leading to better generalization and more accurate sparsity recovery compared to hard thresholding and post-hoc pruning baselines, particularly in high-dimensional, data-heterogeneous federated settings.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) is a distributed machine learning (ML) paradigm with collaboration among multiple clients without sharing data. FL is challenging under data heterogeneity and partial client participation. Learning sparse models is useful for communication and computational efficiency in FL, but it is especially difficult in the small-sample high-dimensional regime (d >> N) where optimization can yield parameter configurations that fail to generalize to unseen test data. While magnitude-based pruning doesn't account for uncertainty exploration in the parameter space, a formulation with probabilistic gates and an L0 constraint allows sampling from competing sparse configurations during training. In this work, we study entropy regularization of gate distributions as a mechanism to maintain uncertainty in sparse federated optimization by preventing early commitment to sparse support. We examine its impact under data heterogeneity, client participation heterogeneity, and sparsity. Experiments on synthetic and real-world benchmarks show consistent improvements over federated iterative hard thresholding (Fed-IHT) and pruning after dense federated averaging (FedAvg) training, both in statistical performance on test data and in sparsity recovery accuracy.

</details>


### [97] [Testing Frontier Large Language Models' Physics Literacy in Parallel Physical Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00276)
*Dong Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a four-stage diagnostic (induction, formulation, prediction, review) to audit LLM reasoning in unfamiliar physics frameworks. Across three counterfactual/historical physics worlds and three SOTA LLMs, composite pass rates are low (≤6/15). Key findings: qualitative vs. quantitative asymmetry, poor self-review, and non-transferable LLM-judge reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard LLM physics benchmarks rely on answer accuracy, which cannot distinguish genuine reasoning from pattern recall of familiar problems, nor reveal where reasoning breaks down. The authors seek an auditable, stage-wise diagnostic that probes reasoning inside unfamiliar physics frameworks where memorization is useless.

Method: A four-stage diagnostic protocol: (1) Induction — infer physics rules from examples; (2) Formulation — express them formally; (3) Prediction — apply to new scenarios; (4) Review — self-check prior answers. The protocol uses locked pre-registrations, fresh sessions between stages, dual-LLM judging, and a human-audit pathway. Applied across three parallel physics worlds: a counterfactual world (F=mv), Aristotelian mechanics, and "Decay World" (four-domain counterfactual). Three LLMs tested: Claude Opus 4.7, GPT-5.5, Gemini 3.1 Pro.

Result: Composite PASS rates: 6/15 for F=mv, 6/15 for Aristotelian, 0/15 for Decay World. A qualitative-vs-quantitative asymmetry emerges: models rarely predict wrong direction of change but frequently compute wrong ratios, defaulting to standard-physics relations. LLM-judge reliability does not transfer across frameworks. Stage 4 self-review is consistently weak — models incorrectly report no error in ≥2/3 of trials containing actual errors.

Conclusion: LLMs struggle to sustain coherent reasoning in unfamiliar physics frameworks, particularly on quantitative predictions. Self-review is unreliable as a corrective mechanism, and automated judging requires framework-specific validation. The auditable staged protocol offers a more granular alternative to accuracy-only benchmarks for diagnosing reasoning failures.

Abstract: Current large-language-model (LLM) physics benchmarks are usually scored by answer accuracy, which cannot distinguish genuine reasoning from recall of familiar problem patterns and reveals little about where a model's reasoning breaks down. We introduce an auditable four-stage diagnostic that evaluates whether an LLM can reason inside an unfamiliar physics framework through induction, formulation, prediction, and review. The diagnostic combines locked pre-registrations, fresh sessions between stages, dual-LLM judging, and a human-audit pathway, and we apply it to three parallel physics worlds: a single-equation counterfactual world ($F=mv$), a historical framework (Aristotelian mechanics), and a four-domain counterfactual world (Decay World). Across Claude Opus 4.7, GPT-5.5, and Gemini 3.1 Pro, the three worlds yield composite PASS rates are 6/15, 6/15, and 0/15 respectively (content $\land$ structural for $F=mv$ and Aristotelian, content axis only for Decay World where the structural axis is out of scope). The most pointed empirical pattern is a qualitative-versus-quantitative asymmetry: in Decay World, models almost never predict the wrong direction of change, but frequently compute the wrong ratio by slipping back to standard-physics relations. The protocol also surfaces two methodology findings: LLM-judge reliability does not transfer across frameworks, and Stage 4 self-review is weak in every framework, with the model's own review wrongly reporting no earlier error in at least two-thirds of the trials that actually contained one. We release the full prompts, responses, verdicts, and audit records.

</details>


### [98] [Understanding Guest Preferences and Optimizing Two-sided Marketplaces: Airbnb as an Example](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00280)
*Yufei Wu,Daniel Schmierer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Airbnb applies economic modeling and causal inference to understand guest booking behavior in response to host pricing, leveraging heterogeneity in price sensitivity to optimize pricing tools for hosts and personalize the guest experience.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Airbnb's mission is to connect guests and hosts through a sense of belonging. To fulfill this, Airbnb must help hosts set competitive prices that balance affordability for guests with higher booking rates for hosts, while also personalizing search results to match guests with suitable listings. A rigorous understanding of how price sensitivity varies across different guest segments and listing types is essential to optimize both the host-side pricing tools and the guest-side personalization algorithms.

Method: The authors combine economic modeling with causal inference techniques to estimate guest booking demand as a function of host-set prices and other factors. They explicitly model heterogeneity in price responsiveness across different guests and listings, enabling segmentation of the market by differential sensitivity to price changes.

Result: The analysis quantifies the degree to which guests respond to different price levels and reveals substantial heterogeneity in price sensitivity across guest segments and listing types. These insights identify actionable opportunities: (1) optimizing host-facing pricing tools to encourage price-setting that better equilibrates supply and demand, and (2) tailoring the guest search and recommendation experience based on individual-level responsiveness to price and other listing attributes.

Conclusion: Understanding heterogeneous guest price sensitivity through causal economic modeling enables Airbnb to simultaneously improve marketplace efficiency — by guiding hosts toward competitive pricing — and enhance personalization, thereby strengthening the connection between guests and hosts at the core of Airbnb's community-driven platform.

Abstract: Airbnb is a community based on connection and belonging -- many hosts on Airbnb are everyday people who share their worlds to provide guests with the feeling of connection and being at home; Airbnb strives to connect people and places. Among our efforts to connect guests and hosts, we provide tools to enable hosts to set competitive prices, which helps improve affordability for guests while helping hosts get more bookings. We also personalize the guest experience to show them the listings that match their needs.
  To help inform these efforts, we combine economic modeling and causal inference techniques to understand how guests book stays based on the prices hosts set, among other factors, and how that preference varies across different guests and listings. Such understanding helps us identify opportunities for Airbnb to support the marketplace and better connect guests and hosts. For example, understanding how much guests respond to different prices helps optimize the tools that we provide to hosts, in order to enable hosts to choose and set competitive prices that further balance demand and supply. As another example, understanding heterogeneity in guest preferences helps us personalize the guest experience and better match them with the listings that meet their needs, based on how much they respond to different prices and other factors.

</details>


### [99] [EPC: A Standardized Protocol for Measuring Evaluator Preference Dynamics in LLM Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00297)
*Zewen Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces EPC (Evaluator Preference Coupling), an RFC-style standardized protocol for measuring and reproducing how evaluator biases propagate into LLM agent strategy distributions in closed-loop systems. It includes a versioned Reference Snapshot v1.0 with coupling measurements across eight evaluator conditions and provides open-source protocol specifications, implementation code, and usage guidelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work has empirically documented evaluator preference coupling across multiple evaluator families and model versions, but the field lacks a standardized, reproducible measurement protocol. Proprietary evaluators silently update over time, causing measurement decay, and there is no mechanism for third-party researchers to (i) reproduce coupling measurements, (ii) compare results across evaluators and time points, or (iii) detect when measurements become obsolete due to evaluator updates.

Method: The authors specify EPC, a detailed four-phase isolation paradigm protocol covering: executor/evaluator configuration, strategy and task design, the TTRL (Task-Tuned Reinforcement Learning) update rule, and metric computation (gamma, Jensen-Shannon Divergence, Expected Calibration Error, Brier score). They accompany this with a versioned Reference Snapshot v1.0 containing coupling measurements for eight evaluator conditions (N=122 unique experimental repetitions across GPT-4o, Qwen, DeepSeek, and others), derived from five independent studies, annotated with evaluator version identifiers, API endpoints, and measurement dates. A versioning convention (vX.Y-Z) is defined to encode protocol version, snapshot version, and evaluator generation.

Result: The paper delivers the protocol specification and Reference Snapshot v1.0 as concrete artifacts. The snapshot explicitly acknowledges time-bound validity: all values are conditional on specific model versions and are expected to decay as proprietary evaluators update.

Conclusion: The EPC protocol, reference snapshot, and implementation code are released as open infrastructure, providing the research community with a standardized foundation for reproducible measurement, cross-evaluator comparison, and decay detection in evaluator preference coupling studies.

Abstract: When LLM agents use evaluator feedback to adapt their behavior in closed loops, evaluator biases propagate through the agent's strategy distribution -- a phenomenon known as evaluator preference coupling. Prior work has documented coupling across multiple evaluator families and model versions, but the field lacks a standardized protocol that enables third-party researchers to (i) reproduce coupling measurements, (ii) compare results across evaluators and time points, and (iii) detect measurement decay as proprietary evaluators silently update. This paper provides the protocol. We specify EPC (Evaluator Preference Coupling) -- a detailed, RFC-style protocol specification for the four-phase isolation paradigm, covering executor and evaluator configuration, strategy and task design, the TTRL update rule, metric computation (gamma, JSD, ECE, Brier), and output schema. We accompany the protocol with a versioned Reference Snapshot v1.0: coupling measurements for eight evaluator conditions (N=122 unique experimental repetitions across GPT-4o, Qwen, DeepSeek, and others) derived from five independent studies, annotated with evaluator version identifiers, API endpoints, and measurement dates. The snapshot is explicitly time-bound: all values are conditional on specific model versions and are expected to decay as proprietary evaluators update. We define a versioning convention (vX.Y-Z, encoding protocol version, snapshot version, and evaluator generation) and provide a usage guide covering adoption, interpretation, and known pitfalls. The protocol, reference snapshot, and implementation code are released as open infrastructure.

</details>


### [100] [Generative Modeling of Quantum Distribution with Functional Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00301)
*Jaehoon Hahm,Tak Hur,Joonseok Lee,Daniel K. Park*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Quantum Flow Matching (QFM) is a novel generative model that learns quantum distributions by combining spin Wigner functions with flow matching, enabling accurate modeling of multi-qubit quantum states and their physical properties such as trace, purity, and entanglement entropy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning quantum distributions is challenging because existing deep generative models (diffusion and flow matching) struggle to accurately capture the meaningful physical properties of quantum states. There is a need for a method that can faithfully model quantum distributions while preserving their underlying physics.

Method: QFM operates by first converting the density matrix into its spin Wigner function representation (a phase-space quasi-probability distribution). It then applies functional flow matching to learn distributions directly in function space, rather than in the original state representation, enabling accurate learning of multi-qubit quantum distributions.

Result: The method demonstrates effectiveness by generating quantum states whose physical quantities — including trace, purity, and entanglement entropy — accurately match those of the target quantum distributions, confirming that the underlying physics is faithfully captured.

Conclusion: QFM provides an accurate and effective framework for learning quantum distributions by integrating spin Wigner functions with flow matching, successfully preserving essential physical properties of the modeled quantum states.

Abstract: The emergence of powerful deep generative models based on diffusion and flow matching has enabled the learning and modeling of complex distributions. Learning quantum distributions, however, remains challenging due to the inherent difficulty of accurately modeling the meaningful physical properties of quantum states. We propose Quantum Flow Matching (QFM), a novel generative model designed to learn quantum distribution by utilizing spin Wigner function and flow matching. By converting density matrix into the spin Wigner function and leveraging functional flow matching to learn distributions in function space, QFM enables accurate and effective learning of multi-qubit quantum distributions. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method by evaluating physical quantities such as trace, purity, and entanglement entropy of the generated quantum states, accurately capturing the underlying physics of the given quantum distributions.

</details>


### [101] [Mapping the Evaluation Frontier: An Empirical Survey of the Bias-Reliability Tradeoff Across Eleven Evaluator-Agent Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00304)
*Zewen Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper empirically validates the bias-reliability tradeoff in LLM evaluation systems across 11 conditions, confirming that evaluator coupling (gamma), strategy diversity (H), and measurement reliability (CV) cannot be simultaneously optimized at fixed sample size N=5.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior evidence for the bias-reliability tradeoff rested on only n=5 conditions from a single study. The authors seek to expand the empirical base to strengthen confidence in the conjecture and identify whether any condition can escape the tradeoff constraints.

Method: The study expands the empirical base from 5 to 11 conditions, measuring evaluator coupling (gamma), strategy diversity (H), and small-sample measurement reliability CV(N=5). Nine conditions yielded valid weight vectors for gamma and H; seven had sufficient seeds (N≥5) for CV computation; five provided the complete (gamma, H, CV) triple. Correlation analysis and region-mapping were used to assess the tradeoff.

Result: The tradeoff is confirmed: low-coupling conditions (gamma < 0.2) exhibit high measurement noise (CV > 1.0), while high-coupling conditions (gamma > 0.9) achieve low noise (CV < 0.16). Correlation r(H, gamma) = -0.989 confirms that evaluator coupling suppresses strategy diversity. No condition occupies the desirable region {gamma < 0.2, CV < 0.3}. Four GPT-4o conditions show gamma=0.000, H=1.000 — attributed to API version drift.

Conclusion: The bias-reliability tradeoff holds robustly across an expanded set of conditions. Evaluator coupling and measurement reliability are fundamentally at odds at small sample sizes, and GPT-4o's anomalous behavior highlights API stability as a confounding factor. The authors release all per-condition metrics as a standardized benchmark dataset.

Abstract: The bias-reliability tradeoff conjectures that LLM evaluation systems are constrained in (gamma, H, CV) space, where evaluator coupling (gamma), strategy diversity (H), and small-sample measurement reliability (CV(N)) cannot be simultaneously optimized at fixed sample size N. Prior evidence rests on n=5 conditions with complete metrics from a single study. We expand the empirical base to 11 conditions, measuring gamma and H for all 11 (nine with valid weight vectors) and CV(N=5) for seven with sufficient seeds (N >= 5). Five conditions provide the complete (gamma, H, CV) triple. The data confirm the trade-off: conditions with low evaluator coupling (gamma < 0.2) exhibit high measurement noise (CV(N=5) > 1.0), while conditions with strong coupling (gamma > 0.9) achieve low noise (CV(N=5) < 0.16). The correlation r(H, gamma) = -0.989 (n=5, excluding GPT-4o conditions) confirms that evaluator coupling suppresses strategy diversity. Four GPT-4o conditions show gamma=0.000 and H=1.000 across all seeds -- a pattern we attribute to version drift in the June 2026 GPT-4o API. No condition occupies the region {gamma < 0.2, CV(N=5) < 0.3}. We release all per-condition metrics as a standardized benchmark dataset for evaluator comparison.

</details>


### [102] [Watermarking for Proprietary Dataset Protection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00325)
*John Kirchenbauer,Brian R. Bartoldson,Bhavya Kailkhura,Tom Goldstein*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes using output watermarking techniques to make training data membership inference more tractable for generative language models, leveraging the "radioactivity" phenomenon where watermarked outputs persist through training. The authors benchmark watermark-based dataset inference against traditional loss-based membership inference, finding comparable performance under sufficient subset exposure but with a different assumption set.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training data membership inference is known to be fundamentally hard in modern LM settings. Prior work has shown that LMs exhibit residual watermark "radioactivity" when trained on partially watermarked datasets. The authors hypothesize that watermarking can serve as a more tractable alternative to traditional loss-based methods for membership detection.

Method: The authors conduct a head-to-head empirical comparison between watermark-based dataset inference and traditional loss-based membership inference methods. The watermarking approach exploits the property that watermarked training samples leave detectable residual traces ("radioactivity") in the trained model's outputs, enabling membership tests under a different set of assumptions than loss-based approaches.

Result: Watermark-based dataset inference achieves comparable membership detection performance to traditional loss-based methods when the subset exposure (proportion of watermarked data in training) is sufficiently high. The two approaches operate under alternate sets of assumptions, suggesting watermarking provides a viable complementary path to membership inference.

Conclusion: Output watermarking is a promising gadget for making membership inference more tractable in generative settings. While not strictly superior to loss-based methods, it offers comparable performance under different operational assumptions, broadening the toolkit for dataset inference and auditing.

Abstract: A growing body of literature suggests that training data membership inference problems are fundamentally hard tasks in modern language modeling settings. We argue that output watermarking techniques are the right gadget to make training membership tests for generative models more tractable, based on prior results showing that language models exhibit residual watermark "radioactivity" under partially watermarked training datasets. We pit a watermark-based dataset inference approach head-to-head against traditional loss-based membership inference methods and show that watermarking can achieve comparable membership detection performance when subset exposure is high enough, under an alternate set of assumptions.

</details>


### [103] [K-Inverse-RFM: A Modified RFM that Bridges the Gap to Neural Networks for Data-Corrupted Mathematical Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00329)
*Gil Pasternak*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Recursive Feature Machines (RFMs) lag behind neural networks in data-corrupted settings. The authors introduce a label transformation that enables RFMs to match or surpass FNN performance on noisy, complex, and class-imbalanced data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite RFMs replicating the feature learning dynamics of feedforward neural networks, they underperform FNNs in data-corrupted scenarios (noise, complex representations, class imbalance). The work seeks to diagnose and overcome this performance gap.

Method: The authors analyze RFM limitations in mathematical problem settings and propose a novel, simple transformation applied to the training labels. This transformation promotes robust learning under corruption without altering the core AGOP-based kernel mechanism.

Result: The proposed label transformation enables RFMs to close the performance gap with feedforward neural networks and, in certain cases, even surpass them on corrupted data.

Conclusion: A well-designed label transformation is a remarkably effective and simple remedy for RFM vulnerability to data corruption, restoring competitiveness with neural networks.

Abstract: Recursive Feature Machines (RFMs) are a class of kernel machines that utilize the Average Gradient Outer Product (AGOP) as a mechanism for feature learning. They have been shown to effectively replicate the learning dynamics and feature representations of Feedforward Neural Networks (FNNs) across various settings. However, despite comparable capacity for feature learning and the similarities in the features they acquire, RFMs exhibit significantly lower performance than neural networks in certain data-corrupted scenarios. In this work, we investigate these limitations in mathematical problems. As a solution, we introduce a remarkably effective transformation applied to the training labels which promotes learning in noisy, complexly represented, and class-imbalanced data. This simple yet powerful adjustment enables RFMs to close the performance gap with FNNs and, in some cases, even surpass them.

</details>


### [104] [PRISM: Prioritized Channel Importance with Semi-supervised Domain Adaptation for Cross-Subject EEG Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00358)
*Xin Zhou,Xiang Zhang,Hao Deng,Lijun Yin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PRISM is a semi-supervised domain adaptation framework for EEG emotion decoding that uses learnable channel weighting and pseudo-label-driven consistency regularization to achieve robust cross-subject generalization with limited labeled data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG-based emotion decoding suffers from two major challenges: (1) channel redundancy — not all EEG electrodes contribute equally, and many serve as distractors; (2) pronounced inter-subject variability that hinders scalable generalization across individuals. Existing methods require abundant labeled data per subject, which is impractical.

Method: PRISM consists of two complementary components: (1) a channel-wise expert ensemble that learns differentiable, data-dependent importance weights for each EEG electrode, amplifying informative channels and suppressing noisy ones; (2) a semi-supervised domain adaptation module that generates confidence-filtered pseudo-labels on unlabeled target-subject data, then enforces consistency regularization and domain alignment to reduce inter-subject distribution shifts.

Result: PRISM surpasses state-of-the-art methods on three benchmark datasets — DEAP, DREAMER, and SEED — demonstrating superior cross-subject emotion decoding performance under limited annotation regimes.

Conclusion: The proposed PRISM framework effectively addresses both channel redundancy and inter-subject variability through prioritized channel importance and semi-supervised domain adaptation, enabling practical, label-efficient EEG emotion decoding that generalizes well across subjects.

Abstract: Electroencephalogram (EEG) captures endogenous brain activity with high temporal fidelity and holds substantial promise for precise emotion decoding. However, channel redundancy and pronounced inter-subject variability remain key obstacles to scalable generalization. To address these limitations, we propose a novel framework termed PRioritized channel Importance with Semi-supervised doMain adaptation (PRISM), enabling label-efficient cross-subject emotion decoding. On the channel side, PRISM assigns differentiable, data-dependent channel weights via a lightweight expert ensemble, amplifying reliable electrodes while suppressing distractors. On the domain side, PRISM leverages unlabeled data through confidence-filtered pseudo-labels to drive consistency regularization and domain alignment, mitigating subject-specific heterogeneity. Extensive experiments show that PRISM surpasses state-of-the-art methods on DEAP, DREAMER, and SEED datasets, achieving robust cross-subject generalization given limited annotations.

</details>


### [105] [SAOT: Self-Supervised Continual Graph Learning with Structure-Aware Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00377)
*Yuting Zhang,Yanbei Liu,Zhitao Xiao,Lei Geng,Yanwei Pang,Xiao Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAOT proposes a Structure-Aware Optimal Transport framework for self-supervised continual graph learning that preserves global relational structure across sequential tasks, outperforming instance-level consistency methods by up to 15% on benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-supervised CGL methods use instance-level consistency objectives that optimize node embeddings in isolation, which fails to maintain global relational structure and causes progressive distortion of inter-node correspondences during continual learning.

Method: SAOT leverages optimal transport theory to capture global inter-node correspondences for enhanced graph representation learning, combined with a cross-task knowledge distillation mechanism to preserve structural knowledge from previous tasks.

Result: SAOT outperforms existing self-supervised baselines on four CGL benchmarks, achieving up to 5% improvement on CoraFull-CL and over 15% on Products-CL in the Class-IL setting compared to state-of-the-art methods.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling and preserving global relational structure via optimal transport is an effective approach for self-supervised continual graph learning, addressing a fundamental limitation of instance-level consistency methods.

Abstract: Self-supervised Continual Graph Learning (CGL) aims to successively learn from a graph sequence with different tasks without label supervision - a paradigm that has attracted widespread attention. Most existing self-supervised CGL methods rely on instance-level consistency objectives that enforce stability of individual node (or node-pair) embeddings. Due to optimizing nodes in isolation, these methods fail to maintain global relational structure, causing inter-node correspondences to progressively distort under continual learning. To this end, we propose a novel Structure-Aware Optimal Transport (SAOT) framework that explicitly captures and preserves relational structure within graph representations across sequential tasks. Specifically, SAOT leverages optimal transport theory to capture global inter-node correspondences, thereby facilitating and enhancing graph representation learning. Simultaneously, SAOT incorporates a cross-task knowledge distillation mechanism to preserve the previous structural knowledge. Extensive experiments on four CGL benchmark datasets demonstrate that SAOT outperforms existing self-supervised baselines. In particular, SAOT achieves significant performance gains, improving average accuracy by up to 5% on CoraFull-CL and over 15% on Products-CL compared with state-of-the-art methods in the Class-IL setting.

</details>


### [106] [Learning Generalizable Skill Policy with Data-Efficient Unsupervised RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00392)
*Jongchan Park,Seungjun Oh,Seungho Baek,Yusung Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GenDa is a unified unsupervised RL framework that addresses non-stationary skill semantics and brittle generalization via skill relabeling and a Complementary Information Bottleneck, achieving improved data efficiency and robustness for downstream tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current off-policy unsupervised RL methods suffer from two overlooked bottlenecks: (1) non-stationary skill semantics during pre-training, and (2) brittle generalization to downstream tasks, both of which limit scalability and data efficiency.

Method: GenDa introduces two key components: (1) a skill relabeling mechanism that mitigates non-stationarity in skill semantics, improving pre-training data efficiency; (2) a Complementary Information Bottleneck (CIB) that encourages the skill policy to prioritize ego-centric features, making it robust to distribution shifts in downstream tasks.

Result: Experiments demonstrate that GenDa significantly enhances the scalability of unsupervised RL, achieving superior generalizability and data efficiency compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: GenDa provides a unified and robust solution for unsupervised RL pre-training by addressing key bottlenecks, enabling more scalable, data-efficient, and generalizable skill-conditioned policies for downstream control tasks.

Abstract: Unsupervised Reinforcement Learning (URL) aims to pre-train scalable, skill-conditioned policies without extrinsic rewards, serving as a foundation for downstream control tasks. Despite recent progress, we argue that current off-policy URL methods are limited by two critical, overlooked bottlenecks: (1) non-stationary skill semantics and (2) brittle generalization. To address these challenges, we propose GenDa (Generalizable Data-efficient Agent), a unified framework for robust unsupervised reinforcement learning. First, we introduce a skill relabeling mechanism to mitigate non-stationarity and significantly improve data efficiency for pre-training. Second, we propose a Complementary Information Bottleneck (CIB), encouraging the learned skill policy to focus on ego-centric features and become robust to distribution shifts for downstream tasks. Through various experiments, we demonstrate that GenDa significantly enhances the scalability of URL with superior generalizability and data efficiency. Our code and videos are available at https://ihatebroccoli.github.io/official-GenDa.

</details>


### [107] [Timesynth: A Temporal Fidelity Framework for Health Signal Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00431)
*Md Rakibul Haque,Shireen Elhabian,Warren Woodrich Pettine*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Standard pointwise error metrics fail to detect when forecasting models destroy critical physiological signal dynamics (frequency, phase, state transitions). The authors introduce TimeSynth, a benchmarking framework with a parametric physiological signal generator and fidelity diagnostics, revealing that architectural choice—not aggregate error—determines dynamical preservation, with localized-temporal architectures outperforming attention-based models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Health-signal digital twins require forecasting models that preserve oscillatory, frequency, phase, and state-transition dynamics. However, standard pointwise error metrics (e.g., MSE, MAE) are blind to these properties, creating a dangerous evaluation gap where models with comparable error scores can exhibit vastly different dynamical fidelity—up to 53° phase error (~123 ms at 1.2 Hz), which is clinically significant yet invisible to conventional benchmarking.

Method: The authors propose TimeSynth, a controlled benchmarking framework with two components: (1) a physiologically grounded parametric signal generator fitted to real EEG, ECG, and PPG signals that produces synthetic data with analytically known ground-truth dynamics (frequency, phase, amplitude, state transitions); (2) a suite of diagnostics that quantify fidelity along four axes—amplitude, frequency, phase, and state-transition accuracy. Eleven forecasting architectures are evaluated under this framework.

Result: Linear and full-sequence attention models systematically lose frequency and phase information despite maintaining acceptable amplitude error. Architectures with localized temporal structure (e.g., convolutional, state-space models) better preserve dynamical fidelity and adapt to observable state transitions. No architecture reliably preserves stochastic switching dynamics. Architectural choice is the dominant determinant of fidelity, meaning model selection should be use-case-driven rather than a search for a universally optimal model.

Conclusion: TimeSynth fills a critical gap as a preclinical stress test for health-signal forecasting models, enabling fidelity-aware model development and principled, use-case-driven architectural selection before deployment on patient data. The reusable generator and diagnostics support ongoing development of models that preserve clinically meaningful signal dynamics.

Abstract: Forecasting models for health-signal digital twins must preserve the oscillatory, frequency, phase, and state-transition dynamics of physiological signals, yet the pointwise metrics used to benchmark them cannot detect when these fundamental properties are lost. We show that this blind spot misranks models: across 11 architectures, models with comparable pointwise error diverge by up to 53° in phase accuracy, equivalent to roughly 123 ms for a 1.2 Hz cardiac rhythm and invisible to standard metrics. To enable development of models that escape such failures, we introduce TimeSynth, a controlled benchmarking framework with two reusable components: a physiologically grounded generator producing signals with analytically known ground-truth dynamics from parametric models fitted to real electroencephalography, electrocardiography and photoplethysmogram signals, along with diagnostics quantifying amplitude, frequency, phase, and state-transition fidelity. Linear and full-sequence attention models systematically lose frequency and phase information despite acceptable amplitude error, whereas architectures with localized temporal structure better preserve dynamical fidelity and adapt to observable state transitions; none, however, reliably preserves stochastic switching. Because the dominant determinant of fidelity is architectural, model choice becomes a principled, use-case-driven decision rather than a search for a single winner. TimeSynth thus supplies the controlled preclinical stress test missing before models are coupled to patient data, with a reusable generator and diagnostics for fidelity-aware development.

</details>


### [108] [Gauging, Measuring, and Controlling Critic Complexity in Actor-Critic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00452)
*Konstantin Garbers*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces "critic complexity" — measured via spectral effective-rank entropy of critic weight matrices — as a novel diagnostic dimension for actor-critic RL. Tracking this alongside return and value-estimation bias in TD3 and PPO experiments reveals systematic but heterogeneous associations with training behavior. A spectral-entropy penalty on the critic loss is shown to directly control critic complexity, with return effects treated as task-dependent rather than universally beneficial.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Critic quality in actor-critic RL is typically evaluated only indirectly (via return, TD error, or value loss). The authors argue that these metrics fail to capture the internal representational complexity of the critic network, motivating the need for an additional diagnostic dimension — critic complexity — to better understand and potentially intervene on critic learning dynamics.

Method: Critic complexity is operationalized using spectral effective-rank entropy, which summarizes the singular-value distribution of critic weight matrices. This metric is tracked alongside return and Monte Carlo value-estimation bias across experiments with TD3 and PPO. A direct intervention is then tested: augmenting the critic loss with a spectral-entropy penalty term to actively control critic complexity during training.

Result: Critic complexity is measurable throughout training and systematically associated with training behavior. However, the relationship between complexity and performance is heterogeneous — varying across algorithms (TD3 vs. PPO), tasks, and hyperparameters. The spectral-entropy penalty reliably alters the targeted spectral quantity, confirming that critic complexity can be controlled. Return effects of this intervention are task-dependent rather than uniformly positive or negative.

Conclusion: Critic complexity serves as a viable additional diagnostic dimension for actor-critic RL, complementing traditional metrics. It can be both passively monitored and actively controlled via a spectral-entropy penalty. The heterogeneity of results cautions against universal claims about complexity-control benefits, instead framing return effects as evidence contingent on the specific algorithmic and task context.

Abstract: Actor-critic methods depend on learned critics, but critic quality is often evaluated only indirectly through return, temporal-difference error, or value loss. Critic complexity is introduced as an additional diagnostic and intervention dimension for actor-critic reinforcement learning. The analysis uses spectral effective-rank entropy, a rank-like summary of the singular-value distributions of critic weight matrices, to assess critic model complexity. Across TD3 and PPO experiments, critic complexity is tracked together with return and Monte Carlo value-estimation bias. The results show that critic complexity is measurable throughout training and is systematically associated with training behavior, while also making clear that the relationship is heterogeneous across algorithms, tasks, and hyperparameters. A direct complexity-control intervention is then evaluated by adding a spectral-entropy penalty to the critic loss. This intervention reliably changes the targeted spectral quantity, demonstrating that critic complexity can be controlled rather than only observed. Return effects are treated as task-dependent evidence rather than as a general performance claim, because overall complexity-control results vary.

</details>


### [109] [MolSafeEval: A Benchmark for Uncovering Safety Risks in AI-Generated Molecules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00464)
*Tong Xu,Xinzhe Cao,Zhihui Zhu,Keyan Ding,Huajun Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MolSafeEval introduces a safety-focused benchmark for molecular generation that leverages a molecular safety knowledge graph and LLM-based reasoning to systematically detect and explain unsafe features in AI-generated molecules across four task types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current molecular generation benchmarks emphasize complexity, novelty, and property alignment but critically overlook safety risks. Many generative models inadvertently produce toxic, reactive, or hazardous molecules, posing hidden dangers that remain insufficiently addressed by existing evaluation frameworks that rely on narrow toxicity predictors.

Method: MolSafeEval integrates heterogeneous safety knowledge from toxicological databases and hazard rules into a structured molecular safety knowledge graph. This graph underpins LLM-based reasoning for systematic detection and explanation of unsafe molecular features. The benchmark further categorizes generative models into four task types — unconditional generation, property optimization, target protein-based design, and text-based generation — each with standardized datasets and safety evaluation protocols.

Result: The benchmark systematically reveals safety vulnerabilities across current molecular generative approaches, demonstrating that existing models frequently produce molecules with hazardous characteristics that prior evaluation frameworks fail to capture.

Conclusion: MolSafeEval provides a new lens for benchmarking molecular models with an emphasis on safety, offering essential guidance toward safer and more trustworthy molecular design. It fills a critical gap by making safety evaluation a first-class concern in molecular generation research.

Abstract: Current molecular generation benchmarks emphasize task complexity, molecule novelty, and property alignment; they largely overlook a critical concern: the potential safety risks of AI-generated molecules. In practice, many generative models may produce molecules with toxic, reactive, or otherwise hazardous characteristics - posing hidden dangers that remain insufficiently addressed. To address this gap, we introduce MolSafeEval, a benchmark dedicated to evaluating and analyzing the safety risks of molecular generation. Unlike prior approaches that rely on narrow toxicity predictors, MolSafeEval integrates heterogeneous safety knowledge - ranging from toxicological databases to hazard rules - into a structured molecular safety knowledge graph. This graph serves as a foundation for large language model-based reasoning, enabling systematic detection and explanation of unsafe features in generated compounds. We further categorize molecular generative models into four representative task types - unconditional generation, property optimization, target protein-based design, and text-based generation - and provide standardized datasets and safety evaluation protocols for each. By systematically revealing the safety vulnerabilities of current generative approaches, MolSafeEval offers a new lens for benchmarking molecular models and provides essential guidance toward safer, more trustworthy molecular design.

</details>


### [110] [How Early Is Early Enough? Design-Dependent Observation-Window Sufficiency in Subscription Churn Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00473)
*Xiao Han,Yao Xiao,Chenyu Wu,Tongchen Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates how many days of early behavioral data are sufficient for predicting subscription churn. Using the KKBox music-streaming dataset, it finds a diminishing-returns knee at 45–90 days, but demonstrates that this sufficiency curve is highly sensitive to cohort construction, target definition, and feature families — cautioning against universal window-sufficiency claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Churn prediction typically relies on contract-status indicators, especially in auto-renewal contexts. However, in manual-renewal segments with heavy churn, early behavioral signals may substantially improve prediction. The paper aims to determine how much early behavioral data is "enough" for churn prediction and whether the answer generalizes across different experimental designs.

Method: Using the public KKBox dataset, the authors construct a nine-window sufficiency curve plotting predictive performance (PR/precision-recall) against increasing days of early behavioral data. They then stress-test this curve across three distinct cohort/task designs, including a moving-target setup, and compare performance across different feature families (contract-based vs. behavioral).

Result: For the manual-renewal segment, early behavioral data yields a PR gain of +0.10 at 120 days. The sufficiency curve exhibits a diminishing-returns knee in the 45–90 day band. However, stress-testing reveals the curve is design-dependent: under a moving-target setup, the curve inverts, and its shape shifts with different feature sets. Thus, no single sufficiency window holds across all designs.

Conclusion: Any claim about the sufficiency of early behavioral windows for churn prediction must explicitly state the cohort construction, target definition, and feature families used. The mechanism should generalize beyond the music-streaming domain, though exact magnitudes may not transfer.

Abstract: How many days of early behavior suffice for subscription churn prediction? In the public KKBox dataset, the early indicator of churn is typically an indicator of someone's contract status; however, when looking in the heavily churned manual-renewal segment, having access to early behavior creates a substantial increase in prediction for that specific segment (PR +0.10 at 120 days). A nine-window sufficiency curve shows a diminishing-returns knee in a 45-90 day band. However, stress-testing over three cohort/task designs shows that this curve is singular to the design being tested; for example, in our test with a moving target, the curve inverts and can shift depending on the feature set used. Therefore, any window-sufficiency claim should state its cohort construction, target definition, and feature families. All evidence is from one music-streaming dataset; the mechanism should generalize but the magnitudes may not.

</details>


### [111] [Interpretable vs Learned Encoders for High-Cardinality Fraud Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00477)
*Xiao Han,Jingjing Liu,Moxuan Zheng,Zhen Zhang,Chenyu Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Seven categorical encoding methods were benchmarked on the IEEE-CIS fraud dataset using stratified 5-fold CV. Entity embeddings achieved the highest AUC-ROC (0.9612), statistically tied with CatBoost (0.9602) and superior to tier group encoding (0.9548). On AUC-PR, CatBoost led (0.822 vs. 0.793). TabNet underperformed tree-based pipelines, especially under data scarcity. The embedding advantage stems from joint multi-column representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically compare categorical encoding methods on a large-scale, real-world fraud detection dataset with high-cardinality categorical features, using controlled experimental conditions to isolate encoder performance from downstream learner effects.
