Method: Seven categorical encoding methods were tested on the IEEE-CIS fraud benchmark dataset (590,540 records, 3.5% positive class, 8 high-cardinality columns). Five encoders used identical frozen LightGBM downstream learners for controlled comparison; CatBoost and TabNet served as cross-paradigm baselines with different learners. Evaluation employed stratified 5-fold cross-validation with three repetitions, measuring AUC-ROC and AUC-PR, with statistical significance testing and per-column analysis.

Result: Entity embeddings achieved the highest AUC-ROC (0.9612), statistically tied with CatBoost (0.9602) and significantly outperforming tier group encoding (0.9548). Target encoding trailed tier group encoding by only 0.0023 while preserving auditor-friendly tier boundaries. TabNet failed to surpass tree-based pipelines and collapsed under data scarcity. On AUC-PR, CatBoost led (0.822 vs. 0.793 for embeddings); no single encoder dominated both metrics. Per-column analysis confirmed embeddings' advantage derives from joint multi-column representation learning.

Conclusion: Entity embeddings and CatBoost are top performers for high-cardinality categorical encoding in fraud detection, with embeddings excelling on AUC-ROC and CatBoost on AUC-PR. The negligible gap between target encoding and tier group encoding supports using auditor-friendly tier boundaries with minimal performance loss. TabNet is not recommended for this task under data-scarce conditions.

Abstract: A total of seven categorical encoding methods were tested on the IEEE-CIS fraud benchmark dataset (590,540 records, 3.5% positives, 8 high-cardinality columns). The encoders were evaluated using a stratified 5-fold cross-validation (CV) with three repetitions. Five of the encoders had identical frozen LightGBM learners in the downstream phase, allowing for controlled comparisons of their performance to each other. CatBoost and TabNet were included as comparisons across paradigms using different learners. The entity embeddings produced the highest AUC-ROC (0.9612), with a statistically significant tie with that of CatBoost (0.9602) and statistically superior to tier group encoding (0.9548), whereas target encoding was only 0.0023 worse than tier group encoding and the auditor-friendly tier boundaries were maintained. Off-the-shelf TabNet did not outperform tree-based pipelines and collapsed under data scarcity. On AUC-PR, CatBoost leads (0.822 vs. 0.793); no encoder dominated both metrics. Per-column analysis confirmed the embedding advantage arises from joint multi-column representation.

</details>


### [112] [Ghost in the Kernel: In-Context Learning with Efficient Transformers via Domain Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00479)
*Peilin Liu,Ding-Xuan Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides a theoretical analysis of linear transformers' in-context learning abilities through the lens of domain generalization, yielding a dimension-independent convergence rate that reveals tradeoffs between data distribution regularities and latent features. The theory guides a novel activation and loss design for linearizing pretrained softmax LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Softmax transformers suffer from quadratic computational/memory complexity w.r.t. context length. Linear transformers reduce this to linear complexity, but the theoretical understanding of feature mapping design in linear attention remains unclear, particularly regarding approximation and generalization in in-context learning settings.

Method: The authors analyze linear transformers under a two-staged sampling process from domain generalization. They frame in-context learning as learning a mapping from context distributions to response functions, then derive a dimension-independent convergence rate for generalization error bounds. The theoretical framework is then applied to guide activation function and loss function design for linearizing pretrained softmax LLMs.

Result: A dimension-independent convergence rate is established for the generalization analysis, which explicitly characterizes the tradeoff between data distribution regularities and latent feature properties. The theoretical insights lead to a principled approach for designing activations and losses when linearizing pretrained softmax models.

Conclusion: Linear transformers provably perform in-context learning as distribution-to-response mapping, with generalization guarantees independent of dimension. This theoretical understanding provides actionable design principles for converting pretrained softmax transformers into efficient linearized variants.

Abstract: Transformer-based large models have demonstrated remarkable generalization abilities across different tasks by leveraging a context-aware attention module for in-context learning. With richer context, transformers adapt more effectively to the current use case without any parameter updates. However, the quadratic computational and memory complexity with respect to context length significantly slows data processing in softmax transformers. Linear transformers were proposed to address this issue by reducing the complexity to linear dependence on context length, but the design and understanding of the feature mapping in linear attention, from a theoretical viewpoint, remain unclear. In this paper, we investigate the approximation and generalization abilities of linear transformers under a two-staged sampling process from domain generalization. We show that linear transformers perform in-context learning as learning a mapping from context distributions to response functions. A dimension-independent convergence rate is obtained for our generalization analysis, which also exhibits the tradeoff between the regularities of data distributions and latent features. Guided by our theoretical framework, we propose a new perspective on activation and loss design for linearizing pretrained softmax large language models.

</details>


### [113] [PAPA: Online Personalized Active Preference Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00486)
*Anindya Sarkar,Nasik Muhammad Nafi,Isaac Lyngaas,Muralikrishnan Gopalakrishnan Meena,Yevgeniy Vorobeychik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PAPA is a method for fine-tuning diffusion models to user preferences without needing a pre-trained reward model. It uses real-time interactive feedback within a variational inference framework for feedback-efficient preference alignment, with an enhanced variant (EPAPA) that reduces computational cost and accelerates fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In personalized recommender systems, the goal is to model specific high-preference regions of a data distribution, which can be framed as RL-based fine-tuning of diffusion models. However, learning a parameterized reward model requires large-scale preference data that is often infeasible to collect in practice. The authors aim to bypass this bottleneck.

Method: PAPA directly optimizes the diffusion model using real-time user feedback instead of a parameterized reward model, drawing on variational inference. An enhanced variant, EPAPA, is proposed based on theoretical insights to reduce computational budget and accelerate fine-tuning.

Result: Extensive experiments and ablation studies demonstrate PAPA's effectiveness across diverse class-conditioned and fine-grained alignment tasks. EPAPA further improves computational efficiency, enhancing suitability for real-world deployment.

Conclusion: PAPA offers a practical, feedback-efficient approach to preference alignment in diffusion models without requiring large-scale preference data, making it well-suited for real-world personalized systems.

Abstract: Diffusion models are highly effective at modeling complex data distributions, including images and text. However, in applications like personalized recommender systems, the objective often shifts to modeling specific regions of the distribution that maximize user preferences-initially unknown but gradually uncovered through interactive feedback. This can naturally be framed as a reinforcement learning problem, where the goal is to fine-tune a diffusion model to maximize a reward function based on preferences. However, the main challenge lies in learning a parameterized reward model, which typically requires large-scale preference data-something that is often not feasible in practice. In this work, we introduce Personalized Active Preference Alignment PAPA, a novel method that bypasses the requirement for a parametrized reward model by directly optimizing the diffusion model using real-time user feedback. PAPA enables feedback-efficient preference alignment, drawing inspiration from the variational inference framework. We demonstrate PAPA's effectiveness through extensive experiments and ablation studies across diverse class-conditioned and fine-grained alignment tasks. Additionally, based on theoretical insights, we propose an enhanced fine-tuning strategy, referred to as EPAPA, that requires less computational budget and accelerates the fine-tuning process, further boosting PAPA's suitability for real-world deployment. Our code is made publicly available at https://github.com/NasikNafi/papa.

</details>


### [114] [Prototype Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00510)
*Dan Ley,Giang Nguyen,Himabindu Lakkaraju,Julius Adebayo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PRISM introduces a prototype-based LM architecture where predictions are made via sparse, non-negative mixtures of learned prototypes anchored to coherent training data neighborhoods. It matches dense baselines within 2.5% accuracy, enables ~500x faster training data attribution via a more tractable Hessian, and supports interpretable model editing through prototype manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard transformer LMs route predictions through dense pathways, distributing training data influence across all parameters. This makes training data attribution expensive, approximate, and post-hoc. The authors seek an architecture where attribution is built-in by design, enabling efficient auditing, correction, and understanding.

Method: PRISM replaces standard feed-forward layers with a prototype layer where each prediction is a sparse, non-negative mixture of learned prototype vectors. Prototypes are trained with clustering objectives that anchor them to coherent neighborhoods of training examples. The sparsity and non-negativity yield a more tractable Hessian (localized curvature), enabling fast attribution. Linear prototype controllers can be calibrated for targeted corrections, and specific prototypes can be suppressed to remove undesired behaviors without finetuning.

Result: PRISM models (130M–1.6B parameters, up to 50B training tokens) match or stay within 2.5 percentage points of dense baseline downstream accuracy. Training data attribution is ~500x faster than post-hoc baselines at equivalent memory. Calibrating linear prototype controllers improves downstream accuracy by ~3 points while enabling traceability to training neighborhoods. Targeted prototype suppression removes model behaviors without finetuning or loss in generation quality.

Conclusion: PRISM demonstrates that prototype-based architectures can be competitive with dense transformers while offering built-in interpretability, efficient attribution, and controllable model editing — suggesting a viable path toward more auditable and correctable language models.

Abstract: Knowing which training examples drive outputs is fundamental to auditing, correcting, and understanding language models, yet for modern LLMs this remains expensive, approximate, and largely post-hoc. Standard language models generate tokens through a dense network pathway, causing training data's influence to be distributed across parameters rather than organized along explicit, traceable components. We introduce a prototype language model architecture, Prototypes for Interpretable Sequence Modeling (PRISM), that forms each prediction via a sparse, non-negative mixture of learned prototypes, trained with clustering objectives that anchor each prototype to coherent neighborhoods of training examples. Across architectures from 130M to 1.6B parameters trained on up to 50B tokens, prototype language models either surpass or remain within 2.5 percentage points on average downstream accuracy of matched dense baselines. We show that sparse prototype structure localizes curvature in the loss landscape, yielding a more tractable Hessian and enabling training data attribution that is ~500x faster than post hoc baselines when consuming equivalent memory. Calibrating linear prototype controllers can improve downstream accuracy by roughly 3 points while tracing those corrections back to training neighborhoods, and targeted prototype suppression can remove model behaviors without finetuning or measurable loss in generation quality.

</details>


### [115] [From Structural Equation Modelling to Double Machine Learning: Robustness Analysis for Survey-Based Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00512)
*Ka Ching Chan,Qiana Liu,Sanjib Tiwari,Ranga Chimhundu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a staged robustness analysis framework combining SEM, OLS regression, and Double Machine Learning (DML) to assess the stability of structural path findings in survey-based research, demonstrated via a FinTech application with publicly available reproducible code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SEM path significance is contingent on a specific model specification and may not hold under alternative estimation frameworks. There is a need for a systematic, practical workflow to assess whether SEM findings remain robust across conventional and machine-learning-based robustness checks.

Method: A three-stage framework: (1) SEM is used to refine measurement structure and estimate a baseline model retaining the full theory-specified structural path system; (2) OLS regression on SEM-derived construct scores provides a transparent benchmark; (3) DML-style residualisation tests focal relationships under flexible ML-based adjustment for controls, with learner-sensitivity checks (Random Forest, Gradient Boosting, SVM) and reverse-direction diagnostics. Demonstrated on a FinTech Digital Customer Intimacy survey model with a reproducible Google Colab workbook.

Result: The framework identifies which structural relationships remain stable across SEM, OLS, and DML checks and which require cautious interpretation. Specific relationships in the FinTech model demonstrate varying degrees of robustness.

Conclusion: The paper contributes a practical, reproducible robustness workflow and interpretation guide enabling survey-based researchers to complement SEM with both conventional regression and machine-learning-based robustness diagnostics.

Abstract: Structural equation modelling (SEM) is widely used in survey-based business and information systems research to assess latent constructs and theory-driven structural relationships. However, SEM path significance is obtained within a particular model specification and may not show whether findings remain stable under alternative estimation frameworks. This study develops and demonstrates a staged robustness analysis framework that connects SEM, ordinary least squares (OLS) regression, and Double Machine Learning (DML). SEM is first used to refine the measurement structure and estimate the robustness-baseline SEM model, in which the full theory-specified structural path system is retained for downstream robustness analysis before final structural path evaluation. OLS regression is then applied to SEM-derived construct scores as a transparent regression benchmark. Finally, DML-style residualisation is used to examine whether each tested focal relationship remains stable after flexible machine-learning-based adjustment for observed controls. Learner-sensitivity checks compare Random Forest, Gradient Boosting, and Support Vector Machine learners, and selected reverse-direction diagnostics are used to examine directional sensitivity. The framework is demonstrated using a FinTech Digital Customer Intimacy survey model. The findings identify which relationships are stable across SEM, OLS, and DML-style checks, and which require more cautious interpretation. A reproducible Google Colab workbook and generated result files are publicly available, providing a reusable template that researchers and students can adapt to other survey-based latent-construct studies. The paper contributes a practical robustness workflow and interpretation guide for survey-based researchers seeking to complement SEM with conventional and machine-learning-based robustness checks.

</details>


### [116] [Active-GRPO: Adaptive Imitation and Self-Improving Reasoning for Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00531)
*Xuefeng Liu,Mingxuan Cao,Qinan Huang,Thomas Brettin,Rick Stevens,Le Cong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Active-GRPO addresses the limitations of answer-only SFT and sparse-reward RLVR in molecular optimization by letting the policy dynamically switch between imitating references and reinforcing its own discoveries, while continuously upgrading the reference itself to prevent performance ceilings from weak references.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for training scientific reasoning in LLMs for molecular optimization face a dilemma: answer-only SFT collapses multi-step reasoning, RLVR suffers from sparse feedback, and reference-guided methods like RePO are bottlenecked by reference quality. The authors aim to break through this performance ceiling imposed by weak or misaligned references.

Method: Active-GRPO introduces two coupled mechanisms: (1) Active Imitate-Reinforce — the policy imitates the reference when the reference outperforms its own candidates, and switches to RL-based self-improvement once policy-generated molecules surpass the reference; (2) Active Referencing — the reference is continuously upgraded by replacing it with the best policy-generated candidate discovered so far, progressively raising the imitation target throughout training.

Result: On TOMG-Bench MOLOPT, Active-GRPO achieves an average SRxSim of 0.1773, outperforming GRPO (0.0959) and RePO (0.1665) under matched three-seed evaluation, with statistically significant improvements on LogP, MR, and QED metrics.

Conclusion: The active reasoning paradigm, instantiated as Active-GRPO, effectively overcomes the reference quality ceiling in reference-guided policy optimization by enabling the policy to adaptively balance imitation and self-improvement while progressively upgrading the reference target.

Abstract: Scientific reasoning is an increasingly important capability of large language models, yet improving the robustness and efficiency of training such reasoning remains a key open challenge. We study this problem in instruction-based molecular optimization, where answer-only supervised fine-tuning (SFT) collapses multi-step reasoning and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) suffers from sparse feedback. Reference-guided Policy Optimization mitigates both by anchoring policy updates to dataset-provided references, but its effectiveness is tightly coupled to reference quality: weak or misaligned references impose a performance ceiling. To overcome this ceiling, we propose active reasoning, a paradigm in which the policy actively decides, on a per-instance basis, when to imitate a reference and when to reinforce its own discoveries, while continuously upgrading what it imitates. We instantiate this paradigm as Active Group Relative Policy Optimization (Active-GRPO), realized through two coupled mechanisms: active imitate-reinforce and active referencing. The former performs imitation learning when the reference still outperforms the policy's own candidates, and shifts to self-improvement via reinforcement learning once the policy has generated molecules that surpass the reference. The latter continuously upgrades the reference itself by replacing it with the best policy-generated candidate discovered so far, progressively raising the imitation target and ensuring that reference guidance remains informative-rather than restrictive-throughout training. Across TOMG-Bench MOLOPT, Active-GRPO improves average SRxSim from 0.0959 for GRPO and 0.1665 for RePO to 0.1773 under matched three-seed evaluation, with statistically significant gains on LogP, MR, and QED.

</details>


### [117] [Flow-Map GRPO: Reinforcement Learning for Few-Step Flow-Map Generators via Anchored Stochastic Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00535)
*Zhiqi Li,Wen Zhang,Bo Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Flow-Map GRPO, a reinforcement learning post-training framework that enables optimization of deterministic few-step flow-map generators (like consistency models and MeanFlow) by introducing a novel stochasticization mechanism called Anchored Stochastic Flow Map Composition (ASFMC), which preserves the original marginal probability path while enabling RL training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deterministic few-step flow-map generators (e.g., consistency models, MeanFlow) are difficult to optimize with RL post-training because RL methods require stochastic trajectories and well-defined likelihood ratios. Existing SDE-based stochasticization techniques are designed for velocity-based samplers with fine time discretization and do not apply to long-range flow maps. This creates a gap: how to apply RL fine-tuning to these efficient few-step generators without altering their parameterization.

Method: The authors propose Flow-Map GRPO with two key components: (1) Anchored Stochastic Flow Map Composition (ASFMC), a path-preserving stochasticization mechanism that injects randomness via anchor-based conditional resampling while maintaining the original marginal probability path of the deterministic flow map; (2) GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) objectives derived for both single-time and two-time flow-map parameterizations, enabling online RL post-training.

Result: Experiments on few-step FLUX-based text-to-image generators (MeanFlow and sCM) demonstrate that Flow-Map GRPO improves pretrained deterministic flow-map models across reward-based, perceptual, and task-level evaluation metrics, without modifying the original model parameterization or requiring retraining as native stochastic models.

Conclusion: Deterministic few-step flow-map generators can be effectively aligned with RL post-training without architectural changes. ASFMC bridges the gap between deterministic long-range transport maps and stochastic RL optimization, enabling practical post-training alignment of efficient few-step generative models.

Abstract: Few-step flow-map generators, such as consistency models and MeanFlow, accelerate sampling by directly learning long-range transport maps between noise and data. However, these models are typically deterministic, which makes them difficult to optimize with reinforcement learning (RL) post-training methods that require stochastic trajectories and well-defined likelihood ratios. Existing SDE-based stochasticization techniques are designed for velocity-based samplers with infinitesimal or finely discretized transitions, and therefore do not directly apply to long-range flow maps. In this work, we propose Flow-Map GRPO, an online RL post-training framework for deterministic few-step flow-map generators. The key component is Anchored Stochastic Flow Map Composition (ASFMC), a path-preserving stochasticization mechanism that introduces randomness through anchor-based conditional resampling while preserving the original marginal probability path of the deterministic flow map. We derive GRPO objectives for both single-time and two-time flow-map parameterizations. Experiments on few-step FLUX-based text-to-image generators, including MeanFlow and sCM, show that Flow-Map GRPO improves pretrained deterministic flow-map models across reward-based, perceptual, and task-level evaluation metrics. Our results demonstrate that deterministic few-step flow-map generators can be effectively aligned with RL post-training without modifying their original model parameterization or retraining them as native stochastic models.

</details>


### [118] [Group-Equivariant Poincaré Convolutional Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00556)
*Aiden Durrant,Rahul Baburajan,Georgios Leontidis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Equivariant Poincaré ResNets, which integrate discrete symmetry groups (C₄, D₄) into hyperbolic residual networks. It introduces geometrically safe tensor reshaping, left-regular permutations for hyperbolic group convolutions, and joint-orientation Poincaré Midpoint Batch Normalisation to overcome key obstacles in translating Euclidean equivariance to hyperbolic space, reducing optimisation complexity, accelerating convergence, and maintaining boundary constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing hyperbolic networks (e.g., Poincaré ResNet) suffer from: (1) computationally expensive Riemannian gradient optimisation due to strict manifold boundaries, and (2) redundant parameter usage from treating spatial transformations of the same object as distinct hierarchical concepts, which leads to vanishing signals. The authors aim to inject discrete equivariance into hyperbolic networks to constrain the optimisation space and improve efficiency.

Method: The authors propose Equivariant Poincaré ResNets that embed C₄ and D₄ discrete symmetry groups into hyperbolic geometry. Three key technical contributions address the incompatibility between Euclidean equivariance mechanisms and hyperbolic space: (1) geometrically safe tensor reshaping to preserve manifold constraints, (2) left-regular permutations enabling group convolutions in hyperbolic space, and (3) a joint-orientation Poincaré Midpoint Batch Normalisation that respects both hyperbolic geometry and group equivariance.

Result: Empirically, embedding equivariance into the Poincaré ResNet significantly reduces the optimisation space, accelerates convergence, and ensures that the Poincaré ball boundary constraints are respected while preserving spatial-group equivariance.

Conclusion: Injecting discrete group equivariance into hyperbolic residual networks is both feasible and beneficial: it mitigates the computational and optimisation challenges of pure hyperbolic learning, yielding faster convergence and more parameter-efficient representations without violating geometric constraints.

Abstract: While recent advancements like the Poincaré ResNet have demonstrated the potential of learning visual representations directly in hyperbolic space, their optimisation remains hampered by the computationally intensive nature of Riemannian gradients and the strict boundaries of the manifold. Furthermore, standard hyperbolic networks treat spatial transformations of the same object as distinct hierarchical concepts, leading to redundant parameter usage and vanishing signals. We propose Equivariant Poincaré ResNets, combining hyperbolic geometry with discrete symmetry groups ($C_4$ and $D_4$). We identify critical roadblocks in applying Euclidean equivariance to hyperbolic space and propose geometrically safe tensor reshaping, left-regular permutations for hyperbolic group convolutions, and joint-orientation Poincaré Midpoint Batch normalisation. Empirically, embedding equivariance drastically reduces the optimisation space, accelerating convergence while accelerating convergence while respecting the boundary constraints of the Poincaré ball and preserving spatial-group equivariance.

</details>


### [119] [Decision-focused Sparse Tangent Portfolio Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00581)
*Haeun Jeon,Seunghoon Choi,Hyunglip Bae,Yongjae Lee,Woo Chang Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes an end-to-end decision-focused learning framework for sparse tangent portfolio optimization, replacing NP-hard cardinality constraints with a smooth top-k operator in a differentiable convex programming layer, achieving superior Sharpe ratios across major equity markets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard predict-then-optimize pipelines for sparse tangent portfolios suffer from misalignment between forecasting accuracy and downstream portfolio quality, while the cardinality-constrained formulation is NP-hard, motivating an end-to-end differentiable approach that directly optimizes portfolio performance.

Method: Reformulates Sharpe ratio maximization as a DPP-compliant convex programming layer integrated with a smooth top-k operator for exact cardinality k, enabling gradient flow through prediction, asset selection, and re-optimization in a decision-focused learning framework.

Result: The method achieves competitive and often superior out-of-sample Sharpe ratios versus historical and prediction-focused baselines across four major equity markets, with particularly strong gains in larger asset universes.

Conclusion: Decision-focused learning with differentiable sparse selection effectively bridges the gap between prediction and portfolio construction for cardinality-constrained tangent portfolios, with code publicly available.

Abstract: Sparse tangent portfolio optimization aims to learn an interpretable, low-cardinality portfolio in the tangency direction of the mean-variance frontier. However, the associated cardinality-constrained formulation is NP-hard, and standard predict-then-optimize pipelines often misalign forecasting accuracy with downstream portfolio quality. We propose an end-to-end decision-focused learning framework that reformulates Sharpe ratio maximization as a Disciplined Parametrized Programming (DPP)-compliant convex programming layer and replaces discrete selection with a smooth top-$k$ operator enforcing an exact cardinality $k$. This enables gradient flow through prediction, asset selection, and re-optimization, allowing the predictive model to directly optimize portfolio performance. Across four major equity markets, our method achieves competitive and often superior out-of-sample Sharpe ratios compared with historical and prediction-focused baselines, with particularly strong gains in larger asset universes. Our \href{https://github.com/feuerwerksh/Diffble-card-SR}{code} is publicly available.

</details>


### [120] [Measuring Dead Directions: Decomposing and Classifying Singular Structure off Canonical Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00603)
*Tejas Pradeep Shirodkar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a method to recover singular structure (dead directions and their order k) from a single frozen neural network checkpoint without requiring gradient descent or weight alignment. Using the directional-Fisher rate as a master invariant, it deterministically extracts per-direction learning coefficients, classifies singularities vs. gauge symmetries, and assembles them into global Watanabe (λ, m) coefficients. The method works across architectures (transformers, CNNs, normalization layers) and connects the recovered order to universal singular fluctuation ν(k).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for recovering singular structure in neural networks (via the Watanabe learning coefficient) require gradient descent traces and canonical alignment of the parameter basis—conditions rarely met in practice. The authors seek a deterministic, architecture-general read that works from a single frozen checkpoint, removing these preconditions.

Method: The core method computes the directional-Fisher rate along each parameter direction, which serves as a master invariant encoding the order k of dead directions (yielding learning coefficient λ = 1/(2k)). This read classifies each direction as a genuine singularity (order fixed by architecture) or a flat gauge symmetry. A pluggable detector handles transformer, convolutional, and normalization layers. The per-direction orders are assembled through typed intersection of loci into the global (λ, m) coefficient matching known closed forms. The method also maps to the Watanabe triple, relating order k to the universal singular fluctuation ν(k).

Result: The read successfully recovers architecture-predicted orders across constructed cells and trained networks, including: (1) a fine-tuned vision transformer where dead structure manifests as the LayerNorm-kernel gauge, and (2) a from-scratch vision transformer where the compressed MLP exhibits node-death at its activation order. The assembled global (λ, m) matches closed-form predictions. The realized ν in trained networks falls below the universal ν(k), explained by live structure absorbing dead direction data fluctuations. Multiplicity recovers under a single-locus assumption from dominant structure.

Conclusion: The directional-Fisher rate provides a descent-free, alignment-free, deterministic method to read singular structure from any frozen network checkpoint. This generalizes order-recovery across architectures and connects local singular geometry to global Watanabe learning coefficients and singular fluctuation statistics, advancing the practical applicability of singular learning theory to real trained networks.

Abstract: We give a descent-free, alignment-free measurement of singular structure on trained networks. At a single frozen checkpoint the read recovers the order $k$ of each dead direction from the directional-Fisher rate, the master invariant from which the per-direction learning coefficient $1/(2k)$ follows exactly, in whatever basis the optimizer left. The same read classifies each direction, separating a genuine singularity, whose order the architecture fixes, from a flat gauge symmetry; the directional-Fisher magnitude settles the cases the order cannot. A pluggable detector supplies the directions for transformer, convolutional, and normalisation layers. The read recovers the architecture-predicted order across constructed cells and trained networks, including a fine-tuned vision transformer whose dead structure is the LayerNorm-kernel gauge and a from-scratch one whose compressed MLP forms a node-death at its activation order. Where the singular structure enumerates, the per-direction orders assemble, through the typed intersection of the loci, into the global coefficient $(λ, m)$ matching the closed form. The method removes the canonical-alignment and descent preconditions of the underlying rate result, turning order-recovery into a deterministic, architecture-general reading. We then map its reach into the Watanabe triple: the order determines the universal singular fluctuation $ν(k)$, though a trained network's realized $ν$ falls below it as the live structure absorbs the dead direction's data fluctuation, and the multiplicity recovers from the dominant structure under a single-locus assumption.

</details>


### [121] [Loss Smoothing for Stable Adaptation Under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00634)
*Darshan Patil,Ekaterina Lobacheva,Razvan Pascanu,Sarath Chandar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes loss smoothing — interpolating between source and target objectives during early adaptation — to prevent abrupt distribution shifts from distorting useful learned features. Demonstrates consistent improvements across supervised shifts, vision adaptation, RL, and LM fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard adaptation methods abruptly switch from the source training objective to the target objective, which can distort or destroy learned representations that remain useful for the new task. The authors investigate whether a gradual transition between objectives can mitigate this representational distortion.

Method: Loss smoothing: a simple interpolation between the source training objective and the target training objective during the initial phase of adaptation. The interpolation weight gradually shifts from source to target, creating a smooth objective transition that preserves useful source-distribution features while allowing specialization to the target distribution.

Result: Loss smoothing consistently improves performance across diverse settings: controlled supervised distribution shifts, pretrained vision model adaptation, offline-to-online RL, online RL, and language model fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Smoother objective transitions are a broadly useful tool for model adaptation, suggesting that preserving source features through gradual objective interpolation is generally beneficial across domains.

Abstract: In settings such as fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, neural networks are often adapted under distribution shift. Standard adaptation methods typically optimize the target objective directly, inducing an abrupt change from the source training objective. This abrupt transition can distort learned representations, including features that may still be useful for the new task. We investigate whether a more gradual transition can improve adaptation. We propose loss smoothing, a simple approach that interpolates between the source and target training objectives at the start of adaptation. This smooth transition helps to preserve useful features from the source distribution while still enabling the model to specialize to the target distribution. Across controlled supervised shifts, pretrained vision adaptation, offline-to-online and online reinforcement learning, and language model fine-tuning, we find that loss smoothing consistently improves performance, suggesting that smoother objective transitions are a broadly useful tool for model adaptation.

</details>


### [122] [Multi-Label Node Classification with Label Influence Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00671)
*Yifei Sun,Zemin Liu,Bryan Hooi,Yang Yang,Rizal Fathony,Jia Chen,Bingsheng He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Label Influence Propagation (LIP), a novel GNN-based model for multi-label node classification that explicitly quantifies and propagates high-order label influence correlations through a constructed label influence graph, outperforming SOTA across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MLNC methods either use GNNs to capture label co-occurrence or employ label embeddings for proximity, but both fail to model the intricate, non-Euclidean influence relationships between labels. The paper argues that understanding how labels influence each other during message passing (propagation and transformation) is critical for improving MLNC performance.

Method: The authors decompose GNN message passing into propagation and transformation operations, then analyze and quantify label influence correlations in each. They construct a label influence graph from integrated label correlations and propagate high-order influences through this graph. The learning process is dynamically adjusted by amplifying positively contributing labels and mitigating negatively influencing ones.

Result: LIP consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods on comprehensive benchmark datasets across various experimental settings for multi-label node classification tasks.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling high-order label influence correlations via a label influence graph and dynamic adjustment mechanism significantly improves multi-label node classification, validating the importance of capturing label-level interactions in GNN message passing.

Abstract: Graphs are a complex and versatile data structure used across various domains, with possibly multi-label nodes playing a particularly crucial role. Examples include proteins in PPI networks with multiple functions and users in social or e-commerce networks exhibiting diverse interests. Tackling multi-label node classification (MLNC) on graphs has led to the development of various approaches. Some methods leverage graph neural networks (GNNs) to exploit label co-occurrence correlations, while others incorporate label embeddings to capture label proximity. However, these approaches fail to account for the intricate influences between labels in non-Euclidean graph data. To address this issue, we decompose the message passing process in GNNs into two operations: propagation and transformation. We then conduct a comprehensive analysis and quantification of the influence correlations between labels in each operation. Building on these insights, we propose a novel model, Label Influence Propagation (LIP). Specifically, we construct a label influence graph based on the integrated label correlations. Then, we propagate high-order influences through this graph, dynamically adjusting the learning process by amplifying labels with positive contributions and mitigating those with negative influence. Finally, our framework is evaluated on comprehensive benchmark datasets, consistently outperforming SOTA methods across various settings, demonstrating its effectiveness on MLNC tasks.

</details>


### [123] [Distributed Online Bandit Submodular Maximization with Bounded Sampling Violations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00680)
*Bin Du,Chang Liu,Dingqi Zhu,Lintao Ye,Dengfeng Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper studies distributed online submodular maximization under partition matroid constraints, where multiple agents sequentially select actions from their own subsets. A unified algorithmic framework is proposed that handles both full-information and bandit feedback, achieving sublinear (1-1/e)-regret comparable to centralized methods. A bounded stochastic pipage rounding scheme is developed to address sampling violations, with the probability of violation vanishing asymptotically and cumulative violation bounded sublinearly in T, shown to be unimprovable under certain conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing online submodular maximization algorithms are primarily centralized, yet many real-world applications (e.g., sensor networks, multi-agent resource allocation) are inherently distributed. There is a need to extend these algorithms to distributed settings while maintaining comparable regret guarantees, and to address the practical issue of sampling violations that arise from continuous relaxation and rounding in combinatorial settings.

Method: The authors develop a unified algorithmic framework for distributed online submodular maximization under partition matroid constraints. The framework covers both full-information and bandit feedback models. A key technical contribution is a bounded stochastic pipage rounding scheme designed to mitigate sampling violations caused by continuous relaxation and rounding procedures.

Result: For both feedback models, the proposed algorithms achieve sublinear (1-1/e)-regret guarantees, matching centralized counterparts. The bounded stochastic pipage rounding ensures the probability of sampling violation vanishes asymptotically, with cumulative sampling violation remaining sublinear in T. This bound is shown to be tight (unimprovable) under certain conditions. Numerical experiments validate the theoretical findings.

Conclusion: The paper successfully extends online submodular maximization to distributed settings with partition matroid constraints, providing a unified framework with strong regret guarantees for both full-information and bandit feedback. The introduction and analysis of bounded stochastic pipage rounding resolves the sampling violation issue, establishing that cumulative violation is sublinear and tight, thereby offering both theoretical and practical contributions.

Abstract: We study distributed online submodular maximization under partition matroid constraints, in which multiple agents select a limited number of actions from their own subsets sequentially to maximize the cumulative value of a sequence of objective functions. We develop a unified algorithmic framework that accommodates full-information and bandit feedback models. For both feedback models, we prove that the proposed algorithms achieve sublinear $(1-1/e)$-regret guarantees, which are comparable to those achieved by existing centralized counterparts. Furthermore, to tackle the sampling violation issue caused by continuous relaxation and rounding, we develop a bounded stochastic pipage rounding scheme and show that the probability of sampling violation vanishes asymptotically. As a result, the cumulative sampling violation remains sublinear in $T$, which is further shown to be not improvable under certain conditions. Numerical results validate the theoretical findings in this paper.

</details>


### [124] [AdaBoosting Text Prompts for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00684)
*Seokhee Jin,Changhwan Sung,Sunung Mun,Hoyoung Kim,Jungseul Ok*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TPB is an AdaBoost-inspired framework that iteratively builds text-prompt-based weak classifiers, focusing on misclassified examples to form a strong ensemble, achieving robust cross-model transfer and sustained shot-driven accuracy gains across 11 benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing few-shot text prompting methods for VLMs do not explicitly target misclassified examples during prompt construction, resulting in diminishing returns as the number of labeled shots increases. The authors aim to fully exploit few-shot supervision by adaptively focusing on hard examples.

Method: Text Prompt Boosting (TPB) treats each text-prompt-based classifier as a weak learner and applies an AdaBoost-inspired sequential aggregation process. Each iteration reweights or selects hard, misclassified examples, progressively constructing an ensemble of complementary text prompts that collectively form a strong classifier.

Result: Across eleven classification benchmarks, TPB improves accuracy on the source model and uniquely preserves shot-driven gains when transferred to larger, more capable VLMs, where existing few-shot prompting methods fail to sustain improvements.

Conclusion: TPB effectively leverages few-shot supervision by explicitly focusing on hard examples in text space, producing model-agnostic prompts that transfer robustly across heterogeneous VLMs and scale with additional shots.

Abstract: The classification accuracy of pretrained Vision-Language Models (VLMs) relies on the quality of the text prompts. Handcrafted templates and Large Language Model (LLM)-generated descriptions not only make predictions more interpretable, but also enable reuse of the same prompts across heterogeneous VLMs. Recent works construct task-adapted text prompts with a small number of labeled images. However, existing few-shot text prompting methods do not explicitly focus on misclassified examples during prompt construction, leading to only marginal improvements even as more shots become available. To fully exploit few-shot supervision, we propose Text Prompt Boosting (TPB), an AdaBoost-inspired framework that treats each text-prompt-based classifier as a weak learner and sequentially aggregates them into a strong ensemble by explicitly targeting hard, misclassified examples. Extensive experiments show that TPB preserves task-intrinsic, model-agnostic cues in text space, enabling robust cross-model transfer. Across eleven classification benchmarks, TPB improves accuracy on the source model and preserves shot-driven gains when transferred to larger, more capable VLMs, where existing methods struggle to sustain such improvements.

</details>


### [125] [Generative Refinement for Low-Budget Black-Box Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00691)
*Edouard R. Dufour,Pascal Fua*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SPARROW decouples generative priors from reward signals to enable efficient black-box optimization under low evaluation budgets, handling noisy rewards and complex search geometries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods that use generative models for black-box optimization require aligning the sampler to reward distributions, which demands many costly function evaluations. This is impractical when evaluations are expensive, noisy, or failure-prone, and when optimal solutions lie in thin, curved, or disconnected regions.

Method: SPARROW decouples the generative prior from the reward signal by using any sampler with a known corruption process (trained on unevaluated data) as a fixed, structured proposal operator. Optimization proceeds via rank-based guidance over an archive of evaluated candidates, avoiding the need to align the generative model to reward distributions.

Result: SPARROW provides asymptotic convergence guarantees over the sampler support and demonstrates strong empirical performance on problems with unreliable rewards and geometrically complex landscapes, even under very low evaluation budgets.

Conclusion: Decoupling the generative prior from reward signals enables practical black-box optimization in low-budget, noisy, and geometrically challenging settings where prior generative model-based methods fail.

Abstract: Black-box optimization is a fundamental science and engineering tool that makes it possible to optimize objectives without gradient information. Unfortunately, as it often requires many function evaluations, it can be challenging when each one is costly. This is especially true when the evaluation function is noisy or failure-prone, and when high-performing solutions are confined to thin, curved, or disconnected regions of the search space. Existing methods leveraging generative models to navigate these subspaces are built to sample from reward-aligned distributions. As a result, they require a large number of evaluations to align their sampler effectively, making them impractical in low-budget settings. We propose SPARROW, an algorithm that completely decouples the generative prior from the reward signal. SPARROW can use any sampler with a known corruption process and trained on unevaluated data, as a fixed, structured proposal operator. Optimization proceeds by rank-based guidance over an archive of evaluated candidates. SPARROW can navigate complex geometries, handle unreliable reward signals, and perform effective optimization under very low evaluation budgets. We provide asymptotic convergence guarantees over the sampler support and demonstrate strong empirical performance on problems with unreliable rewards and geometrically complex landscapes.

</details>


### [126] [Detecting the Undetectable: Enhancing Unsupervised time series Anomaly Detection via Active Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00720)
*Seung Hun Han,Hyeongwon Kang,Jinwoo Park,Pilsung Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes an active learning framework to improve unsupervised time-series anomaly detection. It introduces a masked reconstruction feedback strategy and a minimax learning approach to better distinguish subtle anomalies from normal patterns under noise, achieving a 12.39% AUC improvement across 28 test cases with multiple backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unsupervised anomaly detection is essential in industrial settings due to prohibitive labeling costs, but existing methods struggle with two key issues: (1) difficulty distinguishing near-normal anomalies from normal patterns, and (2) vulnerability to noise contamination within nominally normal training samples. These limitations degrade reliability in real-world deployments.

Method: The framework uses active learning to iteratively refine unsupervised models. Its two core contributions are: (1) a masked time-series reconstruction feedback strategy that forces the model to learn robust temporal dependencies by masking portions of the input during reconstruction; and (2) a minimax learning strategy that differentially weights normal versus abnormal samples, promoting robustness against noisy and subtle patterns. The approach is model-agnostic and plugs into existing reconstruction-based detectors.

Result: Evaluated on four multivariate time-series datasets across seven unsupervised backbone models (28 test cases total), the proposed framework yields an average 12.39% improvement in AUC over the original models, demonstrating consistent gains and easy integrability.

Conclusion: The active learning framework effectively enhances unsupervised anomaly detection by improving robustness to subtle anomalies and noisy normal data. Its plug-and-play nature allows straightforward integration into existing reconstruction-based systems, offering a practical path to stronger industrial anomaly detection.

Abstract: Despite the increasing sophistication of industrial AI systems, the ability to reliably detect subtle and noisy anomalies in complex time series data remains a critical yet unresolved challenge. In large-scale industrial applications, labeling time series data is often prohibitively expensive and time-consuming, making unsupervised learning a practical and widely adopted approach. However, existing unsupervised methods frequently struggle to distinguish near-normal anomalies from normal patterns and are vulnerable to noise contamination within normal samples. To address these limitations, we propose a novel framework that leverages active learning to iteratively enhance the performance of unsupervised models. Our framework's core contributions are (1) a masked time-series reconstruction feedback strategy that forces the model to learn robust temporal dependencies, and (2) a minimax learning strategy that promotes robustness by differentially treating normal and abnormal samples. This process encourages the model to better capture the dynamics of subtle and noisy patterns. The proposed framework is evaluated across 28 test cases involving four multivariate time-series datasets and seven unsupervised backbone models. Experimental results demonstrate a 12.39% improvement in AUC compared to the original models, confirming that our method can be readily integrated into existing unsupervised reconstruction-based anomaly detection systems to significantly enhance their performance.

</details>


### [127] [LLM-Guided ODE Discovery and Parameter Inference from Small-Cohort Aggregate Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00733)
*Hanning Yang,Meropi Karakioulaki,Lennart Purucker,Tim Litwin,Cristina Has,Moritz Hess*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AgentODE proposes an end-to-end framework that jointly discovers ODE structures and refines parameter distributions from population-level summary statistics, using an LLM to propose candidate equations and a tool-augmented inference agent in a diagnosis-update loop. It is evaluated on benchmarks and rare disease (RDEB) data, demonstrating mechanistically principled structure discovery even when individual-level data is unavailable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rare disease modeling faces the challenge that both ODE structure and parameters are unknown, while individual-level data is scarce, noisy, heterogeneous, and privacy-constrained. Population-level summary statistics offer a practical privacy-preserving alternative, but no existing method jointly discovers ODE structure and refines parameter distributions from such data alone.

Method: AgentODE uses an LLM to propose candidate ODE structures and a tool-augmented inference agent that iteratively refines parameter distributions through a diagnosis--update loop, operating exclusively on population-level summary statistics rather than individual-level data.

Result: AgentODE recovers functionally consistent ODE structures across three benchmark problems and two clinical datasets. On the RDEB rare disease dataset (231 observations, 46 patients), it discovers mechanistically principled structures, while baselines with access to individual-level data recovered implausible structures despite better predictive performance.

Conclusion: AgentODE enables mechanistic modeling of rare diseases directly from population-level summary statistics, opening new possibilities where data scarcity and privacy constraints have traditionally limited such analyses.

Abstract: Mechanistic modeling via ordinary differential equations (ODEs) provides interpretable descriptions of complex dynamics and enables inference of underlying mechanisms, which is particularly valuable in clinical settings. However, in rare diseases, both the structure and parameters of the model are typically unknown, while individual-level data is scarce, noisy, heterogeneous, and subject to privacy constraints. In such settings, population-level summary statistics provide a practical privacy-preserving data representation, while capturing heterogeneity further requires modeling parameters as distributions rather than fixed values. Yet no existing method jointly discovers ODE structure and refines parameter distributions solely from summary statistics. We present AgentODE, an end-to-end framework that addresses this gap. An LLM proposes candidate ODE structures, while a tool-augmented inference agent iteratively refines parameter distributions through a diagnosis--update loop, operating on population-level summary statistics alone. We evaluate AgentODE on three benchmark problems across different fields and two clinical datasets, including the rare disease recessive dystrophic epidermolysis bullosa (RDEB), with only 231 observations across 46 patients. AgentODE recovers functionally consistent ODE structures across all settings, and experiments on RDEB demonstrates that in sparse and noisy data settings reasoning from summary statistics promotes mechanistically principled structure discovery, whereas baselines with individual-level data access recover implausible structures despite better predictive performance. AgentODE opens new possibilities for mechanistic modeling of rare diseases directly from population-level summary statistics, where data scarcity and privacy constraints have traditionally limited such analyses.

</details>


### [128] [MosaicKV: Serving Long-Context LLM with Dynamic Two-D KV Cache Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00760)
*Sheng Qiang,Ruiwei Chen,Yinpeng Wu,Jinyu Gu,Zhichao Hua,Yubin Xia,Binyu Zang,Haibo Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MosaicKV is a dynamic two-dimensional KV cache compression system that compresses both sequence and channel dimensions by exploiting non-uniform importance of cache elements, achieving up to 16x attention speedup, 3x memory reduction, and only 1.76% accuracy loss on long-context benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context LLM serving (hundreds of thousands to millions of tokens) makes KV cache a dominant cost, growing linearly with context length and causing GPU memory exhaustion, reduced batch sizes, and lower throughput. Existing methods compress only one dimension (sequence or channel), limiting headroom, while naive two-dimensional compression causes significant accuracy degradation.

Method: MosaicKV performs dynamic two-dimensional compression by: (1) identifying important elements per KV vector based on non-uniform importance distributions, (2) selecting compression strategies at KV cache segment granularity rather than globally, and (3) introducing compressed KV cache management that leverages underutilized GPU/CPU resources to maintain compressed caches and accelerate attention computation, overcoming fine-grained sparsity management overhead.

Result: On an H800 GPU across multiple LLMs, MosaicKV achieves up to 16x attention speedup, 4.8x lower decode latency, 7.3x higher throughput versus uncompressed baselines, 3x memory reduction, and only 1.76% average accuracy loss on LongBench and RULER benchmarks.

Conclusion: Two-dimensional KV cache compression is viable for extremely long-context LLM serving when compression strategies are dynamically adapted to per-element importance and managed efficiently, offering substantial throughput and latency gains with minimal accuracy degradation.

Abstract: Long-context LLM services now sustain prompts with hundreds of thousands to millions of tokens, making the key-value (KV) cache a first-order serving cost. Because the cache grows linearly with context length, it can exhaust GPU memory, force smaller batches, and reduce serving throughput. Prior KV cache compression techniques typically target only the sequence dimension or only the channel dimension, which leaves limited headroom as context windows scale. Compressing both dimensions promises higher memory reduction, but applying the two forms of compression directly leads to significant accuracy loss.
  This paper introduces MosaicKV, a dynamic two-D (dimensional) KV cache compression system for extremely long-context serving. MosaicKV uses dynamic two-D compression to address the accuracy challenge, exploiting the non-uniform importance distribution of elements within the KV cache. Instead of applying one compression pattern globally, MosaicKV identifies important elements for each KV vector and selects compression strategies at the granularity of KV cache segments. To address the performance challenge, where fine-grained sparsity and compression management overhead can offset the gains from compression, MosaicKV introduces compressed KV cache management. This mechanism uses underutilized GPU and CPU resources to maintain compressed KV caches and accelerate attention computation.
  Evaluation on an H800 GPU with multiple LLMs shows that MosaicKV delivers up to 16x attention speedup, 4.8x lower decode latency, and 7.3x higher throughput than the uncompressed baseline. At the same time, it reduces memory usage by 3x and incurs only 1.76% average accuracy loss on LongBench and RULER.

</details>


### [129] [Accelerating Discrete Diffusion Models with Parallel-In-Time Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00773)
*Yu Yao,Huanjian Zhou,Andi Han,Wei Huang,Masashi Sugiyama*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a parallel-in-time sampler for absorbing discrete diffusion models by reformulating the τ-leaping algorithm within a continuous-time Markov chain framework, achieving exponential-factorial convergence and reducing time complexity from O(d log S) to O(log(d log S) · log d), yielding up to 7–9× runtime speedup in synthetic settings and 1.45–1.86× speedup with 50% fewer NFEs on image/text tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete diffusion models generate samples sequentially, which is inherently slow. The τ-leaping algorithm accelerates this by taking multiple steps at once, but remains bottlenecked by its sequential time-stepping nature. The authors aim to further accelerate sampling by parallelizing the τ-leaping algorithm itself across time.

Method: The authors reformulate the τ-leaping algorithm for absorbing discrete diffusion in a Continuous-Time Markov Chain (CTMC) framework. They express τ-leaping as a continuous-time stochastic integral and then apply the Picard iteration method to solve it in a parallel-in-time fashion. This yields an algorithm with exponential-factorial convergence, provably reducing the number of sequential function evaluations (NFE) required.

Result: The proposed parallel sampler achieves up to 7–9× runtime speedup on synthetic distribution tasks. On real-world image and text generation tasks, it maintains generation quality with 50% fewer NFEs and 1.45–1.86× runtime speedups on a single GPU. The time complexity improves from O(d log S) to O(log(d log S) · log d) in terms of NFE.

Conclusion: Parallel-in-time sampling for discrete diffusion is both theoretically sound and practically effective. The method significantly accelerates inference while preserving sample quality, broadening the applicability of discrete diffusion models in domains like molecular generation and language modeling where efficient parallel inference is critical.

Abstract: Discrete diffusion models are widely used for learning and generating discrete distributions. As the generation process is inherently sequential, the acceleration of sampling is of significant importance. In this work, we parallelize the mainstream $τ$-leaping algorithm for absorbing discrete diffusion in a Continuous-Time Markov Chain (CTMC) framework. By leveraging the continuous-time stochastic integral form of the $τ$-leaping algorithm and the Picard iteration method, we achieve parallel-in-time sampling acceleration and provide a proof of exponential-factorial convergence for our algorithm. We improve the overall time complexity of $τ$-leaping under absorbing settings from ${\mathcal{O}}(d \log S)$ to ${\mathcal{O}}(\log (d\log S)\cdot \log d)$ with respect to NFE. Empirically, our method shows consistent acceleration across synthetic and real-data settings. The new sampler achieves at most $7$--$9\times$ runtime speedup for synthetic distribution, and maintains the same quality with $50\%$ fewer NFE and $1.45$--$1.86\times$ runtime speedups in image/text tasks on a single GPU. Our research expands the potential of discrete diffusion models for efficient parallel inference, with broader implications for applications such as molecular structure and language generation.

</details>


### [130] [Which Metric Reflects the Spelling Rate Accuracy in Event-Related Potential-Based Brain-Computer Interfaces?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00794)
*Okba Bekhelifi,Naoual El Djouher Mebtouche*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates which performance metrics best correlate with spelling rate in ERP-based BCIs, given the imbalanced class distributions typical of such systems. Evaluating 13 metrics on two datasets, the authors find that Brier score, MCC, ROC AUC, PR AUC, Average Precision, and partial AUC most accurately reflect user spelling performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard metrics like loss and accuracy are insufficient for ERP-based BCI systems because they fail to account for severe class imbalance and do not adequately capture the spelling rate, which directly determines information transfer rate (ITR) — the key real-world performance measure for communication BCIs.

Method: The authors evaluate 13 classification metrics on two datasets (private LARESI ERP and public OpenBMI ERP) and compute their correlation with the spelling rate. They also examine how these correlations evolve as a function of trial repetition, a critical factor in ERP-based BCI operation.

Result: The Brier score, Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC), ROC AUC, PR AUC, Average Precision (AP), and partial AUC (pAUC) showed the strongest correlation with spelling rate and robustly handled class imbalance, outperforming accuracy, loss, and other standard metrics.

Conclusion: Researchers and practitioners should preferentially report Brier score, MCC, and imbalance-aware metrics (ROC AUC, PR AUC, AP, pAUC) in ERP-based BCI experiments to better reflect actual user spelling performance.

Abstract: For predictive models, the often-reported performance metrics are the loss and accuracy. In synchronous Brain- Computer Interface (BCI) systems, these metrics are informative for most BCI paradigms; however, for Event-Related Potential (ERP) applications the spelling rate, which measures the number of characters correctly selected is more important as it influences the estimation of information transfer rate (ITR) and any related metric measuring spelling performance. Moreover, ERP-based BCIs hold imbalanced data class distributions, which require reporting metrics that can handle the imbalance, such as the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (ROC AUC). In this work, we study the correlation of the spelling rate with 13 metrics to identify which among them best reflect user spelling performance and how they are affected by trial repetition. The Results of two datasets (a private LARESI ERP dataset and the public OpenBMI ERP dataset) favor the Brier score, Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC), and the metrics that account for class imbalance in binary classification: ROC AUC, area under the Precision-Recall curve (PR AUC), Average Precision (AP), and partial AUC (pAUC). These findings encourage researchers and practitioners to report those metrics in ERP-based BCI experiments.

</details>


### [131] [Task-Relevant Representation Decoupling for Visual Reinforcement Learning Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00796)
*Jinwen Wang,Youfang Lin,Xiaobo Hu,Qian Xu,Shuo Wang,Zhuo Chen,Kai Lv*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces T2RD, a self-supervised algorithm that decouples visual observations into task-relevant and task-irrelevant representations to improve generalization in Visual RL. Achieves SOTA on DeepMind Control Suite and Robotic Manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VRL agents tend to overfit to task-irrelevant visual features (e.g., background, textures, lighting) in training environments, severely limiting policy generalization to novel environments. Existing methods lack explicit mechanisms to separate task-relevant from irrelevant information in visual observations.

Method: Proposes the Task-Relevant Representation Decoupling (T2RD) algorithm with three self-supervised components: (1) task-relevant representation consistency to enforce invariance across augmented views; (2) cross-reconstruction to separate content (task-relevant) from style (task-irrelevant) features; (3) cross-dynamic prediction to further refine task-relevant features from content representations by predicting forward dynamics, ensuring the content features are predictive of state transitions needed for control.

Result: T2RD achieves State-Of-The-Art (SOTA) generalization performance and sample efficiency on the DeepMind Control Suite and Robotic Manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: Explicitly decoupling task-relevant from task-irrelevant representations via the proposed three-component self-supervised objective is an effective strategy for improving generalization in Visual RL.

Abstract: Visual Reinforcement Learning (VRL) has achieved considerable success in solving control tasks. However, generalizing learned policies to new environments remains a major challenge, as agents often overfit to task-irrelevant features in the training environment. To solve this problem, we introduce the concept of decoupling observations into task-relevant and task-irrelevant representations. Building on this idea, we propose a self-supervised Task-Relevant Representation Decoupling (T2RD) algorithm for VRL. This algorithm consists of three components: task-relevant representation consistency, cross-reconstruction, and cross-dynamic prediction. The first two components achieve the decoupling of content and style features, but the resulting content representations are not necessarily task-relevant. To further refine task-relevant features from content representations, we design the third component that introduces dynamic prediction. T2RD achieves State-Of-The-Art (SOTA) generalization performance and sample efficiency in the DeepMind Control Suite and Robotic Manipulation tasks.

</details>


### [132] [Local Motion Matters: A Deconstruct-Recompose Paradigm for Reinforcement Learning Pre-training from Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00808)
*Jinwen Wang,Youfang Lin,Xiaobo Hu,Shuo Wang,Kai Lv*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes DRP, a paradigm that deconstructs videos into local Atomic Actions and recomposes them via a learnable token, enabling transferable motion representations that significantly improve RL sample efficiency across diverse robotic tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video pre-training for RL models global motion patterns tied to specific morphologies, preventing cross-domain transfer. The key insight is that local components (joints, end-effectors) exhibit similar motion patterns across different agents despite vastly different global motions, motivating a local-motion-centric approach.

Method: The Deconstruct-Recompose Paradigm (DRP) operates in two phases. In the Deconstruct phase, local points are identified and tracked frame-wise as Atomic Actions; a Dual-Attention Encoder (DAE) learns spatiotemporal representations from these. In the Recompose phase, a learnable Motion Aggregation Token [MAT] composes local representations via latent dynamics modeling, and an adapter bridges local motion to action-specific dynamics for downstream policy learning.

Result: Extensive experiments show DRP effectively transfers to diverse robotic control and manipulation tasks, significantly improving both sample efficiency and performance compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Learning transferable local motion representations by deconstructing global motions into atomic components and recomposing them is an effective paradigm for cross-domain video pre-training, yielding substantial gains in RL efficiency and generalization.

Abstract: Pre-training on large-scale videos to improve reinforcement learning efficiency is promising yet remains challenging. Existing methods typically treat the agent as an indivisible entity, modeling motion patterns globally. Such global modeling is tightly coupled with the morphology, hindering transfer across domains. In contrast, despite the vast disparity in global motions, the local components exhibit similar motion patterns across different agents. Building on this insight, we propose a novel Deconstruct-Recompose Paradigm (DRP) for learning transferable local motion representations. Specifically, in the Deconstruct phase, we identify multiple local points and track their frame-wise motions, defining each as an Atomic Action. We introduce a Dual-Attention Encoder (DAE) to learn local motion representations from these Atomic Actions, capturing their spatiotemporal relationships. In the Recompose phase, we compose local motion representations with a learnable Motion Aggregation Token [MAT] via latent dynamics model learning. Additionally, an adapter bridges local motion and downstream action-specific dynamics to accelerate policy learning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method effectively transfers to diverse robotic control and manipulation tasks, significantly improving sample efficiency and performance.

</details>


### [133] [From Pixels to Temporal Correlations: Learning Informative Representations for Reinforcement Learning Pre-training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00811)
*Jinwen Wang,Youfang Lin,Xiaobo Hu,Siyu Yang,Sheng Han,Shuo Wang,Kai Lv*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MTCL proposes a multi-scale temporal contrastive learning method for unsupervised video pre-training that balances attention across all elements in videos, avoiding the common pitfall of over-emphasizing stationary information, thereby improving RL sample efficiency and performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unsupervised pre-training methods for RL rely on single-step transition prediction and image reconstruction from action-free videos, but these approaches disproportionately preserve large-proportion stationary information in pixel space while neglecting small yet critical dynamic elements. This imbalance leads to less informative representations for downstream policy learning.

Method: The authors propose a temporal correlation space to distinguish each element in videos and introduce Multi-scale Temporal Contrastive Learning (MTCL), which models multi-scale temporal correlations separately. This explicitly balances attention across different elements — both stationary and dynamic — yielding richer, more informative representations.

Result: Experimental results show that MTCL improves both sample efficiency and asymptotic performance across a variety of downstream RL tasks compared to existing unsupervised pre-training approaches.

Conclusion: Paying equal attention to all video elements via multi-scale temporal contrastive learning produces more informative visual representations that effectively support policy learning, addressing a key limitation of prior methods that over-emphasize static scene content.

Abstract: Unsupervised pre-training on large-scale datasets has demonstrated significant potential for improving the sample efficiency and performance of Reinforcement Learning (RL). Given the large-scale action-free internet videos, existing methods utilize single-step transition prediction and image reconstruction to learn representations. However, these methods prefer to preserve large-proportion stationary information in the pixel space, neglecting small but crucial information. To preserve enough information in the representation, it is essential to pay equal attention to each element in videos. Specifically, we propose a temporal correlation space to distinguish each element. For implementation, we introduce the Multi-scale Temporal Contrastive Learning (MTCL) method to model multi-scale temporal correlations separately. This approach can balance the attention of different elements and yield more informative representations, effectively supporting policy learning in various downstream tasks. Experimental results demonstrate that our method improves sample efficiency and asymptotic performance across various downstream tasks.

</details>


### [134] [Spectroscopy Analysis with Machine Learning Regression for the Quantification of Carbon and Nitrogen Contents in Inceptisol and Oxisol Soil Types: Comparing Different Preprocessing and Validation methods as well as Feature Importance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00834)
*Vinicius Herique Kieling,Guilherme Macedo Baggio,Felipe Augusto Bueno Rossi,Marco Antonio de Castro Barbosa,Dalcimar Casanova,Larissa Macedo dos Santos Tonial,Jefferson Tales Oliva*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine learning framework using ensemble stacking (PLS, SVR, Ridge base models + linear regression meta-model) was developed to predict soil carbon and nitrogen from portable NIR spectroscopy. Savitzky-Golay filtering with Huber-based robust NIPALS outlier removal proved most effective. Models achieved RPD > 2.0 with low overfitting, demonstrating viable rapid soil analysis for Oxisols and Inceptisols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional soil analysis methods for carbon and nitrogen are time-consuming, expensive, and destructive. There is a need for rapid, low-cost, non-destructive alternatives that enable timely decision-making for sustainable agricultural management. Portable NIR spectroscopy offers such potential, but requires robust calibration models that account for spectral preprocessing, outlier handling, and pedological variability across different soil types.

Method: NIR spectral data were acquired using a portable MyNIR device on Oxisol and Inceptisol samples. Preprocessing compared multiple methods, with Savitzky-Golay filtering and NIPALS-based robust outlier removal (Huber loss) selected as optimal. Three validation strategies were compared: 10-fold cross-validation, leave-one-out, and Kennard-Stone holdout with standardization. Stacking ensemble models combined PLS, SVR, and Ridge as base learners with linear regression as the meta-learner. Performance was evaluated via R², RMSE, MAE, and RPD metrics.

Result: The SG filter with Huber-NIPALS outlier removal was the most effective preprocessing pipeline. The stacking ensemble achieved RPD > 2.0 with low overfitting, indicating reliable predictive capability for both C and N quantification.
