A performance gap between Oxisols and Inceptisols was observed, attributable to differing pedological characteristics. The approach demonstrated sufficient accuracy for practical field application.

Conclusion: Portable NIR spectroscopy combined with stacking ensemble ML and appropriate preprocessing/validation strategies provides a viable, rapid method for soil C and N quantification. The technique supports sustainable agriculture by enabling faster, environmentally friendly decision-making on organic matter, fertility, and nutrient availability. Future work should address pedological variability to improve cross-soil-type generalizability.

Abstract: Near-Infrared (NIR) spectroscopy has emerged as a promising alternative to traditional soil analysis methods, offering advantages such as speed, low cost, and non-destructive testing. This work proposes a machine learning (ML) approach to calibrate predictive models for carbon (C) and nitrogen (N) content in Oxisols and Inceptisols, utilizing NIR spectral data acquired with a portable MyNIR device. Various preprocessing methods were evaluated, with the most effective being the Savitzky-Golay (SG) filter and a robust outlier removal method based on the Nonlinear Iterative Partial Least Squares (NIPALS) algorithm coupled with a Huber loss function. Multiple validation strategies were compared, including 10-fold cross-validation, leave-one-out, and holdout via the Kennard-Stone method, followed by standardization. Stacking ensemble learning models were employed, using Partial Least Squares (PLS), Support Vector Regression (SVR), and Ridge as base models, with linear regression as the meta-model. The models were evaluated using R2, Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE), Mean Absolute Error (MAE), and Ratio of Performance Deviation (RPD) metrics. The performance gap between soil types suggests the influence of pedological characteristics. Furthermore, the models achieved an RPD > 2.0 with low overfitting, validating the potential of this approach for rapid C and N quantification. This study contributes to the optimization of sustainable agricultural practices, aligning with the demand for efficient and environmentally friendly analytical methods. The developed technique enables faster decision-making for producers and consultants based on organic matter content, fertility indicators, and nutrient availability.

</details>


### [135] [Constrained Bayesian Optimisation with Multiple Information Sources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00865)
*Hauke Maathuis,Roeland De Breuker,Saullo Castro,Maike Osborne*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a multi-source Bayesian Optimization framework extending constrained Max-value Entropy Search to incorporate auxiliary data sources (e.g., surrogate models, simplified simulations) for efficient exploration under unknown constraints, especially when feasible regions are small. The method captures inter-source correlation while balancing evaluation cost and information gain, outperforming existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constrained BO struggles when feasible regions are small because methods relying solely on true objective/constraint evaluations fail to explore efficiently. Many real-world problems have auxiliary data sources (surrogate models, simplified simulations) that could aid early exploration, but their integration into constrained BO remains largely unexplored.

Method: A general multi-source framework that extends constrained Max-value Entropy Search (MES). The approach models inter-source correlation (e.g., between cheap auxiliary sources and the expensive true function) using multi-output Gaussian processes, and designs an acquisition function that balances evaluation cost against information gain when selecting both the next query point and which information source to query.

Result: On synthetic and physics-based benchmarks, the proposed method efficiently identifies feasible and optimal solutions even when auxiliary data are only weakly correlated. It consistently outperforms existing methods, with particularly pronounced advantages during early-stage exploration.

Conclusion: The multi-source constrained BO framework effectively leverages auxiliary information to accelerate discovery of feasible optima in challenging constrained settings, filling a gap in the literature and demonstrating robust performance across varying degrees of source correlation.

Abstract: Bayesian Optimisation (BO) under unknown constraints is particularly challenging when feasible regions are small. In such settings, existing methods that typically rely solely on evaluations of the true objective and constraints struggle to efficiently explore the design space. However, many real-world applications offer auxiliary data sources (e.g. surrogate models or simplified simulations) that can support early exploration. Despite this potential, their integration into constrained BO remains largely unexplored. We propose a general multi-source framework that extends constrained Max-value Entropy Search, capturing inter-source correlation while balancing evaluation cost and information gain. Experiments on both synthetic and physics-based benchmarks show that our method efficiently identifies feasible and optimal solutions, even when auxiliary data are only weakly correlated. The proposed approach consistently outperforms existing methods, particularly in early-stage exploration.

</details>


### [136] [Beyond Activation Alignment:The Alignment-Diversity Tradeoff in Task-Aware LLM Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00908)
*Fei Wang,Chao Xue,Taoran Liu,Li Shen,Ye Liu,ChangXing Ding*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies a "Perplexity Illusion" where perplexity-based layer importance does not correlate with reasoning-task importance, and an "Alignment-Diversity Tradeoff" in calibration data. It proposes TASA, a two-level framework for task-aware mixed-precision quantization that jointly optimizes calibration-data composition and bit allocation, achieving 3.5-bit models that rival or surpass 4-bit baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MPQ methods rely on perplexity-based sensitivity metrics for bit allocation, but the authors show these metrics poorly predict layer importance for complex reasoning tasks (Kendall τ ≈ 0). Additionally, the impact of calibration-data composition on task-specific quantization performance is underexplored. The paper aims to bridge the gap between compression objectives and real-world task performance.

Method: TASA (Task-Aware Sensitivity Analysis) is a two-level framework: (1) a training-free gradient-trace alignment criterion searches for an optimal calibration-data mixture balancing target-task and general-domain data; (2) aggregated perplexity and reasoning-oriented sensitivity signals guide both inter-layer and intra-layer mixed-precision bit allocation. This jointly optimizes data composition and precision assignment.

Result: On LLaMA-3-8B and Qwen2.5-7B, TASA exhibits a "precision inversion" where 3.5-bit models match or exceed 4-bit baselines. At 3.5 average bits, TASA matches/outperforms competitive 4-bit uniform baselines in aggregate accuracy and improves over the strongest W3 baseline on GSM8K by 20+ absolute points on LLaMA-3-8B.

Conclusion: Calibration-data composition is a critical but underexplored factor in task-sensitive quantization. Jointly optimizing data mixture and bit allocation via TASA enables more efficient deployment of LLMs, where appropriately configured lower-bit models can outperform higher-bit uniform counterparts on reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Mixed-precision quantization (MPQ) has become a key technique for deploying large language models under stringent memory and compute constraints. We first identify a phenomenon that we term the Perplexity Illusion: layers ranked as important by perplexity-based sensitivity show little rank correlation with those that are most influential for complex reasoning performance, with Kendall $τ\approx 0$ in our analysis. We further reveal an Alignment-Diversity Tradeoff: using only target-task calibration data can degrade post-quantization performance, whereas incorporating general-domain data stabilizes sensitivity estimation and improves robustness across tasks. Based on these observations, we propose TASA (Task-Aware Sensitivity Analysis), a two-level framework that jointly optimizes calibration-data composition and mixed-precision bit allocation. Specifically, TASA searches for a calibration-data mixture using a training-free gradient-trace alignment criterion, and then aggregates perplexity and reasoning-oriented sensitivity signals to guide both inter-layer and intra-layer bit allocation. Experiments on LLaMA-3-8B and Qwen2.5-7B reveal a precision inversion: appropriately allocated 3.5-bit models can match or surpass less task-aware 4-bit baselines. At an average precision of 3.5 bits, TASA matches or outperforms several competitive 4-bit uniform baselines in aggregate accuracy, and improves over the strongest W3 baseline on GSM8K by more than 20 absolute points on LLaMA-3-8B. These results show that calibration-data composition substantially affects task-sensitive quantization, a factor underexplored in prior work.

</details>


### [137] [Valdi: Value Diffusion World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00917)
*Christopher Lindenberg,Kashyap Chitta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Valdi combines value-guided model predictive control with a latent diffusion dynamics model, using single-step diffusion at both training and inference. On CarRacing, it matches a deterministic MLP baseline while exposing a trade-off between predictive multimodality and control performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models for MPC require dynamics prediction that is both fast (low-latency, suitable for online use) and expressive (capturing uncertain, multimodal futures). Diffusion models naturally handle uncertainty but are slow due to iterative inference. The authors aim to bridge this gap by making diffusion viable for online latent planning.

Method: Valdi is an end-to-end online training framework for MPC that uses a latent diffusion dynamics model. Crucially, it employs only a single diffusion step at both training and inference time, avoiding the costly iterative denoising typically associated with diffusion models. The approach is evaluated on the CarRacing environment against a deterministic MLP baseline.

Result: Valdi with a single diffusion step matches the control performance of a deterministic MLP baseline on CarRacing. However, the authors identify a trade-off: increased predictive multimodality (the diffusion model's ability to represent diverse futures) comes at the cost of degraded control performance in this setup.

Conclusion: Single-step latent diffusion dynamics models can be competitive with deterministic alternatives for online MPC, but the benefits of multimodality in dynamics prediction do not straightforwardly translate to better control — suggesting a nuanced relationship between prediction diversity and decision quality that warrants further investigation.

Abstract: World models can enable Model Predictive Control (MPC), but this requires dynamics prediction that is both fast enough for online use and expressive enough to represent uncertain futures. Diffusion models offer a natural mechanism for modeling uncertain dynamics, yet their iterative inference procedure makes them difficult to use for low-latency latent planning. We bridge this gap with Value Diffusion World Models (Valdi), combining end-to-end online training for MPC with a latent diffusion dynamics model. In preliminary experiments on the CarRacing environment, we show that Valdi, using a single diffusion step at both training and inference, matches a deterministic MLP baseline. Our experiments expose a trade-off between predictive multimodality and control performance in this setup. Code is available at https://github.com/Kit115/ValueDiffusionWorldModels.

</details>


### [138] [Human-Machine Collaboration on Generative Meta-Learning: Model and Algorithm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00926)
*Midhun Parakkal Unni,Samuel Kaski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GMHF proposes a framework that uses human expert feedback to guide a generative model (cNODE) and RL agent in synthesizing target-domain-like data, with theoretical generalization bounds showing that aligning generated data distribution with human beliefs about target physics reduces risk. Validated on a Duffing oscillator and a probabilistic model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning models fail under distribution shift when target domain data is scarce or unavailable. Existing domain generalization methods lack a principled mechanism to incorporate human physical intuition about the target domain to steer data synthesis.

Method: A Conditional Neural ODE (cNODE) serves as a generative digital twin. An RL agent iteratively refines the latent physical parameters of generated trajectories based on human feedback about whether synthesized samples resemble the target domain. The framework is grounded in theoretical generalization bounds that show distribution alignment with human beliefs minimizes risk under divergence measures.

Result: On a nonlinear Duffing oscillator, deployment loss decreases substantially as expert reliability increases; divergence between generated and target data falls under reliable feedback, corroborating the theory's divergence-minimization mechanism. A non-dynamical probabilistic model experiment confirms the framework extends beyond ODE-governed systems.

Conclusion: Human-AI collaboration via expert feedback can serve as a rigorous catalyst for robust generalization under distribution shift. GMHF effectively bridges domain gaps by operationalizing human physical intuition through a theoretically grounded generative meta-learning framework.

Abstract: Generalizing machine learning models to environments that differ from their training distribution remains a critical hurdle, particularly when data from the target domain is entirely or partially unavailable. We propose Generative Meta-Learning with Human Feedback (GMHF), a novel framework that bridges this domain gap by leveraging expert intuition to guide data synthesis. Grounded in a theoretical analysis of generalization error, we derive bounds demonstrating that aligning the distribution of generated data with human beliefs regarding the target physics significantly mitigates risk. GMHF operationalizes this insight by employing a Conditional Neural ODE (cNODE) as a generative digital twin, coupled with a Reinforcement Learning (RL) agent. The agent iteratively refines the latent physical parameters of the generated trajectories based on feedback, effectively steering the meta-learner toward the unobserved target distribution. Empirical validation on a nonlinear Duffing oscillator shows that GMHF substantially reduces deployment loss as expert reliability increases, and that the divergence between generated and target data falls under reliable feedback, directly corroborating the divergence-minimisation mechanism predicted by our theory. Further experiments on a non-dynamical probabilistic model confirm that the framework extends beyond ODE-governed systems, establishing human-AI collaboration as a rigorous catalyst for robust generalisation under distribution shift.

</details>


### [139] [Explainable AI for Cancer Drug Response Prediction: Beyond Univariate Feature Attributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00931)
*Martino Ciaperoni,Margherita Lalli,Simone Piaggesi,Martina Varisco,Francesco Carli,Riccardo Guidotti,Dino Pedreschi,Francesco Raimondi,Fosca Giannotti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ILLUME+ is a post-hoc explainability framework for drug response prediction models that goes beyond univariate gene importance to capture coordinated gene activities, yielding more stable, biologically meaningful explanations and enabling AI-assisted hypothesis generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current explainability methods for cancer drug response prediction suffer from high computational cost, lack of robustness, and an overly reductionist approach—they produce univariate gene importance scores that fail to capture the coordinated, multi-gene programs driving drug sensitivity and resistance. There is a need for scalable, robust explanations that reflect the biological reality of pathway-level and interaction-driven mechanisms.

Method: ILLUME+ is a scalable, post-hoc explainability framework integrated into an end-to-end pipeline. It generates multiple complementary explanation types that capture coordinated gene activity rather than isolated gene scores. The framework is evaluated against existing baselines on stability of importance scores, recovery of known drug-gene associations and mechanisms of action, and capacity for AI-assisted hypothesis generation to discover novel interaction-driven molecular signals.

Result: ILLUME+ produces more stable gene importance scores than existing baselines, successfully recovers established drug-gene associations and known mechanisms of action, and enables the discovery of novel interaction-driven molecular signals in cancer biology through AI-assisted hypothesis generation.

Conclusion: ILLUME+ addresses key limitations of existing explainability methods by providing robust, multi-faceted biological explanations that go beyond single-gene rankings. It demonstrates that explainability frameworks can simultaneously improve interpretability, recover known biology, and drive new discoveries, strengthening the translational value of machine learning models in precision oncology.

Abstract: Predicting cancer drug response from transcriptomic profiles is a cornerstone of precision oncology, yet the scientific value of machine learning models hinges not solely on predictive accuracy, but also on their capacity to generate reliable biological insights. Current explainability approaches in this setting are computationally costly, lack robustness, and reduce complex drug response to univariate gene importance scores, overlooking the coordinated gene activity that drives sensitivity and resistance. In this work, we present ILLUME+, a scalable post-hoc explainability framework that moves beyond single-gene assessments to capture multiple, complementary forms of explanation. Integrated into our end-to-end pipeline, ILLUME+ produces more stable gene importance scores than existing baselines, recovers established drug-gene associations and mechanisms of action, and enables AI-assisted hypothesis generation to uncover novel interaction-driven molecular signals in cancer biology.

</details>


### [140] [Diffeomorphic Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00947)
*Ludwig Winkler,Andrew Leaver-Fay,Joseph Kleinhenz,Pan Kessel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Diffeomorphic optimization leverages the map from data manifold to a simpler base space provided by diffusion/flow models, enabling Riemannian gradient descent on the manifold. It is extended to Lie groups SO(3)/SE(3) for protein design, achieving superior secondary-structure targeting, peptide binding affinity, and Rosetta energy minimization compared to existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative models learn distributions on low-dimensional manifolds, but optimizing differentiable objectives directly in ambient space fails because gradient descent drifts off the manifold due to the high-dimensional, rugged, non-convex loss landscape. There is a need for optimization methods that respect the intrinsic manifold geometry.

Method: Diffeomorphic optimization uses the diffeomorphic map from diffusion/flow models (data manifold → base space) to perform gradient descent in the simpler base space. Differential geometry shows this equals Riemannian gradient descent on the manifold with O(λ²) corrections, keeping trajectories on-manifold. For proteins, the method is extended to matrix Lie groups SO(3) and SE(3) with an autograd-compatible SO(3) gradient and a generalized adjoint-state method for backpropagation through Lie-group ODE solvers.

Result: On secondary-structure targeting with FrameFlow: 91.3% vs. 63.3% of residues in Ramachandran target; outperforms OC-Flow on peptide binding affinity at 2× speed; reduces Rosetta energies by thousands of units across the PDB test set for structures with hundreds of residues.

Conclusion: Diffeomorphic optimization provides a principled, geometry-aware framework for optimizing objectives on learned manifolds, with strong empirical gains in protein design tasks including structure targeting, binding affinity, and energy minimization.

Abstract: Generative models learn data distributions that reside on a low-dimensional manifold within a higher-dimensional ambient space. Optimizing differentiable objectives on this manifold is challenging: the ambient loss landscape is high-dimensional, rugged, and non-convex. Direct gradient descent, blind to the manifold's geometry, quickly drifts off it. Diffeomorphic optimization starts from the observation that diffusion and flow models provide a map from the data manifold to a much simpler base space in which we perform gradient descent. Using differential geometry, we show this is equivalent to Riemannian gradient descent on the data manifold up to $\mathcal{O}(λ^2)$ corrections, keeping trajectories on-manifold by construction and yielding a smoother optimization surface. For protein design, we extend diffeomorphic optimization to the matrix Lie groups $\mathrm{SO}(3)$ and $\mathrm{SE}(3)$, deriving an autograd-compatible $\mathrm{SO}(3)$ gradient and a generalized adjoint-state method for backpropagation through Lie-group ODE solvers. Diffeomorphic optimization improves over tuned guidance on secondary-structure targeting with FrameFlow ($91.3\%$ vs. $63.3\%$ of residues in the Ramachandran target), outperforms OC-Flow on peptide binding affinity at $2\times$ the speed, and reduces Rosetta energies by thousands of units across the PDB test set for structures with hundreds of residues.

</details>


### [141] [Aionoscope: Debugging Latent-State Accessibility in Time-Series Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00956)
*Alexander Chemeris,Ming Jin,Randall Balestriero*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Aionoscope is a diagnostic tool that generates synthetic time-series data with exact labels to evaluate whether frozen representations preserve fine-grained process state (timing, phase, amplitude, frequency, regime). Testing 37 systems reveals a gap: coarse component presence is easily recovered, but dense state variables remain poorly accessible, exposing a critical failure mode in time-series representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard evaluation of time-series models via forecasting or classification scores fails to reveal whether learned representations retain interpretable process state variables (event timing, phase, amplitude, frequency, regime). Users debugging or inspecting models need to access these variables, but no existing tool systematically diagnoses this latent-state accessibility in frozen representations.

Method: The authors introduce Aionoscope, a generator-based diagnostic framework that decouples process generation from observation rendering. It produces seeded synthetic streams with exact categorical and dense labels across varying mixture complexity and nuisance factors. The instantiation, Primitive Process Mixtures, generates controlled time series. Evaluation uses a common pooled linear-probe protocol across 37 model-plus-adapter systems, measuring both coarse (component presence) and fine-grained (dense state) recoverability via mean masked R².

Result: A clear mismatch emerges: most systems easily recover component presence (coarse signal type), but dense process state recovery is substantially worse. The best dense-probe model achieves only 0.689 mean masked R², while a dense-feature oracle reaches 0.999, indicating that representations that appear informative at coarse granularity systematically obscure fine-grained temporal structure.

Conclusion: The failure mode Aionoscope surfaces is that time-series representations can look useful for "what kind of signal is present" while concealing critical debugging variables like timing, phase, amplitude, frequency, and regime. This highlights the need for diagnostic tools that go beyond task-performance metrics to assess latent-state accessibility.

Abstract: Time-series models are often evaluated by what they can forecast or classify, but those scores do not show whether their representations preserve the process state a user may want to inspect: event timing, phase, amplitude, frequency, or regime variables. We introduce Aionoscope, a generator-based diagnostic tool for debugging latent-state accessibility in frozen time-series representations. Aionoscope separates process generation from observation rendering, producing seeded synthetic streams with exact categorical and dense labels across mixture complexity and nuisance variation.
  We instantiate Aionoscope as Primitive Process Mixtures and evaluate 37 model-plus-adapter systems with a common pooled linear-probe protocol. The main result is a mismatch between coarse and fine-grained accessibility. Most systems make component presence easy to recover, but expose dense process state much less reliably: the highest observed dense-probe row reaches 0.689 mean masked $R^2$, while a dense-feature oracle reaches 0.999. This is the failure mode Aionoscope is designed to surface: a representation can look informative at the level of "what kind of signal is present" while hiding the timing, phase, amplitude, frequency, or regime variables needed for debugging.

</details>


### [142] [LeNEPA: No-Augmentation Next-Latent Prediction for Time-Series Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00958)
*Alexander Chemeris,Ming Jin,Randall Balestriero*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LeNEPA is a no-augmentation self-supervised learning method for time series that replaces stop-gradient/EMA with isotropy regularization and uses a lightweight projected latent prediction loss. Under a fixed-recipe stress test, it preserves frozen-probe performance across datasets better than an ECG-tuned JEPA recipe and achieves competitive UCR-128 accuracy, supporting latent prediction as a robust recipe for low-retuning time-series SSL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-series SSL performance is highly sensitive to view/augmentation choices that encode domain-specific invariances. The authors investigate how SSL recipes behave when reused unchanged across different pretraining signal families, framing this as a fixed-recipe stress test rather than a comparison of optimally tuned methods. The goal is to find a robust recipe that transfers with minimal retuning.

Method: LeNEPA (Latent Euclidean Next-Embedding Prediction Architecture) uses a causal backbone with a no-augmentation next-latent-token objective. It replaces the stop-gradient/EMA stabilization of vanilla NEPA with SIGReg-based isotropy regularization. The predictive loss is computed in a lightweight projected space that is discarded at evaluation time. Both LeNEPA and a JEPA recipe (tuned for ECG) are retrained independently on PTB-XL and Diag (a synthetic diagnostic corpus from Aionoscope) under a fixed-horizon frozen-probe protocol, keeping method-specific configurations unchanged. An external frozen-encoder check uses a CauKer-pretrained LeNEPA variant evaluated on UCR-128 with Random Forest.

Result: The ECG-tuned JEPA recipe performs strongly in-domain on PTB-XL but degrades when reused unchanged on Diag. LeNEPA preserves useful frozen-probe gains on both datasets. LeNEPA reaches 80% of its final AUROC/AUPRC gain after 2–5k updates vs. 5–10k for JEPA, indicating faster early representation learning. On UCR-128, a CauKer-pretrained LeNEPA achieves 77.65% mean Random-Forest accuracy, within 1.16 points of Mantis and 0.24 points of MOMENT (77.89%).

Conclusion: No-augmentation latent prediction with isotropy regularization is a promising candidate recipe for time-series SSL that requires minimal retuning. LeNEPA demonstrates cross-domain robustness under a fixed-recipe protocol, faster early representation acquisition, and competitive frozen-encoder performance, supporting its viability for practical low-retuning deployment across diverse time-series datasets.

Abstract: Time series are central to modern data mining applications, from industrial telemetry and server metrics to finance and physiology, yet time-series self-supervised learning often depends on view and augmentation choices that encode domain-specific invariances. We study how an SSL recipe behaves when its method-specific configuration is reused unchanged after the pretraining signal family changes, framing this as a fixed-recipe stress test rather than a comparison against optimally tuned methods. We introduce Latent Euclidean Next-Embedding Prediction Architecture (LeNEPA), a no-augmentation next-latent-token objective with a causal backbone. LeNEPA replaces the stop-gradient/EMA stabilization used by vanilla NEPA with SIGReg-based isotropy regularization and computes the predictive loss in a lightweight projected space that is discarded for evaluation. We compare LeNEPA with an ECG-tuned JEPA recipe under a fixed-horizon frozen-probe protocol on PTB-XL and Diag, a synthetic diagnostic corpus generated with Aionoscope. Both methods are retrained independently on each dataset while keeping their method-specific recipes unchanged. In this protocol, the ECG-tuned JEPA recipe is strong in-domain on PTB-XL but weaker when reused unchanged on Diag, whereas LeNEPA preserves useful frozen-probe gains on both datasets. Learning curves suggest faster early representation acquisition: LeNEPA reaches 80% of its final AUROC/AUPRC gain after 2--5k updates, compared with 5--10k updates for the faster JEPA readout. As a separate external frozen-encoder check, a CauKer-pretrained LeNEPA variant reaches 77.65% mean UCR-128 Random-Forest accuracy in a single-seed, best-checkpoint run, within 1.16 points of Mantis and within 0.24 points of MOMENT (77.89%). Overall, the results support no-augmentation latent prediction as a useful candidate recipe for low-retuning time-series SSL.

</details>


### [143] [Automatic Detection of Stress from Speech in the Trier Social Stress Test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00986)
*Hanna Drimalla,Wieland R. Cremer,Christine Kraus,Oliver T. Wolf*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Speech-based acoustic-prosodic features can automatically detect stressful vs. non-stressful situations (using TSST paradigm) and partially predict physiological/affective stress responses, demonstrating speech as an unobtrusive multidimensional stress indicator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether speech can serve as an unobtrusive, automatic indicator for detecting stress situations and predicting physiological and affective stress responses, enabling applications in behavioral research and clinical assessment without invasive measurements.

Method: 50 participants underwent either the Trier Social Stress Test (TSST) or a non-stressful control condition. Speech was recorded and processed through a pipeline involving speaker diarization, acoustic-prosodic feature extraction, and machine learning models. Feature importance analyses were conducted to identify the most informative predictors.

Result: Stress detection performance significantly exceeded a mean baseline. Physiological and affective stress responses were partially predictable from acoustic-prosodic features. Feature-importance analyses identified key predictors driving model performance.

Conclusion: Speech carries meaningful information across multiple dimensions of the human stress response, validating its potential as an unobtrusive, multidimensional marker for stress assessment in both research and clinical contexts.

Abstract: Automatically detecting stress in speech provides an unobtrusive way to gain insights relevant to behavioral research or clinical assessment. This study investigates the automatic differentiation between a stressful and non-stressful situation, and the prediction of physiological and affective stress responses. Speech data was collected from 50 participants who either completed the Trier Social Stress Test (TSST) or a non-stressful control condition. With a processing pipeline that included speaker diarization and machine learning models, we achieved stress detection performance significantly above a mean baseline. Moreover, relevant physiological and affective stress responses were partially predictable from acoustic-prosodic features. Feature-importance analyses identified the most informative predictors contributing to model performance. The findings demonstrate that speech can serve as a meaningful and unobtrusive indicator of multiple dimensions of the human stress response.

</details>


### [144] [Generative Model Proposal based Particle Filtering for Data Assimilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01012)
*Chandni Nagda,Mayank Shrivastavam Gudrun Thorkelsdottir,Gan Zhang,Morteza Mardani,Arindam Banerjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Flow Proposal Particle Filters (FPPF) learns a conditional normalizing flow as a particle proposal distribution that approximates the variance-minimizing optimal proposal. This steers particles toward high-likelihood regions before weighting, reducing degeneracy while retaining exact Bayesian updates via tractable likelihood evaluation. Localization strategies extend the approach to high-dimensional settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical filtering solutions (e.g., Kalman filters) rely on restrictive linear-Gaussian assumptions. Particle filters are theoretically general but suffer from weight degeneracy in high dimensions. Recent generative approaches learn conditional state transitions but lack principled Bayesian updates, causing error accumulation. A method is needed that combines flexible generative modeling with correct Bayesian filtering.

Method: FPPF uses a conditional normalizing flow to learn a proposal distribution approximating the optimal (variance-minimizing) proposal for particle propagation. The flow is trained to condition on observations so that particles are steered toward high-likelihood regions before importance weighting. Because the flow admits tractable density evaluation, exact importance weights can be computed, preserving the Bayesian update step. For high-dimensional problems, localization strategies are incorporated to mitigate the curse of dimensionality.

Result: Extensive experiments across multiple dynamical systems demonstrate that FPPF outperforms statistical baselines (e.g., standard particle filters, ensemble Kalman filters) and other generative filtering methods, particularly in non-linear, non-Gaussian, and high-dimensional regimes.

Conclusion: FPPF successfully bridges the gap between flexible generative models and principled Bayesian filtering, offering a particle filter that resists degeneracy, maintains correct posterior updates, and scales to high dimensions via localization.

Abstract: Data assimilation models state dynamics conditioned on sequential observations, and has wide-ranging scientific applications. In the filtering setting, the goal is to model the posterior over the current state given all observations so far. Classical solutions typically make simplifying distributional or functional assumptions, e.g., linear-Gaussian systems, which can be inaccurate in many scenarios. In principle, particle filters (PFs) remove these assumptions, yet often collapse in high dimensions. Recent generative approaches learn conditional state transitions, but without principled Bayesian updates they do not recover the correct filtering posterior and can accumulate error over long horizons. In this work, we introduce Flow Proposal Particle Filters (FPPF), which learn a conditional generative model based proposal approximating the variance-minimizing optimal proposal for particle propagation. Conditioning on observations steers particles toward high-likelihood regions before weighting, reducing weight variance and delaying degeneracy. Since our proposal admits tractable likelihood evaluation, FPPF computes accurate importance weights and retains a Bayesian update step. We further extend FPPF to high-dimensional problems through localization strategies, adressing another standard PF failure mode. Extensive experiments on a variety of dynamical systems show that FPPF outperforms statistical baselines and other generative methods in non-linear, non-Gaussian, and high-dimensional regimes.

</details>


### [145] [Seahorse: A Unified Benchmarking Framework for Spatiotemporal Event Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01022)
*Yahya Aalaila,Gerrit Großmann,Sebastian Vollmer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SEAHORSE is a unified benchmarking framework for neural spatiotemporal point processes (STPPs) that standardizes training, evaluation, and comparison across model families. Paired with the HawkesNest synthetic stress-test suite, it reveals how increasing event-pattern complexity exposes distinct inductive biases, causing some models to degrade sharply while others remain stable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural STPPs have proliferated with diverse architectures (expressive intensity models, conditional density models, latent dynamics, normalizing-flow decoders, score-based mechanisms), but fair comparison is undermined by inconsistent preprocessing, coordinate normalization, data splits, likelihood conventions, and evaluation protocols across implementations. A standardized, reproducible benchmarking framework is needed for both fair comparison and controlled diagnostic studies.

Method: The authors propose SEAHORSE, which formalizes neural STPPs through a unified encode-evolve-decode interface and provides a single executable benchmark protocol with raw-coordinate likelihood reporting. They introduce HawkesNest, a synthetic stress-test suite with controlled event-pattern complexity, to conduct diagnostic studies that probe each model family's inductive biases under increasing difficulty.

Result: Increasing event-pattern complexity in HawkesNest exposes each model family's inductive biases: some models degrade sharply under stress conditions while others remain stable, demonstrating that architectural choices confer distinct robustness profiles not visible under prior inconsistent evaluation regimes.

Conclusion: SEAHORSE enables the first truly fair and reproducible comparison of neural STPPs by eliminating implementation-level confounds. The HawkesNest stress tests further demonstrate that model ranking is not static but depends on data complexity, underscoring the importance of controlled diagnostic benchmarking for guiding model selection in real-world spatiotemporal event modeling tasks.

Abstract: Spatiotemporal point processes (STPPs) model event data in continuous time and space, with applications in mobility, epidemiology, and public safety. Recent neural STPPs span expressive intensity models, conditional density models, continuous-time latent dynamics, normalizing-flow spatial decoders, and score-based generative mechanisms. Yet comparison remains fragile because implementations differ in preprocessing, coordinate normalization, splits, likelihood conventions, and evaluation protocols. We present SEAHORSE, a unified framework for reproducible STPP experimentation. SEAHORSE formalizes neural STPPs through a common encode-evolve-decode interface and trains, tunes, and evaluates every model family under a single executable benchmark protocol with raw-coordinate likelihood reporting. This enables fair comparisons but, more importantly, controlled diagnostic studies. We pair SEAHORSE with HawkesNest, a synthetic stress-test suite, and show that increasing event-pattern complexity exposes each family's inductive bias, degrading some models sharply and leaving others stable. Code: https://github.com/YahyaAalaila/seahorse.

</details>


### [146] [The Model Organism Lottery: Model Organism Interpretability Strongly Depends on Training Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01033)
*Andrzej Szablewski,Gabriel Konar-Steenberg,Raffaello Fornasiere,Nikita Menon,Stefan Heimersheim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper challenges the validity of model organisms (MOs) as testbeds for white-box interpretability by constructing 54 MO variants across 7 training techniques, showing that interpretability depends heavily on training objective, target behavior, model architecture, and data pipeline — with more realistic integrated training often yielding less interpretable MOs than standard post-hoc methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work suggests that post-hoc supervised fine-tuning (SFT) — the standard method for constructing model organisms — may make hidden behaviors unrealistically easy to detect via interpretability techniques. The paper systematically investigates whether this concern is justified and whether current MOs provide valid proxies for evaluating interpretability methods.

Method: The authors construct 54 MOs based on OLMo2-1B and Gemma-3-1B-it using seven training techniques: standard post-hoc SFT, post-hoc DPO, and realistic "integrated training" where MO data is inserted into the OLMo post-training DPO phase. They benchmark these MOs using activation oracles, activation steering, logit lens, and sparse autoencoders, controlling for target behavior expression strength.

Result: MO interpretability varies significantly with training objective, target behavior, model architecture, and data generation pipeline. Substantial variance persists even after controlling for behavior expression strength. Crucially, more realistic integrated training often produces MOs that are less interpretable than those from standard post-hoc methods.

Conclusion: Current MO construction practices — particularly post-hoc fine-tuning — may systematically overestimate the effectiveness of interpretability techniques. The findings cast substantial doubt on the validity of existing MOs as interpretability proxies and call for more realistic MO construction methodologies.

Abstract: Model organisms (MOs) - language models trained to exhibit undesired or unnatural behaviours - are frequently used as testbeds for evaluating white-box interpretability techniques. Current MOs are typically constructed via post-hoc supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on behavioural transcripts or synthetic documents. Prior research has shown that interpretability methods can easily identify hidden behaviours in these MOs. However, recent work suggests that such post-hoc training methods may make interpretability unrealistically easy. We investigate this claim by constructing a suite of 54 $\verb|OLMo2-1B|$- and $\verb|gemma-3-1b-it|$-based MOs trained with seven different techniques, including standard post-hoc SFT, post-hoc DPO, and more realistic integration of MO data into the OLMo post-training DPO phase. We use these MO variants to benchmark activation oracles, activation steering, logit lens, and sparse autoencoders. Our findings show that (i) MO interpretability depends strongly on training objective, target behaviour, model architecture, and training data generation pipeline; (ii) substantial variance remains even after controlling for differences in the strength of target behaviour expression; and (iii) our more realistic $\textit{integrated training}$ often yields less interpretable MOs than standard post-hoc methods. Our results cast substantial doubt on the validity of current MOs as interpretability proxies.

</details>


### [147] [GSRQ: Gain-Shape Residual Quantization for Sub-1-bit KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01065)
*Soosung Kim,Minjae Park,Eui-Young Chung,Jaeyong Chung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Residual Quantization for KV cache compression suffers from centroid shrinkage in high-dimensional ℓ2 K-means. The authors propose Gain-Shape K-Means (GSKM) as a drop-in replacement that improves directional fidelity, then build Gain-Shape Residual Quantization (GSRQ), achieving massive accuracy gains (22.20 pp) over VQLLM at 1-bit on LongBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context LLM deployment is bottlenecked by KV cache memory, which grows linearly with context length. While Residual Quantization (RQ) enables sub-1-bit compression, existing methods use standard ℓ₂ K-means for codebook learning. In high dimensions, Euclidean centroid averaging causes centroid shrinkage, weakening angular alignment and degrading directional preservation — a critical issue for attention mechanisms that rely on dot-product similarity.

Method: The authors propose Gain-Shape K-Means (GSKM), which decouples each vector into a scalar gain and a unit-norm shape, applying K-means clustering on the shape vectors (angular space) while separately quantizing gains. This is then integrated into a weighted RQ pipeline to form Gain-Shape Residual Quantization (GSRQ), where each residual quantization stage uses GSKM with learned step-specific weights.

Result: On LLaMA-3-8B with LongBench benchmarks, GSRQ substantially outperforms existing KV cache quantization baselines. At 1-bit precision, GSRQ achieves 33.54 average accuracy compared to VQLLM's 11.34 — a 22.20 percentage point improvement.

Conclusion: GSKM effectively mitigates centroid shrinkage in high-dimensional KV cache quantization, and GSRQ provides a practical, high-accuracy solution for sub-1-bit KV cache compression, significantly closing the gap to uncompressed performance.

Abstract: The deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs) with extended context windows is increasingly constrained by the linear growth of Key-Value (KV) cache memory. Vector Quantization (VQ), particularly Residual Quantization (RQ), is a promising approach for pushing KV cache storage toward the sub-1-bit regime by progressively encoding residuals with small codebooks. However, most VQ methods still rely on standard $\ell_2$ $K$-means as the core codebook-learning primitive. We identify a subtle high-dimensional issue of this primitive: Euclidean centroid averaging can induce centroid shrinkage, which weakens the angular alignment term in the $\ell_2$ distortion and makes directional preservation harder. To address this issue, we propose Gain-Shape $K$-means (GSKM), a drop-in replacement for $K$-means that improves directional fidelity while matching, and in some regimes improving, $\ell_2$ distortion. We then build Gain-Shape Residual Quantization (GSRQ) by incorporating a weighted extension of GSKM into an RQ pipeline. On LLaMA-3-8B, GSRQ substantially improves over strong KV cache quantization baselines across bit rates. At 1-bit, it improves the average accuracy across LongBench tasks from 11.34 to 33.54, a gain of 22.20 percentage points over VQLLM.

</details>


### [148] [Balancing Expressivity and Learnability in Quantum Kernel Bandit Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01080)
*Yuqi Huang,Vincent Y. F. Tan,Sharu Theresa Jose*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes approximate/projected quantum kernels for GP bandit optimization to reduce model complexity and regret, deriving misspecified regret bounds that guide optimal complexity selection. Outperforms full quantum kernels in sample efficiency and scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NISQ-era tasks (quantum control, state preparation, VQAs) motivate GP bandits with quantum kernels, but full high-dimensional quantum kernels increase information gain and cumulative regret, harming learnability. There is a need for reduced-complexity quantum kernel methods that retain quantum inductive biases while enabling tractable optimization.

Method: Authors propose (1) projected quantum kernels and classical kernel approximation techniques to reduce feature dimensionality while preserving key quantum properties; (2) misspecified GP bandit algorithms using these approximate kernels; (3) regret bounds characterizing the approximation-error vs. information-gain trade-off, providing principled guidance for optimal model complexity selection.

Result: Empirically, the proposed approximate/projected methods outperform full quantum kernels in sample efficiency and substantially reduce computational overhead, enabling scalable GP optimization for quantum-native applications.

Conclusion: Approximate quantum kernels with misspecified GP bandits provide a principled, scalable approach to quantum kernel optimization, with regret bounds that explicitly guide the complexity-approximation trade-off.

Abstract: We investigate Gaussian process (GP) bandit optimization with quantum kernels, assuming the mean reward function lies in the reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) induced by the quantum kernel. This setting is motivated by NISQ-era tasks such as quantum control, state preparation and variational quantum algorithms. While quantum kernels can offer a `quantum advantage' via domain-specific inductive biases, naïvely using full, high-dimensional kernels increases model complexity and information gain, leading to higher cumulative regret and poor learnability. To address this, we propose projected quantum kernels and classical kernel approximation techniques that reduce feature dimensionality while preserving key quantum properties. Using these approximate kernels, we develop misspecified GP bandit algorithms and derive regret bounds that characterize the trade-off between approximation error and information gain. The regret bounds provide principled guidance for selecting the optimal model complexity. Empirically, our methods outperform full quantum kernels in sample efficiency, while substantially reducing computational overhead, enabling scalable GP optimization for quantum-native applications.

</details>


### [149] [When Context Compensates for Sparse Event History: AlphaEarth for Spatio-Temporal Point-Process Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01082)
*Yahya Aalaila,Mouad Elhamdi,Gerrit Großmann,Daniel Jenson,Elizaveta Semenova,Sebastian Vollmer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: When local event history is sparse, augmenting spatio-temporal point-process models with exogenous spatial context (AlphaEarth embeddings) substantially improves out-of-region forecasting—yielding 2–6× gains at 1–2 weeks of history, diminishing to 10–20% at 20–104 weeks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatio-temporal point-process models struggle to generalise across space when local event histories are sparse. The authors investigate whether exogenous spatial context can compensate in data-scarce regimes, enabling better spatial transfer.

Method: A fixed log-Gaussian Cox process backbone is used as the base model. This event-only model is compared against the same architecture augmented with AlphaEarth embeddings as linear spatial context. Spatial transfer is evaluated on emergency medical services (EMS) forecasting across eight held-out regions, using fixed forecast anchors and a sweep over history length w. Only AlphaEarth embeddings available strictly before each anchor are used to prevent temporal leakage.

Result: AlphaEarth embeddings improve out-of-region predictive performance across all history regimes. Gains are largest under scarce histories: approximately 2–6× multiplicative improvement at 1–2 weeks, tapering to roughly 10–20% at 20–104 weeks.

Conclusion: Contextual information from exogenous spatial embeddings can substantially stabilise spatially transferred point-process forecasts when event history is limited, demonstrating that spatial context is a viable remedy for data sparsity in spatio-temporal forecasting.

Abstract: Spatio-temporal point-process models must often generalise across space when local event histories are sparse. We study whether exogenous spatial context can compensate in such regimes. Using a fixed log-Gaussian Cox process backbone, we compare an event-only model with the same model augmented by AlphaEarth embeddings as linear spatial context. We evaluate spatial transfer on emergency medical services (EMS) forecasting across eight held-out regions, fixed forecast anchors, and a sweep over history length $w$, using only AlphaEarth (AE) embeddings available strictly before each anchor. AE improves out-of-region predictive performance across all history regimes, with the largest gains under scarce histories: approximately $2$--$6\times$ multiplicative improvements at $1-2$ weeks, tapering to roughly $10$--$20\%$ at $w=20$--$104$ weeks. These results show that contextual information can substantially stabilise spatially transferred point-process forecasts when event history is limited.

</details>


### [150] [Staleness-Learning Rate Scaling Laws for Asynchronous RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01083)
*Jingwei Song,Haofeng Xu,Jie Xiao,Chengke Bao,Jingwei Shi,Pengbin Feng,Weixun Wang,Yuhang Han,Chuan Wu,Linfeng Zhang,Bill Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes how stale rollouts in asynchronous GRPO affect policy optimization stability. It formalizes the behavior policy in the surrogate objective, derives a per-step surrogate-gradient bias of O(S·η), and establishes a two-constraint stability condition revealing when staleness vs. cumulative drift dominates collapse behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-throughput RLHF systems commonly decouple rollout generation from policy optimization for efficiency, but this introduces staleness where the learner uses outdated rollouts. The impact of this staleness on training stability and convergence in GRPO is not well understood theoretically.

Method: The authors make the behavior policy explicit in the GRPO surrogate objective and distinguish between the surrogate-gradient mapping and the true total derivative of the distribution-dependent population objective. Under assumptions of local boundedness, distributional smoothness, and behavior-policy smoothness, they derive bias bounds and stability conditions analytically.

Result: Stale rollouts introduce a per-step surrogate-gradient bias of order O(S·η), where S is maximum rollout lag and η is the learning rate. A conditional collapse-time scaling law is derived: when within-cycle drift stays below a batch-level clipping radius, collapse depends primarily on cumulative learner drift T·η; when the stale-rollout constraint is active, stability depends explicitly on S·η. This yields a two-constraint stability condition: η ≪ min{R_batch/(S·G_upd), R_crit/(T·G_upd)}.

Conclusion: The analysis explains why maximum stable learning rate may appear weakly dependent on staleness in horizon-limited regimes, and provides theoretical grounding for designing more robust asynchronous RLHF training pipelines with explicit stability constraints.

Abstract: High-throughput RLHF systems often decouple rollout generation from policy optimization, leading to the use of stale rollouts during learner updates. In this work, we study the effect of such staleness in asynchronous GRPO. We make the behavior policy explicit in the GRPO surrogate objective and distinguish between the surrogate-gradient mapping used by the learner and the true total derivative of a distribution-dependent population objective. Under assumptions of local boundedness, distributional smoothness, and behavior-policy smoothness, we show that stale rollouts introduce a per-step surrogate-gradient bias of order O(S * eta), where S denotes the maximum rollout lag and eta denotes the learning rate. We further derive a conditional collapse-time scaling law: when within-cycle drift remains below a batch-level clipping radius, collapse is governed primarily by cumulative learner drift T * eta; when the stale-rollout constraint is active, stability instead depends explicitly on S * eta. This yields a two-constraint stability condition eta << min{R_batch / (S * G_upd), R_crit / (T * G_upd)}, explaining why the maximum stable learning rate may appear weakly dependent on staleness in the horizon-limited regime.

</details>


### [151] [CausalMix: Data Mixture as Causal Inference for Language Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01104)
*Zinan Tang,Yukun Zhang,Shaomian Zheng,Zhuoshi Pan,Qizhi Pei,Dingnan Jin,Jun Zhou,Yujun Wang,Biqing Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CausalMix frames LLM data mixture optimization as a causal inference problem, using Conditional Average Treatment Effect (CATE) estimation to extrapolate optimal data mixtures from small proxy models to larger ones without costly retraining, outperforming static methods like RegMix.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing data mixing methods (e.g., RegMix) assume static data distributions and require expensive retraining when the data pool shifts, preventing seamless scaling from small proxy experiments to larger models and datasets.

Method: CausalMix treats statistical features of the data pool as covariates and domain mixture ratios as a treatment. A causal model is fit on 512 training runs of Qwen2.5-0.5B to estimate CATE, then the learned optimal mixture is extrapolated to an 800K data pool for training a 7B model. The framework also generalizes to long chain-of-thought data on Qwen3-4B-Base. A CATE Interpreter provides visual analysis of the mixing strategy.

Result: CausalMix consistently improves downstream task performance over RegMix and other baselines. The CATE-based extrapolation successfully transfers optimal mixtures from 0.5B proxy runs to 7B model training.

Conclusion: CausalMix provides a causal, interpretable, and scalable framework for LLM data mixture optimization that dynamically adapts to shifting data distributions without retraining proxy models from scratch.

Abstract: In Large Language Model (LLM) training, data mixing plays a pivotal role in determining model performance. Recent methods optimize mixture weights via proxy models, but they rely on the assumption of static data distributions. As a result, when the underlying data pool shifts, these methods require costly retraining from scratch. This limitation restricts their ability to scale seamlessly from small settings to larger data pools and model sizes. In this paper, we propose CausalMix to address this limitation by casting data mixture optimization as a causal inference problem. We formulate the statistical features of the data pool as covariates and the domain mixture as the treatment. After fitting a causal model on 512 runs of Qwen2.5-0.5B to estimate the Conditional Average Treatment Effect (CATE), we extrapolate the optimal mixture for an 800K data pool and apply it to train a 7B model. Furthermore, we successfully generalize the framework to long chain-of-thought data on Qwen3-4B-Base. By leveraging causal modeling to isolate confounding biases, CausalMix dynamically infers state-dependent optimal data mixtures. Extensive experiments show that the mixture guided by CausalMix consistently improves performance across multiple downstream tasks, outperforming RegMix and other baselines. In addition, we use the CATE Interpreter to provide visual analysis of the learned mixing strategy. Overall, CausalMix offers a causal and interpretable framework for optimizing LLM data mixtures.

</details>


### [152] [SynLaD: Latent Diffusion for Generating Synthesizable Molecules Conditioned on 3D Pharmacophore Profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01105)
*Miruna Cretu,John Bradshaw,Patricia Suriana,Saeed Saremi,Omar Mahmood,Kirill Shmilovich,Kangway Chuang,Vishnu Sresht,Colin Grambow*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SynLaD is a latent diffusion model that jointly optimizes pharmacophore-conditioned 3D molecular generation and synthetic accessibility by learning a shared latent space decoded into both 3D structures and synthesis pathways, outperforming baselines in generating diverse, synthesizable, shape-aligned hits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative models for small molecules typically optimize either ligand-based design objectives (e.g., pharmacophore matching) or synthetic accessibility, but not both simultaneously. This creates a bottleneck where high-scoring molecules lack feasible synthesis routes, impeding practical drug discovery.

Method: SynLaD uses an encoder to map molecules into a latent space, with two decoder heads: (1) a geometric head reconstructing atom types and 3D coordinates, and (2) an autoregressive synthesis head outputting synthetic routes in a serialized, reaction-based notation. A diffusion transformer then generates novel latents conditioned on pharmacophore profiles, enabling reaction-constrained generation with pharmacophore-conditioned 3D design within a single unified latent diffusion framework.

Result: In analogue generation tasks for bioactive ligands, SynLaD outperforms existing baselines in generating synthesizable and diverse hits that are shape-aligned with target pharmacophores, demonstrating effective joint optimization of both objectives.

Conclusion: A single latent diffusion model can successfully unify pharmacophore-guided 3D molecular design with synthetic pathway prediction, producing molecules that are both high-scoring and practically synthesizable, thereby addressing a key bottleneck in computational drug discovery.

Abstract: We present SynLaD, a latent diffusion framework for small-molecule generation that unifies ligand-based drug design objectives (what to make) with synthetic accessibility (how to make it). Current models typically optimize one objective at the expense of the other, creating a bottleneck for discovering high-scoring and synthesizable molecules. SynLaD combines reaction-constrained generation with pharmacophore-conditioned 3D design by learning a latent space that decodes to both 3D structures and synthesis pathways. An encoder maps molecules to a latent representation used by two decoder heads: (i) a geometric head that reconstructs atom types and coordinates and (ii) an autoregressive synthesis head that outputs synthetic routes in a serialized, reaction-based notation. A diffusion transformer generates novel latents in the learned space, conditioned on pharmacophore profiles. Across analogue generation tasks for bioactive ligands, SynLaD outperforms existing baselines in synthesizable and diverse hit generation, demonstrating that a single model can produce shape-aligned molecules with feasible synthesis plans.

</details>


### [153] [Muon as a Residual Connection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01124)
*Hao Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Muon's effectiveness is explained as an implicit residual connection: orthogonalizing updates trades immediate gradient fidelity for better representation preservation, benefiting downstream layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Muon optimizer has strong empirical performance in large neural network training, but its success lacks a unified, principled explanation. Existing perspectives are fragmented.

Method: The authors propose a mechanistic interpretation of Muon as an implicit residual connection. They analyze the trade-off between gradient fidelity and representation preservation introduced by orthogonalization, and study this in controlled linear optimization settings.

Result: Muon learns representations that are slower to fit a local target but are easier for downstream layers to exploit, demonstrating a favorable trade-off in representation quality.

Conclusion: Muon can be understood through the lens of implicit residual connections, providing both a conceptual explanation for its success and a design perspective for future optimizers that balance local descent with downstream usability.

Abstract: Muon has recently emerged as one of the most effective optimizers for training large neural networks, yet its empirical success has been explained from several different perspectives. In this paper, we propose a simple mechanistic interpretation: Muon can be understood as an implicit residual connection during training. Specifically, orthogonalizing the update can sacrifice some immediate gradient fidelity while improving representation preservation for downstream layers. We study this trade-off in controlled linear optimization settings, where Muon can learn representations that are slower to fit a local target but easier for downstream layers to exploit. Our results suggest a conceptual explanation for Muon and a design perspective for optimizers that balance local descent with downstream usability.

</details>


### [154] [ZO-Act: Efficient Zeroth-Order Fine-Tuning via One-Shot Activation-Informed Low-Rank Subspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01125)
*Xun Dong,Yibo Xu,Naigang Wang,Xin Li,Penghang Yin,Zi Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ZO-Act proposes an activation-informed zeroth-order fine-tuning method that restricts perturbations to a low-rank subspace derived from input activations, reducing variance and enabling Adam-based optimization while naturally supporting quantized LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing zeroth-order fine-tuning methods suffer from high-variance gradient estimates because they perturb full model weights or randomly constructed low-dimensional subspaces. There is a need for a more structured, lower-variance ZO approach that is compatible with modern optimizers and quantized models.

Method: ZO-Act computes a small activation basis for each linear layer once at initialization using input activations. It then optimizes only lightweight coefficient matrices within this fixed low-rank subspace via forward-only loss evaluations. This exposes explicit trainable variables compatible with momentum-based optimizers like Adam and keeps low-bit weights frozen for quantized LLM support.

Result: Experiments on Llama-3-8B, OPT-13B, and INT4 Llama-3-8B show consistent improvements over strong ZO fine-tuning baselines on language understanding, question answering, and commonsense reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Restricting ZO perturbations to an activation-derived low-rank subspace reduces both the variance-dependent convergence term and the finite-difference error, with the subspace approximation bias mitigated by the inherent low-rank structure of LLM activations and gradients.

Abstract: Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization enables fine-tuning large language models when backpropagation is unavailable or memory-prohibitive, but existing methods often perturb full model weights or randomly constructed low-dimensional subspaces, yielding high-variance estimates and limited performance. We propose ZO-Act, an activation-informed ZO fine-tuning method that restricts perturbations to a fixed low-rank subspace derived from input activations. For each linear layer, ZO-Act computes a small activation basis once at initialization and optimizes only lightweight coefficient matrices using forward-only loss evaluations. This reduces the effective perturbation dimension, exposes explicit trainable variables compatible with momentum-based optimizers such as Adam, and naturally supports quantized LLM fine-tuning by keeping low-bit weights frozen. We analyze ZO-Act as zeroth-order optimization over a restricted coefficient space and show that perturbing the low-dimensional coefficients reduces both the variance-dependent convergence term and the finite-difference error of the ZO estimator, at the cost of a controlled subspace approximation bias that is mitigated by the low-rank structure of LLM activations and gradients. Experiments on Llama-3-8B, OPT-13B, and INT4 Llama-3-8B show consistent gains over strong ZO fine-tuning baselines across language understanding, question answering, and commonsense reasoning.

</details>


### [155] [GAIA: Geometry-Adaptive Operator Learning for Forward and Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01128)
*Meenakshi Krishnan,Pranav Pulijala,Ke Chen,Haizhao Yang,Ramani Duraiswami*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GAIA introduces a geometry-adaptive integral autoencoder that encodes domain boundaries and interior fields into geometry tokens, enabling a single architecture to solve both forward (including BVPs) and inverse PDE problems on arbitrary geometries without retraining. It achieves state-of-the-art results across seven benchmarks, reducing error by up to 64% on inverse tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing geometry-adaptive neural operators are limited to forward problems where input and output share the same spatial domain. This restricts their use for boundary value problems (BVPs) and inverse problems, where inputs (e.g., boundary conditions, sensor measurements) and outputs (e.g., interior fields) reside on different domains. A unified architecture capable of handling both forward and inverse problems on arbitrary geometries without retraining or iterative optimization is needed.

Method: GAIA uses an integral autoencoder framework. The domain boundary and interior field distribution are encoded into geometry tokens. Integral transform layers are then conditioned on these tokens via cross-attention, allowing convolution kernels to adapt locally to geometric features. This single architecture handles forward problems, BVPs, and inverse problems on arbitrary domains in one forward pass, without iterative optimization, retraining, or graph construction.

Result: GAIA achieved state-of-the-art on every inverse and BVP benchmark tested. Median relative L² error was reduced by 64% on airfoil flow reconstruction and 27% on electrical impedance tomography (EIT) compared to the next best amortized method. It outperformed all baselines on every shape category of the Mechanical Components Benchmark (MCB). On forward problems, GAIA remained competitive with specialized solvers and maintained stable accuracy across varying point resolutions, where transformer-based baselines degraded.

Conclusion: GAIA provides a unified, geometry-adaptive operator learning framework effective for both forward and inverse PDE problems on arbitrary geometries. Its cross-attention conditioning on geometry tokens enables robust performance across diverse tasks without task-specific modifications, setting new benchmarks particularly for inverse problems and BVPs.

Abstract: Operator learning for partial differential equations (PDEs) on arbitrary geometries builds fast neural surrogates for large-scale simulation. Although recent geometry-adaptive neural operators have made substantial progress, they are mainly designed for forward problems in which inputs and outputs share the same spatial domain. This limits their applicability for boundary value problems (BVPs) and inverse problems, where inputs and outputs may live on different domains. We introduce the Geometry-Adaptive Integral Autoencoder (GAIA), an operator learning model that encodes the domain boundary and the interior field distribution into geometry tokens, and conditions integral transform layers on these tokens via cross-attention, allowing the kernel to adapt locally to geometric features. This yields a single architecture for forward (including BVPs) and inverse problems on arbitrary domains in one pass, without retraining, iterative optimization, or graph construction. We evaluate GAIA on seven 2D and 3D benchmarks, four of which are new or substantially extended benchmarks for inverse problems and BVP: electrical impedance tomography, optical tomography, 3D Darcy flow on varying geometries, and a modified setting of Poisson BVP on mechanical components benchmark (MCB). GAIA sets new state-of-the-art results on every inverse and BVP task, reducing median relative $L^2$ error by 64% on airfoil flow reconstruction and 27% on EIT relative to the next best amortized method, and outperforming all baselines on every shape category of MCB. On other forward problems, GAIA is competitive with specialized solvers while maintaining stable accuracy across point resolutions on which transformer-based baselines degrade.

</details>


### [156] [Sequentially-Controlled Interactive Multi-Particle Flow-Maps for Online Feedback-Driven Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01144)
*Binglin Ji,Anindya Sarkar,Hengchang Lu,Jens Sjölund,Yevgeniy Vorobeychik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IMPFM is a sequential Monte Carlo-style framework that uses interactive multi-particle flow maps with posterior sample sharing to enable sample-efficient, global exploration for online feedback-driven reward alignment while preventing mode collapse and reward over-optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative model-based reward alignment methods only perform local exploration within narrow distributional regions, failing when preferences are unknown a priori and must be inferred through sequential feedback. This creates a need for broad, global exploration to discover high-utility regions without prior preference knowledge.

Method: IMPFM transports an ensemble of interacting particles toward a target distribution using flow maps. At each resampling step, individual particle drift is corrected via collective posterior samples from the entire ensemble, enabling posterior sample sharing. A multi-particle interaction-aware reweighting mechanism balances exploration and exploitation, while a derived Feynman-Kac corrector progressively steers the system toward a KL-tilted target distribution to preserve structural diversity.

Result: Extensive empirical evaluations and ablations across diverse search and alignment tasks demonstrate IMPFM's efficacy against existing baselines.

Conclusion: The IMPFM framework provides a principled, sample-efficient solution for online feedback-driven search and heterogeneous preference alignment, overcoming weight degeneracy and mode collapse through sequentially corrected multi-particle dynamics.

Abstract: While generative models have enabled training-free reward alignment, current methods typically excel in local exploration within narrow regions of the underlying distribution. These approaches struggle when preferences are unknown a priori and only revealed through sequential feedback-a scenario demanding broad exploration to uncover high-utility regions. To address this, we propose Sequentially-Controlled Interactive Multi-Particle Flow-Maps (IMPFM), a framework for sample-efficient online feedback-driven search. IMPFM progressively transports a group of interactive particles toward the target distribution, maintaining the broad coverage essential for heterogeneous preference alignment. IMPFM introduces a principled and efficient posterior sample sharing mechanism across particles powered by flow maps. By correcting individual particle drift with the collective posterior samples of the entire ensemble at each resampling step, the framework maximizes sample utility to enable global exploration while actively mitigating reward over-optimization, typical of standard control frameworks. Paired with a principled exploration-exploitation reweighting mechanism involving multi-particle interaction, this sequentially corrected multi-particle dynamics explicitly preserves structural diversity and overcomes the weight degeneracy inherent to standard SMC samplers. Crucially, we prove that the resulting sampling framework yields a multi-particle interaction-aware Feynman-Kac corrector that progressively steers the multi-particle system toward a KL-tilted target distribution, facilitating global exploration and preventing mode collapse. Extensive empirical evaluations and rigorous ablations across diverse search and alignment tasks confirm the efficacy of IMPFM over existing baselines.

</details>


### [157] [A Lightweight Self-Supervised Learning Framework for Multivariate Time Series using Hierarchical-JEPA on ECG Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01145)
*Siwon Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ER-JEPA is a lightweight hierarchical self-supervised learning framework for multivariate ECG time series, inspired by cardiologists' diagnostic workflow. It uses two stacked Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures with a Vision Transformer backbone, pretrained on ~180k recordings, achieving SOTA on the ST-MEM benchmark with minimal resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical ECG analysis often faces limited annotated data paired with abundant unannotated data. SSL methods can exploit large unlabeled datasets, but existing approaches may be computationally heavy or fail to capture hierarchical temporal structure. The authors aim to design a lightweight, hierarchical SSL framework that mirrors cardiologists' diagnostic reasoning — first inspecting individual time intervals, then analyzing their sequential relationships.

Method: ER-JEPA (Event Reconstruction Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture) features: (1) a two-stage structure that builds per-interval representations and then treats them as a univariate series; (2) hierarchical integration of two JEPAs (H-JEPA), encoding multiple abstraction levels; (3) a ViT backbone. The model is pretrained on ~180,000 10-second 12-lead ECG recordings and evaluated on the ST-MEM benchmark, with sensitivity analysis of hierarchical representations during pretraining.

Result: The H-JEPA model achieves state-of-the-art downstream performance on the ST-MEM benchmark, with rapid computation and minimal resource usage compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: Hierarchical JEPA architectures (H-JEPA) are effective for multivariate ECG representation learning, successfully encoding multi-level abstract representations. The lightweight design enables strong performance without heavy computational demands, validating the cardiologist-inspired two-stage diagnostic structure for time-series SSL.

Abstract: Data analysis in the medical domain often encounters scenarios involving a limited target dataset and a large, unannotated dataset with a general distribution. Under such circumstances, self-supervised learning (SSL) methods are highly effective for utilizing large datasets, making them a popular choice for electrocardiogram (ECG) analysis. This work presents the Event Reconstruction Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (ER-JEPA), a lightweight SSL framework for multivariate time series, whose name and two-fold hierarchical structure are inspired by the diagnostic approach of cardiologists. At its core, ER-JEPA features: (1) a two-stage structure that constructs representations for each time interval and subsequently processes these representations as a univariate time series, (2) the hierarchical integration of two Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs), and (3) a Vision Transformer (ViT) backbone. The structural concatenation of two JEPAs categorizes the model as a Hierarchical JEPA (H-JEPA), designed to encode multiple levels of abstract representations for enhanced prediction on complex tasks. This study reports a successful application of H-JEPA to 12-lead ECG data as a multivariate time series alongside an analysis of the sensitivity of hierarchical representation during the pretraining stage. Pretrained on approximately 180,000 10-second recordings, the model achieves state-of-the-art downstream performance on the ST-MEM benchmark, with rapid computation and minimal resource usage.

</details>
