### [158] [Efficient Compression of Structured and Unstructured Volumes via Learned 3D Gaussian Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01164)
*Landon Dyken,Sharmistha Chakrabarti,Nathan Debardeleben,Steve Petruzza,Qi Wu,Will Usher,Sidharth Kumar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes an explicit volume data compression model based on 3D Gaussian primitives, reinterpreted as a scalar field representation. It eliminates the need for mesh storage in unstructured volumes, achieves competitive reconstruction quality with faster training on structured volumes, and significantly outperforms existing implicit neural representations (INRs) on unstructured volumes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing implicit neural representations (INRs) for unstructured volume data compression do not encode geometry, requiring partial mesh storage for later sampling, which limits achievable compression ratios. Additionally, novel view synthesis methods using 3D Gaussians have shown promise for volume visualization, motivating the adaptation of explicit Gaussian primitives for volume compression to overcome INR limitations.

Method: The authors reinterpret collections of 3D Gaussians as an explicit scalar field representation, reconstructing scalar values via weighted aggregation of intersecting Gaussians. Key technical contributions include: (1) optimized CUDA-accelerated pipelines for both structured and unstructured model sampling, (2) loss functions promoting accurate domain encoding, and (3) a novel sampling-error-based densification strategy. The explicit formulation naturally encodes domain geometry, removing the mesh storage requirement for unstructured volumes.

Result: On structured volumes, the explicit Gaussian model achieves competitive reconstruction quality with significant training speedups compared to INRs. On unstructured volumes, it markedly outperforms existing INR methods across all evaluation metrics while naturally encoding domain geometry without mesh storage.

Conclusion: The proposed explicit 3D Gaussian-based model represents a compelling alternative to implicit neural representations for volume data compression, particularly for unstructured volumes where it eliminates mesh storage overhead and achieves superior reconstruction performance.

Abstract: Recent work has shown that implicit neural representations (INRs) can be trained to effectively compress structured and unstructured volume data, allowing for direct data querying with a reduced memory footprint. However, as existing INRs for unstructured volumes do not encode geometry, they require partial mesh storage for later sampling, limiting achievable compression. At the same time, novel view synthesis methods have shown that explicit collections of 3D Gaussians can be used to accurately visualize volume data. In this work, we introduce an explicit model for volume data compression based on 3D Gaussian primitives. We reinterpret collections of 3D Gaussians as an explicit representation of a scalar field and use a sampling strategy that reconstructs scalar values at spatial locations through weighted aggregation of intersecting Gaussians. We develop optimized CUDA-accelerated pipelines for structured and unstructured model sampling, loss functions that encourage accurate domain encoding by our models, and a novel sampling-error based densification strategy. Our explicit formulation naturally encodes domain geometry, eliminating the need for mesh storage in unstructured volumes and introducing significantly higher compression opportunities. Compared to existing INRs, we demonstrate that our explicit model achieves competitive reconstruction quality with significant training speedups on structured volumes, while markedly outperforming in all metrics on unstructured volumes.

</details>


### [159] [QuasiMoTTo: Quasi-Monte Carlo Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01179)
*Michael Y. Li,Anthony Zhan,Kanishk Gandhi,Noah D. Goodman,Emily B. Fox*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QuasiMoTTo replaces i.i.d. parallel sampling with correlated samples via quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC) applied to the inverse-CDF reparameterization of autoregressive sampling. This reduces redundancy while preserving marginal distributions, achieving 25–47% sample efficiency gains on reasoning benchmarks and 50% fewer training steps in GRPO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parallel sampling for inference-time compute scaling and RL relies on i.i.d. samples, which are inherently redundant — independently drawn samples frequently cover overlapping regions of the output space. The authors argue this waste is not fundamental: a design space of marginal-preserving yet correlated samplers exists that can improve sample efficiency without sacrificing the parallelism that makes scaling trivial.

Method: QuasiMoTTo reparameterizes autoregressive token sampling as inverse-CDF sampling, then draws the underlying uniform random variables using quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC) — specifically, low-discrepancy sequences (e.g., Sobol) that spread points more evenly than i.i.d. uniforms. This yields a correlated batch where each sample is marginally distributed according to the LM, enabling use in policy-gradient training. A novel unbiased bootstrap estimator is developed to evaluate correlated samplers, since dependence breaks standard pass@k estimators.

Result: On four reasoning benchmarks, QuasiMoTTo matches i.i.d. pass@k accuracy with 25–47% fewer samples and often saturates a theoretical upper bound for any marginal-preserving sampler. In GRPO policy-gradient RL, it matches i.i.d. performance with 50% fewer training steps. Gains stem from higher output-space coverage per batch, yielding stronger learning signals.

Conclusion: Correlated sampling via QMC is a practical, drop-in replacement for i.i.d. sampling that substantially improves the compute-efficiency of both inference-time scaling and RL training for language models, without altering model architecture or the marginal distribution of samples.

Abstract: Scaling inference compute, by generating many parallel attempts per problem, is a costly but reliable lever for improving language model capabilities. By default these attempts are generated independently, wasting inference compute on redundant solutions. This waste seems unavoidable. After all, independence is what makes parallel sampling trivial to scale. However, this tradeoff is not fundamental: there is a rich design space of samplers that generate correlated but exact samples entirely in parallel. We explore this design space as an avenue for improving sample efficiency in scaling inference compute and reinforcement learning (RL). Concretely, we introduce QuasiMoTTo, which uses correlated samples as a drop-in replacement for i.i.d. samples. To generate these samples, QuasiMoTTo uses a reparameterization of autoregressive sampling as inverse-CDF sampling and draws the underlying uniforms with quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC); because QMC spreads the uniforms out more evenly than i.i.d., the resulting samples cover the output space with far less redundancy. Even though the batch is correlated, each sample is marginally distributed according to the language model, so we can use the batch for policy-gradient training. Our empirical analysis focuses on understanding how efficiently QuasiMoTTo can turn compute into performance. To evaluate correlated samplers, whose dependence breaks standard pass@k estimators, we first develop an unbiased bootstrap estimator. Across four reasoning benchmarks, QuasiMoTTo matches i.i.d. pass@k accuracy with 25-47% fewer samples. Strikingly, QuasiMoTTo often saturates an upper bound on pass@k that holds for any marginal-preserving sampler. We also apply QuasiMoTTo to policy-gradient RL (GRPO) where it matches i.i.d. performance with 50% fewer training steps. These gains come from higher coverage, which yields a stronger learning signal per batch.

</details>


### [160] [Decision-Aware Training for Sample-Based Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01171)
*Kornelius Raeth,Nicole Ludwig*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes decision-aware training for sample-based generative models by augmenting the energy score with a differentiable decision loss, ensuring forecasts are optimised for downstream cost structures while retaining full probabilistic outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard training of sample-based generative models uses strictly proper scoring rules (e.g., energy score) that allocate supervision proportional to data density, ignoring the asymmetric costs of forecast errors in high-stakes decision settings. This misalignment means models may perform well statistically but poorly on downstream decision tasks.

Method: The authors augment the energy score objective with a differentiable decision loss that directly penalises the cost incurred by acting on the model's forecast. The decision loss is shown to be a proper scoring rule itself, providing theoretical grounding. The combined loss is optimised end-to-end for sample-based generative models.

Result: The method is validated on one synthetic and two real-world tasks, demonstrating targeted improvements in cost-sensitive regions while retaining full probabilistic forecast capability.

Conclusion: Decision-aware training bridges the gap between probabilistic forecast quality and downstream decision utility, enabling sample-based generative models to produce forecasts that are both well-calibrated and cost-effective for specific decision contexts.

Abstract: Sample-based generative models are increasingly used for probabilistic forecasting in high-stakes decision settings, yet their training objectives are blind to the decision maker's cost structure. These models are commonly trained with strictly proper scoring rules, such as the energy score, which allocate their training signal in proportion to data density, with no awareness of where forecast errors are most costly for downstream decisions. We therefore propose decision-aware training for sample-based generative models, augmenting the energy score objective with a differentiable decision loss that directly penalises the cost incurred by acting on the model's forecast. This combined loss is theoretically grounded, as the decision loss is itself a proper scoring rule. We validate our method on one synthetic and two real-world tasks, showing targeted improvements in cost-sensitive regions while retaining full probabilistic forecasts.

</details>


### [161] [Right in the Right Way: LM Training with Verifiable Rewards and Human Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01181)
*Mehul Damani,Isha Puri,Idan Shenfeld,Jacob Andreas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes an adversarial generator-discriminator framework that combines RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR) with a learned discriminator signal from human demonstrations, enabling joint optimization of both verifiable task accuracy and non-verifiable human-like qualities such as style and diversity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RLVR methods optimize only objectively measurable metrics, ignoring subjective qualities like style, structure, and naturalness. This leads to diversity collapse, unnatural outputs, and reward hacking. The authors aim to bridge this gap by incorporating a learned signal that captures the human output distribution.

Method: An adversarial framework where: (1) a generator model is trained via RL to maximize both task accuracy (verifiable reward) and an adversarial reward from a discriminator; (2) the discriminator is trained concurrently to distinguish human-written outputs from model-generated ones, serving as a learned proxy for the human output distribution that provides feedback on non-verifiable aspects.

Result: Across bug fixing, story generation, and a reward hacking benchmark: (1) bug fixing — significantly lower edit distance vs. RLVR baselines while matching end performance; (2) story generation — improved win rate with diverse, more human-like stories; (3) reward hacking benchmark — near-elimination of misbehavior while maintaining high scores.

Conclusion: The proposed framework effectively bridges RL and SFT, offering a scalable approach to jointly optimize verifiable and non-verifiable properties, mitigating key RLVR failure modes without sacrificing task accuracy.

Abstract: RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for training LMs on tasks with well-defined success metrics, such as code generation and mathematical reasoning. However, current RLVR methods optimize only what can be objectively scored, often neglecting subjective, non-verifiable aspects of human-like outputs, such as style and structure. This limitation leads to well-documented failure modes such as diversity collapse, unnatural-sounding responses, and reward hacking. We propose an adversarial generator-discriminator framework that augments verifiable rewards with a learned signal from human demonstrations. A generator model is trained using RL to maximize both task accuracy and an adversarial reward derived from a discriminator. The discriminator, trained alongside the generator policy, learns to distinguish human-written outputs from model-generated ones. The discriminator serves as a learned proxy for the human output distribution, providing feedback on aspects of generation that are difficult to formalize as scalar rewards. Across diverse domains, including bug fixing and open-ended generation, our approach consistently improves non-verifiable properties while preserving the accuracy gains of RLVR. In bug fixing, our method produces solutions with significantly lower edit distance compared to RLVR baselines while matching end performance. In story generation, our method significantly improves win rate while producing stories that are diverse and more human-like. And in a simple reward hacking benchmark, our method nearly eliminates model misbehavior while maintaining high benchmark scores. Together, these results show that our approach bridges RL and SFT, offering a scalable path toward jointly optimizing the verifiable and non-verifiable properties of a task.

</details>


### [162] [Neural Certificate Pricing for Combinatorial Optimization Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01185)
*Jingyi Chen,Xinyuan Zhang,Xinwu Qian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural Certificate Pricing (NCP) trains a neural network to predict dual prices for combinatorial optimization problems, enabling fast primal solution recovery via a structured layer. It amortizes the separation oracle, provides theoretical guarantees linking price errors to objective loss, and achieves strong empirical performance with superior generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Combinatorial optimization suffers from exponential search due to discrete structure, yet feasibility verification is polynomial. The asymmetry between hard certification and easy verification motivates exploiting dual certificate prices to bypass exhaustive enumeration, using unsupervised learning to amortize the separation oracle.

Method: NCP uses an unsupervised framework with two components: (1) a neural network that predicts certificate-level dual prices, and (2) a structured recovery layer that constructs the induced primal marginal from those prices. Under a certificate-consistency condition, the recovered marginal is globally feasible. Theoretically, first-order errors in predicted prices induce only second-order loss in objective value.

Result: Across three classes of CO problems, NCP either outperforms state-of-the-art neural baselines by large margins or matches them at significantly lower computation time. It also demonstrates stronger out-of-distribution generalization.

Conclusion: NCP provides a principled, efficient, and theoretically grounded approach to learning for combinatorial optimization by leveraging the price-certificate duality structure, offering both empirical gains and generalization robustness.

Abstract: Combinatorial optimization (CO) problems are difficult because certifiable discrete structure induces exponential search. One needs to search over the set exponentially many candidates to certify optimality, however, the structural feasibility of a path, packing, or cover can be verified in polynomial time once supplied. In this study, we introduce Neural Certificate Pricing (NCP) that exploits this asymmetry under an unsupervised learning framework. A neural network is trained to predict certificate-level dual prices, while a structured recovery layer constructs the induced primal marginal. NCP can be viewed as amortized separation: instead of enumerating violated inequalities, it learns the residual prices through which their aggregate effect enters recovery. When the certificate-consistency condition holds, the recovered marginal is globally feasible, and a local theory shows that first-order errors in the predicted price induce only second-order loss in objective value. Across three classes of CO problems, NCP either outperforms state-of-the-art neural baselines by large margins or matches them at a fraction of the computation time, and shows stronger out-of-distribution generalization.

</details>


### [163] [Is One Layer Enough? Training A Single Transformer Layer Can Match Full-Parameter RL Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01232)
*Zijian Zhang,Rizhen Hu,Athanasios Glentis,Dawei Li,Chung-Yiu Yau,Hongzhou Lin,Mingyi Hong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RL post-training gains in LLMs are highly concentrated in a small subset of middle transformer layers, rather than distributed uniformly. Training just a single middle layer can recover or exceed full-parameter RL performance. This pattern—termed "layer contribution"—is remarkably stable across models, RL algorithms, and task domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL post-training updates all LLM parameters uniformly, implicitly assuming equal contribution across layers. This work challenges that assumption, seeking to understand how RL adaptation is actually distributed across transformer layers.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic layer-wise ablation study of RL training across seven models (Qwen3, Qwen2.5), three RL algorithms (GRPO, GiGPO, Dr. GRPO), and multiple tasks (math reasoning, code generation, agentic decision-making). They introduce a metric called "layer contribution," which quantifies the fraction of full RL improvement recovered by training a single layer in isolation.

Result: Training a single transformer layer recovers most gains from full-parameter RL and sometimes surpasses it. RL gains are concentrated in a small subset of layers, with high-contribution layers consistently located in the middle of the transformer stack. Input and output layers contribute substantially less. Layer contribution rankings are strongly correlated across datasets, tasks, model families, and RL algorithms.

Conclusion: The uniform-update assumption in RL post-training is incorrect. RL adaptation is structurally concentrated in middle layers, a robust phenomenon with implications for more efficient RL training strategies (e.g., sparse or layer-selective fine-tuning).

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a central component of post-training large language models (LLMs), yet little is understood about how RL adaptation is distributed across transformer layers. Existing approaches typically update all model parameters uniformly, implicitly assuming that every layer contributes similarly to the gains obtained during RL post-training. In this work, we challenge this assumption through a systematic layer-wise study of RL training. Surprisingly, we find that training a single transformer layer can recover most of the gains achieved by full-parameter RL training, and in some cases even surpass it. To quantify this phenomenon, we introduce the quantity layer contribution, which measures the fraction of full RL improvement recovered by training a layer in isolation. Across seven models spanning two model families (Qwen3, Qwen2.5), three RL algorithms (GRPO, GiGPO, Dr. GRPO), and multiple task domains including mathematical reasoning, code generation, and agentic decision-making, we observe a remarkably stable pattern: RL gains are highly concentrated in a small subset of, and in many cases even a single, transformer layers. More strikingly, the same structural pattern consistently emerges: high-contribution layers concentrate in the middle of the transformer stack, while layers near the input and output ends contribute substantially less. The resulting layer rankings remain strongly correlated across datasets, tasks, model families, and RL algorithms.

</details>


### [164] [Quantum vs. Classical Machine Learning: A Unified Empirical Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01197)
*Chuanming Yu,Jiaming Liu,Zihao Ge,Xiongfei Wu,Lulu Zhu,Pengzhan Zhao,Jianjun Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An empirical study comparing seven QML model pairs against classical baselines across supervised and reinforcement learning, finding QML models do not yet surpass classical ones in prediction, stability, or training time, though they show promise in noise filtering and false positive control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Insufficient empirical evidence exists to support claims of quantum advantage in machine learning; the authors aim to systematically benchmark QML models against classical counterparts to fill this gap.

Method: The authors conduct a comparative empirical evaluation of seven quantum-classical model pairs across two domains: supervised learning and reinforcement learning. Models are assessed on prediction performance, policy stability, and training time.

Result: QML models do not yet outperform classical baselines in overall prediction performance, policy stability, or training time. However, QML shows potential advantages in noise filtering and controlling false positives.

Conclusion: QML, while not yet superior to classical methods in most metrics, has niche strengths (noise filtering, false positive control). The study identifies key challenges in hardware, training efficiency, and convergence stability, and provides a foundation for future research on QML robustness and parameter optimization.

Abstract: Quantum computing has emerged as a promising computational paradigm for machine learning (ML), with the potential to offer computational advantages over classical approaches. At this stage, the evidence supporting the performance and advantages of quantum machine learning (QML) models relative to classical models is insufficient.To address this gap, this paper presents an empirical study on the performance of QML models and their classical counterparts. We compare seven model pairs spanning supervised learning and reinforcement learning. Our results indicate that the evaluated quantum machine learning models do not yet surpass the classical baselines in overall prediction performance, policy stability, or training time. Nevertheless, QML remains a promising approach for filtering noise and controlling false positives. Our research findings summarize the challenges facing quantum machine learning across hardware environments, training efficiency, and convergence stability, providing a foundation for research into the robustness and parameter optimization of QML. This work is publicly available at https://github.com/Z-537-437/QML.

</details>


### [165] [TiRex-2: Generalizing TiRex to Multivariate Data and Streaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01204)
*Patrick Podest,Marco Pichler,Elias Bürger,Levente Zólyomi,Bernhard Voggenberger,Wilhelm Berghammer,Daniel Klotz,Sebastian Böck,Günter Klambauer,Sepp Hochreiter*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TiRex-2 is a recurrent xLSTM-based time series foundation model that extends univariate TiRex to multivariate forecasting with both past and future covariates, achieving constant per-patch cost under streaming, state-of-the-art zero-shot performance, and stable operation at arbitrary context lengths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world forecasting is inherently sequential: observations arrive continuously, variables evolve jointly, and some covariates are known ahead of time. Existing Transformer-based time series foundation models suffer from quadratic complexity in context length and require full-history recomputation when new observations arrive, making them inefficient for streaming scenarios.

Method: TiRex-2 employs a memory-centric recurrent design using xLSTM that operates at constant per-patch cost under streaming. It combines: (1) a bidirectional time mixer with an asymmetric grouped-attention variate mixer to integrate future-known covariates while preserving strict causality over target variables; and (2) a synthetic coupling pipeline that composes diverse multivariate samples on the fly from large univariate corpora for scalable multivariate pretraining.

Result: TiRex-2 achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot performance on GIFT-Eval and fev-bench, remains stable when streamed to arbitrary context lengths, and maintains constant inference cost per patch. The model uses 38.4M active parameters in univariate mode, with an additional 44.1M parameters activated for multivariate forecasting.

Conclusion: TiRex-2 is the first time series foundation model to combine recurrent efficiency with future-covariate integration and strict causality, demonstrating that memory-centric architectures can match or exceed Transformer-based models while offering superior streaming properties.

Abstract: We introduce TiRex-2, a recurrent xLSTM-based time series foundation model that generalizes the univariate TiRex to multivariate forecasting with both past and future covariates. Real-world forecasting is inherently sequential: observations arrive continuously, variables evolve jointly, and a subset of covariates is known ahead of time. Existing Transformer-based time series foundation models capture cross-variate dependencies but incur quadratic complexity in context length and require full-history recomputation as new observations arrive. TiRex-2 addresses these limitations through a memory-centric recurrent design that operates at constant per-patch cost under streaming. The model combines a bidirectional time mixer with an asymmetric grouped-attention variate mixer, enabling the integration of future-known covariates while preserving strict causality over target variables. To our knowledge, this is the first time series foundation model that achieves this combination of properties. To support scalable multivariate pretraining, we propose a synthetic coupling pipeline that composes diverse multivariate samples on the fly from large univariate corpora. Empirically, TiRex-2 achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot performance on GIFT-Eval and fev-bench, remains stable when streamed to arbitrary context lengths, and maintains constant inference cost per patch. The model uses 38.4M active parameters in univariate mode, with an additional 44.1M parameters activated for multivariate forecasting.

</details>


### [166] [Language-Critique Imitation Learning from Suboptimal Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01225)
*Chih-Han Yang,Dai-Jie Wu,Yun-Ping Huang,Ping-Chun Hsieh,Kenneth Marino,Shao-Hua Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a language-critique framework for imitation learning from suboptimal demonstrations, replacing scalar supervision signals (confidence scores, importance weights) with structured natural language critiques that describe task progress, failure modes, and corrective actions. The method includes a language-critique loss for training policies directly on text feedback, with instantiations for behavior cloning (LC-BC) and diffusion policies (LC-DP), and a theoretical guarantee that the objective upper-bounds the expert performance gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing imitation learning from suboptimal demonstrations relies on compressed scalar signals (e.g., confidence estimates, discriminator scores, importance weights), which are inherently limited because they cannot explicitly convey intermediate reasoning about task progress, failure modes, or corrective actions. This collapses expressive feedback into a single number, discarding rich supervisory information.

Method: The authors propose a language-critique framework with two components: (1) automatic construction of natural language labels from demonstrations that explicitly describe current progress, identify suboptimal behaviors, and provide fine-grained corrective guidance; (2) a language-critique loss that directly trains policies using these structured text signals without reducing them to scalars. The framework is instantiated for behavior cloning (LC-BC) and diffusion policies (LC-DP). A theoretical result shows that the proposed objective upper-bounds the expert performance gap under standard assumptions.

Result: The methods (LC-BC and LC-DP) were evaluated on diverse continuous control tasks spanning navigation, manipulation, and gameplay, consistently outperforming strong imitation learning and offline reinforcement learning baselines.

Conclusion: Natural language serves as a powerful and structured form of supervision for learning robust policies from suboptimal demonstration data, outperforming approaches that rely on scalar feedback signals.

Abstract: Prior work on imitation learning from suboptimal demonstrations typically relies on compressed supervision signals such as confidence estimates, discriminator scores, or importance weights. These scalar signals are inherently limited, as they cannot explicitly express intermediate reasoning about task progress, failure modes, or corrective actions. We propose a language-critique framework for imitation learning from suboptimal demonstrations that instead leverages natural language as a structured supervision signal, avoiding the collapse of expressive feedback into scalars. Our method first constructs language labels from demonstrations that explicitly describe current progress, identify suboptimal behaviors, and provide fine-grained corrective guidance. We then introduce a language-critique loss that directly trains policies using these structured signals without reducing them to scalars, and instantiate it for both behavior cloning and diffusion policies, yielding LC-BC and LC-DP. We further provide a theoretical result showing that the proposed objective upper-bounds the expert performance gap under standard assumptions. Empirically, we evaluate on diverse continuous control tasks spanning navigation, manipulation, and gameplay, where our methods consistently outperform strong imitation learning and offline reinforcement learning baselines. These results demonstrate that language can serve as a powerful and structured form of supervision for learning robust policies from suboptimal data.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [167] [Constructive Alignment: Governing Preference Dynamics in Human-AI Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00001)
*Max Kanwal,Caryn Tran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Constructive Alignment, a paradigm shift that treats AI alignment not as satisfying static human preferences, but as a control problem over evolving preference trajectories shaped dynamically through human-AI interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard alignment approaches assume human preferences are fixed targets to be inferred and optimized. This conflicts with extensive empirical evidence from behavioral economics and psychology showing that preferences are layered, dynamic, and constructed through interaction — particularly with adaptive technologies. As AI systems become more persistent, personalized, and socially embedded, they increasingly shape what people value over time, rendering static preference models inadequate and potentially dangerous.

Method: The authors draw on behavioral economics, psychology, and constructivist social theory to model preferences as layered state variables that evolve under interaction with AI systems. They formalize this view using a control-theoretic framework where system actions and interaction design jointly influence both world states and human evaluative states. The framework treats alignment as regulating how AI systems influence the evolution of human preferences, rather than controlling AI behavior directly.

Result: The framework yields a set of normative criteria for constructive alignment: value trajectories should remain coherent, reflectively endorsed, epistemically grounded, bounded against manipulation, and empowering under uncertainty. Alignment is reconceptualized as governing long-term value formation.

Conclusion: Alignment should be reframed as the governance of long-term value formation rather than static preference satisfaction. Constructive Alignment provides a control-theoretic foundation for designing AI systems that responsibly shape — rather than merely serve — evolving human preferences.

Abstract: Most approaches to AI alignment treat human preferences as fixed targets to be inferred and optimized. This assumption conflicts with extensive empirical evidence showing that preferences are layered, dynamic, and constructed through interaction--particularly with adaptive technologies. As AI systems become more persistent, personalized, and socially embedded, they increasingly participate in shaping what people attend to, value, and endorse over time. We introduce Constructive Alignment, a paradigm that reframes alignment as a control problem over evolving human preference trajectories rather than static preference satisfaction. Drawing on behavioral economics, psychology, and constructivist social theory, we model preferences as layered state variables that evolve under interaction with AI systems. We formalize this view using a control-theoretic framework in which system actions and interaction design jointly influence both world states and human evaluative states. We argue that alignment is not primarily about controlling AI behavior, but about regulating how AI systems influence the evolution of human preferences--ensuring that value trajectories remain coherent, reflectively endorsed, epistemically grounded, bounded against manipulation, and empowering under uncertainty. Alignment thus becomes a problem of governing long-term value formation rather than simply satisfying static preferences.

</details>


### [168] [Bounded Morality: Defining the Space of Moral Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00002)
*Max Kanwal,Caryn Tran,Patrick Mineault*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes "Bounded Morality," a formal framework extending Herbert Simon's bounded rationality to moral cognition. It models moral situations along two orthogonal dimensions—moral breadth (scope of morally relevant entities) and moral depth (inferential integration required)—and argues that limited computational resources force a tradeoff between them. Ethical theories (deontology, consequentialism, virtue ethics) are reinterpreted as locally efficient strategies adapted to different demand regimes rather than competing accounts of moral truth. The framework introduces formal notions of moral regret and moral progress under constraint, with implications for AI moral alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional moral cognition is modeled as adherence to fixed ethical theories implemented as static rules or value functions, which neglects the computational constraints faced by finite agents. The authors aim to provide a formal, resource-aware account of moral reasoning that explains why different ethical approaches emerge and how they relate to one another under bounded computational capacity.

Method: The authors construct a formal framework with two orthogonal dimensions: (1) moral breadth—the scope of entities accorded moral status, and (2) moral depth—the inferential integration needed to evaluate interactions among those entities. They model limited cognitive resources as imposing a tradeoff boundary that defines a feasible space of moral computation. Within this space, canonical ethical theories are characterized as locally efficient strategies adapted to specific demand regimes along the breadth-depth frontier.

Result: The framework yields a formal definition of moral regret as the gap between an agent's constrained moral computation and what would be possible under unbounded resources. Moral progress is formalized as expansion of the feasible frontier through improved allocation or scaling of moral reasoning capacity. For AI alignment, the framework implies that alignment depends on scaling and allocating moral reasoning capacity rather than on direct imitation of human moral judgments.

Conclusion: Bounded Morality reframes ethical theories not as competing truths but as complementary computational strategies shaped by resource constraints. The framework offers a unified perspective on moral pluralism and suggests that both human moral development and AI moral alignment should be understood in terms of expanding and efficiently allocating bounded moral computation resources.

Abstract: Moral cognition has traditionally been modeled as adherence to fixed ethical theories--deontology, consequentialism, virtue ethics--implemented as static rules or value functions. We propose Bounded Morality, a formal framework for analyzing the computational demands of moral problems faced by finite agents. Extending Herbert Simon's notion of bounded rationality, we formalize moral situations along two orthogonal dimensions: moral breadth, the scope of entities treated as morally relevant, and moral depth, the inferential integration required to evaluate their interactions. Limited resources impose an unavoidable tradeoff between these dimensions, defining a feasible space of moral computation. Within this space, ethical theories correspond to locally efficient strategies adapted to different demand regimes rather than competing accounts of moral truth. The framework yields a formal notion of moral regret and moral progress under constraint, and implies that moral alignment in artificial systems depends on the scaling and allocation of moral reasoning capacity rather than on direct imitation of human judgments.

</details>


### [169] [The MMM Data Model -- A Normative Specification for Knowledge Interoperability in a Decentralisable Knowledge Commons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00032)
*Mathilde Noual*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MMM is a lightweight, label-based data model for knowledge documentation that balances formal structure with human usability, enabling interoperable knowledge sharing across disciplines without requiring semantic convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional document-centric information systems constrain how knowledge is structured, updated, shared, and reused. Formal approaches (e.g., ontologies, structured databases) address these limitations but fail to achieve broad adoption due to poor usability. AI-driven document tools lack a portable, unified alternative to documents for human knowledge exchange. The authors identify a gap in the design space for a system that combines normative constraints with expressive freedom.

Method: The paper presents MMM, a data model derived from practical needs in interdisciplinary collaborative research. It is positioned through a comparative analysis of the design space of information systems. MMM employs a small set of normative constraints paired with free-text labels, deliberately avoiding mandatory semantic convergence to ensure broad applicability. A reference implementation and pilot deployment provide evidence of implementability and early usability.

Result: A reference implementation was built and pilot deployment data were collected, demonstrating that MMM is implementable and exhibits early usability in real-world interdisciplinary settings.

Conclusion: MMM occupies a previously underexplored region in the information system design space — combining minimal normative structure with free-text expressivity — offering a viable path toward interoperable, human-usable knowledge documentation that bridges the gap between rigid formal systems and unstructured documents.

Abstract: Many information systems are built around documents: self-contained units optimised for print production and linear reading. While effective for large-scale dissemination, the document-centric organisation constrains how knowledge can be structured, updated, shared, and reused. Formal approaches address some of these limitations but struggle to achieve widespread contribution and adoption due to their prioritisation of formal structure over other system properties such as human usability and scope. AI systems are reshaping document production, but without providing a unified portable alternative to traditional documents for humans' expression and exchange of knowledge. This paper presents MMM, a data model for knowledge documentation that emerged from the practical needs of interdisciplinary collaborative research, and positioned here within a comparative analysis of the design space of information systems. MMM combines a small set of normative constraints with the expressive freedom of free-text labels. It is designed for interoperability across disciplines, applications and deployments without requiring semantic convergence. A reference implementation and pilot deployment data demonstrate implementability and early usability.

</details>


### [170] [Making Failure Safe: A Constrained, Verifiable Agent Framework for Open-Web Data Collection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00035)
*Bo Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A constrained, verifiable agent framework replaces free-form LLM code generation with typed JSON collector configurations for web scraping. Using a six-type taxonomy, template constraints, static Airflow DAGs, and rule-based quality checking, the system achieves zero execution-stage LLM token usage on 80 verified tasks while providing deterministic, reusable, and low-cost repeated data collection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct LLM-based web scraper generation from natural language is unreliable due to dependency errors, broken selectors, schema mismatches, and heterogeneous page structures. The authors aim to shift from unreliable free-form code generation to a constrained, verifiable, and reusable execution paradigm.

Method: The framework constrains LLM output to typed JSON collector configurations rather than free-form code. It employs: (1) a six-type collector taxonomy for description-based requirement typing, (2) template and utility-function constraints, (3) static Airflow DAG execution, (4) rule-based quality checking, and (5) structured feedback correction that enforces source, field, and execution constraints beyond the initial description.

Result: On 138 tasks, the taxonomy successfully supports description-based requirement typing, but stable instantiation requires completing source, field, and execution constraints. On 80 independently source-verified tasks, the framework achieves zero execution-stage LLM tokens and the lowest average wall-clock time, trading moderate one-shot quality for deterministic, verifiable execution.

Conclusion: The framework offers a reusable, low-cost, and verifiable execution path suited to repeated scheduled open-web data collection, positioning constrained JSON configuration as a viable alternative to free-form LLM code generation for web scraping.

Abstract: LLMs and agents can generate web scrapers from natural-language requirements, but direct generation remains unreliable because of dependency errors, broken selectors, schema mismatches, and heterogeneous page structures. We propose a constrained, verifiable agent framework that shifts LLM output from free-form code to typed JSON collector configurations, combining a six-type collector taxonomy, template and utility-function constraints, static Airflow DAG execution, rule-based quality checking, and structured feedback correction. Experiments on 138 tasks show that the taxonomy supports description-based requirement typing, while confirming that stable instantiation requires completing source, field, and execution constraints beyond the initial description. On 80 independently source-verified tasks, the framework runs with zero execution-stage LLM tokens and the lowest average wall-clock time, trading moderate one-shot quality for a reusable, deterministic, and verifiable execution path suited to repeated scheduled collection. These results position the framework as a reusable, low-cost, and verifiable execution path for repeated open-web data collection.

</details>


### [171] [From Signals to Structure: How Memory Architecture Drives Language Emergence in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00233)
*Yashar Talebirad,Eden Redman,Ali Parsaee,Osmar R. Zaiane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates how two LLM agents invent a shared language de novo in a Lewis signaling game, systematically comparing five memory architectures across varying channel capacities. The key finding: memory architecture is more important than channel capacity for successful coordination. Agents with persistent private notebooks achieve the most reliable performance and benefit from surplus capacity, while stateless agents collapse at high capacity. The authors also show that an information bottleneck-inspired prediction fails—surplus capacity is generally beneficial, not harmful.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on emergent communication in Lewis signaling games has focused primarily on channel capacity constraints, with an information bottleneck argument suggesting an optimal capacity equal to the number of objects. This paper challenges that view by asking whether memory architecture—how agents store and use interaction history—matters more than raw channel capacity for developing stable shared conventions.

Method: The authors employ LLM-based agents in a Lewis signaling game where a sender and receiver must coordinate on a code from scratch. Five memory architectures are systematically compared: stateless (rolling context window only), externalized notebook, and several intermediate variants. Channel capacity (vocabulary size) is varied across conditions. Coordination success rate is the primary metric, and results are analyzed through the lens of an information bottleneck framework.

Result: Memory architecture dominates channel capacity in predicting coordination success. Agents with a persistent private notebook achieve the highest reliability (0.867 ± 0.023 at capacity = 25). Stateless agents peak at moderate capacity and then degrade—a "high-capacity collapse" as vocabulary outgrows what the context window can track. The information bottleneck prediction (optimal at capacity = number of objects) is refuted: bottleneck capacity (= 8) is actually a fragility point, and surplus capacity is generally advantageous. Channel capacity alone cannot predict coordination outcomes.

Conclusion: To understand how signals become language, both channel capacity and memory architecture must be considered jointly. Memory architecture determines whether agents can transform interaction history into stable conventions, with externalized memory (notebooks) enabling agents to leverage surplus channel capacity rather than being overwhelmed by it. The work suggests that the cognitive machinery for storing and retrieving learned conventions is more critical to emergent communication than the raw expressiveness of the signaling channel.

Abstract: How do two agents invent a shared language from scratch? In a Lewis signaling game, a sender and receiver must coordinate on a code using only their interaction history. We study five memory architectures across varying channel configurations with LLM agents and find that memory architecture matters more than channel capacity. Agents with a persistent private notebook benefit from surplus channel capacity and avoid the high-capacity collapse seen in stateless agents, achieving the most reliable coordination ($0.867 \pm 0.023$ at capacity = 25). Stateless agents peak at moderate capacity and then degrade as the vocabulary grows beyond what a rolling context window can track The notebook externalizes learned conventions, freeing agents from having to re-derive codes each round. An information bottleneck-inspired argument predicts an optimal capacity equal to the number of objects. Instead, the bottleneck (capacity = 8) proves to be a fragility point, and surplus capacity is generally better. We show that channel capacity alone cannot predict coordination; memory architecture determines whether agents turn interaction history into stable conventions, and both dimensions are needed to understand how signals become language.

</details>


### [172] [Solution space path planning for supporting en-route air traffic control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00064)
*Yiyuan Zou,Wenying Lyu,Clark Borst*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a conflict-free path-planning algorithm for en-route ATC that prioritizes interpretability, computational efficiency, and alignment with controller decision logic. It integrates three intent-based conflict detection methods into a solution-space framework and proposes two SSPP variants (SSPPV and SSPPE), with SSPPV + zone-based detection achieving 3.69 ms average computation time on operational MUAC Delta sector scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite numerous path-planning algorithms proposed for ATM, operational adoption in tactical ATC remains limited due to a misalignment between algorithmic design priorities and controllers' actual needs. Controllers require decision-support tools that are interpretable, computationally efficient, and explicitly designed for human use, motivating a human-centric algorithmic design approach.

Method: The algorithm integrates three intent-based conflict detection methods (distance-based, time-interval-based, and zone-based) within a solution-space framework. Two search node representations are proposed: vertex-based (SSPPV) and edge-based (SSPPE). Evaluation is conducted on operational scenarios based on the MUAC Delta sector using a 5 nmi grid, comparing both variants across computational speed and solution quality.

Result: SSPPV paired with zone-based conflict detection achieves the best overall performance, computing conflict-free paths in an average of 3.69 ms. This combination outperforms other variant–detection method pairings in both computational speed and solution quality on the MUAC Delta sector benchmark.

Conclusion: A human-centered algorithm design — one that exposes feasible safe actions via solution-space displays and mirrors controllers' natural decision logic — can yield both high interpretability and real-time computational performance, making it suitable for operational tactical ATC decision support.

Abstract: As technology advances, many path-planning algorithms have been proposed for Air Traffic Management, yet their operational adoption in tactical control remains limited, revealing a misalignment between algorithmic design priorities and air traffic controllers' needs. This underscores the need for decision-support solutions that are inherently interpretable, computationally efficient, and explicitly designed for human use. Focusing on this design challenge, this study develops a conflict-free path-planning algorithm for en-route Air Traffic Control (ATC) designed to be compatible with two guiding considerations: (1) the interpretability and flexibility offered by solution-space displays, which motivate constructing an algorithm that exposes all feasible safe actions and accommodates shifting optimization goals; and (2) the decision logic controllers naturally apply when enforcing operational constraints, such as separation standards, maneuverability limits, waypoint minimization, and routing practicality. Centered on these principles, the algorithm integrates three intent-based conflict detection methods -- distance-based, time-interval-based, and zone-based -- within a solution-space framework to identify conflict-free paths in computationally efficient ways. Additionally, vertex-based and edge-based search nodes are proposed for solution space path planning (SSPP), resulting in two variants -- SSPPV and SSPPE, respectively, which are evaluated in terms of computational speed and solution quality. Empirical results show that SSPPV paired with zone-based conflict detection achieves the best performance, computing paths in 3.69 ms on average in operational-relevant scenarios based on the Delta sector of the Maastricht Upper Area Control Centre (MUAC) using a 5 nmi grid.

</details>


### [173] [Agri-SAGE: Simulation-Grounded Multi-Agent LLM for Context-Aware Agricultural Advisory Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00454)
*Vedant Balasubramaniam,Geetha Charan,Manojkumar Patil,Rohit P Suresh,V Priyanka,Kodur Sai Vinay Sathvik,Y. Narahari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agri-SAGE combines retrieval-augmented multi-agent LLM reasoning with APSIM biophysical simulation to generate and validate agronomic advisories, overcoming the rigidity of static guidelines and the physiological implausibility of pure LLM recommendations. Among three reasoning strategies tested over 10 years, Tree of Thoughts achieves the highest yields, while Reflexion delivers comparable results with lower computational cost via episodic memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static agronomic guidelines (e.g., Package-of-Practice) are consistent but cannot adapt to in-season variability, while LLM-based advisory systems can produce credible-sounding but physiologically unsound recommendations. There is a need for a framework that combines the flexibility of LLM reasoning with the biophysical rigor of crop simulation models.

Method: Agri-SAGE is a closed-loop framework integrating retrieval-grounded multi-agent LLM reasoning with APSIM (Agricultural Production Systems sIMulator) biophysical simulation. Three reasoning approaches are evaluated: Plan-and-Solve, Tree of Thoughts, and Reflexion (which uses cross-seasonal episodic memory). A 10-year retrospective analysis compares these against static Package-of-Practice baselines.

Result: All three LLM-based reasoning approaches significantly outperform static PoP baselines. Tree of Thoughts achieves the highest peak yields. Reflexion achieves comparable agronomic outcomes while incurring substantially lower computational cost due to its cross-seasonal episodic memory mechanism.

Conclusion: Integrating multi-agent LLM reasoning with biophysical simulation offers a viable path toward adaptive, physiologically grounded agricultural advisories. The choice of reasoning strategy involves a trade-off between yield optimization (Tree of Thoughts) and computational efficiency (Reflexion).

Abstract: Agricultural advisory systems face a fundamental tension: static agronomic guidelines offer consistent, evidence-based recommendations, yet remain blind to in-season variability and dynamic uncertainties. Recent advisory systems powered by LLMs are liable for a different risk of generating recommendations that are agronomically credible but physiologically unconvincing. Agri-SAGE is a closed-loop framework designed to resolve the above two limitations by integrating retrieval-grounded multi-agent LLM reasoning with APSIM-based biophysical simulation, to generate and validate agronomic advisories. To assess this framework, we evaluate three reasoning approaches, namely Plan-and-Solve, Tree of Thoughts, and Reflexion, over a 10-year retrospective analysis. All three significantly outperform static PoP (Package-of-Practice) baselines, with Tree of Thoughts achieving impressive peak yields. At the same time, Reflexion achieves comparable agronomic outcomes at substantially lower computational cost by leveraging cross-seasonal episodic memory.

</details>


### [174] [RareDxR1: Autonomous Medical Reasoning for Rare Disease Diagnosis Beyond Human Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00147)
*Deyang Jiang,Haoran Wu,Ziyi Wang,Yiming Rong,Yunlong Zhao,Ye Jin,Bo Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RareDxR1 is an end-to-end reasoning LLM for open-domain rare disease diagnosis from unstructured clinical notes, using progressive training, knowledge internalization, reflection-enhanced reasoning sampling, and dual-level curriculum RL to surpass retrieval-based and phenotype-pipeline methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI approaches for rare disease diagnosis rely on pipelined phenotype extraction or retrieval-augmented generation, which introduce critical information loss from predefined ontologies, retrieval bottlenecks, and lack of genuine diagnostic reasoning. An end-to-end, reasoning-centric model is needed that can directly process unstructured clinical notes without structured phenotype dependence or closed-set constraints.

Method: The authors propose (1) a progressive end-to-end training framework that internalizes fragmented rare-disease knowledge directly into model parameters, bypassing RAG and phenotype ontologies; (2) Reflection-Enhanced Reasoning Sampling (RERS), which synthesizes expert-level diagnostic trajectories by learning from failures without human annotation; and (3) a dual-level curriculum reinforcement learning strategy for gradually mastering complex rare disease diagnosis.

Result: RareDxR1 achieves state-of-the-art accuracy across different benchmarks for rare disease diagnosis, demonstrating a significant breakthrough in open-domain settings compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: An end-to-end reasoning-centric LLM with internalized knowledge and autonomous evolutionary learning can overcome the fundamental limitations of retrieval-based and ontology-dependent approaches, enabling robust open-domain rare disease differential diagnosis directly from unstructured clinical text.

Abstract: Rare disease differential diagnosis is a critical yet arduous clinical task, requiring physicians to identify precise phenotypes from complex, unstructured patient symptoms and execute intricate reasoning within a vast search space. However, existing AI approaches typically rely on pipeline-based phenotype extraction or retrieval-augmented generation, which suffer from critical information loss due to predefined ontologies, retrieval bottlenecks, and a lack of diagnostic logic. To address these challenges, we introduce RareDxR1, an end-to-end reasoning-centric large language model designed for open-domain rare disease diagnosis directly from unstructured clinical notes. We design a progressive end-to-end training framework by synergizing knowledge internalization with autonomous evolutionary learning, thereby bypassing reliance on structured phenotypes and closed-set decision-making. To overcome the limitations of RAG and phenotype restriction, we enabled the deep internalization of fragmented rare-disease knowledge directly into the model's parameters. Moreover, to bridge the gap between model generation and expert reasoning, we propose Reflection-Enhanced Reasoning Sampling (RERS), a strategy that synthesizes expert-level diagnostic trajectories by learning from failures without human annotation. Additionally, we propose a dual-level curriculum reinforcement learning approach for gradually mastering rare disease diagnosis. Experimental results demonstrate that RareDxR1 achieves state-of-the-art accuracy across different benchmarks, marking a significant breakthrough in open-domain rare disease diagnosis. Our code and dataset will be publicly available.

</details>


### [175] [A Contextual-Bandit Oversight Game with Two-Sided Informational Asymmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00155)
*Yunjin Tong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies human-AI interaction with two-sided private information — the human knows her own reward function privately, and the AI privately knows the quality of its proposed action. Using a contextual-bandit framework based on CIRL, the authors characterize a "gap of avoidable harm" where a myopic human fails to oversee harmful AI-proposed actions due to non-credible oversight communication, and analyze how this gap resolves dynamically through learning and signaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In real-world human-AI teams, information asymmetry runs both ways: humans have private preferences, and AI agents have private knowledge about action quality (e.g., from inspecting situations the human cannot directly assess). Existing frameworks like CIRL and the Oversight Game do not fully capture this two-sided asymmetry. The authors aim to formally characterize when and why human oversight fails under these conditions and how such failures can be mitigated over time.

Method: The authors introduce a contextual-bandit team game with two-sided asymmetric information, eliminating physical state transitions from the full POMDP setting while retaining a dynamically controlled common belief across rounds. The interface allows the human to play (accept), ask (defer to AI), trust (delegate), or oversee (inspect and decide). They derive two one-shot characterizations: (1) a team optimum benchmark and (2) a behaviorally natural myopic decision rule. The gap between these characterizes a "slab of avoidable harm." They then provide a partial dynamic analysis showing how passive learning and active signaling with one-period-lagged oversight responses can close this gap over repeated rounds.

Result: The analysis reveals a "slab of avoidable harm" — a region where the AI privately knows a proposed action is harmful and shutdown would be beneficial, yet a myopic human, trusting her prior belief, declines to oversee. This gap is identified as the price of non-credible oversight communication. Over repeated rounds, passive learning and active signaling with lagged oversight responses partially resolve this gap dynamically.

Conclusion: Two-sided private information in human-AI oversight creates a fundamental credibility problem: a myopic human's trust in her prior can prevent oversight even when the AI knows the action is harmful. This "avoidable harm" stems from non-credible communication and can be partially mitigated through dynamic learning and signaling across repeated interactions. The contextual-bandit abstraction provides tractable one-shot characterizations that illuminate dynamics otherwise opaque in full POMDP settings.

Abstract: We study runtime human oversight of an AI agent when private information runs in both directions: the human privately knows her reward function, while the AI privately knows the quality of the action it proposes. This is the kind of asymmetry that arises naturally when an autonomous robot or software agent has inspected a situation its human supervisor cannot directly assess. Building on Cooperative Inverse Reinforcement Learning (CIRL) and the Oversight Game, we introduce a contextual-bandit team game with two-sided asymmetric information and a play/ask/trust/oversee interface. The bandit structure removes physical state transitions and thereby yields exact one-shot characterizations that would remain conjectural in the full POMDP setting, though the common belief remains a dynamically controlled state across rounds. We give two one-shot characterizations, a team optimum and a behaviorally natural myopic rule, whose gap is a slab of avoidable harm: a region in which the AI privately knows the proposed action is harmful and shutdown would help, yet a myopic human, trusting her prior, declines to oversee. We show this gap is the price of non-credible oversight communication, and give a partial analysis of how it resolves dynamically over repeated rounds through passive learning and active signaling with a one-period-lagged oversight response.

</details>


### [176] [AutoMem: Automated Learning of Memory as a Cognitive Skill](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01224)
*Shengguang Wu,Hao Zhu,Yuhui Zhang,Xiaohan Wang,Serena Yeung-Levy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoMem is a framework that treats memory management as a trainable skill for LLMs by promoting file-system operations to first-class actions and using two automated loops: one that iteratively refines memory structure (prompts, schemas, action vocabulary) via a strong reviewer LLM, and another that fine-tunes the agent on its own successful memory decisions. Optimizing memory alone yielded ~2-4x performance gains on long-horizon games, making a 32B model competitive with frontier systems like Claude Opus 4.5 and Gemini 3.1 Pro Thinking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon tasks involve thousands of steps where a single memory mistake can remain latent before surfacing, making manual optimization of memory structure and proficiency impractical. The authors reframe memory management as an independently learnable metamemory skill that can be optimized separately from task-action behavior.

Method: AutoMem operates along two automated loops: (1) a structure-refinement loop where a strong LLM reviews full agent trajectories and iteratively revises the memory scaffolding (prompts, file schemas, action vocabulary); (2) a proficiency loop that identifies the agent's own successful memory decisions across many episodes and uses them as supervised fine-tuning signals to improve the model's memory decision-making. File-system operations (read, write, organize) are elevated to first-class actions alongside task actions.

Result: Across three procedurally generated long-horizon games (Crafter, MiniHack, NetHack), memory-only optimization improved base agent performance by ~2-4x, elevating a 32B open-weight model to competitiveness with frontier systems such as Claude Opus 4.5 and Gemini 3.1 Pro Thinking.

Conclusion: Memory management is an independently learnable skill and a high-leverage optimization target for LLM agents on long-horizon tasks, yielding substantial gains without modifying task-action behavior.

Abstract: Memory expertise is a learned skill: knowing what to encode, when to retrieve, and how to organize knowledge--a capacity known in cognitive science as metamemory. We bring this perspective to LLMs by treating memory management as a trainable skill. We promote file-system operations to first-class memory actions alongside task actions, letting the model itself decide how to manage its memory. This memory skill improves along two axes: the structure that supports it (prompts, file schemas, action vocabulary), and the proficiency of the model exercising it. Both axes resist manual optimization: episodes in long-horizon tasks run for thousands of steps, and a single memory mistake can hide long before it surfaces, making human review of full trajectories impractical. We introduce AutoMem, a framework that automates both axes. In the first loop, a strong LLM reviews complete agent trajectories and iteratively revises the memory structure that shapes how the agent interacts with its memory files. In the second loop, the agent's own good memory decisions are identified from many episodes and used as training signal to sharpen the model's memory proficiency directly. Across three procedurally generated long-horizon games (Crafter, MiniHack, and NetHack), optimizing memory alone--without modifying the model's task-action behavior--improved the base agent's performance ~2x-4x, bringing a 32B open-weight model competitive with frontier systems such as Claude Opus 4.5 and Gemini 3.1 Pro Thinking. Our results show that memory management is an independently learnable skill, and a high-leverage objective yielding large gains on long-horizon tasks.

</details>


### [177] [Constructing Epistemic AI Literacy: Detecting Epistemic Aims and Processes in Student-AI Co-Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00211)
*Mengqian Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Epistemic AI Literacy (EAIL), a framework analyzing how students cognitively engage with GenAI during programming. Using the AIR framework on human-AI dialogue data, it identifies epistemic aims and processes, finding 78.8% of interactions lack mastery-oriented aims and rely on shallow strategies like outsourcing, while only 11.1% exhibit high epistemic engagement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI literacy frameworks inadequately capture the process-oriented, epistemic nature of how learners think and reason during human-AI interactions, particularly in programming contexts where students must formulate queries, critically evaluate AI outputs, and regulate their problem-solving. There is a need for a scalable, operationalizable framework to measure epistemic quality in these interactions.

Method: The study proposes the EAIL conceptual framework grounded in the AIR (epistemic aims, ideals, and reliable epistemic processes) model. It then operationalizes this framework on a large dialogue dataset of human-AI co-programming interactions, coding for observable dimensions: mastery-oriented aims as the epistemic aim, and five epistemic processes — outsourcing, explanation seeking, verification seeking, prompt monitoring, and epistemic justification.

Result: 78.8% of student-GenAI interactions relied on non-mastery-oriented aims and less reliable strategies (outsourcing, verification-seeking). Only 11.1% exhibited high epistemic engagement, combining mastery-oriented aims with advanced strategies like epistemic justification within reliable processes.

Conclusion: There is a prevalent deficit in Epistemic AI Literacy among students using GenAI for programming. The EAIL framework provides a scalable method for diagnosing epistemic engagement quality in human-AI interaction data, with implications for designing pedagogical interventions that foster deeper, mastery-oriented epistemic practices.

Abstract: Epistemic thinking plays a central role in students' learning processes when applying generative artificial intelligence (GenAI), particularly in programming contexts where learners must construct queries, evaluate and validate AI-generated outputs, and regulate problem-solving strategies. This study introduces the conceptual framework of Epistemic AI Literacy (EAIL), reframing AI literacy as a process-oriented epistemic phenomenon that emerges through dynamic human-AI interactions across different domains. Drawing on the AIR (epistemic aims, ideals and reliable epistemic processes) framework, this study examines how epistemic aims and epistemic processes are enacted in GenAI-supported co-programming activities and explores scalable approaches for operationalizing these constructs in interaction data. Using a large dialogue dataset of human-AI co-programming, this study identifies observable dimensions of epistemic aims (i.e., mastery-oriented aims) and epistemic processes (i.e., outsourcing, explanation seeking, verification seeking, prompt monitoring, and epistemic justification). The results reveal a prevalent lack of EAIL, with 78.8% of student-GenAI interactions relying on non-mastery-oriented aims and less reliable epistemic strategies like outsourcing and verification-seeking. Conversely, only 11.1% of interactions showed high epistemic engagement, where mastery-oriented aims were coupled with advanced epistemic strategies like epistemic justification in a more reliable epistemic process.

</details>


### [178] [Seed2.0 Model Card: Towards Intelligence Frontier for Real-World Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00248)
*Bytedance Seed*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Seed2.0 is a model series designed to solve complex, real-world tasks by building a benchmark-driven evaluation system grounded in user needs, targeting long-tail knowledge and complex instruction following to improve reliability on long-horizon tasks, while also delivering strong reasoning, visual understanding, and search capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current models struggle with complex, real-world tasks due to gaps in long-tail knowledge coverage and difficulty following intricate instructions over long horizons. The authors aim to bridge this gap by grounding model development in genuine user needs rather than arbitrary benchmarks.

Method: The approach involves: (1) identifying genuine user needs; (2) constructing a forward-looking evaluation system by selecting and abstracting benchmarks rooted in realistic, complex scenarios; (3) using this evaluation system to guide model optimization targeting two challenges — long-tail knowledge and complex instruction following.

Result: Seed2.0 achieves world-leading performance in reasoning intelligence, visual understanding, and search capabilities. Through extensive real-world use cases, the model demonstrates the ability to handle initial complex, real-world tasks, delivering value to hundreds of millions of users.

Conclusion: Seed2.0 represents a meaningful step toward solving complex real-world tasks by aligning model development with user-centric evaluation, and it begins to exhibit practical competence in handling such tasks at scale.

Abstract: We present Seed2.0, a model series that takes a meaningful step toward solving complex, real-world tasks. Our approach begins with identifying users' genuine needs and constructing a reliable, forward-looking evaluation system by selecting and abstracting benchmarks grounded in these needs and in realistic, complex scenarios. Guided by this evaluation system, Seed2.0 targets two persistent challenges, long-tail knowledge and complex instruction following, substantially improving the model's reliability on intricate, long-horizon tasks. Beyond these, Seed2.0 delivers world-leading reasoning intelligence, visual understanding, and search capabilities that address the most common needs of a broad user base. Through extensive real-world use cases documented in this model card, we demonstrate that Seed2.0 begins to exhibit the ability to handle initial complex real-world tasks, delivering greater value to hundreds of millions of users.

</details>


### [179] [Mnemosyne: Agentic Transaction Processing for Validating and Repairing AI-generated Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00269)
*Edward Y. Chang,Longling Geng,Emily J. Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Agentic Transaction Processing (ATP), a transaction model where generated actions from LLMs/agents are treated as untrusted proposals that must pass deterministic runtime admission under a declared constraint set. Implemented in the Mnemosyne runtime, ATP ensures committed-state correctness is independent of the proposing layer's competence, with proven safety properties and low overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Actions generated by LLMs, solvers, and agent teams may be syntactically valid but semantically dangerous—stale, infeasible, conflicting, or destructive of evidence that triggered a repair. Existing approaches lack a principled mechanism to prevent untrusted generated actions from corrupting system state, especially when unforeseen disruptions occur.

Method: ATP is a two-sided transaction model: anything may propose actions, but only the runtime admits and commits them after deterministic checking against a declared, executable constraint set C. Mnemosyne implements this via an append-only transition log, effective-state projection, dependency-safe compensation, and active commitment records. A localized repair protocol (LCRP) handles disruptions reactively within bounds rather than trusting fresh proposals.

Result: Four safety properties are proven (authority separation, serial-equivalent generative admission, evidence-preserving repair, obligation containment) plus a bounded-reactive-repair guarantee. A reproducible artifact passes nine falsification tests rejecting targeted violations while admitting valid work, with under 6% projection-and-validation overhead. Bounded local repair edits an order of magnitude fewer operations than global recompute.

Conclusion: ATP decouples committed-state correctness from proposer trustworthiness, providing a principled transaction model for agentic workflows. Mnemosyne demonstrates the approach is practical with low overhead and significant efficiency gains in repair scenarios.

Abstract: LLMs, solvers, and agent teams increasingly generate workflow actions, repairs, and plans, but a generated action may be syntactically valid yet stale, infeasible, conflicting, or destructive of the evidence that triggered a repair. We introduce Agentic Transaction Processing (ATP), a transaction model that treats generated actions as untrusted proposals until they pass deterministic admission under a declared, executable constraint set C. The principle is two-sided: a proposal is not truth, and no proposal foresees every disruption: anything may propose, but only the runtime admits and commits, and when an unforeseen disruption strikes it repairs reactively within bounds rather than trusting a fresh proposal. Relative to C, committed-state correctness becomes independent of the competence, honesty, or learning of the proposing layer. We realize ATP in Mnemosyne, a runtime with an append-only transition log, effective-state projection, dependency-safe compensation, and active commitment records, and prove four safety properties relative to C (authority separation, serial-equivalent generative admission, evidence-preserving repair, and obligation containment) together with a bounded-reactive-repair guarantee for its localized repair protocol (LCRP). A reproducible artifact rejects the targeted violations across nine falsification tests while still admitting valid work, at under 6% projection-and-validation overhead, and bounded local repair edits an order of magnitude fewer operations than global recompute. Mnemosyne is open source: https://github.com/eyuchang/Mnemosyne/tree/arxiv-atp-rq1-rq9b-r8-v2.

</details>


### [180] [Managed Autonomy at Runtime: Gear-Based Safety and Governance for Single- and Multi-Agent Cyber-Physical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00334)
*Srini Ramaswamy,Wang Miaosheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a discrete-time control system with five "execution gears" and utility-gated dispatch for autonomous agents. It provides formal proofs of stability, safety, and fallback guarantees for single-agent and multi-agent CPS, validated via 10,000 Monte Carlo episodes on a UR5 robotic assembly cell, achieving 99.6% anomaly detection versus 2.1% baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous agents (LLM-driven or robotic) operating without continuous human oversight suffer from three common failure modes: safety violations from unverified actions, behavioral instability from unconstrained loops, and continuity loss from unhandled error states. Existing solutions lack integrated formal guarantees that span both action-level control and autonomy governance.

Method: The authors propose a discrete-time control system with five execution gears (G_obs, G_sug, G_plan, G_exec, G_int) combined with utility-gated dispatch and event-driven fallback.
