For single agents, they prove monotonic stability, execution safety, eventual stabilization, fallback completeness, and equivalence to a gear-constrained MDP. For multi-agent CPS, they integrate the SMART managed-autonomy lifecycle, mapping runtime evidence into four governance states (Stable/Meta/Assisted/Regulated) with consensus gating, swarm-level Lyapunov analysis, per-agent gear authority, and rendezvous control for distributed safety guarantees.

Result: Evaluated on a three-agent UR5 robotic assembly cell using NIST degradation dataset fault magnitudes across 10,000 Monte Carlo episodes: achieved 99.6% anomaly detection rate (vs. 2.1% single-agent baseline), reduced detection latency by 3.5×, and provided a formal physical-workspace safety certificate.

Conclusion: The execution gears function as micro-level permissions beneath the SMART runtime governance states, cleanly separating action control from autonomy governance. The approach delivers formal safety and stability guarantees validated empirically on real-world robotic assembly tasks.

Abstract: Autonomous agents, whether LLM-driven software agents or robotic physical agents, face a common class of failure modes when operating without continuous human oversight: safety violations from unverified actions, behavioral instability from unconstrained loops, and continuity loss from unhandled error states. We develop \system{}, a discrete-time control system that combines five execution gears (\Gobs{}, \Gsug{}, \Gplan{}, \Gexec{}, \Gint{}) with utility-gated dispatch and event-driven fallback. For the single-agent case, we prove monotonic stability, execution safety, eventual stabilization, fallback completeness, and equivalence to a gear-constrained Markov decision process. For multi-agent cyber-physical systems (CPS), we apply the established \smart{} managed-autonomy lifecycle and map runtime evidence into its four governance states (\Stable{}/\Meta{}/\Assisted{}/\Regulated{}). Consensus gating, swarm-level Lyapunov analysis, per-agent gear authority, and rendezvous control provide distributed safety and stability guarantees, including zero collision under the stated assumptions. We evaluate the resulting runtime on a three-agent UR5 robotic assembly cell using fault magnitudes calibrated from the NIST \emph{Degradation Measurement of Robot Arm Position Accuracy} dataset across 10,000 Monte Carlo episodes. It achieves a 99.6\% anomaly detection rate versus 2.1\% for the single-agent baseline, reduces detection latency by $3.5\times$, and supplies a formal physical-workspace safety certificate. The execution gears act as micro-level permissions beneath the \smart{} runtime governance states, separating action control from autonomy governance.

</details>


### [181] [Personalization as Inverse Planning: Learning Latent Design Intents for Agentic Slide Generation via Structural Denoising](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00407)
*Tianci Liu,Zihan Dong,Linjun Zhang,Haoyu Wang,jing Gao,Emre Kiciman,Ranveer Chandra,Wei-Ting Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Current AI slide design methods fail at page-level personalization because they rely on templates or verbose instructions rather than inferring latent design intents. This paper frames page-level slide personalization (PSP) as inverse planning and proposes SPIRE, a multi-agent RL framework that learns design intent through structural denoising of corrupted slides, with theoretical guarantees on consistency and variance reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI methods for slide design operate either at the deck level (themes) or require explicit user specifications, failing to capture subtle, latent design intents at the individual page level. This leaves the problem of Page-level Slide Personalization (PSP) unsolved.

Method: SPIRE frames PSP as inverse planning without assuming knowledge of specific execution tools (e.g., PowerPoint, Beamer). It introduces structural denoising—intentionally corrupting clean slide visual structures and training two collaborative agents via reinforcement learning to recover the original design. This denoising task serves as a tractable surrogate for PSP, and the multi-agent formulation is proven to reduce policy gradient variance.

Result: The authors provide theoretical proofs that structural denoising is a consistent surrogate for PSP and that the multi-agent formulation strictly reduces policy gradient variance in RL. Extensive experiments demonstrate the superiority of SPIRE over existing methods.

Conclusion: SPIRE offers a principled, tool-agnostic solution to page-level slide personalization by reframing it as inverse planning solved through multi-agent structural denoising, with both theoretical validation and empirical evidence supporting its effectiveness.

Abstract: Slide design requires personalizing both deck themes and page layouts. Yet, current AI agent-based methods struggle with fine-grained, page-level design. Solely relying on prespecified templates or user verbose instructions, they fail to capture latent design intents, leaving Page-level Slide Personalization (PSP) unresolved. To close this gap, this work formulates PSP as an inverse planning problem. We propose to learn a design intent without assuming any knowledge of the specific executing tools (e.g., PowerPoint, Beamer) being used. However, relinquishing control over these tools makes the problem intractable to optimize end-to-end. To overcome this, we propose SPIRE, a principled framework to solve PSP approximately. By intentionally corrupting the visual structures of clean slides, SPIRE creates a verifiable task to denoise the corruption, whereby two agents learn to collaboratively refine executable designs via reinforcement learning (RL). We present a proof that structural denoising is a consistent surrogate for PSP, and that the multi-agent formulation strictly reduces policy gradient variance in RL. Extensive experiments demonstrate the superiority of SPIRE.

</details>


### [182] [PHREEQC-MCQ-200: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Tool-Augmented Scientific Simulator Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00436)
*Ke Zhang,Sahchit Chundur,Mohammad Javad Qomi,Maziar Raissi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PHREEQC-MCQ-200 is a 200-question benchmark for evaluating LLM agents on deterministic aqueous-geochemistry simulations. Tool-augmented agents improve aggregate accuracy but also regress on previously correct items. Output-access interface design matters — table-of-contents helps strong models but hurts mid-tier ones. The paper argues for multidimensional evaluation beyond simple accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are increasingly connected to scientific software, but it is unclear whether tool access genuinely improves reliability in scientific computation or merely adds complexity. Existing evaluations lack rigorous measurement of when and how tool use fails, particularly on item-level regressions and interface sensitivity.

Method: The authors created PHREEQC-MCQ-200, a benchmark of 200 multiple-choice questions across 21 validated PHREEQC (aqueous geochemistry) simulation scenarios. Agents must construct simulator inputs, execute PHREEQC, parse structured outputs, and commit to answers. They evaluated multiple frontier and mid-tier model families under different output-access protocols (full output vs. table-of-contents interface), measuring accuracy, item-level retention, trajectory failures, and computation-chain breakpoints.

Result: Tool access substantially improves aggregate accuracy across model families, confirming grounded execution is necessary. However, tool-augmented agents also lose items they previously answered correctly without tools — a regression masked by average accuracy. A table-of-contents interface reduces token cost and preserves/improves accuracy for stronger models but degrades mid-tier model performance due to unreliable output navigation.

Conclusion: Scientific tool use should be framed as an end-to-end diagnostic problem, not merely a tool-calling capability check. Evaluations of scientific agents must report accuracy alongside item-level retention, output-access sensitivity, trajectory failures, and computation-chain breakpoints to capture the full reliability picture.

Abstract: Large language model agents are increasingly connected to scientific software, yet it remains unclear when tool access makes scientific computation more reliable rather than merely more complex. We introduce PHREEQC-MCQ-200, a benchmark for evaluating tool-augmented agents on deterministic aqueous-geochemistry simulations. The benchmark contains 200 multiple-choice questions derived from 21 validated PHREEQC scenarios, requiring agents to construct simulator inputs, execute PHREEQC, inspect structured outputs, and commit to final answers.
  Across multiple frontier and mid-tier model families, simulator access substantially improves aggregate accuracy, confirming that grounded execution is necessary for many scientific-computation tasks. However, the gains are not monotonic: tool-augmented agents also lose items they answered correctly without tools, revealing regressions that average accuracy alone hides. We further show that output-access protocol matters. A table-of-contents interface can reduce token cost while preserving or improving accuracy for stronger models, but it degrades performance for mid-tier models that cannot reliably navigate structured simulator outputs.
  PHREEQC-MCQ-200 therefore frames scientific tool use as an end-to-end diagnostic problem rather than a simple tool-calling capability. We argue that evaluations of scientific agents should report not only accuracy, but also item-level retention, output-access sensitivity, trajectory failures, and where the computation chain breaks.

</details>


### [183] [Multi-scale Mixture of World Models for Embodied Agents in Evolving Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00457)
*Jinwoo Jang,Daniel J. Rho,Sihyung Yoon,Hyunsuk Cho,Honguk Woo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MuSix introduces a scale-aware Mixture of Experts framework for embodied agents, using experiential distance to route queries to appropriate scales and scale-dependent forgetting rates for dynamic knowledge adaptation, outperforming baselines on multi-scale reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodied agents need multi-scale reasoning (low-level skills to high-level abstractions) and adaptive knowledge updating as environments change. Existing MoE approaches lack explicit scale notions in routing, preventing targeted updates, and use uniform update policies that fail to account for different knowledge obsolescence rates across scales.

Method: MuSix employs a two-stage routing mechanism grounded in Construal Level Theory: a meta-router maps experiential distance (situational novelty) to a continuous scale-space weight, then per-scale base routers select world models. For adaptation, scale-dependent forgetting rates enable rapid refreshing of low-scale knowledge while high-scale abstractions persist longer, with gated inter-scale transfer preserving cross-hierarchy coherence.

Result: Experiments on EmbodiedBench and HAZARD demonstrate that MuSix outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on both multi-scale reasoning and dynamic adaptation benchmarks.

Conclusion: MuSix effectively addresses the dual challenges of scale-aware routing and heterogeneous knowledge adaptation in embodied agents by integrating Construal Level Theory-inspired experiential distance with scale-dependent update policies, yielding superior performance in dynamic, real-world-style environments.

Abstract: Embodied agents operating in the real world require multi-scale reasoning and knowledge adaptation as conditions change. We identify two challenges in applying Mixture of Experts (MoE) to this setting: routing lacks an explicit notion of scale, preventing targeted updates at specific scales, and a uniform update policy cannot accommodate the different rates at which knowledge at each scale becomes outdated. We present MuSix, a framework that addresses both challenges through scale-aware world model mixture and evolution. A two-stage routing mechanism grounds scale selection in experiential distance, a measure of situational novelty inspired by Construal Level Theory: a meta-router first maps this quantity to a weight over continuous scale space, then per-scale base routers select world models within the identified scale. For adaptation, scale-dependent forgetting rates allow low-scale knowledge to refresh rapidly while high-scale abstractions persist, and gated inter-scale transfer maintains coherence across the hierarchy. Experiments on EmbodiedBench and HAZARD show that MuSix improves over state-of-the-art baselines on multi-scale reasoning and dynamic adaptation.

</details>


### [184] [AI Native Games: A Survey and Roadmap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00527)
*Zhiyue Xu,Fandi Meng,Kaijie Xu,Clark Verbrugge,Simon Lucas,Jian Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a strict definition of AI-native games based on whether generative AI is constitutive of the core gameplay loop, analyzes 53 existing artifacts using a dual-axis G/N taxonomy, and argues that the fundamental design challenge is organizing semantic openness into stable gameplay through mechanical invariants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative AI increasingly appears in games, but "generation alone does not make a game AI-native, nor does it guarantee playability." The authors identify a lack of clear definitional boundaries separating AI-native games from superficially similar artifacts like AI-augmented games, chatbots, or procedural content generation. Without such criteria, design discourse and research progress remain unfocused.

Method: The authors define AI-native games using a counterfactual criterion: if the generative AI component were removed or trivially replaced, the core loop would collapse. They then screen publicly available artifacts against this definition, yielding a corpus of 53 AI-native games and prototypes. These are classified using a dual-axis G/N taxonomy — the G-axis for player-facing game type (e.g., narrative adventure, epistemic interaction) and the N-axis for the dominant AI mechanic (e.g., generative narrative, semantic adjudication, multi-agent simulation).

Result: The corpus concentrates on language-forward designs: narrative adventure, epistemic interaction, and generative narrative dominate. Other categories — semantic adjudication, multi-agent simulation, generative construction, and relationship/companion play — remain underrepresented. The authors identify that the central design challenge is organizing AI's semantic openness into stable, interpretable, and consequential gameplay via mechanical invariants: goals, rules, state, feedback, pacing, and player agency.

Conclusion: AI-native game design requires marrying open-ended generation with robust mechanical structure. The paper outlines a roadmap addressing controllable generation, AI-as-mechanic design patterns, multimodal and multi-agent systems, inference economics, evaluation methodologies, safety, and regulation — framing these as the key research and development frontiers for the field.

Abstract: Generative AI now enables games to produce dialogue, quests, characters, images, and worlds at runtime. Yet generation alone does not make a game AI-native, nor does it guarantee playability. This paper defines AI-native games by whether runtime generative AI is constitutive of the core loop: if the AI component were removed or trivially replaced, the central form of play would collapse or become fundamentally different. This counterfactual criterion separates AI-native games from AI-augmented games, boundary artifacts, chatbots, tavern-style role-play, procedural content generation, and AI-assisted production. Using this definition, we screen candidate artifacts and analyze 53 publicly available AI-native games and prototypes. We introduce a dual-axis G/N taxonomy: the G-axis captures player-facing game type, while the N-axis captures the dominant AI mechanic that makes generative AI indispensable to play. The corpus is concentrated around language-forward designs, especially narrative adventure, epistemic interaction, and generative narrative, while categories such as semantic adjudication, multi-agent simulation, generative construction, and relationship/companion play remain less represented. We argue that the central design problem is organizing semantic openness into stable gameplay. AI-native design depends on mechanical invariants: goals, rules, state, feedback, pacing, and player agency that make open-ended AI outputs interpretable and consequential. We conclude with a roadmap for controllable generation, AI-as-mechanic design, multimodal and multi-agent systems, inference economics, evaluation, safety, and regulation.

</details>


### [185] [HARC: Coupling Harmfulness and Refusal Directions for Robust Safety Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00572)
*Shei Pern Chua,Fangzhao Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Aligned LLMs encode harmfulness and refusal as separable directions in the residual stream. Jailbreaks succeed by suppressing one of these directions at prompt-side positions. The paper introduces HARC, a fine-tuning method that couples harmfulness and refusal directions across prompt and response positions, achieving state-of-the-art robustness without degrading general capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how aligned LLMs internally represent safety is critical for diagnosing why jailbreaks succeed and for designing robust alignment strategies. Prior work identified separable harmfulness and refusal directions but did not explain how jailbreaks exploit them or extend the analysis to response-generation positions.

Method: The authors first analyze how jailbreaks manipulate the harmfulness-refusal plane at both prompt-side and response-side token positions, revealing that attacks suppress either the refusal or harmfulness direction. They then introduce HARC (Harmfulness-And-Refusal Coupling), a fine-tuning method that pairs the two directions across both prompt and response positions while confining the intervention to the harmfulness-refusal subspace to preserve general capabilities.

Result: Jailbreak attacks occupy separable regions of the harmfulness-refusal plane at prompt encoding. The model recognizes harmful content during response generation even when it failed at the prompt side. HARC achieves the strongest robustness-capability-usability trade-off among six baselines spanning training-time and inference-time safety methods, and the directions transfer across five model families and two scales without architecture-specific tuning.

Conclusion: The harmfulness-refusal representation framework provides both diagnostic and interventional value for LLM safety. Coupling these directions via HARC yields robust alignment without the common trade-offs of over-refusal or capability degradation, and the approach generalizes across diverse model architectures and scales.

Abstract: Understanding how aligned LLMs internally represent safety is critical for diagnosing alignment vulnerabilities, as it explains why jailbreaks succeed and informs the design of robust alignment strategies. Prior work shows that aligned LLMs encode harmfulness and refusal as separable directions in the residual stream at prompt-side token positions. We show that jailbreaks succeed at prompt encoding by suppressing either the refusal or harmfulness direction before any token is generated, with distinct attack classes occupying separable regions of the harmfulness-refusal plane. Extending the analysis to response-token positions, we find that the model recognizes harmful content while it is generating that content, even when it failed to recognize the input as harmful at the prompt side. Motivated by our findings, we introduce HARC (Harmfulness-And-Refusal Coupling), a fine-tuning method that pairs the two directions across both prompt and response positions. Since the intervention is confined to the harmfulness-refusal subspace, it leaves the rest of the residual stream intact and does not degrade general capability or inflate over-refusal. Across extensive experiments, HARC achieves the strongest robustness-capability-usability trade-off among six baselines spanning the major training-time and inference-time safety methods. The harmfulness and refusal directions at prompt and response positions transfer across the five model families and two scales we tested without architecture-specific tuning.

</details>


### [186] [AGI Maze as a Benchmark Framework for World-Modeling Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00627)
*Alexey Potapov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AGI Maze is a lightweight benchmark framework of grid-based maze tasks designed to test whether LLMs can build and manipulate persistent world-state representations, rather than relying on static pattern completion. Initial evaluations show vanilla LLMs fail at internal maze representation, and even a baseline agent using message history as working memory cannot reliably solve small mazes within generous step budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs default to next-token prediction from static context, which does not naturally yield persistent, manipulable world models. Tasks requiring reasoning over partially observable, stateful environments with hidden state expose this limitation. The authors aim to create benchmarks that specifically test structured hypothesis formation and memory, moving beyond surface-level text inference.

Method: AGI Maze provides a clean API for grid-based maze environments with multiple difficulty regimes and no high-dimensional sensory inputs. The authors evaluate several vanilla LLMs on simple mazes at inference time, and introduce a baseline agent that uses its message history as working memory to construct observation descriptions at runtime.

Result: Vanilla LLMs fail to represent mazes internally during inference. The baseline agent with message-history-based working memory improves performance but remains insufficient to reliably solve even small mazes within a step budget that humans easily exceed.

Conclusion: Current LLMs, even with working-memory scaffolding, lack the capacity to form and manipulate persistent world-state representations needed for partially observable, stateful environments. AGI Maze serves as a focused testbed for developing agents that can learn and use structured internal world models.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are powerful pattern-completion systems, but their default operating mode - predicting the next token from a static context - does not reliably produce persistent, manipulable representations of an external world. Many tasks that look like "reasoning" in text become substantially harder once the environment is partially observable, stateful, and requires memory and structured hypotheses about hidden state. AGI Maze is a lightweight framework for building such environments without requiring high-dimensional sensory inputs. It provides a family of grid-based maze tasks with a clean API and multiple difficulty regimes. The goal is to create benchmarks where agents must learn and use world state representations, not just infer a local rule over readily provided observations. We provide an initial evaluation of several vanilla LLMs on simple mazes showing that they fail to represent mazes internally at LLM inference time. We also introduce a baseline agent, which is allowed to use its message history as a working memory to construct descriptions of observations at agentic runtime. Although this can improve performance, it is still insufficient for an LLM agent to reliably solve even small mazes within a step budget that is more than enough for humans.

</details>


### [187] [Coachable agents for interactive gameplay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00642)
*Roberto Capobianco,Harm van Seijen,Nolan D. Bard,Neil Burch,Fatima Davelouis,Josh Davidson,Alisa Devlic,Yunshu Du,Ishan Durugkar,Siddhant Gangapurwala,Daniel Hernandez,G. Zacharias Holland,Sahil Jain,Kenta Kawamoto,Raksha Kumaraswamy,Patrick MacAlpine,Dustin R. Morrill,Declan Oller,Francesco Riccio,Akanksha Saran,Craig Sherstan,Kaushik Subramanian,Thomas J. Walsh,Samuel Barrett,Kizza N. Frisbee,Mady Govil,Johannes Günther,Varun R. Kompella,James A. MacGlashan,Maxwell Svetlik,Michael D. Thomure,Jaden B. Travnik,Kevin Waugh,Elahe Aghapour,Florian Fuchs,Andreanne Lemay,Shruti Mishra,Takuma Seno,Peter Stone,Michael Spranger,Peter R. Wurman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework combining universal value function approximators (UVFAs) with tailored training scenarios and data augmentation enables RL agents to exhibit user-controllable "styles" of behavior at runtime, demonstrated across car racing, game combat, and humanoid locomotion domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL typically learns a single near-optimal behavior, but many real-world applications require flexible, runtime control over *how* a task is solved — referred to as "styles." Existing methods lack a general framework for coaching agents to exhibit diverse, user-specified behavioral styles in complex environments.

Method: The authors propose a framework that combines universal value function approximators (UVFAs) with carefully designed training scenarios, specialized learning algorithms, and data augmentation techniques. Styles are encoded as part of the state/goal representation, allowing a single policy to condition on style parameters and produce diverse behaviors at runtime without retraining.

Result: The framework was validated in three distinct domains: stylized combat in Horizon Forbidden West, racing in Gran Turismo, and humanoid walking in an open-source testbed. In each case, agents exhibited strong coherence to requested styles while maintaining task performance, with end users able to select desired behaviors at runtime.

Conclusion: UVFA-based coaching with style conditioning provides a practical, general-purpose solution for deploying controllable RL agents in complex production environments, enabling runtime behavioral flexibility without sacrificing core task competence.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has proven to be a valuable tool in the creation of advanced AI and robotic systems, contributing to everything from game playing to robotics to foundation models. Through trial-and-error, these AI systems typically learn one, near-optimal behavior to solve their tasks. However, there are many use cases in which one would like to assert some level of control, preferably in real time, over how the task is solved. We refer to these modifications of a core task as styles. We combine universal value function approximators (UVFAs) with carefully selected training scenarios, learning algorithms, and data augmentation to create a framework for coaching agents that exhibit styles in complex domains. We demonstrate the framework's application in the AAA video games Horizon Forbidden West and Gran Turismo, and in an open-source humanoid test domain. Despite the different nature of the domains -- car racing, stylized game combat, and humanoid walking -- each agent shows strong coherence to the style requests while still satisfying the main task in its domain. Importantly, the techniques outlined in this paper allow an end user to choose the final behavior at run time, giving them flexible control over the final executed performance.

</details>


### [188] [Self-GC: Self-Governing Context for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00692)
*Xubin Hao,Hongjin Meng,Xin Yin,Jiawei Zhu,Chenpeng Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Self-GC is a context management system for long-horizon LLM agents that treats context as indexed, recoverable objects governed by a planner that proposes fold/mask/prune actions, achieving ~44% prefix token pruning with ~85% future continuation preservation, outperforming heuristic baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon LLM agents accumulate structured artifacts (tool results, files, plans, constraints) that are too structured for naive text-suffix treatment. Existing approaches — chronological pruning and tool-output masking (cheap but blind to future dependencies) or end-of-context self-summarization (preserves narrative but loses exact evidence, locators, and editable artifacts) — are insufficient. A principled lifecycle management approach is needed.

Method: The system, named Self-GC (self-governing context, echoing garbage collection), structures agent context into indexed objects (user turns, tool spans, skill state). A side-channel planner proposes three actions: fold (compress), mask (hide), and prune (delete). A harness enforces recoverable sidecars, safe commit boundaries, and cache-aware commits, ensuring safety and recoverability of governed objects.

Result: On a 33-session Hard Set: 43.95% prefix token pruning with 84.85% of future continuations unaffected (vs. 54.55%–69.70% no-impact rates for heuristics). On a 332-session production suite: planner backbones achieve 91.27%–94.58% no-impact rates (vs. 77.71%–87.46% for baselines). In production A/B testing: 10–15% daytime average input token reduction, with ~20% peak reduction.

Conclusion: Effective context management for LLM agents should be framed as runtime lifecycle control over indexed, recoverable objects rather than post-hoc text cleanup, enabling aggressive token savings while preserving downstream task performance.

Abstract: Long-horizon LLM agents accumulate tool results, files, plans, and user constraints that are too structured to be treated as a disposable text suffix. Current systems mostly rely on in-run heuristics such as chronological pruning and tool-output masking, or on final self-summary near a context limit. Heuristics are cheap but blind to future dependencies; summaries preserve narrative state but often hide exact evidence, locators, and editable artifacts. We present Self-GC, where GC denotes self-governing context while deliberately echoing garbage collection: the system does not merely reclaim unused tokens, but governs the lifecycle of agent context objects. Self-GC turns user turns, tool spans, and skill state into indexed objects; asks a side-channel planner to propose fold, mask, and prune actions; and lets the harness enforce recoverable sidecars, safe commit boundaries, and cache-aware commit. On a 33-session Hard Set, Self-GC prunes 43.95% of prefix tokens while leaving 84.85% of future continuations unaffected, compared with no-impact rates of 54.55% to 69.70% for heuristic baselines. On a 332-session production-derived suite, three planner backbones reach no-impact rates of 91.27% to 94.58%, while baselines remain at 77.71% to 87.46%. In production, an online account-level split reduces daytime average input tokens by 10% to 15%, with peak reductions near 20%. These results point to context management as runtime lifecycle control over indexed, recoverable objects rather than post hoc text cleanup.

</details>


### [189] [Self-Evolving Agents with Anytime-Valid Certificates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00871)
*Biswa Sengupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SEA is an architecture that constrains self-evolving agents by freezing the base model and routing all modifications through auditable, anytime-valid gates. Five verifier-in-the-loop mechanisms supply grader-free signals, achieving +4–5 point gains on SWE-bench Verified without regressions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard learning-theoretic guarantees fail for self-evolving agents because the data, evaluator, components, and hypothesis space are all generated by the policy being updated. A principled architecture is needed to enable safe self-modification with formal error control.

Method: SEA freezes the base model and restricts self-modification to a small steering adapter and a versioned harness. Every modification passes through an anytime-valid gate producing auditable certificates against a fixed error budget. Five loop controllers — best-of-N, micro-step search, self-authored reproduction oracles, search-layer control, and self-repair — provide dense, grader-free signals computed solely from issue text, enabling the gates to select among behaviors the frozen base already produces.

Result: On 52-instance SWE-bench Verified across four base models, base capability is the dominant confound-free effect. A no-op-composite control on two strong models isolates SEA's contribution at +4 (GLM 5.2: 24→28) and +5 (GPT: 29→34, 65th percentile best). Event logs confirm mechanisms fire and prevent regressions. Results are single-run due to evaluation cost.

Conclusion: SEA demonstrates that safe, auditable self-evolution is feasible by restricting modifications to verified selections from a frozen base model's existing behaviors. Confirming run-to-run variance and adapting the per-task algorithm mix remain future work.

Abstract: Self-evolving agents violate the assumption behind most learning-theoretic guarantees: the data, evaluator, components, and hypothesis space are produced by the policy being updated. We present \textbf{SEA}, an architecture that confines self-modification to a small steering adapter and a versioned harness around a \emph{frozen} base model and admits each modification only through an anytime-valid gate that emits an auditable certificate against a fixed error budget. Five loop controllers compose published guarantees; because such gates can only \emph{select} among behaviors the frozen base already produces, five verifier-in-the-loop mechanisms -- best-of-$N$, micro-step search, self-authored reproduction oracles, search-layer control, and self-repair -- supply the dense, grader-free signal the gates require, computed from the issue text alone. On a $52$-instance SWE-bench Verified subset across four base models, base capability is the dominant, confound-free effect, and on two strong base models a deliberate no-op-composite control isolates the suite's contribution at $+4$ and $+5$ (\textsc{Glm}~5.2 $24\to28$; \textsc{Gpt} $29\to34$, the $65\%$ best), with event logs confirming that its mechanisms fire and prevent regressions. Results are single-run on expensive evaluations; confirming run-to-run variance and adapting the per-task algorithm mix are future work.

</details>


### [190] [Two AI Metrics Diverged: Will it Make All the Difference?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00913)
*Alex Fogelson,Zachary A. Brown,Hans Gundlach,Jayson Lynch,Neil Thompson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: As compute scales, whether AI capabilities concentrate among wealthy actors or proliferate to all depends on whether the relevant performance metrics are bounded or unbounded. Bounded metrics favor "meek models," while unbounded metrics allow frontier models to grow their lead indefinitely.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether the compute scaling of frontier AI models leads to permanent capability concentration among wealthy actors or eventual democratization, and to provide rigorous mathematical conditions for predicting which outcome prevails based on how capabilities are measured.

Method: The authors classify performance metrics by their functional forms relative to training/inference compute, deriving tight mathematical conditions that distinguish bounded vs. unbounded metrics. They show that bounded metrics always favor meek models, while unbounded metrics allow frontier models to maintain a growing lead, and identify closely-related metric pairs that are bounded/unbounded counterparts.

Result: Validation loss shows a shrinking gap (favoring meek models), but on other unbounded metrics, frontier models grow their lead forever. Common bounded metrics often have unbounded counterpart metrics, and vice versa, making metric selection critical for policy implications.

Conclusion: Determining whether a capability metric is bounded or unbounded is a prerequisite for policy: unbounded capabilities (e.g., software engineering, synthetic biology, persuasion) will concentrate in few wealthy hands, while bounded capabilities will proliferate through meek models to the many.

Abstract: As exponential compute scaling continues, will the capabilities of frontier AI models outstrip what is accessible to developers on a small fixed budget? Or will capabilities converge, with "meek models inheriting the earth"? Building on Gundlach et al. (2025b), we show that the answer depends on how we value and measure AI capabilities. We discuss conventional performance measures and show that, while validation loss shows a shrinking gap, on other metrics frontier models grow their lead forever. Classifying performance metrics by their functional forms in relation to training (and inference) compute, we provide tight mathematical conditions for determining which metrics favor meek models, and show that bounded performance metrics always do. But careful interpretation of performance metrics is essential: we show that many common bounded metrics have closely-related counterpart metrics that are unbounded (and vice versa). Determining the apt metric in a domain is a prerequisite for policy, since bounded and unbounded metrics may suggest opposing policy responses. If a particular capability -- like software engineering, synthetic biology, or rhetorical persuasiveness -- is unbounded when measured in the terms we care about, frontier-level capability will likely be concentrated in the hands of a few wealthy actors. Conversely, if that capability is instead bounded, frontier-level capabilities proliferate through meek models into the hands of the many.

</details>


### [191] [Graph-Native Reinforcement Learning Enables Traceable Scientific Hypothesis Generation through Conceptual Recombination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00924)
*Subhadeep Pal,Shashwat Sourav,Tirthankar Ghosal,Markus J. Buehler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Graph-PRefLexOR uses graph-native reasoning models fine-tuned with GRPO to structure materials discovery hypotheses into explicit phases (mechanism exploration, graph construction, pattern extraction, synthesis), achieving 40-65% improvement over baselines with enhanced traceability and semantic diversity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard LLMs produce fluent but poorly traceable responses for materials design problems, making it difficult to verify whether conclusions are supported by coherent intermediate reasoning. There is a need for AI systems that generate scientifically valid, interpretable hypotheses through multi-step, domain-grounded reasoning.

Method: Graph-PRefLexOR, a family of graph-native reasoning models fine-tuned with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). The approach organizes reasoning into four explicit phases: mechanism exploration, graph construction, pattern extraction, and hypothesis synthesis. It links neural language generation with symbolic relational structure. Evaluation uses 100 open-ended materials science questions, with embedding analyses, semantic backtracking, layer-wise hidden-state analyses, and test-time graph expansion experiments.

Result: Graph-PRefLexOR achieves 40-65% improvements over base models, with largest gains in reasoning traceability. Embedding analyses show approximately 2-3x greater semantic diversity and broader semantic exploration. Semantic backtracking and hidden-state analyses reveal stronger alignment between structured reasoning and final answers. Test-time graph expansion shows additional compute increases long-range conceptual recombination within bounded semantic space rather than expanding coverage.

Conclusion: Graph-native reinforcement learning offers a viable pathway toward interpretable AI systems for scientific hypothesis generation, applicable to materials design and broader scientific domains.

Abstract: Accelerating materials discovery requires AI systems that can generate scientifically valid hypotheses through multi-step, domain-grounded reasoning. Standard large language models often produce fluent but weakly traceable responses to open-ended materials design problems, making it difficult to determine whether final answers are supported by coherent intermediate reasoning. We develop Graph-PRefLexOR, a family of graph-native reasoning models fine-tuned with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to organize reasoning into explicit phases for mechanism exploration, graph construction, pattern extraction, and hypothesis synthesis. This design links neural language generation with symbolic relational structure, enabling causal connections to be constructed, inspected, and reused. On 100 open-ended questions from materials science and mechanics literature, Graph-PRefLexOR achieves 40-65% improvements over corresponding base models, with the largest gains in reasoning traceability. Embedding analyses show broader semantic exploration and approximately 2-3 times greater semantic diversity than baselines. Semantic backtracking and layer-wise hidden-state analyses further show stronger alignment between structured reasoning and final answers. Finally, test-time graph expansion reveals that additional compute primarily increases long-range conceptual recombination within a bounded semantic space, rather than simply expanding semantic coverage. These results establish graph-native reinforcement learning as a pathway toward interpretable AI systems for scientific hypothesis generation in materials design and other scientific applications.

</details>


### [192] [Bayesian Uncertainty Propagation for Agentic RAG Pipelines: A Proof-of-Concept Study on Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00972)
*Louis Donaldson,Connor Walker,Koorosh Aslansefat,Yiannis Papadopoulos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes an uncertainty-aware Agentic RAG framework that uses Bayesian Networks to propagate uncertainty signals from planner, evaluator, and generator stages, enabling system-level uncertainty estimation and failure-point diagnosis in multi-stage reasoning pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trustworthy deployment of Agentic RAG systems demands mechanisms to estimate when multi-stage reasoning pipelines may fail, as undetected failures can lead to unreliable outputs in high-stakes applications.

Method: An uncertainty-aware Agentic RAG framework where planner, evaluator, and generator stages produce uncertainty signals based on semantic divergence and generator self-evaluation. These signals are propagated through a Bayesian Network to estimate system-level uncertainty and provide node-level failure-point indicators. Evaluated on StrategyQA and HotpotQA using GPT-3.5-Turbo and GPT-4.1-Nano, with AUROC, AUARC, ECE, and Brier Score as metrics.

Result: Bayesian propagation proves more effective on HotpotQA, where uncertainty accumulates across multi-hop reasoning stages. StrategyQA exposes limitations from miscalibration and unreliable upstream signals. The approach shows promise for discrimination, selective prediction, and calibration tasks but remains preliminary.

Conclusion: Bayesian uncertainty propagation is a promising but preliminary mechanism for monitoring Agentic RAG systems, with future validation needed in industrial domains such as Offshore Wind maintenance decision support.

Abstract: Trustworthy deployment of Agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems requires mechanisms for estimating when multi-stage reasoning pipelines may fail. This paper presents an uncertainty-aware Agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) framework in which planner, evaluator and generator stages produce uncertainty signals derived from semantic divergence and generator self-evaluation. These signals are propagated through a Bayesian Network (BN) to estimate system-level uncertainty and provide node-level indicators of potential failure points across the workflow. The approach is evaluated on StrategyQA and HotpotQA using GPT-3.5-Turbo and GPT-4.1-Nano, with Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic Curve (AUROC), Area Under the Accuracy-Rejection Curve (AUARC), Expected Calibration Error (ECE), and Brier Score used to assess discrimination, selective prediction and calibration. Results show that Bayesian propagation is more effective on HotpotQA, where uncertainty accumulates across multi-hop reasoning stages, while StrategyQA exposes limitations caused by miscalibration and unreliable upstream signals. The study positions Bayesian uncertainty propagation as a promising but preliminary mechanism for monitoring Agentic RAG systems, with future validation required in industrial domains such as Offshore Wind (OSW) maintenance decision support.

</details>


### [193] [PedNStream: Scalable Network Flow Simulation for Pedestrian Traffic Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01021)
*Weiming Mai,Dorine Duives,Serge Hoogendoorn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PedNStream is an open-source, Python-native macroscopic pedestrian simulation framework based on the Link Transmission Model (LTM) that incorporates stochastic link dynamics and utility-based route choice. It supports closed-loop controller integration for interventions like gating and route guidance, and is validated through synthetic scenarios, real-network experiments, and scalability analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale crowd management demands pedestrian simulations that are computationally efficient and compatible with feedback-based control. Existing open-source tools are either microscopic or unsuitable for network-scale closed-loop evaluation, creating a gap for a macroscopic, control-oriented pedestrian simulation framework.

Method: PedNStream extends LTM-based pedestrian models with: (1) stochastic link dynamics capturing diffusion and activity-induced variability, and (2) a utility-based route choice formulation replacing dynamic user equilibrium to handle uncertain, intervention-driven settings. It is implemented as a modular Python framework with built-in controller interfaces for gating, flow separation, and route guidance. Evaluation proceeds in stages: synthetic scenario verification, real-network experiments against observed counts, closed-loop case studies, and runtime scalability analysis.

Result: Synthetic scenarios confirmed key mechanisms including queue formation, spillback, congestion dissipation, and adaptive rerouting. Real-network experiments demonstrated consistency with observed pedestrian counts. The closed-loop case study validated controller integration, and runtime analysis quantified the framework's scalability.

Conclusion: PedNStream establishes itself as an efficient, practical testbed for large-scale pedestrian network simulation and control, filling the gap between microscopic tools and network-scale closed-loop evaluation needs.

Abstract: Large-scale crowd management requires pedestrian simulations that are both computationally efficient and compatible with feedback-based control. However, most open-source tools are either microscopic or not designed for network-scale closed-loop evaluation. This paper presents PedNStream (Pedestrian Network Flow Simulation), an open-source, Python-native simulator for macroscopic pedestrian network loading based on the Link Transmission Model (LTM). The framework extends LTM-based pedestrian models by incorporating stochastic link dynamics that capture diffusion and activity-induced variability, and replaces dynamic user equilibrium route choice with a utility-based formulation suited to uncertain, intervention-driven settings. PedNStream is implemented as a modular framework with built-in controller interfaces for interventions such as gating, flow separation, and route guidance. We evaluate the framework in a staged manner. Synthetic scenarios verify key mechanisms, including queue formation, spillback, congestion dissipation, and adaptive rerouting. Real-network experiments assess large-scale behavior and consistency with observed pedestrian counts. A closed-loop case study demonstrates controller integration, and a runtime analysis quantifies scalability. These results establish PedNStream as an efficient and practical testbed for large-scale pedestrian network simulation and control.

</details>


### [194] [Agentic generation of verifiable rules for deterministic, self-expanding reaction classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01061)
*Daniel Armstrong,Maarten Dobbelaere,Valentas Olikauskas,Helena Avila,Octavian Susanu,Jérôme Waser,Philippe Schwaller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent LLM framework automatically classifies and writes reaction rules from 665,901 US patent reactions, expanding a taxonomy from 68 to 14,073 classes. A lightweight fingerprint classifier achieves 97.7% accuracy on unseen reactions, matching proprietary systems while offering finer resolution and adaptability to new chemistries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual encoding of reaction rules for computer-assisted synthesis planning is intractable due to the long-tailed nature of chemistry. Existing tools rely on fixed, non-adaptive rulesets that cannot incorporate new chemistries, creating a need for automated, self-expanding rule generation.

Method: A multi-agent LLM framework classifies reactions and writes reaction rules from 665,901 US patent reactions. Each rule is generated within a verification loop that tests it against the corpus. A lightweight fingerprint classifier is then trained on these rules for efficient reaction classification.

Result: The taxonomy expanded from 68 to 14,073 classes without human curation. The fingerprint classifier correctly classifies 97.7% of unseen reactions, matching a leading proprietary classifier while providing finer chemical resolution and the ability to extend to out-of-distribution chemistry.

Conclusion: The pipeline produces a living reactivity database and establishes a general methodology for converting generative models into reliable, self-expanding symbolic systems, overcoming the bottleneck of manual rule encoding in synthesis planning.

Abstract: Computer-assisted synthesis planning breaks target molecules into accessible precursors using large libraries of reaction rules that assign each transformation a deterministic, interpretable label. But chemistry is long-tailed, making manual encoding intractable, and existing tools rely on fixed rulesets that cannot adapt to new chemistries. Here we present a fully automated pipeline in which a multi-agent framework of large language models (LLMs) classifies reactions and writes the rules themselves across 665,901 US patent reactions, generating each rule under a verification loop that tests it against the corpus. It expands a standard taxonomy from 68 to 14,073 classes without human curation. With a lightweight fingerprint classifier, it classifies 97.7\% of unseen reactions, matching a leading proprietary classifier while resolving chemistry more finely and extending on demand to chemistry outside its training distribution. The result is a living reactivity database and a general route to turning generative models into reliable, self-expanding symbolic systems.

</details>


### [195] [Can Agents Generalize to the Open World? Unveiling the Fragility of Static Training in Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01084)
*Song-Lin Lv,Weiming Wu,Rui Zhu,Zi-Jian Cheng,Lan-Zhe Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formalizes "OpenAgent," a setting for evaluating LLM agents under distributional shifts across queries, actions, observations, and domains. It constructs a hierarchical sandbox (Perception, Interaction, Reasoning, Internalization) to systematically diagnose performance degradation, and proposes Perturbation-Augmented Fine-Tuning to improve agent robustness in open-world scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents perform well on static benchmarks but fail in real-world deployments due to dynamic shifts in user queries, available tools, and interaction dynamics. There is a lack of systematic frameworks to understand and mitigate this generalization gap.

Method: The authors (1) formalize the OpenAgent problem setting with distributional shifts across four dimensions (query, action, observation, domain); (2) construct a controlled sandbox with a four-tier shift hierarchy (Perception, Interaction, Reasoning, Internalization); (3) conduct systematic experiments evaluating agents trained via SFT and RL under these shifts; and (4) propose Perturbation-Augmented Fine-Tuning, a disturbance-based intervention for SFT.

Result: Both SFT-trained and RL-trained agents exhibit significant performance degradation when exposed to open environmental shifts across all tiers of the hierarchy, revealing systematic vulnerabilities in current training paradigms.

Conclusion: The OpenAgent framework provides a structured approach to diagnosing generalization failures in tool-use agents. Perturbation-Augmented Fine-Tuning offers a promising foundation for building more robust agents capable of handling real-world distributional shifts.

Abstract: While Large Language Model (LLM) agents demonstrate proficiency in static benchmarks, their deployment in real-world scenarios is hindered by the dynamic nature of user queries, tool sets, and interaction dynamics. To address this generalization gap, we formalize OpenAgent (Tool-Use Agent in Open-World), a problem setting characterized by distributional shifts across query, action, observation, and domain dimensions. To systematically diagnose its impact, we construct a controlled sandbox environment where we define fine-grained environmental shifts across a four-tier hierarchy, Perception, Interaction, Reasoning, and Internalization, and conduct a comprehensive series of experiments. Our analysis yields a series of key insights, demonstrating that agents trained via both Supervised Fine-Tuning(SFT) and Reinforcement Learning suffer from varying degrees of performance degradation when confronting open environmental shifts. Building on these insights, we propose Perturbation-Augmented Fine-Tuning, a disturbance-based intervention strategy for SFT that lays the foundation for enhancing agent robustness and utility in realistic environments. Our code will be released at: https://github. com/LAMDA-NeSy/OpenAgent.

</details>


### [196] [Optimal Resource Utilization for Autonomous Laboratory Orchestrators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01188)
*Austin McDannald,Julia Tisaranni,Howie Joress*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A two-step method combining constraint programming for optimal scheduling and status-dependency tracking for robust execution is proposed to maximize resource utilization in autonomous labs with heterogeneous hardware, demonstrated on a metal-organic framework synthesis platform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents in autonomous laboratories excel at suggesting experiments, but efficiently planning and executing those experiments under real-world hardware constraints—especially with multiple instruments of varying capacities and throughputs—remains an unsolved challenge. The authors aim to bridge the gap between experimental suggestion and practical resource-aware execution.

Method: A two-step approach: (1) Constraint programming is employed to compute optimal schedules that minimize total completion time while respecting hardware limitations and capacities. (2) A status-dependency system is implemented for each task, enabling robust execution of the optimized schedules by tracking prerequisite conditions and task state transitions.

Result: The constraint programming step successfully produces schedules that minimize total makespan under hardware constraints. The status-dependency system enables robust, fault-tolerant execution of these optimal schedules on the autonomous metal-organic framework synthesis platform.

Conclusion: Combining constraint programming for offline schedule optimization with status-dependency tracking for online robust execution provides an effective solution for resource scheduling in autonomous laboratories with heterogeneous, capacity-limited hardware.

Abstract: In autonomous laboratories, AI agents suggest the next batch of experiments to do. However, planning and executing those tasks taking full advantage of the available resources is a completely different question. This can be challenging when dealing with real-world hardware constraints, especially so when there are multiple instruments with different capacities and throughputs. Here we demonstrate a 2-step method to address resource utilization for our autonomous platform for metal-organic framework synthesis. First, we use constraint programming to find optimal schedules. This finds schedules that minimizes the total time while still satisfying the limitations and capacities of the hardware. Secondly, we use a system of status dependencies for each task, which allows for the robust execution of the optimal schedules.

</details>


### [197] [Theoria: Rewrite-Acceptability Verification over Informal Reasoning States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01223)
*Ben Slivinski,Michael Saldivar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Theoria is a verification architecture that rewrites AI solutions into sequences of typed state transitions with explicit justifications, enabling independent auditability. It certifies 105/185 HLE-Verified Gold problems at 91.4% strict precision, catches 94.7% of adversarial poisoned proofs (vs. 83.2% for holistic LLM judges), and achieves 97.1% certified precision on GPQA Diamond.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridging the gap between formal proof assistants (high certainty, limited coverage) and scalar LLM judges (broad coverage, opaque/untrustworthy scores). The need is for verifiable, auditable AI reasoning that exposes hidden premises through a "completeness of change" invariant.

Method: Theoria requires candidate solutions to be decomposed into typed state transitions, each justified by a citation, computation, or given fact. The core invariant — completeness of change — mandates that every difference between consecutive proof states is explicitly accounted for; unlicensed mutations surface as hidden premises. This yields human-readable, independently challengeable proof traces.

Result: On HLE-Verified Gold (185 problems), Theoria certifies 105 at 91.4% strict precision (95% CI: 84.5–95.4%). Holistic LLM judges achieve comparable precision at matched coverage but show low Jaccard overlap (0.14–0.36), indicating complementarity. On 95 adversarial poisoned proofs, structured judging catches 94.7% vs. 83.2% for holistic judging (p=0.0017), with the 11.5 pp gap concentrated in hidden premises (90.6% vs. 62.5%) and fabricated citations (100% vs. 90%). On GPQA Diamond (n=65), certified precision is 97.1% (95% CI: 85.1–99.5%).

Conclusion: Theoria provides auditable, high-precision verification that complements holistic LLM judging. The completeness-of-change invariant effectively surfaces hidden premises and fabricated citations where formal analysis predicts advantage, while performing comparably on error classes where no advantage is expected.

Abstract: When should an AI system's answer be trusted? Formal proof assistants offer certainty but cannot reach most of the problem distribution; scalar LLM judges offer coverage but produce opaque scores that cannot be audited after the fact and are subject to the same coherence issues as any LLM. We present Theoria, a verification architecture that closes this gap. A candidate solution is rewritten into a sequence of typed state transitions, each licensed by an explicit justification, whether that be a citation, computation, or problem-given fact, and every transition is independently auditable. The foundational invariant is completeness of change: every difference between consecutive proof states must be accounted for, so hidden premises surface as unlicensed mutations rather than passing silently. On HLE-Verified Gold (185 text-only expert problems), Theoria certifies 105 at 91.4% strict precision (Wilson 95% CI [84.5%, 95.4%]). Every certification produces a human readable proof trace in which each step can be independently challenged. Holistic LLM judges achieve comparable precision at matched coverage but fail on different problems (Jaccard 0.14-0.36), making the approaches complementary. On 95 adversarial poisoned proofs across 15 domains, structured judges catch 94.7% versus 83.2% for holistic judging (p= 0.0017). The overall 11.5 pp gap concentrates in hidden premises (90.6% vs. 62.5%, a 28 pp difference) and fabricated citations (100% vs. 90%), the error classes where the formal analysis predicts an advantage; performance is identical on arithmetic and theorem-misapplication errors, where no advantage is predicted. On GPQA Diamond (n= 65), certified precision is 97.1% (Wilson CI [85.1%, 99.5%]).

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [198] [A Role-Based Multi-Agent Model for Climate Adaptation Deliberation Across Living Labs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00046)
*Önder Gürcan,David Eric John Herbert,F. LeRon Shultz,Christopher Frantz,Ivan Puga-Gonzalez*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A modular multi-level agent-based architecture for simulating climate governance that integrates cognitive individual decision-making (HUMAT/MOA frameworks), social homophily networks, and strategic institutional behavior (NGOs, media, politicians) within a unified, empirically calibratable framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ABMs for environmental policy either model institutional dynamics or individual behavior in isolation, failing to capture the complex, multi-level interactions between citizens, advocacy groups, media, and political actors that characterize real climate governance processes.

Method: The paper proposes a modular multi-level agent-based architecture combining: (1) motive-based individual decision-making via HUMAT and MOA frameworks, (2) social influence through demographic homophily networks, and (3) institutional strategy modules for NGOs, media, and political agents. Political decisions emerge from aggregating expert input, public mobilization, party alignment, and media framing. The model is designed for empirical calibration using synthetic populations from survey data and Living Lab stakeholder engagement.

Result: No empirical results are presented. The paper focuses exclusively on architectural design principles, modular structure, and integration logic, with discussion of contributions to modeling democratic climate governance and pathways for generalization and future validation.

Conclusion: The multi-layered architecture provides a novel, integrative framework for simulating democratic climate governance by bridging cognitive, social, and institutional levels. Future work involves empirical calibration, validation, and generalization of the model.

Abstract: Climate governance processes involve complex interactions between heterogeneous citizens, advocacy groups, media actors, and political decision-makers. While agent-based models (ABMs) have been widely used to study environmental policy and socio-ecological systems, many existing approaches focus either on institutional dynamics or individual behavioural mechanisms in isolation. This paper presents a modular multi-level agent-based architecture that integrates empirically grounded cognitive decision models with strategic institutional behaviour within a unified simulation framework. The architecture combines (i) motive-based individual decision-making operationalised through the HUMAT and MOA frameworks, (ii) socially embedded influence processes via demographic homophily networks, and (iii) institutional strategy modules for environmental non-governmental organisations (NGOs), media agents, and politicians. Political decisions emerge from the aggregation of multiple signals, including expert input, public mobilisation, party alignment, and media framing. The model is designed to be empirically calibrated through synthetic populations derived from survey data and and institutional parameters informed through Living Lab stakeholder engagement, and to support scenario-based exploration of climate-relevant land-use governance processes. Rather than presenting empirical results, this paper focuses on the architectural design principles, modular structure, and integration logic of the model. We discuss how this multi-layered approach contributes to the modelling of democratic climate governance and outline pathways for generalization and future validation.

</details>


### [199] [M2Note: Continual Evolution of Vision Language Models via Mistake Notebook Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00685)
*Haiwen Li,Jing Tang,Rui Chen,Lei Sun,Xiangxiang Chu*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: M2Note is a training-free continual evolution framework that externalizes learning from VLM failures into an editable memory of subject-guidance notes, retrieved at test time via multimodal RAG to steer reasoning away from past mistakes, with batch-level rollback for stable updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs suffer from recurring failures (skipping visual checks, misapplying domain rules, hallucinating concepts), and existing SFT/RL-based fixes are expensive, brittle under distribution shift, and require costly retraining. A training-free, continually improving alternative is needed.

Method: M2Note transforms failed VLM trajectories into subject-guidance notes (subject = domain/concept summary; guidance = actionable verification steps). At test time, relevant notes are retrieved via multimodal RAG and appended to the model context. A batch-level post-verification with rollback mechanism commits notebook edits only when they improve same-batch performance, preventing noisy updates and regressions. Supports self-evolving (same VLM as solver & supervisor) and cross-model evolving (stronger supervisor guides weaker solver) modes.

Result: Experiments on six multimodal reasoning benchmarks show consistent improvements across domains and backbones, with strong cost and sample efficiency, while remaining complementary to Chain-of-Thought prompting.

Conclusion: M2Note provides an effective, training-free approach for continual improvement of VLMs by externalizing error-driven learning into a retrievable notebook, enabling stable evolution without weight updates and offering both self- and cross-model capability transfer.

Abstract: Vision Language Models (VLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in multimodal reasoning tasks, yet they still suffer from recurring failures, such as skipping key visual checks, misapplying domain rules, and hallucinating unsupported concepts. Most existing solutions rely on supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL), which are expensive to iterate and can be brittle under distribution shift. To this end, we propose Multimodal Mistake Notebook Learning (M2Note), a training-free continual evolution framework that externalizes learning into an editable memory. M2Note transforms failed trajectories into compact subject-guidance notes: the subject summarizes the underlying domain and concept, while the guidance provides actionable verification steps that can be reused in future inference. At test time, M2Note retrieves relevant notes via multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and appends them to the model context, steering reasoning away from previously observed pitfalls. To stabilize continual evolution, we adopt batch-level post-verification with rollback, which commits notebook edits only if they improve performance on the same batch, reducing noisy updates and preventing regressions. M2Note supports both self-evolving, where the same VLM acts as solver and supervisor, and cross-model evolving, where a stronger supervisor guides a weaker solver, enabling capability transfer without weight updates. Experiments on six multimodal reasoning benchmarks show consistent improvements across domains and backbones, while achieving strong cost and sample efficiency and remaining complementary to Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting.

</details>


### [200] [Calibrating the Instrument: Controllability of an LLM-Driven Synthetic Population](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00910)
*Mirko Degli Esposti*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: This paper introduces SIVE, an internal validation framework for Generative Synthetic Populations (GSPs). Using 120 synthetic personas with known latent structure exposed to valenced institutional communications, all seven pre-registered controllability criteria pass across temperatures. A key finding: a "weakly positive" message was detected as functionally negative, leading to a successful diagnostic and redesign.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Before deploying GSP instruments on real populations, a logically prior question must be answered: does the instrument respond to stimuli of known valence in an ordered, replicable, group-structured way? The authors term this "controllability" — an internal-validity requirement that the latent structure imposed on the synthetic population is recoverable in its own responses. This must precede any external validity claims, analogous to characterizing an instrument's response function before using it to test theory.

Method: The SIVE (Synthetic Instrument Validation Experiment) framework: a fictional municipality ("Montelago") with 120 synthetic personas of known latent structure exposed to seven conditions spanning strongly positive to strongly negative institutional communications about a water network. Seven pre-registered criteria (fidelity, stability, noise floor, specificity, sensitivity, ordering) are evaluated across a temperature sweep. Includes a noise sub-experiment and individual trajectory analysis.

Result: All seven pre-registered criteria pass at every temperature. A diagnostic success: a message designed as "weakly positive" was identified as functionally negative — traced to unresolved problems, uncertainty, and institutional passivity — and a redesigned version restored expected ordering with unanticipated latent trust interactions. Intrinsic noise is approximately half the cross-agent estimate and temperature-stable. Individual trajectories reveal coherent micro-dynamics obscured by summary statistics.

Conclusion: The SIVE framework demonstrates that GSP instruments can satisfy strict controllability criteria, establishing a necessary internal-validity baseline. The calibration failure turned diagnostic success illustrates the framework's practical value for message design. The authors provide full data via an interactive explorer for further scrutiny.

Abstract: Generative Synthetic Populations (GSP) -- the convergence of population synthesis, agent-based modelling, and LLM agents -- are attracting growing interest for urban simulation and institutional communication research. Before any GSP instrument is used on a real population, a more basic question must be answered: does it respond to stimuli of known valence in an ordered, replicable, group-structured way? We call this controllability. We ask not whether a synthetic population tracks humans, but whether it tracks itself: whether the latent structure we impose on it is recovered in its own responses. This internal-validity question is logically prior to any claim about external validity, just as characterising an instrument's response function must precede using it to test a theory. We report SIVE (Synthetic Instrument Validation Experiment): a fictional municipality (Montelago) with 120 synthetic personas of known latent structure, exposed to seven conditions spanning strongly positive to strongly negative institutional communications about a water network. Seven pre-registered criteria, evaluated across a temperature sweep, jointly assess fidelity, stability, noise floor, specificity, sensitivity, and ordering. All seven pass at every temperature. A central finding turns a calibration failure into a diagnostic success: a message designed as "weakly positive" was identified by the instrument as functionally negative, traced to unresolved problems, uncertainty, and institutional passivity in its text; a redesigned version restored the expected ordering and interacts with agents' latent trust in unanticipated ways. A noise sub-experiment shows the instrument's intrinsic noise is roughly half the cross-agent estimate and stable across temperatures. Individual trajectories reveal coherent micro-dynamics that summary statistics obscure. Full data are available via an interactive explorer.

</details>


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [201] [A Non-Line-of-Sight, Multi-Modality-based Side-Channel IP Theft Attack on Additive Manufacturing Using Dual Smartphones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00186)
*Amirhossein Jamarani,Diba Afroze,Yazhou Tu,Mark Yampolskiy,Xiali Hei*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A side-channel attack using two smartphones' internal sensors (acoustic and magnetic) reconstructs 3D printer G-code commands with 98.89% accuracy from 60 cm away in non-line-of-sight conditions, exposing serious IP leakage vulnerabilities in additive manufacturing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing side-channel attacks on 3D printers suffer from three limitations: low success/accuracy in G-code reconstruction, limited attack distance, and reliance on specialized, overt data-collection equipment. The authors aim to overcome all three with a practical, covert attack using ubiquitous smartphone sensors.

Method: The attack employs two smartphones placed 60 cm from the target 3D printer in a non-line-of-sight configuration. One smartphone captures acoustic emissions while the other captures magnetic emissions using their built-in sensors. These side-channel signals are then analyzed to reconstruct the printer's G-code commands. The approach is further validated via a transferability test on a different 3D printer in a different environment.

Result: The attack achieves 98.89% command-level G-code reconstruction accuracy at a 60 cm distance under non-line-of-sight conditions. The method also demonstrates transferability, successfully applied to a second 3D printer in a different setting.

Conclusion: The demonstrated unauthorized access to reconstructed G-code confirms significant security weaknesses in AM systems and underscores the urgent need for countermeasures against multi-modal side-channel attacks using commodity devices.

Abstract: Additive Manufacturing (AM) has revolutionized major sectors, including aerospace, automotive, and healthcare, by enabling adjustable production. As the usage of AM increases, so does the risk of Intellectual Property (IP) leakage during the printing process due to unintended side-channel emissions. Current studies and attack scenarios on 3D printers face three challenges: low success and accuracy rates in final G-code reconstruction, limited distance range for attacking the 3D printer's IP, and reliance on specialized, overt data-collection tools. This paper presents a side-channel attack that addresses the noted limitations by using two smartphones' internal sensors. We position the smartphones 60 cm away in a non-line-of-sight setup to collect the 3D printer's acoustic and magnetic emissions. Our attack successfully reconstructs the G-code commands of the final objects at a rate of 98.89% on command-level reconstruction accuracy. Additionally, we evaluate the transferability of our attack strategy by applying it to another 3D printer in a different environment. Our proven unauthorized access to the reconstructed G-code and thus to the IP of the AM system indicates the security weaknesses in 3D printing, highlighting the need for mitigating side-channel attacks.

</details>


### [202] [Federated Sovereign Transport Protocol (FSTP): Verifiable Coordination Without Disclosure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00213)
*Ramón Soto C.,Liz Soto*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: FSTP is a federated transport protocol that enforces data confinement at the protocol level using compile-time type system guarantees, unlinkable contextual identifiers, and a Blocklace-based event log, enabling participants to verify processes and credentials without exposing internal data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing federation protocols (e.g., ActivityPub, Matrix) define message formats and delivery semantics but leave data confinement entirely to operator policy — they impose no structural constraints on what a conforming server may emit. This creates a gap for federated networks where nodes have heterogeneous privacy requirements.

Method: FSTP employs three core mechanisms: (1) a synchronization agent whose output type set is formally closed, enforced at compile time by Rust's type system rather than runtime checks; (2) a contextual identity model that derives separate, unlinkable identifiers per federation relationship to prevent cross-context correlation; and (3) a Blocklace-based event substrate providing tamper-evident, partially ordered logging with O(symmetric difference) synchronization cost and support for data erasure without breaking hash chain integrity.

Result: The protocol achieves "proof without exposure": participants can cryptographically verify that a process occurred, that a credential is authentic, and that an outcome is uncorrupted, all without accessing the raw internal data that produced these artifacts. FSTP serves as the inter-node transport layer for Velyzor, a governance platform with strict confidentiality requirements.

Conclusion: FSTP demonstrates that data confinement can be made a structural property of a federation protocol rather than a matter of policy compliance. The specification and reference implementation are released as open-source under Apache 2.0.

Abstract: This paper introduces the Federated Sovereign Transport Protocol (FSTP), a synchronization boundary and transport layer for federated networks in which nodes have heterogeneous privacy requirements. Existing federation protocols leave data confinement to operator policy: they define message formats and delivery semantics but impose no structural constraint on what a conforming server may emit. FSTP addresses this gap by making data confinement a property of the protocol itself.
  The central mechanism is a synchronization agent whose output type set is formally closed. Raw internal data cannot appear in any federation message because the constraint is enforced by the Rust type system at compile time, not by a runtime check. A contextual identity model derives a separate, unlinkable identifier for each federation relationship, preventing cross-context correlation structurally. A Blocklace-based event substrate provides tamper-evident, partially ordered logging with synchronization cost proportional to the symmetric difference between node states, and supports data erasure without breaking the hash chain.
