  The result is proof without exposure: a federation participant can verify that a process occurred, that a credential is authentic, and that an outcome is uncorrupted without accessing the internal data that produced these artifacts. FSTP is developed as the inter-node transport layer of Velyzor, a governance platform for institutions with demanding confidentiality requirements. The specification and reference implementation are released as open-source infrastructure under Apache 2.0; source code and figures accompany this paper.

</details>


### [203] [(A)I Sees What You Don't: Exploiting New Attack Surfaces in Third-Party Mobile Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00333)
*Zidong Zhang,Zhentao Xie,Wenrui Diao,Jianliang Wu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper identifies novel attack surfaces in third-party VLM-powered mobile agents, demonstrates seven concrete attacks (e.g., subliminal text injection, invisible pixel exploitation, screenshot tampering) that hijack agent actions without privileges, and calls for perception-aware security models on multi-tenant platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Third-party mobile agents using VLMs act as high-privilege decision-makers, perceiving screenshots and executing actions. This paradigm transforms how apps interact with the OS and other apps, introducing new attack surfaces or turning previously benign interfaces into exploitable ones. The authors aim to systematically analyze the security posture of these agents and expose fundamental trust mismatches in autonomous agent design.

Method: The authors analyze the interaction model of third-party mobile agent apps versus general apps, identifying two unique attack surfaces: (1) Screen Perception Attack Surface, exploiting the gap between human and machine vision; (2) Misused Channel Attack Surface, targeting the agent's execution pipeline. They design and implement seven concrete attacks spanning subliminal text injection, invisible pixel zone exploitation, screenshot tampering, and host PC command injection. The attacks are evaluated against five popular mobile agent frameworks.

Result: Evaluation across five popular mobile agent frameworks shows that a malicious app can hijack agent actions and achieve arbitrary command execution without requiring any privilege permissions, all while remaining visually indistinguishable to human users. This confirms the viability and stealth of the proposed attack vectors.

Conclusion: The findings reveal a fundamental trust mismatch in autonomous agent design: agents trust visual perceptions and execution channels that can be trivially subverted. The paper highlights the urgent need for perception-aware security models on multi-tenant platforms to address these emerging threats.

Abstract: Third-party mobile agents powered by Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for automating smartphone interactions. These agents act as high-privilege decision-makers, perceiving device states through screenshots and executing actions via VLM reasoning, transforming how an agent app interacts with the environment (i.e., other apps or the OS). Correspondingly, this transformation introduces new attack surfaces or transforms benign/harmless interfaces into exploitable ones for mobile devices. In this paper, we summarize key differences between third-party mobile agent apps and general apps when interacting with the environment, analyze the security posture of agents, and identify two unique attack surfaces compared to general mobile apps: the Screen Perception Attack Surface, which exploits the gap between human and machine vision, and the Misused Channel Attack Surface, which intercepts or manipulates the agent's execution pipeline. We design and implement seven concrete attacks, from subliminal text injection and invisible pixel zone exploitation to screenshot tampering and host PC command injection. Our evaluation of five popular mobile agent frameworks demonstrates that a malicious app can hijack agent actions and achieve arbitrary command execution even without any privilege permissions, while remaining visually indistinguishable to users. These findings reveal a fundamental trust mismatch in autonomous agent design and highlight the urgent need for perception-aware security models on multi-tenant platforms.

</details>


### [204] [ReShift: Aha-Moment-Driven Reasoning-Level Backdoor Attacks on Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00361)
*Zhihao Dou,Qinjian Zhao,Zhiqiang Gao,Sumon Biswas*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: ReShift is a novel backdoor attack framework for Vision-Language Models that redirects chain-of-thought reasoning trajectories rather than manipulating final outputs, achieving high attack success while maintaining stealthiness and clean-task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLM backdoor attacks manipulate only final outputs, producing reasoning traces that are inconsistent with the output and easily detectable. There is a need for reasoning-level backdoors that subtly redirect the internal CoT to evade detection while preserving surface coherence.

Method: ReShift employs two core components: (1) a Poisoned Reasoning-Aware Data Construction (PRDC) pipeline to generate training data with trigger-conditioned reasoning shifts, and (2) a Supervised-Reinforcement Joint Optimization (SRJO) strategy to induce stable reasoning redirection. The authors formalize "Entropy Rebound" as a signal to characterize reasoning redirection and provide theoretical guarantees linking entropy gaps to trajectory-level divergence.

Result: Extensive experiments show ReShift achieves high attack success rates while preserving clean-task performance and generating realistic reasoning traces, significantly improving stealthiness against existing defenses.

Conclusion: ReShift establishes a new class of reasoning-level backdoor attacks that are more stealthy and robust than output-level methods, raising important security implications for safety-critical VLM deployments.

Abstract: Vision--Language Models (VLMs) are increasingly deployed in safety-critical applications, yet remain vulnerable to backdoor attacks. Existing methods primarily manipulate final outputs, often producing reasoning traces that are inconsistent or easily detectable. In this paper, we propose ReShift, the novel aha-moment-driven reasoning-level backdoor framework that explicitly redirects the internal chain-of-thought (CoT) trajectory while preserving surface-level coherence. ReShift introduces a Poisoned Reasoning-Aware Data Construction (PRDC) pipeline and a Supervised--Reinforcement Joint Optimization (SRJO) strategy to induce stable trigger-conditioned reasoning shifts. We further formalize Entropy Rebound as a principled signal for characterizing reasoning redirection and provide theoretical guaranties linking entropy gaps to trajectory-level divergence. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ReShift achieves high attack success rates while maintaining clean-task performance and realistic reasoning traces, substantially improving stealthiness against existing defenses.

</details>


### [205] [SoK: Attack and Defense Landscape of Mobile On-device AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00362)
*Yujin Huang,Xin Zheng,Xingliang Yuan,Kwok-Yan Lam*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper presents the first comprehensive systematization of knowledge (SoK) on security for mobile on-device AI (MoAI) systems, covering security pillars, attack landscapes, and defense mechanisms, while identifying research gaps and future directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MoAI systems that run AI models locally on mobile devices offer privacy, low latency, and offline capabilities. However, local storage of AI models introduces new security risks that are not yet systematically understood. The paper aims to fill this gap by providing the first structured overview of MoAI security.

Method: The authors conduct a systematization of knowledge (SoK), comprehensively surveying and categorizing the security pillars, attack vectors, and defense strategies relevant to MoAI systems. They synthesize existing research to establish a coherent framework and identify unresolved challenges.

Result: The paper delivers a systematic framework that organizes the attack landscape and defense landscape of MoAI systems, highlights unresolved gaps in current research, and proposes promising directions for future investigation.

Conclusion: This work establishes the foundational framework for understanding MoAI security, enabling the construction of more secure on-device AI systems and guiding future research. Companion resources are publicly available to support the community.

Abstract: Mobile on-device AI (MoAI) systems that integrate locally deployed AI models with conventional mobile software components are emerging as a key paradigm for delivering intelligent functionality directly on end-user devices. By moving inference from remote cloud services to the local mobile environment, such systems enable privacy-preserving, low-latency, and offline-capable AI functionality, yet introduce new security risks arising from the local storage of AI models. This paper presents the first comprehensive systematization of knowledge on MoAI security, covering security pillars, attack landscape, and defense landscape of MoAI systems. We further identify unresolved gaps in current attack and defense research and point to promising directions for future research in this emerging area. Our work establishes the first systematic framework for understanding the attack and defense landscapes of MoAI systems, serving as a foundation for building secure MoAI systems and advancing research in this critical domain. Companion resources are available at https://github.com/Jinxhy/Awesome-MoAI-Security.

</details>


### [206] [KidnapRAG: A Black-Box Attack for Hijacking Reasoning in Agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00422)
*Chanwoo Choi,Euntae Kim,Kyuho Lee,Youngsam Chun,Jinhee Jeong,Eunmi Kim,Myunggyo Oh,Junseo Jang,Buru Chang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: KidnapRAG is a black-box poisoning attack against Agentic RAG systems that uses three role-specific documents (Bait, Chain-Link, Mal-Ins) to sequentially hijack the agent's multi-step reasoning chain, outperforming existing baselines without requiring white-box access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing poisoning attacks on Agentic RAG systems assume white-box access (system prompts, reasoning traces, retrievers, model parameters), which is unrealistic. The paper aims to demonstrate effective attacks under black-box conditions where attackers can only publish externally retrievable poisoned documents.

Method: KidnapRAG employs a sequential poisoning strategy with three document types: (1) Bait — attracts initial retrieval by appearing relevant to the user query; (2) Chain-Link — induces query reformulation by the agent, steering subsequent retrieval toward attacker-controlled content; (3) Mal-Ins — provides attacker-controlled evidence that manipulates the final generated output. Experiments span multiple Agentic RAG frameworks, LLM backbones, and benchmarks.

Result: KidnapRAG consistently outperforms existing poisoning baselines under black-box conditions across multiple Agentic RAG frameworks, LLM backbones, and benchmarks. Analyses show the attack progressively weakens original retrieval intent, redirects retrieval behavior, and increases reliance on attacker-controlled evidence.

Conclusion: Agentic RAG systems, despite their iterative retrieval-and-reasoning robustness, remain vulnerable to black-box poisoning attacks like KidnapRAG. The attack's effectiveness highlights a security gap that warrants further attention. Code is publicly released.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are vulnerable to poisoning attacks that inject malicious documents into the retrieval process to manipulate model outputs. Recent Agentic RAG systems are more robust to such attacks because they iteratively perform retrieval and reasoning, allowing them to ignore weakly relevant poisoned documents and preserve the reasoning chain induced by the user query. However, existing attacks on Agentic RAG systems often assume white-box access to system prompts, reasoning traces, retrievers, or model parameters, limiting their applicability in realistic settings. In this paper, we study black-box poisoning attacks against Agentic RAG systems, where the attacker can only publish externally retrievable poisoned documents. We propose KidnapRAG, a sequential poisoning attack that hijacks the agent's multi-step reasoning chain using three role-specific documents: Bait, Chain-Link, and Mal-Ins, which attract initial retrieval, induce query reformulation, and provide attacker-controlled evidence, respectively. Experiments across multiple Agentic RAG frameworks, LLM backbones, and benchmarks show that KidnapRAG consistently outperforms existing poisoning baselines under black-box conditions. Further analyses show that KidnapRAG progressively weakens the original retrieval intent, redirects retrieval behavior, and increases reliance on attacker-controlled evidence. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/chanwoochoi316/KidnapRAG.

</details>


### [207] [Minos: A Multi-Agent Collaborative Framework for Provenance-Based Backward Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00440)
*Jiahui Wang,Zhenyuan Li,Zhengkai Wang,Xiangmin Shen,Fan Zhang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Minos is a multi-agent LLM-driven framework for provenance-based backward tracking of APTs. It uses a two-tier architecture with event-level analysis (hierarchical context, retrieval-augmented reasoning, adversarial deliberation) and graph exploration (four specialized agents coordinated by an FSM with hypothesis-guided reasoning and count-first query protocols). On 14 attack scenarios across 5 datasets, Minos achieves 0.92 recall and 0.64 precision, outperforming baselines while producing 49% more compact attack subgraphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing provenance-based backward tracking methods rely on low-level statistical features and rigid traversal strategies, which fail to capture high-level adversarial intent and suffer from dependency explosion. There is a need for more intelligent, interpretable forensic analysis that reasons like a human analyst.

Method: Minos is a multi-agent framework that casts backward tracking as an LLM-driven reasoning process. It has two tiers: (1) Event-level analysis: employs hierarchical context management, retrieval-augmented reasoning with citation verification, and adversarial deliberation to enhance reasoning quality. (2) Graph exploration: uses four specialized agents coordinated by a finite state machine (FSM), leveraging hypothesis-guided reasoning and count-first query protocols to replace exhaustive traversal and efficiently prune the search space.

Result: On 14 attack scenarios across five public datasets, Minos achieves an average recall of 0.92 and precision of 0.64, significantly outperforming state-of-the-art baselines. The attack subgraphs produced are 49% more compact, and the framework generates interpretable reasoning throughout the tracking process.

Conclusion: LLM-driven multi-agent reasoning is highly effective for automated provenance-based backward tracking. Minos demonstrates that hypothesis-guided exploration with adversarial deliberation can substantially improve both accuracy and conciseness of forensic analysis, while also providing interpretable audit trails.

Abstract: Sophisticated cyber attacks, particularly Advanced Persistent Threats (APTs), require effective post-intrusion forensic analysis. Provenance-based backward tracking reconstructs attack scenarios by tracing causality from security alerts, but existing methods rely on low-level statistical features and rigid traversal strategies, limiting their ability to capture high-level adversarial intent and suffering from dependency explosion. We present Minos, a multi-agent framework that formulates backward tracking as an LLM-driven reasoning process. Minos adopts a two-tiered architecture: for event-level analysis, it combines hierarchical context management, retrieval-augmented reasoning with citation verification, and adversarial deliberation to improve reasoning quality; for graph exploration, it coordinates four specialized agents under a finite state machine (FSM), replacing exhaustive traversal with hypothesis-guided reasoning and count-first query protocols to efficiently prune the search space. Experiments on 14 attack scenarios across five public datasets show that Minos achieves an average recall of 0.92 and precision of 0.64, significantly outperforming state-of-the-art baselines while producing attack subgraphs that are 49% more compact. Moreover, Minos generates interpretable reasoning throughout the tracking process, facilitating forensic auditing and system refinement. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of LLM-driven reasoning for automated provenance-based backward tracking.

</details>


### [208] [Beyond the Prompt: Jailbreaking Function-Calling LLMs via Simulated Moderation Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00481)
*Junlong Liu,Haobo Wang,Weiqi Luo,Xiaojun Jia*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: SMT (Simulated Moderation Traces) is a black-box jailbreak attack that exploits structural vulnerabilities in function-calling LLM systems by distributing adversarial intent across multi-turn trajectories framed as legitimate moderation audits. It consistently outperforms existing baselines on commercial LLMs from five providers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior jailbreak research focuses on prompt-level attacks/defenses, overlooking structural vulnerabilities in stateful, function-calling LLM environments where developer schemas, arguments, and untrusted tool outputs share a single context. This blurs the boundary between trusted control logic and untrusted data, creating an expanded attack surface that prompt-level sanitization cannot address.

Method: SMT constructs a multi-turn trajectory that simulates a legitimate moderation-auditing workflow. A fabricated moderation frame uses red-team testing as a pretext to elicit harmful generations. Subsequent validation feedback treats safety refusals as execution failures, prompting iterative refinements that progressively weaken safety constraints until harmful outputs emerge — all without modifying prompts directly.

Result: SMT achieves the highest average attack success rate (ASR) and HarmScore across five commercial LLM providers on two standardized safety benchmarks, while requiring a near-minimal number of queries, substantially outperforming all existing baselines.

Conclusion: Prompt-level sanitization is fundamentally insufficient for defending tool-enabled LLM systems. The findings call for context-aware validation mechanisms that encompass schemas, arguments, tool outputs, and accumulated conversation state to mitigate this class of structural vulnerabilities.

Abstract: Jailbreak attacks remain a critical threat to the safe deployment of large language models (LLMs). While prior work has primarily studied attacks and defenses at the prompt level, we show that this prompt-centric paradigm overlooks a structural vulnerability in stateful, function-calling environments. In such applications, developer-defined schemas, structured arguments, and untrusted tool outputs are interleaved into a single shared model context. This architecture expands the attack surface by blurring the boundary between trusted control logic and untrusted data, allowing adversarial intent to be distributed across a multi-turn execution path. We exploit this architectural flaw through SMT, a black-box attack framework based on Simulated Moderation Traces. Departing from purely prompt-based interactions, SMT constructs a multi-turn trajectory that simulates a legitimate moderation-auditing workflow. Within this trajectory, a fabricated moderation frame leverages red-team testing as a pretext to elicit harmful generations. The subsequent validation feedback treats safety refusals as execution failures, prompting refinements that gradually weaken the model's safety constraints and ultimately trigger harmful outputs. Extensive empirical evaluations on prominent commercial LLMs from five different providers across two standardized safety benchmarks show that SMT consistently achieves the highest average attack success rate and HarmScore while requiring a near-minimal number of queries, substantially outperforming existing baselines. These findings demonstrate that prompt-level sanitization alone is fundamentally insufficient for defending tool-enabled LLM systems and highlight the urgent need for context-aware validation across schemas, arguments, tool outputs, and accumulated conversation state. The code is available at https://github.com/liujlong27/SMT.

</details>


### [209] [Cross-Domain Generalization Failure in Lightweight Intrusion Detection Models for IIoT Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00553)
*MD Azizul Hakim,Md Shihab Uddin,Talha Ibne Anis*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Lightweight ML models for IIoT intrusion detection are typically evaluated only within their training network. This study shows they rely on a port-category shortcut that fails to generalize across networks, that evaluation protocol choice reverses apparent generalization difficulty, and that adversarial robustness is unrelated to cross-network generalization. Deployment readiness requires cross-network evaluation under realistic class distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lightweight IIoT intrusion detection models are evaluated within-domain, leaving cross-network generalization unverified. This raises concerns about their real-world deployment readiness on resource-constrained edge devices.

Method: Four lightweight architectures are trained on one IIoT dataset and evaluated without retraining on two structurally distinct IIoT datasets using a shared, restricted feature representation. Explainability analysis (SHAP) identifies influential features. Cross-network generalization, adversarial robustness, and recovery through limited target-domain adaptation are assessed under naturally imbalanced class distributions.

Result: Both top-performing models rely overwhelmingly on coarse port-category features; the most influential category appears 96–435× more in source-domain attack traffic than in target domains, indicating a relocated shortcut rather than its removal. The evaluation protocol can reverse which target network appears harder to generalize to. Adversarial robustness does not correlate with cross-network generalization, and adaptation recovery varies significantly by architecture.

Conclusion: Deployment readiness should be assessed via cross-network evaluation under realistic class distributions, not solely by within-domain accuracy.

Abstract: Lightweight machine learning models are increasingly proposed for intrusion detection in Industrial Internet of Things (IIoT) networks due to their suitability for resource-constrained edge deployment. Most reported results evaluate these models only within their training network, leaving behavior on unseen networks unverified. This study trains four lightweight architectures on one IIoT dataset and evaluates them, without retraining, on two structurally distinct IIoT datasets using a feature representation restricted to attributes available across all three sources. Explainability analysis across two top-performing models shows both rely overwhelmingly on coarse port-category features; the most influential category occurs in source-domain attack traffic at 96 to 435 times the rate in the two target domains, indicating that coarsening port resolution relocates rather than removes a documented shortcut. Evaluation under naturally imbalanced class distributions reveals a further effect: the evaluation protocol used can reverse which target network appears to pose the greater generalization challenge. Adversarial robustness and recovery through limited target-domain exposure are also assessed; robustness to adversarial perturbation is unrelated to cross-network generalization, and recovery through adaptation varies considerably by architecture. These findings suggest deployment readiness should be assessed using cross-network evaluation under realistic class distributions, rather than within-domain accuracy alone.

</details>


### [210] [The Rise and Fall of Google's Privacy Sandbox](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00693)
*Rachid Youssef Grib,Alberto Verna,Nikhil Jha,Martino Trevisan,Marco Mellia*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A longitudinal measurement study tracking Privacy Sandbox API adoption across top-10,000 websites, revealing that adoption had already stagnated before Google's October 2025 retirement announcement. Most APIs saw minimal, declining usage by a few actors; only CHIPS showed any growth. The failure leaves privacy-preserving interest-based advertising unresolved.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Google's Privacy Sandbox initiative aimed to replace privacy-invasive cross-site tracking with privacy-preserving advertising mechanisms. Despite five years of development and redesigns, the project was largely retired. This paper provides the first empirical, longitudinal, consent-aware measurement of how these APIs were actually deployed across the web, filling a gap in understanding the initiative's real-world trajectory and failure.

Method: The authors developed a custom call listener instrumented in a browser, combined with weekly crawls of the top-10,000 websites over several months preceding the October 2025 retirement. The measurement is consent-aware, monitoring usage of all major Privacy Sandbox APIs to capture adoption trends longitudinally.

Result: Adoption had stagnated well before Google's announcement. Most APIs were used by only a handful of actors, with activity steadily declining throughout the study period. Even APIs Google planned to retain showed no growth. The sole exception was Cookies Having Independent Partitioned State (CHIPS), which was the only API demonstrating any meaningful adoption trajectory.

Conclusion: The Privacy Sandbox's failure reflects limited ecosystem support and persistent design challenges. Its demise leaves the fundamental problem of enabling privacy-preserving, interest-based advertising on the web unresolved, representing a significant setback for the broader goal of reconciling privacy with the ad-supported web economy.

Abstract: On October 17th, 2025, Google announced the retirement of most Privacy Sandbox APIs, concluding nearly five years of experimentation with its alternative to privacy-invasive data collection on the Web. Designed to balance privacy with advertising functionality and cross-site tracking, the initiative faced repeated redesigns and limited ecosystem support.
  In this work, we present the first longitudinal, consent-aware measurement of the Privacy Sandbox's deployment across the Web. Using a custom call listener and weekly crawls of the top-10,000 websites, we monitor the usage of all major APIs in the months preceding their retirement. Adoption had already stagnated well before Google's announcement: most APIs were used by only a handful of actors, whose activity declined steadily throughout our study. Even the APIs that Google plans to maintain show no sign of growth. The sole exception is Cookies Having Independent Partitioned State (CHIPS). Overall, the demise of the Privacy Sandbox leaves unresolved the challenge of enabling privacy-preserving interest-based advertising.

</details>


### [211] [Know Thy Neighbor: Cross-TEE Mutual Attestation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00695)
*Daniel Andrade,João N. Silva,Miguel P. Correia*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper proposes Hema, a formally-verified heterogeneous mutual attestation protocol enabling TA instances across same or different TEE types (e.g., SGX, TrustZone) to mutually attest without requiring each TEE to support the other's full attestation software stack.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mutual attestation for communicating TA instances relies on bidirectional remote attestation, which works only when both TAs run on the same TEE type. Cross-TEE scenarios would require each TEE type to support the attestation stack of every other TEE type, an impractical burden. A dedicated cross-TEE mutual attestation protocol offers efficiency and security advantages over the naive bidirectional approach.

Method: The authors design the Heterogeneous Mutual Attestation (Hema) protocol, a formally-verified protocol for mutual attestation of TA instances. Hema supports both homogeneous (same TEE type) and heterogeneous (different TEE types, e.g., SGX and TrustZone) scenarios. The protocol is formally verified to ensure its security properties.

Result: The paper delivers Hema, a verified protocol that enables secure cross-TEE mutual attestation without requiring each TEE platform to implement foreign attestation stacks, thereby improving both efficiency and security over the naive bidirectional remote attestation approach.

Conclusion: Hema provides a practical, formally-grounded solution to the cross-TEE mutual attestation problem, facilitating secure inter-component communication in heterogeneous cloud service deployments that span multiple TEE types.

Abstract: Cloud services are composed of multiple heterogeneous distributed components and instances that communicate with one another. This occurs both in applications and services running in traditional execution environments and in trusted applications (TAs) running in trusted execution environments (TEEs). TA instances use attestation before exchanging information to ensure all parties meet the expected security conditions. The straightforward solution to mutually attesting two TA instances that are willing to communicate is employing remote attestation mechanisms in both directions. This is typically the case when the two TA instances are running on TEEs of the same type. In order to support cross-TEE attestation, such an approach, that is, using remote attestation in both directions, would require each TEE type (e.g., SGX, TrustZone) to support the attestation software stack of all other TEE types with which it needs to interact. A dedicated cross-TEE mutual attestation solution has multiple benefits in terms of efficiency and security. This paper presents the Heterogeneous Mutual Attestation (Hema) protocol, a formally-verified protocol for the mutual attestation of TA instances running on the same TEE type or on different TEE types.

</details>


### [212] [Forensic-Oriented Intrusion Detection Using Synthetic Network Traffic Data and Explainable Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00763)
*Jose Luis Vela Alonso,Carmen Pellicer*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A forensic-oriented intrusion detection framework that trains XGBoost classifiers exclusively on synthetic data (via SDV+CTGAN), preserving original evidence integrity per ISO/IEC standards, achieving near-equivalent performance to real-data training (F1=0.96 vs 0.97) while providing SHAP-based instance-level explanations suitable for court testimony.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ML intrusion detection pipelines treat original evidence as training data and produce opaque classifications without per-instance justification, failing forensic requirements for traceability, reproducibility, and court-defensibility under ISO/IEC 27037/27041/27042 and NIST SP 800-86.

Method: The framework enforces strict separation between immutable, hash-verified original evidence and derived artefacts. All training uses parameterized synthetic derivatives generated via SDV + CTGAN. XGBoost performs binary classification on tabular network flow data, and SHAP TreeExplainer generates instance-level feature attributions linking predictions to observable network behaviour. Evaluation uses Train-on-Synthetic, Test-on-Real (TSTR) on CICIDS2017, with cross-dataset validation on UNSW-NB15 and Kitsune, plus Kolmogorov-Smirnov testing for privacy preservation.

Result: TSTR on CICIDS2017 yields F1-macro=0.96 (real-data baseline: 0.97, within cross-validation variance). Mean KS statistic of 0.38 confirms synthetic privacy preservation alongside utility. Cross-dataset experiments identify feature dimensionality as the primary effectiveness driver, establishing a practical boundary of ~30 numeric flow-level features. SHAP attributions for Brute Force, Port Scan, and DoS attacks remain consistent across real and synthetic instances.

Conclusion: Synthetic-data training can satisfy forensic evidence-handling standards without sacrificing detection performance or explainability. The ~30-feature threshold provides a practical deployment guideline, and SHAP consistency across real/synthetic data supports admissibility of model outputs as expert witness testimony.

Abstract: Digital forensic investigations of network intrusions require analytical outputs that are traceable, reproducible, and court-defensible - requirements existing machine learning pipelines do not satisfy, since they treat original evidence as training data and produce opaque classifications without instance-level justification. This paper presents a forensic-oriented intrusion detection framework resolving both problems simultaneously, integrating synthetic data generation, binary classification, and explainability within a single pipeline governed by ISO/IEC 27037, 27041, 27042, and NIST SP 800-86.
  The framework operationalises the ISO/IEC 27037 requirement for strict separation between original digital evidence and derived analytical artefacts. Original datasets are treated as immutable, hash-verified artefacts; all training operates on parameterized synthetic derivatives via SDV + CTGAN. XGBoost binary classification provides high-performance detection on tabular network flow data, and SHAP TreeExplainer produces instance-level feature attributions mapping statistical predictions to observable network behaviour for forensic reporting.
  Train-on-Synthetic, Test-on-Real (TSTR) evaluation on CICIDS2017 achieves F1-macro = 0.96, within cross-validation variance of the real-data baseline (0.97). Kolmogorov-Smirnov testing confirms synthetic privacy preservation (mean |KS| = 0.38) alongside operational utility. Cross-dataset validation on UNSW-NB15 and Kitsune identifies feature space dimensionality as the primary determinant of synthetic training effectiveness, establishing a practical deployment boundary of approximately 30 numeric flow-level features. SHAP attributions for Brute Force, Port Scan, and DoS attacks are consistent across real and synthetic instances, confirming synthetic training preserves forensically relevant attack fingerprints required for expert witness testimony.

</details>


### [213] [No Country for Old Privacy: The Evolving Challenges of Anonymity in Bitcoin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00772)
*Ben Hawkins,Joshua Levett,Siamak F. Shahandashti*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A longitudinal measurement study tracking the on-chain adoption of second-generation Bitcoin privacy protocols (CoinJoin, CoinSwap, CoinShuffle, Stealth Addresses) reveals consistently low usage (<1% of transactions), a post-regulatory decline, and no standardized Stealth Address adoption — suggesting users are migrating to less detectable privacy methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite growing interest in Bitcoin transaction privacy, there is a lack of empirical, longitudinal data on the actual adoption of detectable second-generation anonymisation protocols. The authors aim to quantify real-world usage and assess whether these privacy-enhancing techniques are gaining traction or being abandoned.

Method: The authors implemented and refined a suite of heuristic filters to detect on-chain usage of four protocol classes — CoinJoin, CoinSwap, CoinShuffle, and Stealth Addresses — across the Bitcoin blockchain over an extended longitudinal period. Transactions were classified and counted to measure adoption trends and correlations with external events (e.g., regulatory actions, wallet operation periods).

Result: Over 5.94 million CoinJoin and 23.3 million CoinSwap transactions were identified. CoinShuffle usage was temporally correlated with the Wasabi wallet's operational lifespan. Overall adoption remained below 1% of all Bitcoin transactions, with a sharp decline following key regulatory events. No evidence of standardized Stealth Address adoption was found.

Conclusion: Second-generation on-chain privacy protocols constitute a niche, shrinking ecosystem. The low and declining detectable usage, combined with the absence of a common privacy standard, strongly indicates that privacy-seeking users are migrating toward more opaque, less detectable off-chain or alternative privacy methods.

Abstract: We present a longitudinal measurement study on the adoption of detectable, second-generation anonymisation protocols in the Bitcoin network, including CoinJoin, CoinSwap, CoinShuffle and Stealth Addresses. By implementing and refining a suite of heuristic filters, we identify over 5.94 million CoinJoin and 23.3 million CoinSwap transactions. Besides, the use of CoinShuffle was unexpectedly found to be closely aligned with the Wasabi wallet operation period. Our analysis reveals consistently low adoption rates, with these protocols constituting less than 1% of network transactions, and a sharp decline in detectable usage following key regulatory events. Furthermore, we find no evidence of standardised Stealth Address adoption, indicating a failure to converge on a common privacy standard. This study provides a comprehensive picture of a niche ecosystem whose on-chain visibility has been largely suppressed, strongly suggesting the migration of privacy-seeking users to less transparent and less detectable methods.

</details>


### [214] [Toward a Unified Security and Privacy Framework for AI-Native 6G Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01019)
*Bidushi Barua,Ahsan Khan,Kangfeng Ye,Panagiotis Papanastasiou,Yifan Liu,Mohit Bidikar,Anthony Moulds,Julie McCann,Poonam Yadav*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper surveys security and privacy challenges in AI-native 6G networks, arguing that isolated solutions are insufficient. It proposes a cross-layer framework with a unified threat taxonomy spanning infrastructure, network/architecture, AI, privacy, and security management domains, maps threats to countermeasures including standards harmonization, and identifies research gaps for building trustworthy 6G ecosystems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing security and privacy approaches for 6G are fragmented across different technologies, network architectures, AI systems, and standardization efforts. The convergence of communication, computing, sensing, and AI in 6G creates a heterogeneous threat landscape that cannot be effectively addressed by isolated, technology-specific solutions — motivating the need for a unified, cross-layer security and privacy framework.

Method: The authors conduct a comprehensive literature survey and develop: (1) a unified cross-layer security and privacy framework; (2) a cross-layer threat taxonomy covering five domains (infrastructure, network/architectural, AI, privacy, security management); (3) an analysis of representative threats mapped to key AI-native 6G technologies; and (4) a mapping of threats to cross-layer countermeasures, including standards harmonization as a security function.

Result: The survey identifies critical research gaps in secure, interoperable, and trustworthy AI-native 6G systems. It demonstrates that cross-layer countermeasures and standards harmonization are essential security functions, and provides a structured roadmap of future research priorities for privacy-preserving, resilient, and globally interoperable 6G networks.

Conclusion: Achieving trustworthy AI-native 6G requires a holistic, cross-layer approach rather than isolated solutions. The paper provides researchers, practitioners, and standardization bodies with a foundational framework for designing, evaluating, and deploying secure and privacy-preserving 6G systems, and outlines future directions toward globally interoperable, resilient 6G ecosystems.

Abstract: Sixth Generation (6G) communication networks are expected to evolve into AI-native, highly autonomous ecosystems that integrate communication, computing, sensing, and artificial intelligence. While these capabilities enable unprecedented connectivity and intelligent services, they also create a highly heterogeneous security and privacy landscape that cannot be addressed through isolated, technology-specific solutions. This paper presents a comprehensive survey of security and privacy in AI-native 6G networks from a cross-layer perspective. We first examine the fragmentation of existing security and privacy approaches across emerging technologies, network architectures, AI systems, and standardization efforts, motivating the need for a unified security and privacy framework. Building upon this framework, we develop a cross-layer threat taxonomy encompassing infrastructure, network and architectural, AI, privacy, and security management domains, and analyze representative threats across key AI-native 6G technologies. Furthermore, we map these threats to corresponding cross-layer countermeasures, including standards harmonization as a security function, and identify critical research gaps and future priorities for secure, interoperable, and trustworthy AI-native 6G ecosystems. Finally, we discuss future research directions toward realizing secure, privacy-preserving, resilient, and globally interoperable 6G networks. This survey provides researchers, practitioners, and standardization communities with a holistic foundation for the design, evaluation, and deployment of trustworthy AI-native 6G systems.

</details>


### [215] [Antaeus: Hunting Repository-Level Logic Vulnerabilities via Context-Grounded LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01138)
*Michele Armillotta,Nicolò Romandini,Rebecca Montanari,Lorenzo Cavallaro*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Antaeus is a framework for detecting logic vulnerabilities by grounding LLM reasoning in repository-level code context. It combines function prioritization via lightweight security signals, context-grounded analysis of security sinks and safety conditions, comparative validation to prune false positives, and structured reporting. Evaluated on 28 repositories, it detects 15 confirmed logic vulnerabilities, outperforming function-level and agentic baselines with comparable cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based vulnerability detectors excel at memory-safety bugs with well-defined security properties but struggle with logic vulnerabilities, which require inferring application-specific security invariants and implicit behavioral assumptions. Even frontier agentic models fail because these invariants are buried among unrelated code. This gap motivates a repository-scale approach that grounds reasoning in broader code context.

Method: Antaeus operates in a multi-stage pipeline: (1) Function prioritization using lightweight, repository-wide security signals to rank functions and direct costly LLM analysis toward relevant code; (2) Context-grounded reasoning that combines local code context with a repository-level view of functionality, security resources, and trust boundaries; (3) Identification of security-sensitive sinks and derivation of safety conditions, checking whether they are locally satisfied; (4) Comparative validation to prune findings that reflect project-wide norms rather than true violations; and (5) Structured reporting of sinks, violated conditions, and supporting evidence.

Result: Evaluated on 28 repositories containing confirmed logic vulnerabilities, Antaeus detected and explained 15 vulnerabilities, outperforming both function-level and agentic baseline models while maintaining comparable token usage and cost.

Conclusion: Grounding LLM reasoning in repository-level context, combined with prioritization and comparative validation, enables effective detection of logic vulnerabilities that elude both function-level and agentic approaches, without incurring additional cost overhead.

Abstract: LLM-based vulnerability detectors have shown promising results in identifying memory-safety bugs and vulnerability classes whose violations can often be expressed through established security properties. Logic vulnerabilities, however, pose a different challenge, as their identification requires inferring application-specific security invariants and implicit assumptions about intended behavior. Even frontier agentic models struggle because these invariants are often implicit and buried among unrelated code. Motivated by this gap, we present Antaeus, a framework for detecting logic vulnerabilities that grounds LLM reasoning in repository-level code context. Antaeus follows a repository-scale pipeline combining function prioritization, context-grounded reasoning, comparative validation, and structured reporting. It ranks functions using lightweight repo-wide security signals, directing costly LLM analysis toward relevant code and reducing calls, cost, and triage effort. For each prioritized function, Antaeus combines local code context with a repository-level view of the application's functionality, security resources, and trust boundaries. This enables reasoning about how the function is executed within the broader application rather than as an isolated snippet. Antaeus identifies security-sensitive sinks, derives safety conditions for safe execution, and checks whether they are locally satisfied. Candidate findings undergo comparative validation, pruning concerns that reflect project-wide norms rather than distinctive violations. Finally, Antaeus reports sinks, violated safety conditions, and evidence, making findings actionable and traceable. We evaluate Antaeus on 28 repositories with confirmed logic vulnerabilities and compare it against function-level and agentic models. Antaeus detects and explains 15 vulnerabilities, outperforming baselines with comparable token usage and cost.

</details>


### [216] [Detecting Adversarial Evasion Attacks Against Autoencoder-Based Network Intrusion Detection Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01194)
*Niklas Bunzel,Ashim Siwakoti*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper proposes two complementary detectors — Residual Localisation Detector (RLD) in image space and Feature-Space Perturbation Consistency (FPC) Detector in packet-feature space — to defend against PANDA-style adversarial evasion attacks on network intrusion detection systems (NIDS). Both detectors achieve near-perfect detection (≥0.99 across all metrics) against adversarial examples on IoT traffic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PANDA-style attacks transfer gradient-based adversarial examples from the vision domain to the network domain by converting packet sequences into grayscale images, successfully evading autoencoder-based NIDS without altering attack semantics. Defenders lack a straightforward method to distinguish benign flows from carefully perturbed malicious traffic.

Method: Two detection approaches: (1) RLD monitors spatial concentration of reconstruction errors in inter-arrival time features within image space; (2) FPC Detector operates directly on packet-level inter-arrival time features, checking perturbation consistency in packet-feature space. Evaluated using the UQ-IoT dataset across benign, malicious, and adversarial traffic from multiple IoT devices.

Result: Both RLD and FPC detectors achieved near-perfect detection performance on adversarial examples, with TNR, TPR, precision, recall, and F1-score all ≥ 0.99 across evaluated IoT traffic.

Conclusion: Integrating reconstruction-based scoring with perturbation consistency checks in both image and packet-feature space constitutes an effective, practical defense against PANDA-style adversarial evasion attacks on NIDS.

Abstract: Evasion attacks deliberately manipulate input to an ML-based system to produce an incorrect prediction while the manipulated input still appears benign. The PANDA framework has demonstrated that adversarial examples developed for the vision domain can be transferred to the network domain by converting packet sequences into invertible grayscale images, enabling gradient-based attacks such as masked FGSM against autoencoder-based network intrusion detection systems (NIDS). These attacks manipulate the NIDS anomaly score without altering the underlying attack semantics, leaving defenders without a straightforward way to distinguish between benign flows and carefully perturbed malicious traffic. In this paper, we propose two complementary detectors: the Residual Localisation Detector (RLD), which tracks the spatial concentration of reconstruction errors in the inter-arrival time feature region in image space; and the Feature-Space Perturbation Consistency (FPC) Detector, which operates directly on packet-level inter-arrival time features in packet-feature space. We evaluate both detectors on benign, malicious, and adversarial traffic from multiple IoT devices in the UQ-IoT dataset. Both detectors achieve near-perfect detection performance (TNR, TPR, precision, recall, and F1-score $\geq 0.99$) against adversarial examples across the evaluated IoT traffic. Our results indicate that integrating reconstruction-based scoring with perturbation consistency checks, in both image space and packet-feature space, offers a practical defence against emerging PANDA-style adversarial attacks on NIDS.

</details>


### [217] [All-out Attack: Optimal Block Withholding Under Pay-Per-Share Scheme](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01209)
*Mustafa Doger,Sennur Ulukus*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper analyzes Block Withholding (BWH) attacks under Pay-Per-Share (PPS) and Full-PPS (FPPS) reward schemes, proving these mechanisms are not incentive compatible contrary to prior claims. The optimal adversarial strategy is the All-out Attack (AoA), where the attacker submits only partial PoW shares while withholding full PoW blocks, achieving a relative gain of α/(1-α) post-difficulty adjustment, at the victim pool operator's expense.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior literature claimed that share-based reward schemes (PPS/FPPS) are incentive compatible against BWH attacks, unlike block-dependent schemes. This paper challenges that assertion by rigorously analyzing BWH behavior under modern share-based pool payout mechanisms in Nakamoto-style blockchains.

Method: The authors formally model BWH attacks under PPS and FPPS schemes. They analyze the All-out Attack (AoA) strategy — where the adversary allocates all hashpower to the victim pool, submits only partial PoW (pPoW) shares for immediate compensation, and withholds full PoW (fPoW) blocks. The analysis tracks adversary gains, honest miner benefits, and victim pool operator losses both before and after the first difficulty adjustment, using α as the adversarial hashpower fraction.

Result: Pre-difficulty-adjustment, the adversary incurs negligible loss from withheld fPoWs. Post-adjustment, reduced block difficulty yields more pPoWs per unit time, giving the adversary a relative gain of α/(1-α). All honest miners benefit at the same rate per unit hashpower. The victim pool operator pays out-of-pocket for pPoW submissions without receiving fPoW block rewards. Advanced variants like Fork After Withholding (FAW) provide no additional profit to the attacker under these schemes.

Conclusion: PPS and FPPS are not incentive compatible against BWH attacks, contradicting previous claims. The AoA is the optimal adversarial strategy, inflicting financial damage exclusively on the victim pool operator while honest miners and the attacker both benefit proportionally post-difficulty-adjustment.

Abstract: Classical Block Withholding (BWH) attacks have been extensively studied in block-dependent reward schemes, where pool members are compensated upon a block discovery within the pool. However, most contemporary mining pools operate under share-based scheme wherein participants are paid immediately upon submission of valid shares. In this paper, we analyze BWH under Pay-Per-Share (PPS) and Full-PPS (FPPS) schemes for Nakamoto-style blockchains and prove that these mechanisms are not incentive compatible -- contrary to claims in prior literature. Under PPS/FPPS, the optimal strategy for a BWH attacker is the All-out Attack (AoA): the adversary allocates its entire hashpower toward the victim pool, submitting only partial Proof-of-Work shares (pPoW) while withholding all valid blocks, i.e., full Proof-of-Work (fPoW).
  Under AoA, prior to the first difficulty adjustment, the adversary incurs negligible loss due to the withheld fPoWs. After the first difficulty adjustment, which reduces block difficulty, the adversary generates more pPoWs per unit time, achieving a relative gain of $\fracα{1-α}$ compared to pre-adjustment rates, where $α$ is the fraction of adversarial hashpower. Moreover, per unit time and per unit hashpower, all honest miners benefit at the same rate as the adversary. In contrast, the victim pool operator incurs losses: it pays the attacker out-of-pocket for pPoW submissions but receives no fPoW compensation in return. Finally, advanced variants of BWH, such as Fork After Withholding (FAW), do not yield additional profit to the attacker.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [218] [An SOA-Based Big Data Management Framework for Primary Healthcare Centers in Bahrain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00021)
*Hasan Abdulla,Fatema AlJazeeri,Shaikha Hasan,Zainab Almahafdha,Ehab Adwan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper designs and validates a Service-Oriented Architecture framework (SOA-MHC) for managing big data in a Bahraini primary healthcare center, demonstrating improved data integration, real-time analytics, operational efficiency, and patient satisfaction through a layered modular system built on loose coupling, reusability, and interoperability principles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rapid data growth in the healthcare industry has created significant challenges in managing big data effectively, particularly fragmented data silos and poor interoperability across disparate systems. The study addresses the gap in applying SOA principles within healthcare enterprise architecture to tackle these issues.

Method: The research employs Design Science Research Methodology (DSRM) across six phases: problem identification, objective definition, design/development, demonstration, evaluation, and communication. The SOA-MHC artifact features a three-layer architecture (Presentation, Service, Data Integration). Data collection uses stakeholder interviews via the Delphi technique, document reviews, observations, and benchmarking.

Result: The SOA-MHC implementation yielded improved data integration and interoperability across disparate systems, enhanced real-time data analytics and clinical insights, operational efficiency gains, cost reductions, and elevated patient engagement and satisfaction.

Conclusion: The architecture demonstrates the practical efficacy of an SOA-based enterprise framework in mitigating fragmented data silos. The research contributes to medical informatics knowledge and provides a validated deployment roadmap for healthcare data management transformation.

Abstract: The rapid data growth in the healthcare industry has presented significant challenges in managing big data effectively. This research designs and applies a service-oriented architecture-based approach for managing big data for a Primary Healthcare Center in Bahrain (SOA-MHC) to investigate the application of SOA principles in enterprise architecture. The research employs the Design Science Research Methodology (DSRM) to guide the development of the SOA-MHC artifact, following its six systematic phases: problem identification, objective definition, design/development, demonstration, evaluation, and communication. The SOA-MHC utilizes a layered architecture comprising a Presentation Layer, Service Layer, and Data Integration Layer. The framework leverages core SOA principles - specifically loose coupling, reusability, and interoperability - to create a modular system capable of handling the complexities of healthcare big data. Diverse data collection and validation methods were utilized, including stakeholder interviews via the Delphi technique, document reviews, observations, and benchmarking research. The study's empirical findings highlight significant operational benefits from the SOA-MHC implementation. These include improved data integration and interoperability across disparate systems, enhanced real-time data analytics and clinical insights, clear operational efficiency gains, cost reductions, and elevated patient engagement and satisfaction. The architecture demonstrates the practical efficacy of an SOA-based enterprise framework in mitigating fragmented data silos. This research contributes to the knowledge of applying SOA principles in medical informatics and provides a validated deployment roadmap for practitioners and researchers transforming data management methods in healthcare institutions.

</details>


### [219] [Stop Hand-Holding Your Coding Agent: Engineering the Loops that Replace Step-by-Step Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00038)
*Sandeco Macedo*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper formalizes "loop specification" as a new paradigm in coding agent interaction — a bounded, reusable artifact (trigger, goal, verification, stopping rule, memory) that replaces step-by-step prompting. It defines the discipline, provides a taxonomy and verification ladder, analyzes 50 real-world loops, and derives design principles. Key finding: 70% of loops already use autonomous verification and 74% name terminal states, while automated triggering and durable memory remain underdeveloped.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A 2026 shift in practitioner discourse urged moving from prompting agents to designing the loops that prompt them. The paper takes this claim seriously, observing that no rigorous treatment exists for what a "loop specification" is, how it differs from internal agent cycles or ordinary programming loops, and what design principles govern effective loop engineering.

Method: The authors (1) define loop specification and scope it against adjacent concepts; (2) develop an anatomy and taxonomy along dimensions of trigger, goal type, a five-level verification ladder (manual → autonomous), architecture, and terminal states; (3) conduct a descriptive analysis of the Loop Library — a hand-coded public corpus of 50 real loops; and (4) ground design principles and anti-patterns in existing scientific literature on self-correction, reward hacking, and model-as-judge fragility.

Result: The corpus analysis reveals that practice has matured where the discipline prescribes: 70% of loops verify in the autonomous zone of the verification ladder, and 74% name their terminal states explicitly. However, automated triggering and durable memory remain comparatively underdeveloped. The paper also surfaces critical limits: verification burden, comprehension debt, and risk of cognitive surrender.

Conclusion: Loop engineering constitutes a distinct new layer in the progression prompt → context → harness → loop, but does not retire prompt engineering — they are complementary tools. The discipline's maturity is uneven: autonomous verification and terminal state naming are well-adopted, while triggering and memory need further development. Practitioners must respect inherent limits around verification cost, loss of comprehension, and over-delegation to agents.

Abstract: In mid-2026 a slogan reorganized how practitioners talk about coding agents: stop prompting your agent, start designing the loop that prompts it. We take this claim seriously and give it a careful treatment. We call the object of the new practice the loop specification: a bounded, reusable artifact, made of a trigger, a goal, a verification step, a stopping rule and a memory, that a human hands to an agent harness (such as Claude Code or Codex) so the agent pursues a goal on its own, in place of step-by-step prompting. We distinguish this external loop specification from two things it is often confused with: an ordinary programming loop, and the internal perceive-act-observe cycle that the harness already provides as plumbing. We position loop engineering as a new layer in the progression from prompt to context to harness to loop, and we argue, against the stronger headlines, that it does not retire prompt engineering; loop and prompt are distinct tools with distinct uses. We offer four contributions: a definition and scope for the discipline; an anatomy and taxonomy of loop specifications organized around trigger, goal type, a five-level verification ladder, architecture, and named terminal states; a descriptive analysis of the Loop Library, a public corpus of fifty real loops that we code by hand; and a set of design principles and anti-patterns grounded in the scientific literature on self-correction, reward hacking and model-as-judge fragility. The corpus shows that practice has matured most where the discipline says it matters: seventy percent of loops verify in the autonomous zone of the ladder and seventy-four percent name their terminal states, while automated triggering and durable memory remain comparatively underdeveloped. We close with the limits the practice must respect, including the verification burden, comprehension debt and the risk of cognitive surrender.

</details>


### [220] [Evaluating Hardware Abstraction Layer Concepts for Software Defined Vehicles: Insights into Applicability and Effectiveness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00039)
*Akshay Narla,Johannes Stümpfle,Souvik Saha,Nasser Jazdi,Michael Weyrich*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Evaluates current automotive Hardware Abstraction Layers (HALs) and cross-domain HAL mechanisms for Software-Defined Vehicles (SDVs), finding that hypervisor-based HALs excel in safety and OTA readiness while middleware-based HALs offer portability; recommends a hybrid approach combining both.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Software-Defined Vehicles demand modularity, interoperability, and OTA updates, but existing vehicle systems with tightly coupled hardware/software cannot meet these needs. Decoupling via HALs is critical, yet the applicability of existing HAL approaches in SDV contexts remains uncertain.

Method: A systematic evaluation of current automotive HALs alongside HAL mechanisms from non-automotive domains (smartphones, networking, industrial automation). A criteria-driven evaluation framework was developed to assess HALs against SDV-specific requirements.

Result: Middleware-based HALs provide portability and modularity but fall short on safety and OTA readiness. Hypervisor-based HALs better support safety, OTA readiness, and hardware efficiency. Both approaches exhibit limitations, motivating a hybrid design recommendation.

Conclusion: A hybrid HAL architecture integrating hypervisor isolation with middleware standardization is recommended to deliver flexible, scalable, and future-ready HALs for SDVs across their lifecycle.

Abstract: The emergence of Software-Defined Vehicles represents a fundamental shift in automotive design, prioritizing software-centric architectures over traditional hardware-driven models. SDVs require modularity, interoperability, real-time processing, and over-the-air update capabilities throughout the vehicle lifecycle. However, current vehicle systems, characterized by tightly coupled software and hardware, struggle to meet these demands due to their complexity and heterogeneity. A critical first step toward enabling SDVs is the decoupling of software from hardware, which can be facilitated through a robust Hardware Abstraction Layer. While existing HALs offer hardware independence and standardized interfaces, their applicability and effectiveness in SDV contexts remain uncertain. This paper systematically evaluates current automotive HALs and explores HAL mechanisms from non-automotive domains, including smartphones, networking, and industrial automation, to extract cross-domain insights relevant to SDV development. A criteria-driven evaluation framework is developed to assess HALs against SDV-specific needs. Findings reveal that while middleware-based HALs offer portability and modularity, hypervisor-based approaches better support safety, OTA readiness, and hardware efficiency. Limitations in both approaches are identified, prompting recommendations for a hybrid HAL design that integrates hypervisor isolation with middleware standardization. This paper contributes to the ongoing developments in automotive software architecture by offering insights into the applicability and effectiveness of current HAL strategies. It provides actionable guidance for designing flexible, scalable, and future-ready HALs to support SDVs across their lifecycle.

</details>


### [221] [ATM: CID-Brokered Pre-Write Admission for Multi-Agent Code Co-Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00041)
*Eagl Huang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ATM introduces a governance framework for multi-agent LLM systems that controls concurrent write operations through specification-grounded admission control, using content identifiers, semantic atomization, and a neutral steward to ensure safe, auditable shared mutations within a single governance domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent LLM systems can decompose software tasks into planning, generation, validation, and repair, but a critical gap remains: no existing system adequately determines which concurrently generated write intents can safely proceed in parallel, which require serialization or deterministic composition, and which must be rejected. Without such governance, concurrent modifications to shared code risk inconsistency, corruption, and unrecoverable states.

Method: The AI-Atomic-Framework (ATM) binds task intent, repository scope, write admission, validation, and evidence obligations into a unified governance chain. Key components include: (1) a Content Identifier (CID) broker as the shared-mutation admission subsystem; (2) adapter-guided atomization that maps write intents to semantic atoms with bounded regions; (3) virtual atoms serving as temporary auditable governance units when persistent atom-map coverage is incomplete; and (4) a neutral steward that applies governed writes rather than allowing agents to mutate shared state directly. Evaluation uses a 12-scenario deterministic design matrix, three archived runner cases, ATM-AdmissionBench, three same-file boundary cases, a three-week external-adopter study, and an operational recovery-routing benchmark.

Result: Results support feasibility, auditability, and bounded recoverability within observed single-domain settings. The multi-pronged evaluation—spanning controlled design-matrix scenarios, archived cases, benchmarks, boundary-condition tests, and an external-adopter study—provides convergent evidence that the framework governs concurrent writes effectively under its design constraints.

Conclusion: ATM demonstrates that specification-grounded governance of concurrent LLM agent writes is feasible within a single governance domain, offering auditability and bounded recoverability. However, the authors explicitly refrain from claiming broad comparative superiority or applicability to cross-clone governance scenarios.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems can decompose software-engineering work into planning, generation, validation, and repair, but a narrower systems problem remains: before any governed shared mutation is applied, a system must decide which concurrently formed write intents may proceed in parallel, which require deterministic composition or serialization, and which must take a fail-closed path. We address this problem with the AI-Atomic-Framework (ATM), a specification-grounded governance substrate for software agents operating within a single governance domain.
  ATM binds task intent, repository scope, write admission, validation, and evidence obligations into one governance chain. A Content Identifier (CID) broker serves as the shared-mutation admission subsystem. Adapter-guided atomization maps write intents to semantic atoms and bounded regions; when persistent atom-map coverage is incomplete, virtual atoms provide temporary auditable governance units for conservative comparison and routing. Governed shared writes are ultimately applied by a neutral steward rather than directly by proposing agents.
  Evaluation combines controlled, field, adoption, and extension evidence, including a 12-scenario deterministic design matrix, three archived runner cases, ATM-AdmissionBench, three archived same-file boundary cases, a three-week external-adopter study, and an operational recovery-routing benchmark. The results support feasibility, auditability, and bounded recoverability within the observed single-domain settings, but do not claim broad comparative superiority or cross-clone governance.

</details>


### [222] [A Taxonomy of Single-Turn Textual Prompt Patterns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00043)
*Vennila Sooben,Eugene Syriani*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The report presents a reproducible taxonomy of 30 canonical prompt patterns for single-turn, text-based LLM interactions, organized along two dimensions, addressing inconsistent definitions in existing literature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are widely adopted, but prompt engineering knowledge is fragmented across surveys and catalogs. The term "prompt pattern" is defined inconsistently across sources, and existing classifications vary, creating a need for a unified, reproducible taxonomy.

Method: The authors followed a reproducible method to identify, extract, and classify prompt patterns from existing literature. They distilled 30 unique, canonical patterns and organized them along two orthogonal dimensions.

Result: A taxonomy of 30 canonical prompt patterns was produced, structured along two dimensions, covering single-turn, text-based LLM interactions.

Conclusion: A standardized taxonomy of prompt patterns can unify fragmented terminology and provide a structured reference for both practitioners and researchers in prompt engineering.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are now widely employed in software development and everyday use. Interacting with LLMs requires crafting prompts, which range from simple ad hoc sentences to extensive, detailed, and structured instructions. Knowledge about prompt engineering has been documented in several surveys and catalogs in the literature. However, the term ``prompt pattern'' is defined differently across sources, and existing works have classified prompt patterns in different ways. In this report, we present a taxonomy of prompt patterns for single-turn, text-based interactions. Following a reproducible method, we identified 30 unique and canonical prompt patterns, organized along two dimensions.

</details>


### [223] [Comparing Large Language Models on Scrum Certification-Style Questions: Accuracy, Stability, and Error Patterns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00048)
*Robson Alves Vilar,Emanuel Dantas Filho,Ademar França de Sousa Neto,Mirko Perkusich,Danyllo Wagner Albuquerque,João Paiva,Kyller Gorgônio,Angelo Perkusich*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper benchmarks GPT-5 mini, Gemini 3 Flash, and DeepSeek Chat 3.2 on 993 Scrum PSM I-style questions, finding Gemini 3 Flash leads in accuracy, with systematic error patterns linked to overgeneralization and normative conflicts rather than random failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed for certification-style QA in Software Engineering, yet their ability to adhere to strict normative definitions (roles, artifacts, rules) in frameworks like Scrum remains under-evaluated. The authors aim to systematically assess model performance, prompting strategy effects, intra-model stability, and failure modes in a domain where precise, standards-bound answers are critical.

Method: Three LLMs (GPT-5 mini, Gemini 3 Flash, DeepSeek Chat 3.2) were tested on 993 Scrum PSM I-style questions under three prompting strategies: zero-shot, chain-of-thought, and source-grounded. Each condition was executed repeatedly to measure intra-model stability. Performance was analyzed quantitatively across models, question formats (single-answer MC, multi-select, True/False), and Scrum topics. A qualitative error analysis categorized recurring mistake patterns in incorrect responses.

Result: Gemini 3 Flash achieved the highest accuracy, followed by GPT-5 mini and DeepSeek Chat 3.2; intra-model variability was low across all conditions. Single-answer multiple-choice items yielded highest accuracy; multi-select and True/False were more error-prone. Topic-wise, models performed well on normatively explicit areas (Artifacts, Empiricism, Product Value) but struggled with Scrum Values, Self-Managing Teams, and Stakeholders & Customers. Errors were systematic: overgeneralization, restrictive wording, compound distractors, and conflicts between market interpretations and strict Scrum definitions.

Conclusion: LLMs exhibit clear performance hierarchies in certification-style Scrum QA, but their errors are structural and predictable rather than random. The findings highlight that LLMs still falter when domain knowledge requires strict normative precision, especially where common practice diverges from formal definitions. Prompting strategies and question format robustness remain important considerations for deploying LLMs in such high-stakes, standards-bound settings.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in exam- and certification-style question answering tasks, where their ability to retrieve, interpret, and apply domain-specific knowledge can be systematically assessed. In Software Engineering, such settings are particularly relevant when questions depend on strict adherence to normative definitions, roles, artifacts, and rules. This paper evaluates the performance of three contemporary LLMs, \textit{GPT-5 mini}, \textit{Gemini 3 Flash}, and \textit{DeepSeek Chat 3.2}, in answering 993 Scrum certification-style questions aligned with the Professional Scrum Master I (PSM I) assessment format. We evaluated the models under three prompting strategies (\textit{zero-shot}, \textit{chain-of-thought}, and \textit{source-grounded}), with repeated executions to assess intra-model stability. We also analyzed performance across Scrum topics and question formats, complemented by a qualitative analysis of recurring error patterns in incorrect answers. Results revealed clear differences among models, with Gemini 3 Flash achieving the highest accuracy, followed by GPT-5 mini and DeepSeek Chat 3.2, while intra-model variability remained low across all conditions. By question format, the models achieved the highest accuracy on single-answer multiple-choice items, whereas multi-select and True/False questions were more error-prone. By topic, performance was more consistent in normatively explicit areas such as Artifacts, Empiricism, and Product Value, but more fragile in Scrum Values, Self-Managing Teams, and Stakeholders \& Customers. The qualitative analysis showed that errors were systematic rather than random, involving overgeneralization, restrictive wording, compound distractors, and conflicts between common market interpretations and strict Scrum definitions.

</details>


### [224] [Prompting GPT-5 on Scrum Certification Questions: An Empirical Accuracy Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00049)
*Mirko Perkusich,Danyllo Albuquerque,João Paiva,Robson Vilar,Emanuel Dantas,Ademar França de Sousa Neto,Rohit Gheyi,Kyller Gorgônio,Angelo Perkusich*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper evaluates how different prompting techniques (zero-shot, chain-of-thought, with-source citation) affect GPT-5's factual accuracy on Scrum certification questions. All prompts achieved >85% accuracy, with citation-based prompting performing best (89.1%). Errors clustered into misalignment with the Scrum Guide, out-of-scope content, and outdated interpretations, with modest but consistent improvements from advanced prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly adopted in Agile software development for documentation, coaching, and training (particularly for certifications like Professional Scrum Master), there is a pressing need to evaluate whether these models can reliably reason about the Scrum framework — a domain governed by well-defined, normative rules in the Scrum Guide (2020). The core research question is how different prompt engineering techniques impact factual accuracy and reliability of LLM responses to Scrum certification-style questions.

Method: The authors constructed a dataset of 993 validated PSM-aligned questions and queried GPT-5 using three distinct prompting techniques: (1) zero-shot prompting (no exemplars or reasoning guidance), (2) chain-of-thought prompting (step-by-step reasoning), and (3) with-source citation prompting (requiring the model to cite sources). Accuracy was measured per technique. Error analysis was conducted on questions where at least one prompt failed (16.2% of the dataset), with errors categorized into: misalignment with the Scrum Guide (28%), content outside scope (34%), and outdated or biased interpretations (38%).

Result: All three prompting techniques achieved certification-level accuracy above 85%. The citation-based variant achieved the highest accuracy at 89.1% with the lowest error rate. Correct answers were concentrated in well-defined topic areas: Definition of Done, Events, and Product Backlog Management. Single-answer multiple-choice questions were answered more stably than multi-select questions. More interpretive areas — Scrum Team and Product Value — showed less consistent performance. Among the 16.2% of questions where at least one prompt failed, 34% of errors involved content outside the Scrum Guide's scope, 38% stemmed from outdated or biased interpretations, and 28% from direct misalignment with the Scrum Guide.

Conclusion: Prompt engineering techniques produce modest but consistent improvements in LLM accuracy for Scrum certification tasks, with source-citation prompting particularly effective at reducing misinterpretation and version drift. These findings support more reliable use of LLMs in Agile learning and certification preparation contexts, though caution is warranted in interpretive topic areas and multi-select question formats where performance degrades.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in Agile Software Development for documentation, coaching, and training.
