As practitioners adopt these tools to prepare for certifications such as Professional Scrum Master (PSM), a key question is whether LLMs can reliably reason about Scrum, a framework with normative, well-defined rules described in the Scrum Guide (2020). This paper examines how different prompt techniques affect the factual accuracy of LLM responses to Scrum certification-style questions. A dataset of 993 validated PSM-aligned questions was answered by GPT-5 using three techniques: zero-shot, chain-of-thought, and with-source citation. All prompts achieved certification-level accuracy above 85\%, with the citation-based variant performing best (89.1\%) and yielding the lowest error rate. Correct answers concentrated in well-defined topics, such as \emph{Definition of Done}, Events, and Product Backlog Management, and in single-answer multiple-choice items, while multi-select questions and more interpretive areas, such as Scrum Team and Product Value, were less stable. Among questions where at least one prompt failed (16.2\%), errors clustered into misalignment with the Scrum Guide (28\%), content outside its scope (34\%), and outdated or biased interpretations (38\%). Overall, prompt techniques produced modest but consistent improvements, particularly in reducing misinterpretation and version drift, supporting more reliable use of LLMs in Agile learning and certification preparation.

</details>


### [225] [SWE-Router: Routing in Multi-turn Agentic Software Engineering Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00053)
*Seongho Son,Sangwoong Yoon,Jiahua Tang,Shuhan Wang,Lorenz Wolf,Ilija Bogunovic*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SWE-Router introduces a value-based temporal routing method that allows a cheap LLM to explore a task for a few turns before deciding whether to escalate to an expensive model, improving cost efficiency in agentic software engineering workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM routers for SWE tasks rely solely on the task description/prompt, which suffers from a fundamental Bayes-error floor: the same issue description can hide either a trivial fix (e.g., a typo) or a complex multi-module refactor. Prompt-only routing cannot disambiguate these cases, leading to wasteful use of expensive frontier models for easy tasks.

Method: SWE-Router lets a cheap LLM run for a few exploratory turns, then reads the resulting partial trajectory (state, actions, observations) before making a routing decision — either continue with the cheap model or escalate to an expensive one. The authors provide a Bayes-optimality theorem proving that conditioning on the partial trajectory never degrades routing performance and is strictly better when the exploration yields informative signals.

Result: Across multiple weak–strong LLM pairs spanning the contemporary cost–capability frontier, SWE-Router substantially improves cost efficiency on SWE tasks while retaining the majority of the stronger model's performance. The authors also release a multi-LLM trajectory dataset for reproducibility.

Conclusion: Conditioning routing decisions on intermediate execution trajectories — rather than on the prompt alone — is provably optimal and practically effective, enabling more economical deployment of expensive LLMs in multi-turn agentic software engineering pipelines.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) embedded in multi-turn agentic harnesses are reshaping software engineering (SWE), but routing every task to a frontier model is wasteful when many issues admit cheap fixes. Existing LLM routers operate on the task description alone, which inherits an information-theoretic Bayes-error floor in agentic settings: a similar issue can hide either a localized typo or a multi-module refactor, and the prompt does not separate the two. We introduce SWE-Router, a value-based temporal approach that lets a cheap model run for a few exploratory turns and reads the resulting partial trajectory before deciding whether to continue cheaply or to escalate to an expensive model. We provide a Bayes-optimality theorem showing that conditioning on the partial trajectory never harms routing and is strictly better whenever exploration is informative. Across the LLM pairs of weak and strong models spanning the contemporary cost--capability frontier, we show that SWE-Router greatly improves the cost efficiency of SWE tasks, while maintaining the majority of the performances of the stronger model. We additionally release a multi-LLM trajectory dataset which allows reproduction of our trajectory-level routing.

</details>


### [226] [A Quantitative Framework for Estimating System Complexity and Cost via Component Interface Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00054)
*Ken Y. Chan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a formal modeling framework that represents systems as directed graphs of couplings to estimate the complexity and cost of changes driven by external requirements, even when internal component logic is opaque. It offers multi-view representations (graphical, algebraic, tabular) and is validated through a retail banking platform integration case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating the complexity and cost of system-wide changes induced by external requirements is challenging, especially when internal component logic is opaque (black-box components). Existing approaches fail to provide bounded estimates in such scenarios, and there is a gap between component-based modeling and project cost estimation that needs bridging for effective architecture design and lifecycle management.

Method: The authors model a system as a directed graph of couplings that captures dependencies and information flows between components. Change complexity is estimated through component interfaces using bounded analysis, without requiring visibility into internal logic. External drivers and cost factors are associated with individual system elements to bound system-wide modification costs. The framework supports three complementary views: graphical (visual dependencies), algebraic (formal analysis), and tabular (computational/summary) representations.

Result: The framework provides actionable, bounded estimates of change complexity and cost propagation through a system. It successfully bridges component-based modeling with project cost estimation. Validation through a case study on a large-scale retail banking platform integration demonstrates practical applicability.

Conclusion: The proposed formal modeling framework effectively estimates bounded change complexity and cost in systems with opaque components, offering multi-view representations suited to different stakeholders. It has practical value for software architecture design, engineering, and lifecycle operations, as evidenced by the banking platform case study.

Abstract: This paper introduces a formal modeling framework designed to estimate the complexity and cost associated with system changes induced by external requirements. We model a system as a directed graph of couplings, capturing the intricate dependencies and information flows between components and elements within a specific context. The proposed method enables the estimation of bounded change complexity through component interfaces, even when internal logic remains opaque. Additionally, the framework provides a mechanism for bounding the cost of system-wide modifications by associating external drivers and cost factors with individual system elements. We propose a multi-view approach to the model, providing graphical, algebraic, and tabular representations to suit different levels of abstraction and computational needs. By bridging the gap between component-based modeling and project cost estimation, our method provides actionable insights for architecture design, software engineering, and lifecycle operations. The model is validated through a case study involving the integration of a large-scale retail banking platform.

</details>


### [227] [AlgoBench: Benchmarking Algorithmic Adaptation in Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00062)
*Xinyuan Song,Zekun Cai,Liang Zhao*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ALGOBENCH is a framework that automatically generates novel algorithmic problems from existing competitive-programming tasks via constraint-shifting transformations. Each variant is designed to break the original reference algorithm, forcing models to demonstrate genuine algorithmic adaptation rather than memorization. The paper also introduces complexity-aware metrics (OPTT, OPTS, TRAPRATE, GAPT, CONSENS) beyond pass@k. Experiments show LLM performance drops sharply on ALGOBENCH variants, with failures stemming primarily from algorithmic rather than implementation-level deficiencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing programming benchmarks like HumanEval and LiveCodeBench suffer from data contamination, where public release of problem statements, editorials, and solutions allows later models to improve through exposure rather than genuine algorithmic reasoning. There is a need to disentangle memorization from true algorithmic problem-solving ability.

Method: The authors propose ALGOBENCH, a framework that automatically constructs novel algorithmic problems by applying structured constraint-shifting transformations to known competitive-programming problems. Each variant must be traceable to a source but must cause the original reference algorithm to fail. They introduce five complexity-aware metrics — OPTT (optimal time), OPTS (optimal space), TRAPRATE (rate at which models fall into the trap of using the original algorithm), GAPT (gap between functional correctness and complexity adherence), and CONSENS (consensus on correct complexity) — to evaluate whether solutions meet asymptotic requirements.

Result: Experiments across multiple LLMs and prompting strategies reveal a sharp performance drop on ALGOBENCH variants compared to original benchmarks. Retrieval mechanisms increase the likelihood of reusing the outdated reference algorithm. Many solutions appear functionally correct but fail to satisfy the required complexity constraints. Error analysis confirms that failures are predominantly algorithmic rather than implementation-level.

Conclusion: ALGOBENCH successfully evaluates algorithmic adaptation beyond functional correctness, exposing weaknesses in current LLMs that standard benchmarks mask. The framework and complexity-aware metrics provide a more rigorous assessment of a model's ability to reason about and adapt algorithms to novel constraints.

Abstract: High pass rates on established programming benchmarks such as HumanEval and LiveCodeBench do not always show whether a model can reason about algorithms. Many fixed benchmarks eventually become part of the public training ecosystem through released problem statements, editorials, and generated solutions, allowing later models to improve partly by exposure rather than by stronger algorithmic ability. We introduce ALGOBENCH, a framework that automatically builds novel algorithmic problems from known competitive-programming problems through structured constraint-shifting transformations. Each accepted ALGOBENCH variant is traceable to a source problem, but must make the original reference algorithm fail. Beyond pass@$k$, we introduce complexity-aware metrics -- including OPTT, OPTS, TRAPRATE, GAPT, and CONSENS -- to test whether a solution is not only functionally correct but also asymptotically suitable for the generated problem. Experiments across multiple LLMs and prompting strategies show that performance drops sharply on ALGOBENCH variants, retrieval can increase reuse of the old algorithm, and many correct-looking solutions fail to meet the required complexity. Error analysis shows that failures are mainly algorithmic rather than implementation-level, suggesting that ALGOBENCH evaluates adaptation beyond functional correctness.

</details>


### [228] [The Illusion of Safety: Multi-Tier Verification of AI vs. Human C++ Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00107)
*Saif Mahmud,Fadul Sikder,Yuede Ji,Zhang Haotian,Jeff,Lei*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: VULBENCH-CPP benchmarks 8,918 C++ programs (human and AI-generated) across four verification tiers, finding AI-generated code is ~2x more likely to trigger runtime violations despite appearing equally safe under static analysis alone. Static analysis misleadingly conflates code length with safety, and different verification tiers catch disjoint violation classes, so multi-tier evaluation is essential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs increasingly generate C++ code, but C++ is memory-unsafe and a single violation can be exploitable. Existing security evaluations rely on static analysis alone, which only flags warnings without confirming runtime violations or covering untested paths. The authors aim to rigorously quantify whether AI-generated C++ is measurably less safe than human-written code and whether common verification tools agree on risk assessments.

Method: The authors introduce VULBENCH-CPP: a benchmark of 8,918 C++ programs from three open-weight LLMs (Gemma 3 27B IT, LLaMA 3.3 70B Instruct, Qwen 2.5 Coder 32B Instruct) and human authors across 851 competitive-programming tasks. Each program undergoes four-tier annotation: (1) functional testing, (2) static analysis via cppcheck and clang-tidy, (3) dynamic analysis via ASan/UBSan, and (4) bounded model checking via ESBMC. Statistical analysis controls for code length, test pass-rate, and correlation among solutions to shared tasks.

Result: AI-generated code is roughly twice as likely as human code to trigger a confirmed runtime violation, even after controlling for code length and test pass-rate. Under static analysis, AI and human code appear equally safe, but this is an artifact of code length rather than genuine safety. The four verification tiers detect largely disjoint classes of violation, confirming no single tier suffices. The gap persists across independent LLM generations.

Conclusion: Relying solely on static analysis gives a false impression of AI code safety. Multi-tier verification combining static, dynamic, and bounded model checking is necessary for trustworthy safety assessment. AI-generated C++ remains significantly less safe than human-written code, warranting caution in deployment.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly generate C++, a memory-unsafe language where a single overlooked violation can become an exploitable bug. Yet most security evaluations of AI-generated code rely on static analysis alone, which flags warnings without confirming runtime violations or reasoning about untested paths. We ask whether AI-generated C++ is measurably less safe than human-written code, and whether common verification tools agree on the risk. We introduce VULBENCH-CPP, a benchmark of 8,918 C++ programs from three open-weight LLMs (Gemma 3 27B IT, LLaMA 3.3 70B Instruct, Qwen 2.5 Coder 32B Instruct) and human authors across 851 competitive-programming tasks. Each program is annotated by four verification tiers: functional testing, static analysis (cppcheck, clang-tidy), dynamic analysis (ASan/UBSan), and bounded model checking (ESBMC). Accounting for the correlation among solutions to a shared task, we find that AI-generated code is roughly twice as likely as human code to trigger a confirmed runtime violation, even after controlling for code length and test pass-rate. Under static analysis the two look equally safe, but this is misleading: the apparent similarity reflects code length rather than real safety, and the tiers detect largely different classes of violation, so no single tier is sufficient. The gap is consistent across independent generations.

</details>


### [229] [Registry-Governed Agent Lifecycle:Completing EDDOps with Evaluation-DrivenRegistration, Promotion, and Retirement on AWS AgentCore](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00345)
*Richard Kang,Vincent Wang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper proposes Evaluation-Driven Development and Operations (EDDOps) for enterprise LLM agent deployment, implemented on AWS Bedrock AgentCore, with an empirical cost-to-performance framework for model selection validated through a proof-of-concept comparing three foundation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional TDD/BDD methodologies are inadequate for non-deterministic LLM agents, necessitating a continuous evaluation-driven governance approach that holistically balances quality, reliability, safety, latency, and cost in enterprise model selection.

Method: The authors synthesize EDDOps conceptually, map its reference architecture onto AWS Bedrock AgentCore components (Runtime, Evaluations, Agent Registry, CloudWatch), and empirically compare three foundation models across two deployment paths using 30 single-turn invocations, 9 multi-turn evaluations, and registry-integrated governance.

Result: Evaluation evidence successfully transforms model selection from benchmark-ranking into a governed economic decision. Managed agent platforms support EDDOps when they provide trace-native observability, pluggable evaluator frameworks, and governed registry-based discovery.

Conclusion: EDDOps, when instantiated on platforms like AWS Bedrock AgentCore, enables enterprises to make cost-aware, evidence-based foundation model selection decisions rather than relying solely on benchmark rankings.

Abstract: Enterprise adoption of LLM agents requires model selection methods that balance quality, reliability, safety, latency, and cost. Evaluation-Driven Development and Operations (EDDOps) positions evaluation as a continuous governing function across the agent lifecycle rather than a terminal checkpoint. This paper presents a practitioner-oriented instantiation of EDDOps on AWS Bedrock AgentCore and proposes a cost-to-performance framework for selecting foundation models in enterprise agent architectures. We make three contributions: a conceptual synthesis explaining why traditional TDD/BDD methods are insufficient for non-deterministic LLM agents; an architectural mapping of the EDDOps reference architecture onto AgentCore Runtime, Evaluations, Agent Registry, and CloudWatch observability; and an empirical cost-to-performance decision framework validated through a proof-of-concept comparing three foundation models across two deployment paths. Using trace data from 30 single-turn invocations across six agents, 9 multi-turn evaluations, and registry-integrated governance, we show how evaluation evidence can convert model selection from a benchmark-ranking exercise into a governed economic decision. The results suggest that managed agent platforms can support EDDOps when they provide trace-native observability, pluggable evaluator frameworks, and governed registry-based discovery.

</details>


### [230] [BT-APE: A Computationally Light Backtracking Approach to Automatic Prompt Engineering for Requirements Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00427)
*Mohammad Amin Zadenoori,Waad Alhoshan,Jacek Dąbrowski,Liping Zhao,Alessio Ferrari*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: BT-APE is a lightweight automatic prompt engineering method that optimizes prompts for LLM-based requirements classification through iterative refinement with backtracking search, achieving accuracy comparable to resource-intensive baselines while using ~72% fewer tokens and 66% less time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual prompt design via trial and error in RE tasks yields inconsistent and suboptimal results. Automated prompt engineering remains unexplored in RE, and existing APE methods (like PE2) are resource-intensive, making them impractical for resource-constrained settings.

Method: BT-APE frames prompt design as an optimization problem with three key components: (1) iterative refinement using LLM-generated candidate prompts, (2) backtracking search to escape local optima, and (3) dynamic example selection. Evaluated on three benchmark datasets with five instruction-tuned LLMs against zero-shot, few-shot, chain-of-thought, CoT+few-shot, and PE2 baselines.

Result: BT-APE and PE2 achieve nearly identical accuracy, both significantly outperforming classical prompting baselines with large effect sizes. BT-APE consumes approximately 72% fewer input tokens and 66% less wall-clock time than PE2 at equivalent accuracy.

Conclusion: Three key contributions: (i) a lightweight APE framework with open-source tool and replication package; (ii) the first systematic comparison of APE vs. classical prompting for requirements classification; (iii) insights into how class definitions and prompt evolution influence performance.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to requirements engineering (RE) tasks, yet the prompts guiding them are typically designed manually through trial and error, yielding inconsistent and suboptimal results. Automated prompt construction remains largely unexplored in RE, leaving its effectiveness unclear. To address this, we propose a lightweight Automatic Prompt Engineering approach, Backtracking APE (BT-APE), and apply it to requirements classification. We frame prompt design as an optimization problem, iteratively refining prompts via LLM-generated candidates, backtracking search, and dynamic example selection. Evaluating BT-APE on three benchmark datasets with five instruction-tuned LLMs, we compare it against four classical prompting baselines (zero-shot, few-shot, chain-of-thought, CoT+few-shot) and a state-of-the-art but resource-intensive APE baseline (PE2). BT-APE and PE2 achieve nearly identical accuracy, both substantially outperforming the classical baselines with large effect sizes; however, BT-APE imposes a far lighter computational footprint, consuming roughly 72% fewer input tokens and 66% less wall-clock time at equivalent accuracy, making it better suited to resource-constrained deployment. Our contributions are threefold: (i) a lightweight APE framework with an open interactive tool and replication package; (ii) the first systematic comparison of APE against classical prompting for requirements classification; and (iii) insights into how class definitions and prompt evolution affect performance.

</details>


### [231] [Social Popularity of GitHub Projects: A Lifeline or a Liability?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00435)
*Mohit Kaushik,Kuljit Kaur Chahal*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study analyzes 73,000+ GitHub repositories using an Accelerated Failure Time (AFT) model to identify survival factors. Human capital (contributors) is the strongest protective factor, while excessive social attention (stars, forks) paradoxically accelerates project death—especially when combined with accessibility features like documentation and readable code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite millions of repositories on platforms like GitHub, many projects die quickly, and the interplay between human capital, social attention, and structural features (code readability, documentation) in determining project survival remains poorly understood.

Method: Accelerated Failure Time (AFT) survival analysis applied to a dataset of over 73,000 GitHub repositories, accounting for time-varying predictors to model the hazard of project inactivity over time.

Result: Human capital emerged as the most critical protective factor for project survival. Excessive social attention (popularity metrics) proved to be a liability, and when combined with accessibility/onboarding features (documentation, readable code), it amplified the risk of project cessation. However, when contributor count interacts with social popularity, the protective effect of labor becomes evident.

Conclusion: Open-source project sustainability requires governance strategies that balance visibility with labor capacity. High social attention without sufficient human capital is detrimental; onboarding features, while helpful, may inadvertently accelerate abandonment under high visibility.

Abstract: Social coding platforms such as GitHub host millions of repositories, yet many suffer from high mortality rates. Despite this, several survival factors remain poorly understood. Human capital is widely recognized as essential. Social attention, while often assumed to be a lifeline, can become a liability. Structural features that improve onboarding, such as code readability and documentation, may also accelerate the cessation of active development when combined with massive visibility. To examine these dynamics, we analyzed more than 73,000 GitHub repositories using an Accelerated Failure Time (AFT) survival framework, which accounts for the time-varying nature of predictors. Our study identifies human capital as the most critical determinant of project survival. In contrast, excessive social attention emerges as a liability, and when coupled with accessibility features, it amplifies the risk of project inactivity. Importantly, when the number of contributors interacts with social popularity, the protective effect of labor becomes visible, highlighting the need for governance strategies that balance visibility with labor capacity to ensure the long-term resilience of open-source projects.

</details>


### [232] [Large Language Models for Multi-Lingual Equivalent Mutant Detection: An Extended Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00511)
*Honglin Shu,Zhao Tian,Dong Wang,Junji Yu,Jiazhe Zhang,Xuejie Cao,Junjie Chen,Yasutaka Kamei*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents the first comprehensive empirical study evaluating large language models (LLMs) for equivalent mutant detection (EMD) across Java and C, demonstrating that LLM-based approaches outperform traditional methods in F1-score, offer practical inference efficiency, and exhibit measurable cross-lingual generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Equivalent mutants impose unnecessary costs and bias on mutation testing. Pure-code analysis methods are limited by compiler infrastructure dependencies, while ML-based methods suffer from scarce training data and poor generalization. LLMs excel at capturing program semantics in code-related tasks, yet their potential for EMD remains unexplored, especially across multiple languages.

Method: The authors benchmark LLM-based EMD approaches using 3,302 Java and 1,088 C mutant pairs against state-of-the-art methods. They explore multiple strategy variations (including fine-tuned code embeddings), assess inference efficiency, and evaluate cross-lingual generalization by testing models trained on one language against the other.

Result: LLM-based approaches achieve higher F1-scores than evaluated traditional methods, with fine-tuned code embeddings yielding the highest detection accuracy. Inference times are comparable to existing ML models, striking a practical balance between effectiveness and efficiency. Fine-tuned LLMs demonstrate measurable cross-lingual generalization.

Conclusion: LLMs are a viable and efficient approach for equivalent mutant detection, offering a promising new direction for advancing mutation testing in practice and addressing the longstanding limitations of prior EMD techniques.

Abstract: Mutation testing is a powerful technique for ensuring software quality. However, the presence of equivalent mutants introduces unnecessary costs and biases, limiting its practical effectiveness. Although numerous equivalent mutant detection (EMD) methods have been proposed, they often face distinct challenges: pure-code analysis methods can be limited by their reliance on specific compiler infrastructures, while existing machine-learning approaches remain constrained by scarce training data and limited generalization to unseen mutants. Large language models (LLMs) have recently demonstrated remarkable performance across diverse code-related tasks by better capturing program semantics. Yet their potential for EMD remains largely unexplored, particularly in the multi-lingual context. This paper presents the first comprehensive empirical study on LLMs for EMD, using 3,302 Java and 1,088 C mutant pairs to benchmark against state-of-the-art methods, explore strategy variations, assess efficiency, and evaluate cross-lingual generalization. Experimental results show that LLM-based approaches achieve higher F1-scores than the evaluated traditional methods, with fine-tuned code embedding yielding the highest detection accuracy among the tested strategies. Moreover, LLM-based approaches strike a practical balance between effectiveness and efficiency with inference times comparable to existing machine-learning models. Importantly, fine-tuned LLMs demonstrate measurable generalization across programming languages. These findings establish LLMs as a viable and efficient approach for tackling the longstanding challenge of equivalent mutant detection, offering new directions for advancing mutation testing in practice.

</details>


### [233] [Auditing Empirical Comparisons in Quantum Software](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00516)
*Boshuai Ye,Peng Liang,Maryam Tavassoli Sabzevari,Arif Ali Khan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CLAIMSTAB-QC is a framework for auditing empirical comparisons in quantum software papers. Applied to 455 claims from 119 papers, it reveals a severe materialization gap: only 8 claims provide sufficient matched evidence for audit, yielding 2 Sustained, 4 Unresolved, and 2 Reversed outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum-software papers frequently claim that one tool outperforms another, but such comparisons are contingent on many factors (benchmark scope, circuit construction, compilation, backend assumptions, etc.). Existing methods assess individual components but do not directly audit whether reported comparisons are supported by the evidence provided.

Method: The authors propose CLAIMSTAB-QC, a source-bounded auditing framework. It records baselines, metrics, relations, and admissible evidence; locks the comparison design before computing outcomes; and classifies scalar-directional comparisons as Sustained, Unresolved, or Reversed within a locked audit scope.

Result: Of 455 claims from 119 papers: 175 can be represented for audit planning, 79 become scalar-directional planning records, 53 yield lockable audit designs, and only 8 expose enough matched evidence for audit without proxy reconstruction. These 8 yield 2 Sustained, 4 Unresolved, and 2 Reversed. Controlled diagnostics over 24 benchmark comparisons show that simpler checks can preserve apparent directions that weaken under locked designs.

Conclusion: There is a substantial materialization gap between reported comparative claims and auditable evidence. Most claims in quantum-software papers lack the rigor needed for independent verification, and weaker audit methods risk falsely preserving unsupported superiority claims.

Abstract: Empirical quantum-software papers often report that one compiler, optimizer, backend, or ansatz outperforms another. Such comparisons are not properties of a tool alone: they can change with benchmark scope, circuit construction, compilation, sampling, backend or noise assumptions, optimizer choices, and resource budgets. Existing testing, benchmarking, and reproducibility methods help assess programs, tools, executions, and platforms, but they do not directly audit whether the reported comparison itself is supported by the evidence exposed in the source paper or accompanying materials.
  We present CLAIMSTAB-QC, a source-bounded framework for auditing empirical comparisons in quantum software. Given a reported comparison, the framework records the baselines, metric, relation, and admissible evidence; locks the comparison design before outcomes are computed; and reports either a scoped relation outcome or an explicit evidence boundary. For strict scalar-directional comparisons, the reported direction is classified as Sustained, Unresolved, or Reversed within the locked audit scope.
  We evaluate CLAIMSTAB-QC on 455 comparative claims from 119 quantum-software papers. The central finding is a materialization gap: 175 claims can be represented for audit planning, 79 become scalar-directional planning records, 53 yield lockable audit or diagnostic designs, and only 8 expose enough matched evidence to audit the original comparison without proxy reconstruction. These 8 records yield 2 Sustained, 4 Unresolved, and 2 Reversed outcomes. Controlled diagnostics over 24 benchmark-relevant comparisons further show that simpler checks can preserve apparent directions whose support weakens under locked audit designs.

</details>


### [234] [Rise From The Ashes: LLM-based Static Analysis for Deep Learning Framework Bugs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00555)
*Shaoyu Yang,Haifeng Lin,Chunrong Fang,Xiang Chen,Wei Cheng,Jiawei Liu,Yiyu Zhang,Hongyu Liu,Zhenyu Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Phoenix is an LLM-based static analysis framework for deep learning frameworks that models cross-language tensor flows as a Semantic Bridge Intermediate Representation (SBIR). Using a multi-agent workflow (summarization, extraction, generation, analysis), it detects bugs without runtime execution, finding 31 real bugs in PyTorch with 20 patches already merged upstream.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing dynamic approaches (e.g., fuzzing) for finding bugs in DL frameworks are effective but computationally expensive due to real test execution. Static analysis offers fast, scalable testing without runtime execution, but current work is limited because DL frameworks have multilingual architectures and complex tensor-related program state that are difficult to model statically.

Method: Phoenix introduces a multi-agent LLM-based static analysis workflow: (1) a Summarization Agent distills bug summaries from historical bug-fix patches and CWE rules; (2) an Extraction Agent identifies bug-relevant repository symbols for targeted code retrieval; (3) a Generation Agent synthesizes grounded Semantic Bridge Intermediate Representations (SBIRs) from retrieved context, modeling cross-language tensor flows with concrete code context; (4) an Analysis Agent checks the SBIRs to detect and report potential bugs in tensor semantic propagation.

Result: Phoenix discovered 31 real new bugs in PyTorch across heterogeneous hardware backends (Intel CPU, NVIDIA CUDA, Apple MPS). Of these, 20 submitted bug-fixing patches have been accepted and merged into the upstream repository.

Conclusion: Phoenix demonstrates that LLM-based static analysis is a practical and effective complement to dynamic fuzzing for DL framework testing, successfully bridging the gap posed by multilingual architectures and tensor semantics to uncover real, patchable bugs at scale.

Abstract: Deep learning (DL) frameworks are critical AI infrastructures that often hide bugs with serious security implications. While dynamic approaches such as fuzzing are effective in uncovering these bugs, they require real test execution and incur high computational costs. Static analysis is a natural complement because it can detect bugs without runtime execution, offering fast and scalable testing. Unfortunately, there is still limited work targeting static analysis for DL frameworks due to their multilingual architectures and tensor-related program state.
  We present Phoenix, the first LLM-based static analysis technique for DL frameworks. Our key insight is that cross-language tensor flows in DL frameworks can be modeled, together with concrete code context, as a structured semantic bridge intermediate representation (SBIR) that LLMs can analyze for potential bugs in tensor semantic propagation. We implement this insight through a multi-agent workflow. A summarization agent first distills bug summaries from historical bug-fix patches and CWE rules. Guided by each summary, an extraction agent identifies bug-relevant repository symbols for code retrieval, and a generation agent synthesizes grounded SBIRs from the retrieved context. Finally, an analysis agent is leveraged to check SBIRs and report potential bugs. Our evaluation shows that Phoenix is a practical complement to dynamic DL framework testing for bug finding. To date, Phoenix has found 31 real new bugs in PyTorch for different heterogeneous hardware backends (Intel CPU, NVIDIA CUDA, and Apple MPS). Among them, 20 submitted bug-fixing patches have been merged into upstream.

</details>


### [235] [A Methodology for Investigating AI Patterns Prevalence in Software Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00558)
*Srinath Perera,Hasinthaka Piyumal,Frank Leymann,Rania Khalaf*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper presents a methodology combining literature mining and active learning to identify 14 AI pattern classes and empirically validate their prevalence across 100 GitHub repositories, achieving 56% accuracy (vs. 11% baseline) in an 8-way classification task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many AI patterns have been proposed in the literature, but their actual prevalence in real-world code repositories has not been empirically validated. This gap makes it difficult to assess the practical significance and utility of these patterns.

Method: A two-phase methodology: (1) literature mining of 44 published AI pattern sources to identify 14 AI pattern classes; (2) active learning applied to 100 open-source GitHub AI repositories to classify and estimate the prevalence of the most common pattern class, with accuracy bounds derived via prevalence estimation.

Result: The active learning model achieved 56% accuracy and 55% recall in an 8-way classification task, substantially outperforming the 11% random-chance baseline. Prevalence estimation provided usable bounds for analyzing pattern applications in practice.

Conclusion: The proposed methodology offers a robust, empirically grounded foundation for understanding how AI patterns are used in practice, addressing a field that currently lacks empirical data. The approach demonstrates that literature-derived patterns can be systematically validated in real codebases.

Abstract: As Artificial Intelligence(AI)-based applications take off, a clear understanding of AI patterns can uplift the quality of AI applications. Many AI patterns have been proposed in the literature; however, their prevalence in real-life code has not yet been validated. Understanding the actual use of those patterns in practice can clarify our understanding both of the significance of these patterns and their utility. In this paper, we present a methodology to a) identify relevant patterns by mining the literature and then to b) validate their presence and prevalence in actual code repositories using active learning. To that end, we identify 14 AI pattern classes by mining 44 published AI pattern-related sources. Then we use an active learning approach to determine the prevalence of the most common pattern class across 100 GitHub open AI repositories. Using prevalence estimation, we propose bounds on the accuracy of the occurrences. The model achieves 56\% accuracy and 55\% recall in an 8-way classification task, significantly outperforming the 11\% random-chance baseline. Furthermore, the prevalence estimation offers usable bounds for analyzing pattern applications. This methodology provides a robust foundation to start understanding how AI patterns are used in practice, a field that currently lacks empirical data.

</details>


### [236] [Towards Better Linux Kernel Fault Localization: Leveraging Contrastive Reasoning and Hierarchical Context Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00562)
*Haichi Wang,Ruiguo Yu,Yesong Pang,Yingquan Zhao,Junjie Chen,Jiajun Jiang,Zan Wang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CoHiKer is an LLM-based fault localization technique for the Linux kernel that uses contrastive reasoning on mutated test cases and hierarchical context analysis to achieve superior accuracy at both file and method levels while reducing token consumption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing fault localization techniques, including recent LLM-based approaches, treat kernel code as plain text without leveraging kernel-specific knowledge, limiting their ability to perform fine-grained root-cause analysis in the Linux kernel's complex, low-level environment.

Method: CoHiKer employs two innovations: (1) contrastive reasoning that analyzes behavioral divergence between mutated passing and failing test cases to identify root causes, and (2) hierarchical context analysis that narrows localization from files to methods by integrating crash reports, syscall semantics, inter-file dependencies, and kernel-specific features. It decomposes the task for structured LLM prompting.

Result: On an extended Linux kernel bug dataset, CoHiKer outperforms five SOTA baselines: Top-1 accuracy improves up to 26.07% (file level) and 56.85% (method level) over SOTA LLM-based baselines, with up to 8.84% and 28.9% token reductions. On non-kernel datasets, gains are 15.5% (file) and 5.3% (method) in Top-1.

Conclusion: CoHiKer demonstrates that integrating kernel-specific knowledge via contrastive reasoning and hierarchical context analysis significantly advances fault localization accuracy and efficiency for the Linux kernel, with strong generalizability to non-kernel software.

Abstract: Debugging the Linux kernel remains a formidable challenge due to its vast codebase, complex architecture, and low-level programming intricacies. Effective fault localization (FL) is thus essential for efficient kernel debugging and maintenance. While existing FL techniques (both traditional and LLM-based) have shown promise in general-purpose software, they are ill-suited for the kernel context. In particular, recent LLM-based techniques often treat bug reports and source code as plain text, lacking deep integration of kernel-specific knowledge, which limits their ability to identify root causes and achieve fine-grained localization. We present CoHiKer, a novel LLM-based FL technique tailored to the Linux kernel. CoHiKer introduces two key innovations: (1) contrastive reasoning, which identifies root causes by analyzing the behavioral divergence between carefully mutated passing and failing test cases, and (2) hierarchical context analysis, which systematically narrows the localization scope from files to methods by integrating crash reports, syscall semantics, inter-file dependencies, and kernel-specific features. Unlike prior techniques that rely on static understanding and full-code input, CoHiKer decomposes the localization task and enables structured LLM prompting to reason semantically over meaningful contexts. We evaluate CoHiKer on an extended Linux kernel bug dataset against five state-of-the-art baselines. CoHiKer consistently outperforms all competitors, improving Top-1 localization accuracy by up to 26.07% at the file level and 56.85% at the method level over state-of-the-art LLM-based baselines, while achieving up to 8.84% and 28.9% reductions in token consumption, respectively. Furthermore, CoHiKer demonstrates strong generalizability on the non-kernel dataset, with comparable gains (15.5% and 5.3% in Top-1 at file and method levels).

</details>


### [237] [SchedCheck: Schedule-Robustness Analysis for Event-Driven Block Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00623)
*Yuan Si,Jialu Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Scratch programs exhibit concurrency where script execution order depends on internal sprite/script layering, which is non-deterministic from the programmer's perspective. The authors formalize schedule-robustness, develop a partial-order exploration tool (SchedCheck) to detect schedule-sensitive behaviors, and find ~17-21% of real student projects are affected, including remixes that produce different outputs from identical blocks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scratch's cooperative concurrency model (green-flag scripts, broadcasts, clones) leaves script execution order unspecified. Since editing, saving, or remixing can silently reorder internal layers, programs with identical source blocks can produce different observable results. This schedule sensitivity is a latent correctness problem that prior work has not systematically identified or addressed.

Method: The authors formalize the VM's schedule space as permutations of the initial executable-target order and define schedule-robustness via a lattice of observation lenses over a fixed horizon. A partial-order exploration runs one schedule per dependence-equivalence class. On enumerable projects, an oracle validates full recovery of realizable outcomes. SchedCheck implements this on the production Scratch VM. The approach is parametric in the execution model and validated on 224 real student projects, a public project sample, hand-built fault pairs, and a generated benchmark of 32 spec-defined faults.

Result: At least 21% of concurrent student projects (224 total) are schedule-sensitive at the grading lens; a uniform random sample of public projects replicates at 17.6%, with two real remixes arranging letters differently. SchedCheck detects and localizes every schedule fault on hand-built pairs and a 32-fault benchmark across four classes, with logic-fault controls reporting clean. The oracle exposed four unsoundness gaps in the dependence model, all subsequently repaired.

Conclusion: Schedule sensitivity is a real and prevalent problem in block-based concurrent programs. Partial-order exploration with a validated dependence model can effectively detect and localize schedule-dependent behaviors, and the method generalizes across cooperative event-loop execution models.

Abstract: Block-based languages such as Scratch let beginners assemble interactive programs from sprites and scripts. These programs are concurrent in practice: green-flag scripts, broadcasts, and clones run as cooperatively scheduled threads over shared sprite and stage state, and their authors never write a thread. We show that such programs contain schedule-sensitive behaviors whose observable result depends on an execution order the language leaves open. Editing, saving, or remixing a project can produce a copy with the same blocks but a different layer order, changing the order the virtual machine starts scripts. We formalize the schedule space a Scratch virtual machine can realize as the permutations of the initial executable-target order, and define schedule-robustness against a lattice of observation lenses over a fixed horizon. A partial-order exploration runs one schedule per dependence-equivalence class, and on projects small enough to enumerate, an independent oracle confirms it recovers every realizable outcome. On larger projects, representatives stand in for the factorial under the validated dependence model. SchedCheck implements this on the production Scratch VM. Across 224 real student projects, at least 21% of the concurrent ones are schedule-sensitive at the grading lens, and a uniform random sample of public projects replicates the rate at 17.6%, with two real remixes of a deployed animation arranging its letters differently. On hand-built fault pairs and a generated benchmark of 32 spec-defined faults across four classes, the tool detects and localizes every schedule fault, with a logic-fault control reporting clean. The oracle exposed four unsoundness gaps in the dependence model, all repaired. The method is parametric in the execution model, instantiating unchanged on a second cooperative event loop.

</details>


### [238] [The Perception and Impact of Non-inclusive Language in Software Artifacts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00626)
*Ahmad J. Tayeb,Mohammad D. Alahmadi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A survey of 1,212 open-source contributors reveals that while average reported negative impact of non-inclusive terminology (e.g., whitelist/blacklist, master/slave) is low, perceptions vary significantly by demographics: women, non-binary individuals, and U.S. residents are more likely to view such terms as non-inclusive, with non-binary respondents reporting higher overall negative impact and women reporting greater harm to their sense of belonging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite industry momentum to replace potentially non-inclusive technical terminology, there is no empirical understanding of how developers actually perceive these terms or how the terminology affects their professional experiences, team dynamics, productivity, belonging, and well-being.

Method: The authors conducted a survey targeting open-source software contributors, collecting 1,517 responses, of which 1,212 were complete and used for quantitative analysis measuring perceived non-inclusiveness of terms and self-reported impact on workplace outcomes across demographic groups.

Result: Overall, respondents reported low average negative workplace impact. However, women and non-binary participants, along with U.S.-based respondents, were significantly more likely to perceive the terminology as non-inclusive. Among those who did view terms as non-inclusive, non-binary participants reported higher overall negative impacts than males, while female participants reported a disproportionately higher negative impact specifically on their sense of belonging.

Conclusion: The continued use of non-inclusive terminology in software artifacts has a differential, demographically-linked impact on developers. While the average effect appears modest, the burden falls disproportionately on underrepresented groups, particularly affecting belonging for women and overall well-being for non-binary individuals, underscoring the importance of inclusive language initiatives.

Abstract: Terminology such as "whitelist/blacklist," "master/slave," "man-hours," or "dummy value" has long been part of the technical vocabulary used in software artifacts, including source code, version histories, and documentation. In recent years, however, many of these expressions have been recognized as potentially non-inclusive and unwelcoming to groups historically underrepresented in software development, such as people of color, women, and individuals with disabilities. Consequently, a growing movement within the software industry has sought to replace these terms with more inclusive alternatives. Despite these initiatives, little is empirically known about how software developers perceive such terminology or how its continued use may influence their professional experiences and sense of belonging.
  This paper addresses the knowledge gap by examining how software developers perceive non-inclusive terminology in software and its perceived impact on team dynamics, productivity, belonging, and well-being. We surveyed open-source contributors and received 1,517 responses, of which 1,212 were complete and analyzed. On average, respondents reported low negative workplace impact overall; however, perceptions and impacts varied by demographic group. Women and non-binary participants, as well as respondents residing in the United States, were more likely to view the terms as non-inclusive. Among those who considered the terminology non-inclusive, non-binary participants reported higher overall negative impacts than male respondents, and female participants reported higher impact specifically on their sense of belonging.

</details>


### [239] [Checked Program Recovery from Execution Video: A Sound Oracle for Untrusted Generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00635)
*Yuan Si,Jialu Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Vid2Prog recovers Scratch programs from execution videos using a two-tier validation oracle: a static checker that certifies lens-equivalence (never false accepts) and a renderer that refutes candidates. A closed loop re-renders and re-recovers for ground truth. The exact lens achieves zero false accepts on 246 differing pairs including adversarial examples. In-vocabulary recoveries achieve 80% static certification; real projects recover at 14% with no wrong answers. A frontier VLM recovers none single-shot, highlighting the value of a sound checker paired with an untrusted generator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Program recovery from behavioral observations (e.g., execution videos) is inherently ambiguous — many programs produce identical outputs. Existing approaches use untrusted generators (neural models, search) with no correctness guarantees. The paper addresses how to make such recovery trustworthy, ensuring that when a candidate is accepted, it is genuinely behaviorally equivalent under a precise observational lens, rather than merely plausible.

Method: A two-tier validation oracle with verdict asymmetry: (1) a static checker proves lens-equivalence to a reference and issues a certificate that never accepts a wrong program (soundness via partial-order independence quotient); (2) a renderer that can only refute or witness finite agreement, never certify. Vid2Prog extracts sprite motion, visibility, and timing from video and a known-asset manifest, synthesizing a candidate source-free. A closed loop renders and runs recovery again for ground truth. The exact lens is tested on 246 labeled differing pairs including an adversarial battery targeting the concurrency quotient.

Result: Under the exact lens, zero false accepts on 246 labeled differing pairs, including adversarial examples. On out-of-vocabulary inputs and real projects, the system abstains or refutes, accepting none tested. In-vocabulary recoveries reproduce source frame-for-frame; 80% earn static certification. Whole real projects (mostly out-of-vocabulary) recover at 14% — a rate the system never inflates with wrong answers. A frontier VLM recovers none of the matched programs single-shot; oracle-in-the-loop repair lifts this only to a few, while the structured pipeline recovers all.

Conclusion: A sound static checker paired with an untrusted generator closes a critical trust gap in program recovery from observations. The two-tier oracle design — with deliberate verdict asymmetry — ensures that accepted programs are provably correct under the observational lens, never inflating recovery rates with plausible-but-wrong answers. This structured approach dramatically outperforms end-to-end VLM-based recovery, demonstrating that sound verification is essential when recovery is underdetermined.

Abstract: A growing class of tools recovers a program from observations of its behavior using an untrusted generator, a neural model or a search, that proposes candidates with no correctness guarantee. We study how to make such recovery trustworthy, in the concrete setting of recovering a runnable Scratch program from a recording of its execution. The recording shows what the program does but never its code; many programs produce the same video, so the source cannot be recovered, and the right target is a program that behaves the same as far as the camera can tell, made precise with a lens. The core is a two-tier validation oracle with a deliberate verdict asymmetry. A static checker proves lens-equivalence to a reference and issues a certificate that, granting the partial-order independence quotient adequate, never accepts a wrong program; a renderer can only refute or witness finite agreement, never certify. Around it, Vid2Prog reads each sprite's motion, visibility, and timing from the video and a known-asset manifest and synthesizes a candidate source-free; a closed loop renders and runs recovery again for ground truth. Under the exact lens the oracle makes no false accept on 246 labeled differing pairs, including an adversarial battery built to trap its concurrency quotient; on inputs outside the vocabulary and on real projects it abstains or refutes, accepting none we test. In-vocabulary recoveries reproduce their source frame for frame and 80% earn a static certificate, while whole real projects, mostly outside the vocabulary, recover at 14%, a vocabulary-bound rate the system never inflates with a wrong answer. A frontier vision-language model recovers none of the matched programs single-shot, which oracle-in-the-loop repair lifts only to a few while the structured pipeline recovers all, the gap a sound checker makes for an untrusted generator.

</details>


### [240] [LLVM-Bench: Benchmarking and Advancing Large Language Models for LLVM Compiler Issue Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00700)
*Zhao Tian,Yingquan Zhao,Chenyao Suo,Meng Wang,Junjie Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Introduces LLVM-Bench (a benchmark of 423 real-world LLVM issue resolution tasks), LLVM-Gym (an automated evaluation platform), and LLVM-Ens (a lightweight ensemble method). Evaluates LLMs on compiler-level issue resolution, revealing current limitations and achieving up to 21.99% resolution rate via ensembling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLVM's scale and complexity make issue resolution difficult, and LLM effectiveness on complex system-level compiler issue resolution remains unexplored. The lack of a dedicated benchmark and evaluation platform hinders progress in this domain.

Method: 1) LLVM-Bench: a benchmark of 423 validated, real-world LLVM issue resolution tasks. 2) LLVM-Gym: a scalable evaluation platform automating issue reproduction, patch application, compiler building, and test execution. 3) Comprehensive evaluation of 4 LLMs, 6 retrieval configurations, and 3 agents. 4) LLVM-Ens: a lightweight ensemble approach that integrates patches from diverse techniques, filters incorrect/redundant candidates, and selects the most promising solution.

Result: Current LLM-based issue resolution techniques show limited performance on LLVM-Bench, with patch invalidity and build failures as dominant failure modes. Strong complementarity exists among different LLMs and agents. LLVM-Ens achieves a resolution rate of up to 21.99%.

Conclusion: LLVM-Bench and LLVM-Gym provide a foundation for advancing compiler issue resolution research. The complementarity of diverse LLMs and agents, harnessed via LLVM-Ens, demonstrates a promising direction for improving automated issue resolution in complex system-level software like LLVM.

Abstract: LLVM is a widely used compiler infrastructure whose scale and complexity make issue resolution labor-intensive and challenging. Although large language models (LLMs) have recently achieved remarkable success in issue resolution, their effectiveness on complex system-level LLVM compiler remains largely unexplored. To address this gap, we introduce LLVM-Bench, the first large-scale benchmark for LLVM issue resolution, containing 423 real-world, validated tasks collected from the LLVM project. We further develop LLVM-Gym, a scalable evaluation platform that automates issue reproduction, patch application, compiler building, and test execution. Using LLVM-Bench and LLVM-Gym, we conduct a comprehensive study of four representative LLMs, six retrieval configurations, and three agents. Our results show that current LLM-based issue resolution techniques remain limited on LLVM-Bench, with patch invalidity and build failures as the dominant failure modes. We further reveal a strong complementarity among different LLMs and agents, motivating LLVM-Ens, a lightweight ensemble approach that expands the patch space through integrating the patches generated by diverse techniques, filters incorrect and redundant candidates, and identifies the most promising solution. Our results show that LLVM-Ens achieves a resolution rate of up to 21.99%, further improving LLVM issue resolution.

</details>


### [241] [ClarifyCodeBench: Evaluating LLMs on Clarifying Ambiguous Requirements for Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00711)
*Zheng Fang,Dongming Jin,Yihong dong,Yongmin Li,Kechi Zhang,Zhi Jin,Ge Li*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ClarifyCodeBench is a new benchmark for evaluating how well LLMs proactively clarify ambiguous programming requirements through interactive dialogue, revealing that strong code generation does not imply strong requirement elicitation, and that LLMs struggle with high ambiguity density.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as programming assistants, but real-world requirements are often ambiguous, incomplete, or underspecified. Existing benchmarks assume perfectly specified prompts and overlook the interactive, iterative nature of requirement elicitation. The authors aim to bridge this gap by systematically evaluating LLMs' ability to proactively clarify intent.

Method: The authors introduce ClarifyCodeBench, a manually annotated benchmark derived from real-world programming tasks, featuring multiple ambiguity types, clarification questions, and ground-truth answers. They formalize two metrics: Turn-discounted Key Question Rate (penalizes inefficient questioning) and Optimal Round Adherence (measures elicitation precision). Six state-of-the-art LLMs are evaluated systematically using this benchmark.

Result: Three key findings emerge: (1) Capability Decoupling — strong code generation does not correlate with effective requirement clarification; (2) The Reasoning Paradox — increased reasoning compute helps code correctness but yields marginal gains in ambiguity identification; (3) The Multi-ambiguity Ceiling — LLM clarification performance degrades sharply as ambiguity density increases.

Conclusion: The work highlights a critical gap in current LLM capabilities and underscores the need for AI4SE research to shift from static code synthesis toward interactive requirement elicitation, positioning ClarifyCodeBench as a foundational tool for this transition.

Abstract: Large Language Models have emerged as programming assistants. However, the efficacy of code generation is constrained by the quality of input requirements, which are frequently ambiguous, incomplete, or underspecified. While LLMs excel at one-shot code synthesis, their ability to proactively clarify intent remains underexplored, as a critical trait for robust software engineering. Existing benchmarks largely overlook this interactive bottleneck, assuming perfectly specified prompts that do not reflect the iterative nature of requirement elicitation. To bridge this gap, we introduce ClarifyCodeBench, a novel interactive benchmark for evaluating LLMs' capability in resolving requirement ambiguity. Constructed from real-world programming tasks, ClarifyCodeBench features high-quality manual annotations, including N unique ambiguity types, associated clarification questions, and corresponding ground-truth answers. Furthermore, we formalize two rigorous metrics to assess the interaction quality: Turn-discounted Key Question Rate, which penalizes inefficient questioning, and Optimal Round Adherence, which measures the precision of the elicitation process. We conduct a systematic evaluation of six state-of-the-art LLMs using ClarifyCodeBench. Our empirical results yield three critical insights: 1) Capability Decoupling: Strong code generation performance does not inherently translate to effective requirement clarification; 2) The Reasoning Paradox: While increased computational thinking enhances code correctness, it yields marginal gains in identifying ambiguities; 3) The Multi-ambiguity Ceiling: LLMs' clarification performance degrades sharply as the density of ambiguities increases, revealing a significant bottleneck in handling complex, real-world specifications. Our work underscores the necessity for future AI4SE research to transition from static synthesis to interactive elicitation.

</details>


### [242] [Stochastic Connectivity as the Foundation of a Runtime Model for Microservice Availability Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00740)
*Anatoly A. Krasnovsky,Anna Maslovskaya*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A formal runtime availability model for microservices using stochastic connectivity, derived from traces and deployment metadata, enabling cheaper resilience analysis than fault injection experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fault injection and chaos experiments for assessing microservice availability are costly, operationally risky, and impractical to repeat for every architectural change. Distributed tracing and deployment metadata provide cheaper evidence but remain descriptive—they show which services interacted without revealing endpoint-level availability properties. A formal, analyzable model is needed to bridge this gap.

Method: Proposes a formal availability model with four components: (1) a typed service-dependency graph, (2) a replication map, (3) a probability measure over node (replica) and edge (dependency) states, and (4) request-specific success predicates. The semantics separates computational failures (service replicas) from communication failures (logical dependencies), demonstrating that replication cannot compensate for bottleneck dependencies. The model is reconstructed from traces and deployment artifacts, parameterized for what-if analysis, and analyzed via Monte Carlo simulation.

Result: A synthetic adequacy study shows the model matches closed-form oracle cases within sampling error and exposes boundary conditions: edge bottlenecks, correlated failures, missing traces, and time-dependent failures.

Conclusion: The proposed model enables resilience-oriented availability analysis of microservice endpoints using lightweight trace and deployment data, offering a cheaper, repeatable alternative or complement to fault injection experiments, with well-characterized limitations.

Abstract: Microservice availability is commonly assessed by fault injection and chaos experiments, but such experiments are costly, operationally risky, and difficult to repeat for every architectural change. Distributed tracing and deployment metadata provide cheaper evidence, yet they usually remain descriptive: they show which services interacted, not what endpoint-level availability property follows. This paper proposes a formal runtime availability model based on stochastic connectivity for resilience-oriented analysis of microservice endpoints. It treats endpoint availability under explicit fault scenarios as a measurable facet of microservice resilience, combining a typed service-dependency graph, a replication map, a probability measure over node and edge states, and request-specific success predicates. Its semantics separates computational failures of service replicas from communication failures of logical dependencies, showing that replication cannot compensate for bottleneck dependencies. The model can be reconstructed from traces and deployment artifacts, parameterized for architectural what-if analysis, and analyzed by Monte Carlo simulation before or alongside fault injection. We define the model, its trace-to-model construction, elementary semantic properties, and a synthetic adequacy study. The study matches closed-form oracle cases within sampling error and exposes boundaries caused by edge bottlenecks, correlated failures, missing traces, and time-dependent failures.

</details>


### [243] [Knowledge-Enhanced Agentic Vulnerability Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00820)
*Sicong Cao,Hao Ma,Le Yu,Kangyi Ding,Xiaolei Liu,Terry Yue Zhuo,Bo Wang,Xingwei Lin,Xiaobing Sun,Linzhang Wang,David Lo*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: KeaRepair is an agentic AVR system that combines verified program facts with historical vulnerability knowledge for patch generation. Using retrieval-augmented reasoning with tool support and iterative closed-loop validation, it achieves an 83.64% repair rate on 55 C/C++ vulnerabilities, outperforming existing approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Automated Vulnerability Repair (AVR) systems suffer from two key limitations: (1) unreliable root cause identification of vulnerabilities, and (2) insufficient utilization of prior fix knowledge to guide patch generation. These shortcomings undermine practical effectiveness, creating a gap between vulnerability discovery capabilities (boosted by frontier models) and remediation capabilities.

Method: KeaRepair operates in three stages: First, it extracts multi-dimensional vulnerability knowledge from historical vulnerability-patch pairs using dual complementary views and builds retrieval knowledge bases. Second, a tool-augmented agent performs ReAct-style reasoning to gather verified program facts (e.g., via compilation, PoC execution) for vulnerability diagnosis. Third, based on diagnostic results, it conducts knowledge-level retrieval-augmented patch generation, followed by iterative refinement through a closed-loop validation process (compilation, PoC replay, test-suite execution).

Result: On 55 reproducible C/C++ vulnerabilities, KeaRepair with Gemini-3.1-Pro repairs 46 (83.64%), significantly outperforming all baselines. It fixes six unique vulnerabilities that no baseline can address and demonstrates strong cross-language generalizability.

Conclusion: Grounding patch generation in verified program facts and high-level vulnerability knowledge, combined with iterative closed-loop validation, substantially improves AVR effectiveness. KeaRepair's strong performance and cross-language generalizability suggest this agentic, retrieval-augmented paradigm is a promising direction for automated vulnerability repair.

Abstract: Frontier foundation models have changed the math on vulnerability discovery, but the bigger challenge is how the remediation side keeps up. Despite recent progresses in Automated Vulnerability Repair (AVR), current solutions struggle to reliably identify the root causes of vulnerabilities, and insufficiently utilize the prior fix knowledge to guide the patch generation process, thus undermining their effectiveness in practice.
  To address this gap, we propose KeaRepair, a novel agentic AVR approach that grounds patch generation in verified program facts and high-level vulnerability knowledge. Specifically, KeaRepair first extracts multi-dimensional vulnerability knowledge from historical vulnerability-patch pairs from dual complementary views, and constructs dedicated retrieval knowledge bases. It then employs a tool-augmented agent that performs ReAct-style reasoning to collect verified program facts for vulnerability diagnosis. Finally, based on the diagnostic results, KeaRepair performs knowledge-level retrieval-augmented patch generation and iteratively refines patches through a closed-loop validation process involving compilation, PoC replay, and test-suite execution. Experimental results show that KeaRepair significantly outperforms existing AVR approaches on 55 reproducible C/C++ vulnerabilities. When paired with Gemini-3.1-Pro, KeaRepair successfully repairs 46 vulnerabilities, achieving a repair rate of 83.64%. Moreover, KeaRepair fixes six unique vulnerabilities that none of the baselines can address, and further demonstrates strong cross-language generalizability.

</details>


### [244] [Petrify: Petri-net Based Analysis of Concurrency Properties in Java Bytecode](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00830)
*Akshatha Shenoy,Carlo A. Furia*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Petrify is an automated verification technique that encodes Java bytecode programs into Petri nets, enabling model checking of concurrency properties (e.g., deadlock) with a distinctive balance of expressiveness, precision, and scalability. Its prototype tool, jPetrify, works on any Java version and even some Kotlin code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated formal verification techniques force a trade-off between expressiveness/precision (supporting complex properties) and scalability/practicality (handling larger programs). The authors aim to achieve a better balance for concurrency verification, and also address the limitation that similar tools are often restricted to older Java versions.

Method: Petrify encodes the semantics of Java bytecode programs into Petri nets (PNs), a formal model well-suited for concurrency. These Petri nets are then analyzed using state-of-the-art model checking tools such as LoLA. The encoding is designed to be succinct, keeping the analysis insensitive to parameter size. The prototype tool, jPetrify, operates at the bytecode level, making it language-version-agnostic.

Result: Experiments demonstrate that Petrify's PN-based approach combines expressiveness with practicality: the encoding is fairly precise for concurrent behavior, yet succinct enough for scalable analysis. jPetrify applies to programs in any Java version and a subset of Kotlin, surpassing similar tools limited to older Java versions. The paper focuses on deadlock detection as a case study.

Conclusion: Petrify achieves a distinctive trade-off in concurrency verification by leveraging Petri nets. Future work will extend the approach beyond deadlock analysis to other kinds of concurrency property verification.

Abstract: The landscape of automated formal verification is populated by techniques that make prominently different trade-offs: some focus on expressiveness and precision, supporting the verification of complex properties; others favor scalability and practicality, so that they are applicable to larger programs using different features. This paper presents Petrify, a novel automated verification technique for concurrency properties that achieves a distinctive trade-off. Petrify encodes the semantics of Java bytecode programs into Petri nets (PNs), which can be analyzed by state-of-the-art model checking tools such as LoLA. As our experiments demonstrate, Petrify's approach offers an interesting combination of expressiveness and practicality: PNs are a fairly precise encoding of the concurrent behavior of programs; at the same time, Petrify's PN encoding is succinct, so that its analysis remains quite insensitive to parameter size. Another practical benefit of targeting bytecode is that jPetrify, the prototype tool that implements the Petrify technique, is applicable to programs written in any version of Java and even a subset of Kotlin (another language that compiles to Java bytecode) while other similar tools are limited to older versions of Java. While this paper's experiments focus on analyzing fundamental properties like deadlock, Petrify's approach lends itself to be extended to other kinds of concurrency analysis, which we plan to tackle in future work.

</details>


### [245] [From Registry to Repository: How AI Agent Skills Are Written, Adapted, and Maintained](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00911)
*Haoyu Gao,Jai Lal Lulla,Hong Yi Lin,Sebastian Baltes,Christoph Treude,Mansooreh Zahedi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: First empirical study of AI agent skills as software artefacts, analyzing 41,662 skills across public registries and GitHub. Finds reuse is predominantly one-time copy with 53% never modified; changes focus on operational specs and domain knowledge while behavioural contracts remain stable. Recommends tooling for consolidating fragmented domain knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agent skills have emerged as a widespread unit of reuse (tens of thousands hosted publicly), yet prior work treats them merely as agent capabilities rather than engineered software artefacts whose authorship, reuse, customization, and evolution warrant systematic empirical study.

Method: Mined 18,463 skills from skills.sh and 23,199 personal-use skills from 5,876 GitHub repositories, identifying 3,709 reuse links. Applied LLM-based classification into SWEBOK knowledge areas, thematic analysis of 180 skills for content categories, and qualitative coding of 444 modifications to derive change themes.

Result: Software Construction dominates SWEBOK KAs with a long tail of specialized areas. Six content categories identified. Six modification themes found, with reworking operational specifications and adapting knowledge/resources being primary. Reuse is largely one-time copy: 53% of reused skills never modified; subsequent changes are overwhelmingly additive. Behavioural contracts (user interaction, runtime monitoring, failure recovery) remain nearly untouched.

Conclusion: Maintenance effort should focus on project-specific bindings rather than core behavioural contracts. Registries and tool support should enable consolidating domain knowledge that skills re-author in isolation, reducing fragmentation across the ecosystem.

Abstract: AI coding agents increasingly rely on skills: structured context bundles, typically a SKILL.md file with a YAML header and Markdown body, loaded on demand for domain knowledge, workflows, and scripts. Public registries such as skills.sh now host tens of thousands of skills, making them an emerging unit of reuse in agent-based software engineering. Yet skills have largely been viewed as agent capabilities rather than software artefacts whose content and evolution shape agent behaviour. We present the first empirical study of AI agent skills as engineered artefacts that are authored, reused, customised and maintained, across public registries and personal-use repositories. We mined 18,463 skills from skills.sh and 23,199 personal-use skills from 5,876 GitHub repositories, identifying 3,709 reuse links. LLM-based classification into SWEBOK knowledge areas (KAs) shows Software Construction dominates alongside a long tail of specialised areas. A thematic analysis of 180 skills identifies six content categories. Qualitative coding of 444 modifications reveals six themes, of which reworking operational specifications and adapting knowledge and resources are the primary target of change. Our findings show that reuse is largely a one-time copy operation: most reused skills remain near-verbatim, 53\% are never modified after adoption, and subsequent local maintenance is overwhelmingly additive. Customisation primarily adapts skills to local environments, whereas evolution accretes new inline domain knowledge. Across both, a stable behavioural contract -- how a skill interacts with users, monitors runtime state, and recovers from failures -- remains almost untouched. These results suggest maintenance effort should focus on project-specific bindings, and that registries and tool support should enable consolidating the domain knowledge skills re-author in isolation.

</details>


### [246] [Delta Debugging in the Absence of Test Oracles Through Metamorphic Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00929)
*Mingyue Jiang,Yongqiang Tian,Tsong Yueh Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: DDMT integrates metamorphic testing into delta debugging to create an oracle-independent reduction approach, enabling input minimization for programs where output correctness cannot be directly verified.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional delta debugging relies on test oracles to verify property preservation during input reduction, limiting its applicability to oracle-deficient programs. The oracle problem—where output correctness cannot be directly determined—substantially restricts existing techniques.

Method: DDMT redesigns the test function in delta debugging by incorporating metamorphic testing, a property-based, oracle-independent testing method. It replaces the original oracle-dependent test function with a metamorphic testing-based test function, enabling property-preservation validation without requiring a direct test oracle.

Result: Evaluated on 66 subjects across both oracle-available and oracle-deficient scenarios with different delta debugging approaches, DDMT preserves or improves reduction effectiveness and query efficiency.
