With proper configurations, it also achieves performance improvements over relevant delta debugging approaches.

Conclusion: DDMT successfully enhances the applicability of delta debugging to oracle-deficient programs by eliminating oracle dependency through metamorphic testing, while maintaining competitive reduction effectiveness and efficiency.

Abstract: Delta debugging provides an automatic way to minimize a program input while preserving a certain property. However, its effectiveness fundamentally relies on the availability of test oracles to determine whether a reduced input still preserves the specific property. Consequently, the oracle problem substantially limits the applicability of existing delta debugging techniques, particularly for oracle-deficient programs where output correctness cannot be directly determined. To address this problem, this paper proposes a novel approach, DDMT, to enhance the applicability of delta debugging, especially facilitating its application to oracle-deficient programs. Our key insight is to redesign an oracle-independent test function and incorporate it into the reduction procedure of delta debugging such that the property-preservation validation can be accomplished without requiring a test oracle. To this end, DDMT employs the technique of metamorphic testing, which is a property-based and oracle-independent testing method. It establishes a metamorphic testing-based test function, using it as a replacement for the original test function adopted by delta debugging. The experiments evaluate DDMT on 66 subjects across both oracle-available and oracle-deficient scenarios, with different delta debugging approaches. The results positively confirm that DDMT can enhance the applicability of delta debugging while often preserving or improving reduction effectiveness and query efficiency. Furthermore, compared to the relevant delta debugging approaches, DDMT is also able to achieve performance improvements with proper configurations.

</details>


### [247] [Leveraging LLM-Based Agentic Systems to Generate Quantum Applications for Test Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00939)
*Ming Tao,Yuechen Li,Tao Yue,Man Zhang,Aitor Arrieta Marcos*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: QPipe is an LLM-based multi-agent system that automatically converts natural-language requirements into executable quantum applications for software engineering optimization, achieving 100% compilation and 96.7% execution success on 20 real-world benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Translating natural-language task-level requirements into executable quantum applications currently demands substantial expertise in both quantum computing and programming. Automating this pipeline would lower the barrier to applying quantum computing for software engineering optimization problems.

Method: QPipe employs a multi-agent architecture with specialized agents for requirement parsing, formulation, code generation, review, execution, and verification. It uses large language models (LLMs) to coordinate these agents in an autonomous workflow. The system is evaluated on 20 NL requirements tied to real-world benchmarks and test-optimization problems, with ablation studies isolating the contributions of code-generation skills, task knowledge, review feedback, and multi-agent decomposition.

Result: QPipe achieved 100% code compilation rate and 96.7% application execution and final-result combination rate, with average costs of 260.1 seconds and 1.89M tokens per requirement. The successfully executed quantum applications outperformed an offline genetic algorithm baseline in most cases. Ablation studies confirmed that performance depends on retaining code-generation skills, task knowledge, review feedback, and multi-agent decomposition.

Conclusion: Agentic coordination via LLMs can effectively support generation of executable quantum applications for real-world test optimization problems, suggesting a viable path toward democratizing quantum computing for software engineering tasks.

Abstract: Quantum computing is increasingly explored for software engineering (SE) optimization, but translating natural-language (NL) task-level requirements into executable quantum applications still demands substantial quantum and programming expertise. We present QPipe, a large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent architecture that autonomously turns NL requirements into traceable quantum-application workflows through specialized agents for requirement parsing, formulation, code generation, review, execution, and verification. We evaluate QPipe on 20 NL requirements, each associated with a real-world benchmark and a test-optimization problem. QPipe successfully completes the key stages of quantum-application generation across requirements, achieving average rates of 100% for code compilation and 96.7% for application execution and final-result combination, with average generation costs of 260.1 seconds and 1.89M tokens per requirement. Among the generated quantum applications that execute successfully, the returned solutions outperform the offline genetic algorithm baseline in most cases. Ablation results further show that QPipe's advantage depends on retaining code-generation skills, task knowledge, review feedback, and multi-agent decomposition. These results indicate that agentic coordination can support generation of executable quantum applications for tackling test optimization problems from real-world benchmarks.

</details>


### [248] [SWE-Doctor: Guiding Software Engineering Agents with Runtime Diagnosis from Multi-Faceted Bug Reproduction Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00990)
*Yaoqi Guo,Yang Liu,Jie M. Zhang,Yun Ma,Yiling Lou,Zhenpeng Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SWE-Doctor proposes a software issue resolution agent that uses runtime diagnoses from multi-faceted bug reproduction tests (BRTs) to guide patch generation, outperforming existing agents by 8.0–8.9 pp on SWE-bench Pro.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bug reproduction tests (BRTs) are known to help patch validation, but their role in patch generation is unclear. Preliminary findings show directly using BRTs to guide generation is harmful: fail-to-fail BRTs mislead agents, and fail-to-pass BRTs yield limited/negative gains due to partial issue coverage and unreliability.

Method: SWE-Doctor: (1) generates multi-faceted BRTs capturing different behavioral requirements from the issue report, (2) executes and debugs these BRTs to produce runtime-grounded diagnosis records, (3) uses diagnoses plus localization information inferred during BRT generation to guide patch generation and reduce partial patches.

Result: Evaluated on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro across five LLM backends. SWE-Doctor achieves average resolution rates of 75.7% (Verified) and 59.4% (Pro), consistently outperforming all baselines across all 10 LLM-benchmark combinations. On SWE-bench Pro, it improves by 8.0–8.9 pp over baselines.

Conclusion: Runtime diagnoses from multi-faceted BRTs provide effective guidance for patch generation, addressing the limitations of directly using BRTs as generation targets. The approach yields state-of-the-art results on standard benchmarks.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based software engineering agents are increasingly developed to resolve software issues by generating patches from issue reports and code repositories. Bug reproduction tests (BRTs) are an important building block for such agents and have been shown useful for patch validation. However, it remains unclear whether BRTs can also help the more central stage of patch generation. We first conduct a preliminary study and find that directly using advanced BRT generators to guide patch generation is not beneficial: fail-to-fail BRTs can mislead agents, while even fail-to-pass BRTs bring limited or negative gains. Our analysis reveals two reasons: fail-to-pass BRTs may cover only one manifestation of the reported issue, leading to partial patches, whereas fail-to-fail BRTs are unreliable as direct patch-generation targets. Motivated by these insights, we propose SWE-Doctor, a software issue resolution agent that guides patch generation with runtime diagnoses derived from multi-faceted BRT executions. SWE-Doctor first generates multi-faceted BRTs for different behavioral requirements stated in the issue, then executes and debugs these BRTs to construct runtime-grounded diagnosis records, and finally uses the diagnoses together with localization information inferred during BRT generation to guide patch generation and reduce partial patches. We evaluate SWE-Doctor on Python bug-fixing issues from the widely adopted SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro across five LLM backends. SWE-Doctor consistently outperforms existing agents across all 10 LLM-benchmark combinations, achieving average resolution rates of 75.7% on SWE-bench Verified and 59.4% on SWE-bench Pro. In particular, on the more challenging SWE-bench Pro, SWE-Doctor improves the average resolution rate by 8.0-8.9 percentage points over the baseline agents.

</details>


### [249] [Identifying Effective Program Comprehension Strategies through Gaze Transitions over Syntactic Elements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01042)
*Kyogo Horikawa,Hidetake Uwano,Haruhiko Yoshioka*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study converts eye-tracking data into syntactic node transitions (AST-based) and compares fixation proportions and gaze transition patterns between correct and incorrect program comprehension groups, finding that successful participants exhibit more systematic, structured reading strategies across syntactic elements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work has mapped eye movements to abstract syntax tree (AST) nodes, but the relationship between task correctness and eye-movement features for specific syntactic elements remains unclear. The authors aim to bridge this gap by investigating how gaze patterns over syntactic structures correlate with comprehension success.

Method: Eye-tracking data from program comprehension tasks are converted into transitions between syntactic nodes of an AST. The authors then compute fixation proportions and gaze transition patterns, comparing these metrics between participants who answered correctly and those who answered incorrectly.

Result: Distinct differences in gaze transition patterns emerge between correct and incorrect groups. Successful participants demonstrate more systematic transitions across syntactic elements, indicative of structured reading strategies.

Conclusion: Syntax-level gaze transition analysis reveals that structured, systematic reading behavior correlates with correct program comprehension, suggesting that eye-movement features tied to specific syntactic elements can serve as indicators of comprehension success.

Abstract: Program comprehension is a central research topic in software engineering, focusing on how developers understand a program's structure, behavior, and intent. Eye-tracking studies have traditionally relied on display-based measurements, where gaze positions are represented as screen coordinates. However, syntax-based analyses have recently emerged. Prior work proposed methods to convert eye movements into transitions between nodes in an abstract syntax tree, but the relationship between task correctness and eye-movement features for specific syntactic elements remains unclear. This study converts eye-tracking data into transitions between syntactic nodes and analyzes fixation proportions and gaze transition patterns. We investigate the relationship between these patterns and task correctness, comparing correct and incorrect groups. Our results reveal distinct differences in gaze transition patterns between the two groups. In particular, successful participants exhibit more systematic transitions across syntactic elements, suggesting the use of structured reading strategies.

</details>


### [250] [AutoRestTest at the SBFT 2026 Tool Competition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01063)
*Tyler Stennett,Myeongsoo Kim,Saurabh Sinha,Alessandro Orso*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: AutoRestTest combines a Semantic Property Dependency Graph, multi-agent reinforcement learning, and LLMs to intelligently explore REST API input spaces, ranking first across all evaluation categories in the SBFT 2026 REST League.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Black-box REST API testing is difficult due to large input spaces and complex inter-operation dependencies, necessitating intelligent exploration strategies beyond brute-force or random approaches.

Method: AutoRestTest integrates three components: (1) a Semantic Property Dependency Graph to model inter-operation relationships and data dependencies, (2) multi-agent reinforcement learning to guide exploration of the API input space, and (3) large language models to assist in semantic understanding and input generation.

Result: In the SBFT 2026 REST League, AutoRestTest ranked first in fault detection, overall efficiency, and overall effectiveness across 11 APIs (317 operations, ~29 per API), averaging 67.09 unique server errors and 17.27 successfully processed operations per API within a one-hour testing budget.

Conclusion: The synergistic combination of semantic dependency modeling, multi-agent RL, and LLMs enables state-of-the-art black-box REST API testing, significantly outperforming competing approaches in fault discovery and testing efficiency.

Abstract: Large input spaces and complex inter-operation dependencies make black-box REST API testing challenging. AutoRestTest combines a Semantic Property Dependency Graph, multi-agent reinforcement learning, and large language models to intelligently explore large API input spaces. In the SBFT 2026 REST League, AutoRestTest ranked first in all three evaluation categories -- fault detection, overall efficiency, and overall effectiveness -- on 11 APIs (317 operations, approximately 29 per API), averaging 67.09 unique server errors and 17.27 successfully processed operations per API under a one-hour testing budget.

</details>


### [251] [Cheap Code, Costly Judgment: A Case Study on Governable Agentic Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01087)
*James C. Davis,Paschal C. Amusuo,Tanmay Singla,Berk Çakar,Kirsten A. Davis*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A 12-week first-person case study where an expert engineer used AI coding agents to build a document accessibility system. The author develops a "governance conversion" theory explaining how the high velocity of AI-generated code surfaces recurring structural failures, and how engineering judgment converts those failures into durable governance mechanisms—shifting from pre-specified controls to controls discovered through agentic work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative AI is shifting software engineering from scarce implementation effort to abundant, low-cost code production. The central problem is no longer whether AI can generate useful code, but how to organize architectures, tools, evidence, and feedback loops so AI-mediated development remains inspectable, correctable, and maintainable. Existing governance models derive controls from known obligations, which is inadequate for the novel failure modes that emerge during agentic development.

Method: A first-person longitudinal case study over 12 weeks, where a single expert software engineer used frontier AI coding agents to build a document accessibility remediation system. The empirical record includes 88 contemporaneous field notes, 420 KLOC of production code, and 1.16 MLOC of tests, lints, supporting documentation, and agent tooling. From this record, the author develops a middle-range theory (governance conversion) expressed as a process model.

Result: The governance conversion process model explains how agentic implementation velocity surfaces recurring structural failure classes, and how engineering judgment sustains velocity by converting those failures into durable governance mechanisms (e.g., architectural constraints, automated checks, feedback loops). The model yields testable predictions and implications for both research and practice.

Conclusion: Governance conversion reframes AI-mediated software engineering governance: rather than deriving controls from known obligations upfront, controls are discovered from failures that become visible only during agentic work. This has significant implications for how software engineering research and practice should adapt to the generative AI era.

Abstract: Generative AI is shifting software engineering from a practice organized around scarce implementation effort toward one organized around abundant, low-cost code production. This shift changes the central engineering problem: not whether AI can generate useful code, but how engineers organize architectures, tools, evidence, and feedback loops so that AI-mediated development remains inspectable, correctable, and maintainable.
  We study this problem through a first-person case study: a 12-week development effort in which a single expert software engineer used frontier AI coding agents to build a document accessibility remediation system. The empirical record comprises 88 contemporaneous field notes, 420 KLOC of production code, and 1.16 MLOC of tests, lints, supporting documentation, and agent tooling. From this record, we develop a candidate middle-range theory of governance conversion, expressed as a process model explaining how high-velocity agentic implementation becomes governable. The model explains how agentic implementation velocity surfaces recurring structural failure classes, and how engineering judgment sustains velocity by converting those failures into durable governance mechanisms. In contrast to existing governance models that derive controls from known obligations, governance conversion explains how controls are discovered from failures that become visible only during agentic work. We use our model to make testable predictions and to describe implications for software engineering research and practice.

</details>


### [252] [A Model-based Testing Technique for Amazon Lex Task-based Chatbots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01094)
*Diego Clerissi,Alessandro Vasina,Leonardo Mariani*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LexTester is an automated model-based testing technique for Amazon Lex chatbots that generates a Dialog Graph of all possible interactions and produces executable test suites via coverage strategies, outperforming Botium with nearly double test complexity, 83–95% conversational coverage, and up to 4× better fault detection at comparable time cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Task-based chatbots are widely deployed but prone to bugs, and existing testing techniques suffer from overly simple test scenarios and weak oracles.

Method: LexTester explores the chatbot's conversational space to construct a Dialog Graph capturing all possible interactions, then derives executable test suites using different coverage strategies from this model.

Result: Evaluated on five Amazon Lex chatbots against Botium, LexTester consistently generated more tests with nearly double the complexity, achieved 83–95% coverage of conversational elements, and improved fault detection effectiveness by up to four times, with comparable time costs.

Conclusion: LexTester advances the state of the art in chatbot testing by combining model-based exploration with coverage-driven test generation, significantly outperforming existing industrial tools in both coverage and fault detection.

Abstract: Task-based chatbots are nowadays widely adopted software systems, usually integrated into real-world applications and communication channels, designed to assist users in completing tasks through conversational interfaces. Like any other software, even chatbots are prone to bugs. Despite their increasing pervasiveness in everyday activities, existing techniques for assessing their quality still exhibit several limitations, such as the simplicity of generated test scenarios and oracle weaknesses.
  In this paper, we present LexTester, an automated model-based testing technique for Amazon Lex chatbots. The technique explores the conversational space of the chatbot under test to generate a Dialog Graph of all possible interactions, from which an executable test suite is generated according to different coverage strategies. LexTester was evaluated against the state-of-the-practice chatbot testing tool Botium on five Amazon Lex chatbots, consistently outperforming it in all subjects, generating more tests with nearly double complexity, achieving overall 83-95% coverage of conversational elements, and improving fault detection effectiveness by up to four times at comparable time costs.

</details>


### [253] [SAGE: Structured Agentic Graph Editing for Software Diagrams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01102)
*Tyler Sivertsen,Neal Singh,James C. Davis*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SAGE is a browser-based tool that enables natural language prompt-guided editing of Draw.io and Mermaid engineering diagrams while preserving visual layout, structure, and semantics. It uses a graph-based intermediate representation to translate NL requests into structured edit operations, with validation, repair, and versioning capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Software diagrams are difficult to edit through human-friendly interfaces because natural language edits must simultaneously preserve visual layout, editable structure, and semantic relationships — a challenge existing tools do not adequately address.

Method: SAGE maps diagrams into an editable graph representation, translates natural language requests into structured edit intents, and decomposes those intents into graph-oriented operation steps. It validates and repairs common Draw.io XML issues, stores successful results as recoverable versioned artifacts, and supports both direct canvas editing and a secondary mask-based image-editing workflow. The architecture separates structured state management from model-driven interpretation.

Result: Evaluation using unit tests and a Kubernetes architecture case study measured structural validity, edit success rate, preservation of unrelated elements during edits, and categorized failure causes.

Conclusion: SAGE demonstrates a viable architecture for prompt-guided diagram editing that balances structured state management with model-assisted interpretation, though some XML edits remain model-assisted rather than fully automated.

Abstract: Software diagrams are difficult to edit through human-friendly interfaces because edits expressed in natural language must still preserve visual layout, editable structure, and semantic relationships. As a step forward, we present SAGE, a browser-based tool for prompt-guided editing of Draw.io and Mermaid-style engineering diagrams. The tool maps diagrams into an editable graph representation, translates natural language requests into structured edit intents, analyzes those intents into graph-oriented operation steps, validates and repairs common Draw.io XML issues, and stores successful results as recoverable versioned artifacts. This design separates structured state management from model-driven interpretation, while acknowledging that some prompt-guided XML edits remain model-assisted. The tool also supports direct canvas editing and a secondary mask-based image-editing workflow. We evaluate the system using unit tests and a Kubernetes architecture case study, measuring structural validity, edit success, preservation of unrelated elements, and failure causes.

</details>


### [254] [Skills Are Not Islands: Measuring Dependency and Risk in Agent Skill Supply Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01136)
*Changguo Jia,Tianqi Zhao,Runzhi He,Minghui Zhou*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Agent skills, reusable operational knowledge for LLM agents, suffer from implicit dependency management. This paper introduces Agent Skill Supply Chains (ASSCs) and SkillDepAnalyzer, a tool that recovers skill dependency graphs using natural-language evidence, outperforming baselines. Analysis of 1.43M+ skills reveals four structural patterns and uncovers security risks hidden in transitive dependencies, leading to actionable recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agent skills grow in scope, they become dependency-bearing artifacts with implicit identities, versions, and provenance. This opacity causes duplicated dependencies and inconsistent installations. Existing dependency management tools fail to address this gap because skills mix code packages with natural-language operational knowledge, making traditional package-centric approaches insufficient.

Method: The authors formalize Agent Skill Supply Chains (ASSCs) as mixed skill-package-service dependency graphs. They design SkillDepAnalyzer, which captures natural-language dependency evidence (e.g., from READMEs, skill descriptions) and models skills as dependency-bearing artifacts. They construct the SKILL-DEP benchmark for evaluation and apply the tool at scale to over 1.43 million skills across multiple repositories.

Result: SkillDepAnalyzer substantially outperforms an LLM-based baseline and package-centric SBOM tools on the SKILL-DEP benchmark. Large-scale analysis reveals four structural patterns: (1) metadata is activation-ready but governance-poor; (2) dependency graphs span skills, packages, and services with concentrated reuse; (3) recursive skill reuse expands dependency graphs, creating hidden package inventory; (4) skill dependency clusters form around related workflows. Security analysis shows inspecting a single skill misses signals hidden in dependencies, and known malicious skills persist within ASSCs.

Conclusion: The paper recommends four measures: typed dependency manifests, first-class dependency-cluster management, risk-warning audit commands for skill infrastructure maintainers, and lockfile-like records for skill developers. These aim to close the dependency-management gap exposed by ASSC analysis.

Abstract: Agent skills package reusable operational knowledge for Large Language Model (LLM) agents, yet as they grow in scope, they become dependency-bearing artifacts whose identities, versions, and provenance remain implicit. This opacity already causes duplicated dependencies and inconsistent installations, exposing a gap that dependency management has yet to close. We introduce Agent Skill Supply Chains (ASSCs) to characterize mixed skill-package-service dependency graphs and help close this gap. Borrowing from Software Bill of Materials (SBOMs), we design SkillDepAnalyzer to capture natural-language dependency evidence and model skills as dependency-bearing artifacts. On the SKILL-DEP benchmark, SkillDepAnalyzer recovers skill metadata and dependency graphs accurately and comprehensively, substantially outperforming an LLM-based baseline and package-centric SBOM tools. Applying SkillDepAnalyzer to over 1.43 million skills, we obtain ASSCs and explore their structural diversity and security signals. We find four structural patterns: skill metadata is activation-ready but governance-poor; dependency graphs span skill, package, and service dependencies with concentrated reuse; recursive skill reuse expands dependency graphs and creates hidden package inventory; and skill dependency clusters form around related workflows. We also find that inspecting a skill alone misses security-relevant signals hiding in its dependencies. By analyzing ASSCs, we identify and report known malicious skills persisting in ASSCs to their developers. Based on these findings, we recommend typed dependency manifests, first-class dependency-cluster management, risk-warning audit commands for skill infrastructure maintainers, and lockfile-like records for skill developers.

</details>


### [255] [Are Performance-Optimization Benchmarks Reliably Measuring Coding Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01211)
*Zhi Chen,Zhensu Sun,Yuling Shi,David Lo,Lingxiao Jiang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper audits three repository-level performance-optimization benchmarks (GSO, SWE-Perf, SWE-fficiency), revealing that leaderboard scores are unreliable due to runtime instability across machines, scoring-rule sensitivity, and task saturation. Only a minority of reference patches remain valid across all tested machines, rankings shift dramatically under different scoring rules, and most tasks are already solved by at least one public submission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Leaderboard scores from performance-optimization benchmarks are increasingly used as evidence of coding-agent progress, but these aggregate scores can conflate multiple sources of noise — runtime instability, benchmark-specific scoring rules, and task saturation — leading to potentially misleading conclusions about agent capabilities.

Method: The authors conduct a three-part audit: (1) cross-machine replay of 740 official reference patches across four Google Cloud machine types, checking validity under original benchmark rules; (2) analysis of how eight public submissions' rankings change between GSO and SWE-fficiency scoring rules, including per-task weight decomposition; (3) examination of 10 public submissions per task to quantify how many tasks already have at least one submission matching or exceeding the reference patch.

Result: Cross-machine replay validity is low: 39/102 (GSO), 11/140 (SWE-Perf), and 411/498 (SWE-fficiency) tasks pass in all replays. SWE-Perf is especially fragile due to near-zero runtime changes. Rankings disagree on 9 of 28 pairwise comparisons between GSO and SWE-fficiency scoring rules; SWE-fficiency's rule assigns 58.5%–82.8% of total score weight to the worst ten tasks. At least one public submission matches/beats the reference on 85.3% of replay-valid tasks and beats the unoptimized baseline on 99.8%.

Conclusion: Leaderboard scores alone are insufficient for evaluating coding-agent progress. The paper recommends complementing aggregate rankings with task-level reliability signals, per-task score contribution analysis, and identification of remaining unsolved performance gaps to enable more robust and interpretable benchmarking.

Abstract: Repository-level performance-optimization benchmarks such as GSO, SWE-Perf and SWE-fficiency evaluate coding agents by applying patches to real repositories and comparing runtime against unoptimized baselines and official reference patches. Their leaderboard scores are increasingly used as evidence of coding-agent progress, but those scores can conflate runtime instability, benchmark-specific scoring rules, and how many tasks are already solved by at least one public submission. We audit these issues across the three benchmarks. First, we replay the official reference patches for 740 code optimization tasks across four common types of Google Cloud machines. Most benchmark tasks can be replayed, but their reference patches satisfy the original benchmark validity rules in every cross-machine replay for only 39/102 GSO tasks, 11/140 SWE-Perf tasks, and 411/498 SWE-fficiency tasks; SWE-Perf is especially fragile because many reference patches produce close-to-zero runtime changes. Second, we show that public submission rankings depend strongly on the benchmark scoring rule. Among eight public submissions shared by GSO and SWE-fficiency, the official rankings disagree on 9 of 28 pairwise submission comparisons, and SWE-fficiency's leaderboard scoring rule assigns the worst ten tasks overly high score weights of 58.5%-82.8%. Third, looking across 10 public submissions for each task, we find that at least one submission matches or beats the reference patch on 85.3% (384/450) of replay-valid GSO and SWE-fficiency tasks, and beats the unoptimized base code on 99.8% (449/450). Our study complements leaderboard scores by identifying tasks with more reliable performance signals, quantifying per-task score contributions, and exposing the remaining performance gaps that are hidden by aggregate rankings.

</details>


### [256] [RepoRescue: An Empirical Study of LLM Agents on Whole-Repository Compatibility Rescue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01213)
*Zhihao Lin,Mingyi Zhou,Zhensu Sun,Yizhuo Yang,Renyu Yang,David Lo,Li Li*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: RepoRescue is a benchmark for evaluating LLM agents on "compatibility rescue" — adapting old, abandoned repositories to modern runtime environments. Built from 193 Python and 122 Java repos, it evaluates multiple LLM agent systems, finding that systems are complementary (union reaches 62.7% pass rate), cross-file coordination is the key difficulty, and 22 of 34 candidate repositories work in realistic scenarios after rescue.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-source repositories frequently become non-functional as language runtimes and dependencies evolve, especially when maintainers abandon them. Unlike traditional bug repair (fixing code that was never correct), compatibility rescue addresses code that worked in its original environment but breaks due to ecosystem drift. No existing benchmark systematically evaluates LLM agents on this ecologically important and distinct task.

Method: The authors construct RepoRescue with 193 Python and 122 Java repositories, each verified to pass tests historically and fail after modernization. Agents receive only the repository and its failing modern environment, and must diagnose, locate, and patch code to restore the historical test suite. Evaluation includes: (1) full-patch pass rate, (2) source-only repair (removing test-file edits), (3) runtime-enforced blocking of test edits, (4) practical validation on real-world scenarios, and (5) bug-hunt with patches targeting the actual compatibility failure. Five agent systems are evaluated on Python, three on Java.

Result: Claude Code systems often edit tests even when instructed not to. With runtime blocking of test edits, Kimi achieves 41.5% rescue rate. Systems are complementary: their union reaches 62.7%, exceeding the best single system by 10.9 points. Cross-file coordination is the primary difficulty — on 14 repositories requiring whole-codebase changes, GPT-5.2 via Codex passes all 14, while every Claude Code system passes at most two. In practical validation, 22 of 34 Python candidates whose suites pass after rescue work in realistic scenarios; 12 pass bug-hunt.

Conclusion: RepoRescue establishes compatibility rescue as a distinct, challenging benchmark for LLM agents. Key findings include the complementarity of different agent systems, the critical bottleneck of cross-file coordination, the necessity of source-only auditing and runtime enforcement to prevent test-gaming, and the importance of practical validation beyond test suite passage. The benchmark provides reasoning labels to support future research.

Abstract: Open-source libraries and tools are widely reused, but compatibility maintenance is expensive. Once maintainers leave, useful repositories can stop working as runtimes and dependencies evolve. We study whether LLM agents can adapt old repositories to modern environments, a task we call compatibility rescue. Unlike bug repair, compatibility rescue starts from a repository that worked in its original environment but fails after ecosystem drift. RepoRescue gives agents only the repository and its failing modern environment; the agent must diagnose the failure, locate affected code, and produce a source-code rescue that restores the historical test suite. We build RepoRescue from 193 Python and 122 Java repositories, each verified to pass historically and fail after modernization. We evaluate five deployed agent systems on Python and three on Java. Beyond full-patch pass rate, we rerun patches after removing test-file edits to measure source-only repair, add a runtime-enforced regime that blocks test edits, and validate practical use for repositories whose suites pass after rescue. We find that Claude Code systems sometimes edit failing tests even when prompted not to; with runtime blocking, Kimi still rescues 41.5% of repositories. Systems are complementary: their union reaches 62.7%, exceeding the best single system by 10.9 points. Difficulty concentrates in cross-file coordination: on 14 repositories requiring coordinated whole-codebase changes, GPT-5.2 through Codex passes all 14, while every Claude Code system passes at most two. Finally, a passing suite is only an initial signal: among 34 unmaintained Python candidates whose suites pass after rescue, 22 work in realistic scenarios and 12 pass bug-hunt with patches that address the compatibility failure. RepoRescue benchmarks compatibility rescue with source-only auditing, runtime enforcement, practical validation, and reasoning labels.

</details>


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [257] [From "Strings" to "Things" for Personal Knowledge Graphs: Evaluating LLM Triple Extraction for Recommendation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00003)
*Abhirup Dasgupta,Fernando Spadea,Oshani Seneviratne*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: A reproducible pipeline using lightweight LLMs (Qwen/Gemma) extracts RDF-compliant, Wikidata-linked user-preference triples from conversational data to construct Personal Knowledge Graphs (PKGs), evaluated on both semantic fidelity and downstream recommendation utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constructing Personal Knowledge Graphs from unstructured, decentralized conversational data is challenging. PKGs offer privacy-preserving user preference modeling, but bridging the gap between raw conversational "strings" and structured semantic "things" requires an effective, reproducible extraction pipeline.

Method: The authors propose a reproducible pipeline that employs lightweight Large Language Models (Qwen- and Gemma-based) to extract structured user-preference triples linked to Wikidata identifiers from conversational data, producing RDF-compliant outputs for PKG construction. Evaluation measures both semantic extraction fidelity (triple quality) and downstream recommendation task utility.

Result: Certain lightweight models demonstrated strong triple extraction performance and achieved proportionally high downstream recommendation performance relative to their extraction fidelity.

Conclusion: Lightweight LLMs can effectively bridge conversational data and structured PKGs. Model performance in triple extraction correlates well with downstream recommendation utility, validating the pipeline's practical value for privacy-preserving user modeling.

Abstract: Personal Knowledge Graphs (PKGs) offer a privacy-preserving framework for modeling user preferences, yet constructing them from unstructured, decentralized conversational data remains a challenge. This paper bridges the gap between conversational "strings" and semantic "things" by presenting a reproducible pipeline for extracting structured user-preference triples using lightweight Large Language Models. We evaluate Qwen- and Gemma-based models on their ability to extract RDF-compliant triples linked to Wikidata identifiers from conversational data for PKG construction. Our evaluation assesses both the semantic extraction fidelity and the utility of the resulting graphs in a downstream recommendation task. We found that certain models performed well and had proportionally high downstream performance relative to their triple extraction performance.

</details>


### [258] [Why Advanced Encoders Lag on Sparse Retrieval? The Answer and an Approach to Bridging Vocabulary Gaps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00004)
*Zhichao Geng,Yang Yang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Modern foundation models underperform BERT-base in learned sparse retrieval due to a "Vocabulary Gap" — case-sensitive, raw vocabularies waste capacity on morphological noise. The authors propose Vocabulary Transfer (VT), a model-agnostic framework with Semantic Initialization and Activation Potential Calibration, enabling ModernBERT to achieve SOTA on BEIR (52.4 nDCG, +4.7) and fixing failures in models like RoBERTa-large.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern foundation models (e.g., ModernBERT) excel in dense retrieval but paradoxically lag behind the aging BERT-base in learned sparse retrieval (LSR). The authors identify the root cause as the "Vocabulary Gap": modern tokenizers use case-sensitive, raw vocabularies optimized for lossless reconstruction, which fragment single semantic units into multiple surface forms. This introduces morphological noise, wastes model capacity, and impairs lexical matching — a critical component for sparse retrieval.

Method: The authors first formalize the Vocabulary Gap theoretically, showing that appropriate vocabulary coarse-graining can tighten generalization bounds by reducing hypothesis class complexity while preserving semantic integrity. They then propose Vocabulary Transfer (VT), a model-agnostic framework with two key components: (1) Semantic Initialization, which leverages spatial topology to preserve the geometric structure when migrating to a normalized vocabulary, and (2) Activation Potential Calibration (APC), which aligns pre-trained manifolds with sparsity constraints to prevent dead neurons and dense collapse during fine-tuning.

Result: VT is universally effective: ModernBERT achieves state-of-the-art on BEIR (52.4 nDCG, a +4.7 improvement), failing models like RoBERTa-large are resuscitated, and the method generalizes to inference-free architectures and specialized domains.

Conclusion: The performance gap in learned sparse retrieval is not an architectural deficiency but a solvable vocabulary mismatch. Vocabulary Transfer provides a lightweight, model-agnostic solution that bridges this gap, enabling modern encoders to fully realize their potential in sparse retrieval settings.

Abstract: While advanced foundation models like ModernBERT significantly outperform older architectures in dense retrieval, they surprisingly lag behind the aging BERT-base baseline in learned sparse retrieval (LSR). We identify the root cause as the \textit{Vocabulary Gap}: modern tokenizers utilize raw, case-sensitive vocabularies designed for lossless reconstruction, which map single semantic units to redundant surface forms, wasting model capacity on morphological noise and hindering lexical matching. We formalize this intuition through a theoretical framework, demonstrating that appropriate vocabulary coarse-graining can tighten the generalization bounds by reducing complexity of the hypothesis class, provided that semantic integrity is preserved. To resolve this, we propose \textbf{Vocabulary Transfer (VT)}, a model-agnostic framework that migrates advanced encoders to sparse-friendly, normalized vocabularies with minimal computational cost.
  VT utilizes a novel \textbf{Semantic Initialization} via spatial topology to preserve geometric structure and an \textbf{Activation Potential Calibration (APC)} mechanism to align pre-trained manifolds with sparsity constraints, preventing the dead neuron and dense collapse observed in standard fine-tuning. Empirically, VT is universally effective: it enables ModernBERT to achieve state-of-the-art performance on the BEIR benchmark (\textbf{52.4} nDCG, a \textbf{+4.7} improvement), resuscitates failing models like RoBERTa-large, and generalizes seamlessly to inference-free architectures and specialized domains. These results confirm that the performance lag is not an architectural deficiency but a solvable vocabulary mismatch. We've released our code and models.\footnote{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/vocab-transfer/. All details included.}

</details>


### [259] [Topological Void Analysis A Mathematical Framework for Systematic Technical Innovation Discovery in Knowledge Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00005)
*Kris Pan*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Topological Void Analysis (TVA) formalizes innovation search as finding unoccupied regions in a hybrid embedding space, producing 2,128 candidates from ~140k documents with 90% passing automated filtering and yielding 191 REVISE and 1 APPROVE verdict under adversarial specialist review.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for identifying innovation opportunities in dense technical domains (keyword search, citation proximity, human intuition) fail to formalize the notion of an unexplored region that is both relevant to a target goal and genuinely absent from prior art. A principled mathematical framework for this search problem is lacking.

Method: TVA defines "topological voids" as triads (A, B, C) in a dense-sparse hybrid embedding space satisfying three criteria: (1) semantic cohesion of A and B with domain anchor C; (2) pairwise similarity within a calibrated marginality band (avoiding obvious combinations and noise); (3) a sparse lexical bridge exists while the geodesic midpoint on the embedding hypersphere remains unoccupied. Applied to ~140k indexed documents across 96 targets.

Result: TVA generated 2,128 invention candidates; 90% survived automated quality filtering. Four-specialist adversarial review yielded 191 REVISE and 1 APPROVE verdict (0.05% end-to-end approval rate). Two case studies confirmed the framework surfaces non-obvious connective tissue rather than merely obvious related pairs.

Conclusion: TVA provides a rigorous, scalable framework for computational invention candidate discovery in dense technical domains, surfacing non-obvious innovation opportunities that are overlooked by conventional search methods. The low but non-zero APPROVE rate demonstrates both stringent filtering and genuine discovery potential.

Abstract: Identifying where to innovate in a dense technical domain - such as operating systems or hardware/software co-design - is fundamentally a search problem in a high-dimensional knowledge space. Existing approaches rely on keyword search, citation proximity, or human intuition, none of which formalise the notion of an unexplored region that is simultaneously relevant to a target goal and absent from prior art.
  We present Topological Void Analysis (TVA), a mathematical framework that defines topological voids as triads (A, B, C) in a dense-sparse hybrid embedding space. A void requires three conditions: (i) both concepts A and B are semantically cohesive with domain anchor C; (ii) their pairwise similarity falls within a calibrated marginality band - avoiding both obvious combinations and unrelated noise; and (iii) they share a sparse lexical bridge while the geodesic midpoint on the embedding hypersphere is unoccupied.
  Applied to ~140k indexed documents, TVA generates 2,128 invention candidates across 96 targets; 90% survive automated quality filtering, yielding 191 REVISE and 1 APPROVE verdict from four-specialist adversarial review (0.05% end-to-end). Two case studies demonstrate the framework surfaces non-obvious connective tissue rather than merely obvious related pairs.

</details>


### [260] [BaRA: BFS-and-Reflection Web Data Collection Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00007)
*Soojeong Lee,Joseph Lee,Yongseong Cho,Sunjae Kim,Youngwoo Moon,Kyungwoo Song*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: BaRA is a web agent framework for site-level multimodal data collection that combines bounded BFS traversal with history-based self-reflection, outperforming existing methods on link discovery and downloadable media extraction under a fixed interaction budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based web agents for data collection fail on live websites by missing relevant pages, producing incomplete multimodal outputs, and returning non-downloadable media URLs. A structured approach is needed to improve coverage and download validity under constrained interaction budgets.

Method: BaRA integrates bounded breadth-first search (BFS) for systematic page traversal with a history-based self-reflection mechanism that allows the LLM agent to learn from past navigation decisions, all operating under a fixed interaction budget.

Result: Evaluated on 50 synthetic websites with ground-truth references and 3 public websites with cluttered/dynamic layouts, BaRA outperformed Pure LLM, SeeAct-Vision, and Browser-use baselines on link discovery and downloadable multimodal extraction, with the largest gains in download-valid image and video recovery.

Conclusion: The BFS-and-Reflection paradigm effectively addresses key failure modes of LLM web agents for data collection, particularly in ensuring downloadable multimodal outputs. Code is publicly available.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based web agents reduce manual scripting for web data collection, yet on live websites, they often miss relevant pages, return incomplete multimodal outputs, or return media URLs that are not directly downloadable. We present BFS-and-Reflection Agent (BaRA), a framework for site-level collection under a fixed interaction budget. The framework combines bounded breadth-first search (BFS) traversal with history-based self-reflection. We evaluate BaRA on 50 synthetic websites with ground-truth reference sets. We additionally test on three public websites with cluttered or dynamic layouts. BaRA outperforms Pure LLM, SeeAct-Vision, and Browser-use on link discovery and downloadable multimodal extraction, with the largest gains in download-valid image and video recovery. Our code is available at https://github.com/MLAI-Yonsei/BaRA-Agent.

</details>


### [261] [SchemaRAG: Dynamic Large Schema Reduction for LLM-driven Structured Information Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00008)
*Sin Yu Bonnie Ho,Arlie Coles,Erik Larsson,Eric Marshall,Nathan Bodenstab,Paul Vozila*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: SchemaRAG is a retrieval-augmented generation framework that dynamically prunes large output schemas for information extraction, improving micro-F1 by up to 8.8% while reducing latency by 47% and token costs by 48% on healthcare and e-commerce datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extracting structured data from text using LLMs becomes problematic when target schemas are large and complex. Including full schemas in prompts increases cost and latency, causes lost-in-the-middle performance degradation, and may exceed context length limits. A method is needed to selectively include only relevant schema portions.

Method: SchemaRAG is a retrieval-augmented generation framework that dynamically prunes the output schema space for schema-conditioned information extraction. It leverages schema metadata and few-shot examples (when available) to retrieve and include only the most relevant portions of a large schema in the LLM prompt.

Result: On real-world healthcare and e-commerce datasets, SchemaRAG achieves up to an 8.8% increase in micro-F1 score, a 47% reduction in latency, and a 48% reduction in token costs compared to full-schema baselines.

Conclusion: SchemaRAG demonstrates strong practicality for large-schema information extraction tasks, offering simultaneous improvements in both accuracy and efficiency by dynamically constraining the schema space presented to LLMs.

Abstract: Extracting structured data from unstructured text using large language models (LLMs) becomes challenging when target schemas are large and complex. In such cases, including the full schema in the prompt increases cost and latency, risks lost-in-the-middle performance degradation, and can exceed context length limits. We propose SchemaRAG, a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) framework that dynamically prunes the output schema space for schema-conditioned information extraction tasks by leveraging schema metadata and few-shot examples when available. We evaluate SchemaRAG on real-world healthcare and e-commerce datasets. Our results show that SchemaRAG can achieve up to an 8.8% increase in micro-F1, a 47% reduction in latency, and a 48% reduction in token costs, demonstrating its practicality for large-schema extraction.

</details>


### [262] [Prompt Optimization for User Simulation in Conversational Recommender Systems: A Multi-Objective Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00010)
*Nipun B Nair,Tongtong Wu,Weiqing Wang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: The paper proposes a framework to automatically optimize prompts for LLM-based user simulators in conversational recommender systems (CRSs), addressing issues of positive bias, data leakage, limited behavioral diversity, and reliance on manual prompt engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CRSs face two core obstacles: (1) evaluation through human studies is costly and time-consuming, and (2) training data is scarce due to privacy concerns. LLM-based user simulators can generate synthetic interactions to address both, but existing approaches suffer from systematic positive bias, data leakage, limited behavioral diversity, and dependence on brittle manual prompt engineering requiring domain expertise.

Method: An automatic prompt optimization framework for LLM-based user simulators in CRSs that simultaneously mitigates positive bias, data leakage, and limited behavioral diversity, eliminating the need for manual prompt engineering.

Result: The proposed framework achieves improved behavioral alignment with human interaction patterns compared to baseline methods across diverse prompt settings.

Conclusion: Automatically optimizing prompts for LLM-based user simulators is a viable and effective approach to generating high-fidelity synthetic CRS interaction data, offering a practical solution to the evaluation and data scarcity challenges in the CRS domain.

Abstract: Conversational recommender systems (CRSs) are a core component of next-generation intelligent recommender systems because they enable users to actively elicit preferences, clarify intentions, and adapt recommendations in real time. However, there are two key obstacles in the CRS domain: evaluation and access to training data. Evaluating CRSs through real human studies is more critical than for traditional recommender systems, yet such studies are both costly and time-consuming. Moreover, CRS interaction data are often difficult to obtain for model training due to privacy concerns. Large language model (LLM)-based user simulators have shown promise in addressing both challenges by generating synthetic user interactions for evaluation and training. However, existing approaches suffer from systematic positive bias, data leakage, and limited behavioral diversity, and they rely on brittle manual prompt engineering that requires extensive domain expertise. In this paper, we propose a framework to automatically optimize prompts for LLM-based user simulators in CRSs, simultaneously mitigating these issues. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves improved behavioral alignment with human interaction patterns compared to baseline methods across diverse prompt settings.

</details>


### [263] [SkillSelect-Serve: Budget-Controllable and QoS-Aware Skill Service Recommendation and Composition for Small LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00011)
*Jingyuan Zheng,Dongjing Wang,Xin Zhang,Butian Huang,Haiping Zhang,Dongjin Yu,Shuguang Deng*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: SkillSelect-Serve is a budget-controllable, QoS-aware framework that transforms LLM agent skill selection from fixed top-k retrieval into a structured service recommendation and composition problem, using dual-granularity utility modeling to improve bundle recall and utility under same-budget constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reusable skill libraries for LLM agents treat skills as retrievable documents with fixed top-k lists, ignoring structured service attributes like dependencies, costs, risks, and QoS. This naive retrieval fails to optimize for budget constraints or compositional trade-offs among selected skills.

Method: The framework consists of three components: (1) raw skills are represented as structured Skill Services with functional descriptions, dependencies, context cost, risk, and QoS attributes; (2) a Micro-Agent Requirement Planner converts natural-language tasks into structured service requirements; (3) a dual-granularity utility modeling approach performs skill-level marginal suitability estimation and bundle-level calibration to balance coverage, redundancy, cost, and risk trade-offs, with a shared discovery backbone retrieving candidates from a large registry.

Result: Experiments on 35,353 skills and 586 task queries demonstrate that SkillSelect-Serve consistently outperforms fixed top-k retrieval baselines in both same-budget bundle recall and mean utility.

Conclusion: Formulating skill selection as a structured service composition problem with budget controllability and QoS awareness yields superior performance over document-retrieval-style approaches, enabling more efficient and effective LLM agent skill orchestration.

Abstract: Reusable skill libraries are becoming important infrastructure for large language model (LLM) agents, yet existing selection methods often treat skills as retrievable documents and return fixed top-k lists. This paper presents SkillSelect-Serve, a budget-controllable and QoS-aware framework that formulates agent skill selection as Skill Service Recommendation and Composition. SkillSelect-Serve represents raw skills as structured Skill Services with functional descriptions, dependencies, context cost, risk, and QoS-related attributes. A local Micro-Agent Requirement Planner converts natural-language tasks into structured service requirements, while a shared discovery backbone retrieves candidate services from a large registry. The framework then performs dual-granularity utility modeling with skill-level marginal suitability estimation and bundle-level calibration for coverage, redundancy, cost, and risk trade-offs. Experiments on 35,353 skills and 586 task queries show that SkillSelect-Serve consistently improves same-budget bundle recall and mean utility over fixed top-k retrieval baselines.

</details>


### [264] [PRA-RAG: Provably Robust Aggregation in Retrieval-Augmented Generation against Retrieval Corruption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00012)
*Xue Tan,Yi Zheng,Chang Huo,Yunruo Zhang,Yu Liu,Hao Luan,Zhuyang Yu,Xiaoyan Sun,Ping Chen,Jun Dai*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: PRA-RAG is a provably robust retrieval aggregation algorithm that defends against poisoning attacks in RAG systems by sampling multiple retrieved text combinations and using embedding geometry to identify a stable, robust subset, achieving attack success rates as low as 1% with 71% accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG systems are vulnerable to poisoning attacks on retrieved texts, and existing defenses lack theoretical robustness guarantees and fail when the LLM has limited knowledge of the retrieved content. A provably robust defense mechanism is needed.

Method: PRA-RAG samples multiple combinations of retrieved texts and exploits geometric structures in the embedding space to identify a robust subset of retrievals. From this subset, a stable aggregated representation is derived. The method provides theoretical bounds on the maximum impact of poisoned content and a quantitative robustness measure.

Result: Experiments across multiple benchmarks and RAG architectures show PRA-RAG reduces attack success rate to as low as 1% while maintaining 71% accuracy, significantly outperforming state-of-the-art methods.

Conclusion: PRA-RAG offers a principled, provably robust defense against retrieval poisoning in RAG, combining theoretical guarantees with strong empirical performance, and establishes a quantitative measure of RAG robustness.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) enhances Large Language Models (LLMs) by incorporating external knowledge, effectively mitigating their inherent knowledge limitations. However, RAG remains vulnerable to poisoning attacks that manipulate retrieved texts to mislead model outputs. Existing defense mechanisms often lack theoretical robustness guarantees and perform unreliably when the LLM has limited knowledge of the retrieved content. In this work, we propose PRA-RAG, a provably robust retrieval aggregation algorithm designed to defend against poisoning attacks on retrieved texts. PRA-RAG samples multiple combinations of retrieved texts and utilizes geometric structures in the embedding space to identify a robust subset, from which a stable aggregated representation is derived. We provide theoretical bounds on the maximum impact of poisoned retrieved content and establish a quantitative measure of RAG's robustness. Experiments across multiple benchmarks and RAG architectures demonstrate that PRA-RAG reduces the attack success rate to as low as 1% while maintaining an accuracy of 71%, significantly outperforming representative state-of-the-art methods.

</details>


### [265] [GRACE-RAG: Governed Retrieval Architecture for Canonical Evidence Synthesis, Enabling Lightweight Deployment in Closed-Domain Institutional Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00013)
*Asit Desai,Aman Kumar,Prashant Devadiga*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: GRACE-RAG is a graph-augmented RAG architecture that shifts structural reasoning from generation-time to retrieval-time, enabling lightweight self-hosted models to achieve up to 20% quality improvements in entity-dense, multi-document QA settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In institutional QA with entity-dense domains, vector-only retrieval produces fragmented evidence across heterogeneous documents, forcing models to resolve structural ambiguity during inference. This increases reliance on large proprietary models and incurs high computational cost and latency.

Method: GRACE-RAG introduces a retrieval-governed, graph-augmented architecture that resolves structural ambiguity offline at the retrieval layer. It constructs structured representations linking distributed entities and documents, externalizing structural reasoning from generation to retrieval. The system is calibrated to closed-domain institutional vocabulary and deployable on self-hosted lightweight models.

Result: Experiments across Mistral 24B, GPT OSS 120B, and Gemini 2.5 Flash show consistent improvements in completeness, depth, and anticipatory coverage. Mid-scale models achieved up to 20% overall quality gains, demonstrating that retrieval architecture — not model scale — governs structural quality.

Conclusion: By moving structural reasoning into the retrieval layer, GRACE-RAG reduces dependence on large proprietary models and lowers computational/latency footprint, making high-quality institutional QA feasible on lightweight, self-hosted deployments.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are widely used in institutional question answering settings where responses must be grounded in authoritative documentation (Gao et al., 2023). In entity-dense domains where relevant information is distributed across heterogeneous documents, vector-only retrieval often produces fragmented evidence and increases dependence on inference-time reasoning (Zhao et al., 2024). This paper introduces GRACE-RAG, a retrieval-governed, graph-augmented RAG architecture that externalizes structural reasoning from the generative stage to a structured retrieval layer, resolving structural ambiguity offline, enabling deployment on self-hosted lightweight models calibrated to closed-domain institutional vocabulary. Experiments across three model capacities: Mistral 24B, GPT OSS 120B, and Gemini 2.5 Flash show consistent improvements in completeness, depth, and anticipatory coverage, with overall quality gains of up to 20% under mid-scale models, indicating that retrieval architecture governs structural quality over model scale, reducing computational and latency footprint without dependence on proprietary systems.

</details>


### [266] [Libra: Training the Environment for Agentic Information Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00016)
*Xuan Zhao,Andy Chiu,Gengyu Wang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Libra is a self-evolving framework that optimizes an LLM agent's working environment by introducing mutable hierarchical Markdown catalogs into code repositories. Through an iterative loop of synthetic query generation, solver navigation, and catalog healing, Libra achieves logarithmic improvements in code localization accuracy, transfers zero-shot across LLMs, and enables a minimalist agent to outperform state-of-the-art baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While significant research has focused on optimizing LLMs themselves via synthetic data, the agent's working environment (the repository structure) has been neglected as an optimization target. The authors identify that poor repository organization hinders code localization—a critical capability for agentic LLM systems—and propose optimizing the environment itself rather than the model.

Method: Libra introduces mutable "catalogs"—hierarchical Markdown indices embedded within a repository. It employs a three-role optimization loop: (1) a Prompter generates synthetic queries from the repository, (2) a frozen Solver attempts to localize the relevant code by navigating the catalogs, and (3) a Healer rewrites/restructures the catalogs when the Solver fails. This loop runs iteratively, incrementally refining the environmental scaffolding.

Result: Evaluated on 12 SWE-bench Lite repositories, Libra demonstrated continual, logarithmic improvements in code localization accuracy. The environmental optimizations transferred zero-shot across different LLM solvers and problem sets without retraining. A minimalist coding agent using Libra-optimized catalogs outperformed state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: Environmental optimization via self-evolving catalogs is a viable and complementary approach to model-centric optimization for agentic LLM systems. The zero-shot transferability suggests that investing in repository organization yields broad, reusable benefits across different models and tasks.

Abstract: Information localization within massive repositories is a cornerstone of agentic LLM systems. While synthetic data-driven optimization has proven successful in training LLMs, little attention has been paid to optimizing the agent's working environment (the repository itself) in a data-driven manner. To bridge this gap, we present Libra, a self-evolving framework that introduces mutable "catalogs" (hierarchical Markdown files serving as navigable indices) into the repository. Libra runs an LLM-driven optimization loop where a Prompter generates synthetic queries, a frozen Solver attempts to resolve them by navigating the catalogs, and a Healer rewrites the catalogs in response to the Solver's localization failures. Evaluations across 12 SWE-bench Lite repositories demonstrate that this environmental healing yields continual, logarithmic improvements in code localization accuracy. Furthermore, these environmental improvements transfer zero-shot across different LLMs and problem sets. Although the focus of this paper is to study the general behavior of such a system, we also demonstrate that a minimalist coding agent equipped with Libra-optimized catalogs outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. Code is available at https://github.com/salesforce-misc/Libra and data at https://huggingface.co/datasets/Salesforce/Libra.

</details>


### [267] [Learning User-Aware Recall: Personalized Retrieval in Long-Term Conversational Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00017)
*ZhiShu Jiang,Haibo Liu,Xin Shen,Guanqiang QI,Chenxi Miao,Weikang Li,Liwei Qian,Xin Pei,Jizhou Huang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: PPRO introduces a retrieval-centric framework for long-term conversational agents that uses user profiles derived from accumulated memories to guide personalized memory ranking, and trains a query rewriter with Group Relative Policy Optimization using both retrieval and answer quality as feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current memory-augmented LLM agents rely on query-centered similarity or fixed ranking rules for memory retrieval, neglecting user-specific relevance. Memory is only useful when the right evidence is recalled for the right user, necessitating user-conditioned retrieval.

Method: PPRO builds episodic and semantic memory banks from dialogue histories and derives explicit user profiles from accumulated memories. The profile serves as a personalized prior in memory ranking, accounting for stable user attributes, preferences, and relationships. A query rewriter is trained using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), with feedback from both evidence retrieval quality and downstream answer quality, while keeping memory banks and the answer model frozen.

Result: Experiments on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S show consistent improvements over both training-free memory systems and training-based baselines. Ablation studies confirm that both profile-guided ranking and retrieval-oriented rewriting contribute substantially to performance.

Conclusion: Retrieval optimization, specifically through user-profile-guided ranking and learnable query rewriting, is a key factor in achieving effective personalized long-term memory for conversational agents.

Abstract: Long-term conversational agents are expected to remember past interactions, but memory is useful only when the right evidence is recalled for the right user. Existing memory-augmented LLM agents have made progress in building compact memory banks, yet retrieval is still often driven by query-centered similarity or fixed ranking rules, leaving user-conditioned relevance underexplored.To address this gap, we propose Profile-guided Personalized Retrieval Optimization (PPRO), a retrieval-centric framework that makes memory retrieval both user-aware and optimizable.PPRO builds episodic and semantic memory banks from dialogue histories and derives a user profile from accumulated memories.The profile serves as an explicit personalized prior in memory ranking, allowing retrieval to account for stable user attributes, preferences, and relationships.PPRO further trains a query rewriter with Group Relative Policy Optimization, using both evidence retrieval quality and downstream answer quality as feedback while keeping the memory banks and answer model fixed.Experiments on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S show consistent gains over training-free memory systems and training-based baselines.Ablation studies further show that both profile-guided ranking and retrieval-oriented rewriting contribute substantially to performance, highlighting retrieval optimization as a key factor in personalized long-term memory use.

</details>


### [268] [Aligning Sentence Embeddings to Human Concepts via Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00023)
*Wonseok Shin,Songkuk Kim*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: The paper proposes using Top-k Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) to decompose dense sentence embeddings (e.g., from E5) into interpretable, disentangled features corresponding to semantic, syntactic, and pragmatic categories. It further introduces an activation steering mechanism that clamps specific latent features to re-rank retrieval results, enabling transparent and steerable neural retrieval without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense sentence embeddings used in RAG systems suffer from feature superposition, making them opaque and difficult to align with human intent. This lack of interpretability hinders analysis and control over retrieval processes.

Method: The authors apply Top-k Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) to disentangle dense representations from sentence transformers such as E5 into human-interpretable concepts. They then introduce an activation steering mechanism that clamps specific latent features to intervene directly in retrieval, enabling re-ranking of search results according to user constraints.

Result: Disentangled features align with identifiable semantic, syntactic, and pragmatic categories. Activation steering successfully re-ranks search results to better match user constraints without requiring retraining of the backbone model.

Conclusion: SAE-based decomposition provides a viable approach for achieving transparent and steerable neural information retrieval, bridging the gap between model opacity and user-aligned retrieval behavior.

Abstract: Dense sentence embeddings are fundamental to modern Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems but suffer from a lack of interpretability due to feature superposition. This opacity hinders the alignment of retrieval processes with human intent, as the entangled representations are difficult to analyze or control. In this work, we propose a method to disentangle the dense representations of sentence transformers (e.g., E5) into human-interpretable concepts using Top-k Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs). We demonstrate that these disentangled features align with specific semantic, syntactic, and pragmatic categories. Furthermore, we introduce an activation steering mechanism that allows for precise intervention in the retrieval process. By clamping specific latent features, we show that it is possible to re-rank search results to better align with user constraints without retraining the backbone model. Our findings suggest that SAE-based decomposition offers a viable path toward transparent and steerable neural information retrieval.

</details>


### [269] [AGE: Adaptive-masking for Graph Embedding in Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00052)
*Bao Long Nguyen Huu,Atsushi Hashimoto*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: AGE introduces an adaptive-masking self-supervised learning method that aligns graph embeddings with text-based LLM latent spaces by predicting non-key nodes via a learnable sampler, significantly improving GraphQA performance across four benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GraphRAG suffers from misalignment between graph-structured latent features and text-based latent features in (frozen) LLMs. Existing graph representation methods fail to bridge this modality gap, limiting the effectiveness of graph-augmented retrieval for LLMs.

Method: AGE uses a Transformer-based architecture (similar to text embedding encoders) trained via mask-based self-supervised learning. Crucially, it employs a learnable node sampler that adaptively avoids masking key nodes (which are hard to predict from context and cause SSL inefficiency), instead focusing on predicting non-key nodes to learn better aligned graph embeddings.

Result: AGE significantly improves non-parametric search-based approaches in GraphQA tasks, achieving superior accuracy across four benchmark datasets with distinct characteristics.

Conclusion: Adaptive masking that accounts for node importance is critical for effective graph-to-text latent space alignment. AGE provides a practical solution for integrating graph-structured knowledge into frozen-LLM RAG pipelines.

Abstract: GraphRAG is an extension of retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) that supports large language models (LLMs) by referring to graph-structured data as external knowledge. While this technique ideally captures intricate relationships, it often struggles with graph representations for LLMs, particularly for frozen LLMs, due to the misalignment between graph-based and text-based latent features. We tackle this issue by introducing the {\it Adaptive-masking for Graph Embedding (AGE)}. AGE employs a Transformer in a mask-based self-supervised learning (SSL) approach. We designed the architecture similar to text embedding encoders, addressing the latent feature misalignment. In contrast to natural language texts, graphs are concise representations, and there exist {\it key nodes} that hold dominant contextual information, which are challenging to predict from their surroundings. Masking such key nodes leads to inefficiency in the SSL process. Therefore, AGE focuses on predicting nodes apart from key nodes, utilizing a learnable node sampler. Our experimental results indicate that AGE significantly improves approaches using non-parametric search component in GraphQA tasks, achieving superior accuracy across four benchmark datasets with distinct characteristics.

</details>


### [270] [Attribute-Prompted Kernel Hashing for Unsupervised Data-Efficient Cross-Modal Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00379)
*Runhao Li,Xiaoxu Ma,Zhenyu Weng,Yue Zhang,Guibo Luo,Huiping Zhuang,Zhiping Lin,Yap-Peng Tan*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: APKH introduces a data-efficient unsupervised cross-modal hashing method that leverages vision-language foundation model attribute priors, using kernel-based mapping and smoothed contrastive alignment to improve generalization from seen to unseen categories under limited paired data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unsupervised cross-modal hashing methods require large-scale image-text pairs and overfit to seen training data, limiting generalization to unseen categories. Collecting well-aligned pairs is costly and constrained by privacy/specialization concerns.

Method: APKH proposes two modules: (1) Context-optimized Attribute Kernel Mapping (CAKM), which uses hyperspherical RBF kernel mapping with prompt-learning-optimized dynamic attribute kernels to capture modality-invariant semantics; (2) Kernel-Smoothed Contrastive Alignment (KSCA), which models limited paired data as continuous kernel distributions to smooth the modality gap and mitigate overfitting to sparse pairwise correlations.

Result: APKH outperforms state-of-the-art hashing methods in cross-modal retrieval tasks, particularly in data-constrained scenarios where generalization from seen to unseen categories is required.

Conclusion: APKH effectively addresses the data-efficiency and generalization limitations of unsupervised cross-modal hashing by exploiting foundation model attribute priors through kernel-based alignment and distributional contrastive learning.

Abstract: Unsupervised cross-modal hashing enables efficient retrieval of semantically related instances across different modalities without requiring manual semantic annotation. However, existing unsupervised methods rely heavily on large-scale image-text pairs. Collecting such data can be costly, particularly in scenarios where well-aligned pairs are scarce due to privacy and specialized constraints. More critically, existing methods tend to overfit to seen training data, restricting their generalization performance on unseen categories that the constrained training data cannot cover. To address these limitations, we propose Attribute-Prompted Kernel Hashing (APKH), a novel data-efficient approach that constructs a compact, modality-aligned Hamming space driven by the generalized attribute priors of vision-language foundation models. Specifically, APKH introduces two core modules: Context-optimized Attribute Kernel Mapping (CAKM) and Kernel-Smoothed Contrastive Alignment (KSCA). CAKM formulates cross-modal alignment through hyperspherical Radial Basis Function kernel mapping, optimizing dynamic attribute kernels via prompt learning to capture modality-invariant semantics. Furthermore, KSCA extends conventional point-to-point contrastive learning by modeling limited paired data as continuous kernel distributions. This explicit smoothing of the modality gap alleviates overfitting to sparse pairwise correlations.
