Extensive experiments demonstrate that APKH outperforms state-of-the-art hashing methods in the challenging cross-modal retrieval tasks from seen to unseen categories under data-constrained scenarios.

</details>


### [271] [Real-Time Hard Negative Sampling via LLM-based Clustering for Large-Scale Two-Tower Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00448)
*Ivan Ji,Liuyi Hu,Harrison,Zhao,Lei Huang,Qunshu Zhang,Max,Fan,Aameek Singh*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: A self-supervised hard negative sampling method using LLMs is proposed for two-tower retrieval models, generating challenging negatives from same-cluster items in real time. It outperforms standard in-batch/out-of-batch negative sampling, reduces popularity bias, and breaks feedback loops in industrial-scale recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard negative sampling methods in two-tower models (in-batch and out-of-batch) typically yield easy negatives that models rapidly overfit, failing to provide sufficient training challenge. This limits model discriminative power and exacerbates popularity bias and feedback loops in recommendation systems.

Method: A self-supervised hard negative sampling framework that uses a large language model (LLM) to learn media representations and generate hard negatives drawn from the same semantic cluster as the positive item. The sampling occurs in real time during training, integrates seamlessly into production two-tower architectures, and scales to billions of training examples with minimal computational overhead.

Result: The proposed technique outperforms widely used industry negative sampling baselines on public datasets. In large-scale online deployment, it also demonstrates the ability to break inherent feedback loops and significantly reduce popularity bias in industrial recommendation systems.

Conclusion: LLM-guided hard negative sampling within clusters provides a scalable, effective enhancement to two-tower retrieval training, yielding both improved accuracy and fairer recommendations by mitigating popularity bias and feedback loops.

Abstract: The two-tower model has been widely used for large-scale recommendation systems, particularly in the retrieval stage. Industry standards for training two-tower models typically involve in-batch and/or out-of-batch negative sampling. However, these methods often produce easy negatives that models can quickly learn, failing to sufficiently challenge the model. To address this issue, a novel self-supervised hard negative sampling technique is proposed that leverages a large language model (LLM) to generate hard negatives from the same cluster during model training. By utilizing the LLM to learn media representations, the proposed approach ensures that the generated negatives are more challenging and informative. This real-time sampling framework is designed for seamless integration into production models, capable of handling billions of training data points with minimal computational complexity. Experiments on public datasets, along with deployment to a large-scale online system, demonstrate that the proposed negative sampling technique outperforms widely used industry methods. Furthermore, analysis in industrial applications reveals that this sampling method can help break inherent feedback loops in recommendations and significantly reduce popularity bias.

</details>


### [272] [When RAG Meets Query Planning: Logical Query Trees for Resolving Exploratory Reasoning Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00508)
*Ganlin Xu,Linghao Zhang,Zhitao Yin,Hongda Xi,Chen Yang,Jiaqing Liang,Weijia Lu,Sihang Jiang,Yanghua Xiao,Deqing Yang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: PlanRAG introduces a framework that models exploratory reasoning problems (ERPs) as logical query trees (LQTs), using dynamic programming with a multi-dimensional cost model to decompose complex queries into atomic sub-queries, then executes iterative aggregation, rewriting, retrieval, and generation over the tree structure with parallelization. It outperforms existing iteration-based and graph-based RAG systems on the new WikiWeb-ERP dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RAG systems fail on exploratory reasoning problems (ERPs) — complex queries with high uncertainty and ambiguity that require unclear reasoning paths. These queries suffer from retrieval noise and error accumulation, and lack an end-to-end planning mechanism for generating effective reasoning trajectories. The authors draw inspiration from database query planning to address this gap.

Method: PlanRAG models ERPs as Logical Query Trees (LQTs). It first decomposes ERPs into atomic queries, then organizes them into LQTs via dynamic programming guided by a cost model spanning multiple complementary dimensions. Execution proceeds through iterative aggregation, rewriting, retrieval, and generation over the LQT, processing nodes concurrently and propagating intermediate results upward. Further parallelization across multiple threads improves efficiency.

Result: PlanRAG outperforms state-of-the-art iteration-based and graph-based RAG systems on the newly constructed WikiWeb-ERP dataset, demonstrating the effectiveness of the LQT-based formulation for optimizing natural language queries.

Conclusion: Modeling ERPs as logical query trees with cost-guided dynamic programming construction and parallelized tree execution provides a novel and effective formulation for tackling complex, ambiguous queries in RAG, outperforming existing approaches and establishing a new direction for natural language query optimization.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) effectively grounds large language models (LLMs) in external knowledge but struggles with \textbf{exploratory reasoning problems (ERPs)} that are the complex queries involving high uncertainty and ambiguity. Resolving ERPs requires complex reasoning with unclear paths, tending to result in retrieval noise and error accumulation. Furthermore, the absence of an end-to-end planning mechanism makes it difficult to generate effective trajectories for ERPs. Motivated by database query planning, we introduce \emph{PlanRAG}, an RAG framework that models ERPs of natural language as \textbf{logical query trees (LQTs)}. However, translating ERPs into LQTs is non-trivial due to representation and optimization gaps between structured SQL and unstructured natural language, making it highly challenging to construct high-quality LQTs. To address these problems, we first decompose ERPs into atomic queries and then organize them into LQTs using dynamic programming guided by a cost model involving multiple complementary dimensions. Finally, we execute iterative aggregation, rewriting, retrieval, and generation over LQTs, processing nodes concurrently and propagating intermediate results upward, with further parallelization across multiple threads for efficiency. Our experimental results show that PlanRAG outperforms state-of-the-art iteration-based and graph-based RAG systems on our newly constructed dataset, \textbf{WikiWeb-ERP}, thereby providing a new formulation for optimizing natural language queries. Our source code and dataset are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/PlanRAG-main-B2C8/.

</details>


### [273] [As It Was: Aligning LLM Search Evaluation with Historical User Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01040)
*Ali Vardasbi,Gustavo Penha,Enrico Palumbo,Claudia Hauff,Hugues Bouchard,Mounia Lalmas*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: The paper introduces a behavior-grounded LLM judge for search relevance evaluation that augments SERP items with Query-Relevance-Impressions (QRI) cards summarizing historical user interactions, improving alignment with user preferences by 5% overall (91% on disagreement cases) and increasing correlation with human judgments by 15% across five languages in music search at Spotify.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-a-judge approaches for SERP relevance evaluation can drift from actual user preferences when relying solely on semantic similarity or world knowledge, especially for ambiguous or long-tail queries. Human QA cannot scale with rapidly evolving large-scale search systems, creating a need for evaluation methods grounded in real user behavior.

Method: The authors propose augmenting each SERP item with a lightweight, auditable Query-Relevance-Impressions (QRI) card — a compact summary of how users historically interacted with similar queries and results. This behavioral prior serves as citable empirical evidence that the LLM judge can reference alongside semantic reasoning to resolve ambiguity. Evaluation was conducted across 6,000 recomposed SERPs at Spotify using relevance estimates from historical user interactions, a multilingual human-judged dataset spanning five languages, and outcomes from a live A/B test.

Result: The behavior-grounded judge improved Spearman rank correlation by ~5% overall, with a 91% relative improvement on disagreement cases compared to an ungrounded LLM judge. On the multilingual dataset, correlation with human relevance judgments increased by 15%. Against live A/B test outcomes, the grounded judge showed consistently higher alignment with the observed winning model, though absolute alignment remained moderate.

Conclusion: Lightweight behavioral grounding via QRI cards can meaningfully improve the reliability and practical usefulness of LLM-based evaluation in real-world search systems, making it a viable complement to semantic reasoning for relevance judgment at scale.

Abstract: Large-scale search systems evolve faster than human quality assurance can scale, especially for long-tail intents and multilingual queries. LLM-as-a-judge approaches provide a scalable alternative for evaluating the relevance of search engine result pages (SERPs), but judgments based solely on semantic similarity or world knowledge can drift from actual user preferences, particularly for ambiguous queries. We introduce a behavior-grounded LLM judge that augments each SERP item with a lightweight and auditable behavioral prior in the form of a Query-Relevance-Impressions (QRI) card. Each card summarizes how users have historically interacted with similar queries and results, providing compact empirical evidence that the judge can cite to resolve ambiguity and make more consistent relevance judgments while still relying on semantic reasoning. In a large-scale music search evaluation at Spotify, using relevance estimates derived from historical user interactions across 6,000 recomposed SERPs, the behavior-grounded judge achieves stronger alignment with user preferences, improving Spearman rank correlation by approximately 5% overall and yielding a 91% relative improvement on disagreement cases. On a multilingual human-judged dataset spanning five languages, grounding further increases correlation with human relevance judgments by 15%. Importantly, when evaluated against outcomes from a live A/B test, the grounded judge shows consistently higher alignment with the observed winning model. While absolute alignment remains moderate, these findings demonstrate that lightweight behavioral grounding can improve the reliability and practical usefulness of LLM-based evaluation in real-world search systems.

</details>


### [274] [MemSyco-Bench: Benchmarking Sycophancy in Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01071)
*Zhishang Xiang,Zerui Chen,Yunbo Tang,Zhimin Wei,Ruqin Ning,Yujie Lin,Qinggang Zhang,Jinsong Su*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: MemSyco-Bench is a benchmark that evaluates memory-induced sycophancy in LLM agents — the tendency of agents to over-align with retrieved user memories at the expense of factual accuracy and objective reasoning — across five tasks testing when and how memory should appropriately influence decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory enables LLM agents to become long-term collaborators, but retrieved memories can induce sycophancy — agents over-aligning with user preferences/statements even when they conflict with facts or objective evidence. Existing memory benchmarks only assess storage, retrieval, and update correctness, neglecting how retrieved memories affect downstream reasoning and decision-making. This gap motivates MemSyco-Bench.

Method: The authors construct MemSyco-Bench, a benchmark spanning five evaluation tasks: (1) rejecting memory as factual evidence when it contradicts ground truth, (2) respecting the applicable scope of memory, (3) resolving conflicts between memory and objective evidence, (4) tracking memory updates, and (5) using valid memory for personalization. The benchmark measures two dimensions: when memory should influence a decision and how valid memory should be used.

Result: The abstract does not report quantitative experimental results; it focuses on introducing the benchmark's design, taxonomy, and resource availability.

Conclusion: MemSyco-Bench fills a critical evaluation gap by providing the first comprehensive benchmark for memory-induced sycophancy, enabling the community to systematically assess and mitigate this risk in memory-augmented LLM agents.

Abstract: Memory has emerged as a cornerstone of modern LLM-based agents, supporting their evolution from single-turn assistants to long-term collaborators. However, memory is not always beneficial: retrieved memories often induce a critical issue of sycophancy, causing agents to over-align with the user at the cost of factual accuracy or objective reasoning. Despite this emerging risk, existing memory benchmarks primarily evaluate whether memories are correctly stored, retrieved, or updated, while overlooking how retrieved memories influence downstream reasoning and decision-making. To bridge this gap, we propose MemSyco-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating memory-induced sycophancy in agent systems. MemSyco-Bench measures when memory should influence a decision and how valid memory should be used. Specifically, it covers five tasks that assess whether agents can reject memory as factual evidence, respect its applicable scope, resolve conflicts between memory and objective evidence, track memory updates, and use valid memory for personalization. All related resources are collected for the community at https://github.com/XMUDeepLIT/MemSyco-Bench.

</details>


### [275] [Trie-based Experiment Plans for Efficient IR Pipeline Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01162)
*Irene Anu,Craig Macdonald*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: PyTerrier uses a trie data structure to create efficient experiment plans for comparative evaluation of cascading retrieval pipelines, achieving a 26% reduction in experiment duration on MSMARCO v2, and validated through a user study with students.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Comparative evaluation of cascading search pipelines is computationally expensive due to repeated components across experiments. There is a need for a more efficient experiment planning approach that eliminates redundant computation when evaluating multiple pipeline configurations.

Method: The authors propose formulating comparative pipeline experiments using a trie data structure, where shared pipeline prefixes are computed once and reused across multiple experimental configurations, replacing the traditional sequential linear evaluation plan.

Result: A demonstration experiment on MSMARCO v2 using BM25, MonoT5, and DuoT5 showed a 26% reduction in experiment duration. A user study with undergraduate and postgraduate students further validated the usability of the trie-based experiment plans.

Conclusion: Trie-based experiment planning significantly improves evaluation efficiency for cascading retrieval pipelines in PyTerrier, reducing redundant computation and experiment duration without sacrificing comparative rigor.

Abstract: Search engines are often formulated as cascading pipelines, where successive stages combine the results of different retrievers, and iteratively refine the ranking of candidate documents to obtain a final ranking, which can be presented to a user, or provided as context to an LLM. Such pipelines can be complex to evaluate in an end-to-end manner, necessitating measurement of Recall of early stages, and Precision of later stages, which are often interchangeable. PyTerrier is ideal for building and evaluating cascading retrieval pipelines, due to its declarative nature for pipeline construction and wide ecosystem of retrievers and rerankers. However, comparative evaluation of pipelines can be expensive due to repeated components. In this work, we describe the use of a trie data structure to formulate an experiment plan for comparative pipeline experiments that enhances experiment efficiency compared to a sequential "linear" plan. Empirically, on a demonstration experiment involving BM25, MonoT5 and DuoT5 on MSMARCO v2, we observe a 26% reduction in experiment duration. Finally, we report on a user study of undergraduate and postgraduate research students' use of the experiment plans.

</details>


### [276] [Diffusion-GR2: Diffusion Generative Reasoning Re-ranker](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01170)
*Zhuoxuan Zhang,Kangqi Ni,Yuhang Chen,Mingfu Liang,Xiaohan Wei,Yunchen Pu,Fei Tian,Chonglin Sun,Frank Shyu,Adam,Song,Sandeep Pandey,Luke Simon,Tianlong Chen,Xi Liu*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Diffusion-GR2 converts an autoregressive reasoning re-ranker into a block-diffusion model, addressing structural and distributional accuracy gaps via conversion fine-tuning, on-policy distillation, and RL, achieving 2.4–3.5× inference speedup while recovering near-parity accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive (AR) generative reasoning re-rankers are accurate but slow at inference because each reasoning token requires a sequential forward pass. Block-diffusion language models offer substantial speedups via parallel decoding, but naively converting an AR re-ranker introduces two accuracy gaps: (1) a structural gap where parallel denoising produces invalid rankings (duplicates, drops, out-of-set IDs), and (2) a distributional gap from off-policy fine-tuning on fixed teacher trajectories.

Method: The authors propose Diffusion-GR2, a three-stage recipe: (1) Conversion Fine-Tuning (CFT) adapts the AR-initialized diffusion model to autonomously denoise answers into valid permutations without an external constrained decoder; (2) On-Policy Distillation (OPD) supervises the model on its own decoded trajectories using dense per-token targets from the AR teacher; (3) a Reinforcement Learning (RL) stage fine-tunes against a re-ranking reward on top of the OPD policy.

Result: On Amazon Beauty, Diffusion-GR2 recovers to near-parity with the AR re-ranker while increasing decode throughput by 2.4–3.5× at the model's reasoning output length. Ablations show CFT recovers most of the conversion gap, and OPD further closes the residual gap to the AR reference.

Conclusion: Diffusion-GR2 successfully bridges both structural and distributional gaps when converting AR re-rankers to block-diffusion models, enabling substantial inference speedups with minimal accuracy degradation. CFT alone addresses the bulk of the accuracy loss, with OPD and RL providing further refinement.

Abstract: Generative reasoning re-rankers achieve strong recommendation accuracy by emitting a chain-of-thought before re-ordering a candidate list, but they are slow at inference: an autoregressive (AR) decoder spends one sequential forward pass per reasoning token, and the reasoning trace far exceeds the ranking it produces. To reduce this cost, block-diffusion language models decode many positions in parallel over a few denoising steps and are substantially faster, yet naively converting an AR re-ranker into one opens two accuracy gaps: (1) a structural gap: answer positions are denoised in parallel and scored independently, so the decoder emits invalid rankings (duplicated, dropped, or out-of-set identifiers) that AR avoids through left-to-right masking; and (2) a distributional gap: fine-tuning the converted model on fixed teacher trajectories is off-policy relative to its own decoding at inference, leaving a residual accuracy gap. To close both gaps while keeping the speedup, we propose \textbf{Diffusion-GR2}, a recipe that converts our AR reasoning re-ranker (GR2) into a block-diffusion re-ranker. First, conversion fine-tuning (CFT) adapts the AR-initialized diffusion model to denoise the answer into a valid permutation on its own, without an external constrained decoder. Next, on-policy distillation (OPD) then supervises the model on its own decoded trajectories with dense per-token targets from the AR teacher. Finally, we apply a reinforcement-learning (RL) stage against a re-ranking reward on top of OPD's on-policy policy. Experiments on Amazon Beauty demonstrate that Diffusion-GR2 recovers to near-parity with the AR re-ranker, while block-parallel decoding raises decode throughput by $2.4$--$3.5\times$ at the model's reasoning output length. Ablations show that CFT recovers most of the conversion gap, and that on-policy distillation further closes it to the AR reference.

</details>
